## To the Acts of the Montbéliard Colloquy Published at Tübingen: The First Part of Theodore Beza’s Response

Second edition.

Whatever you do, act prudently and consider the end.

Geneva: printed by Jean Le Preux, 1588.

[Part I | PDF page 7 | logical source scan 6 | unpaginated notice on title verso]

## Theodore Beza to the Christian Reader: Grace and Peace from the Lord

Lest anyone wonder that this second edition of the present response does not correspond to the former one in precisely the same words everywhere, be advised, Christian reader, that because of the shortness of time the former edition was dictated, taken down, printed, and finally dispatched to the Frankfurt fair in such haste that, besides some typographical errors noted at the end of the book, as is customary, many other serious errors could not be prevented. Not a few things were also omitted; indeed, an entire leaf which ought to have been cancelled was inserted in place of another that had been omitted, as happened on page 187. I therefore thought that the whole writing had to be reviewed by me, yet in such a way that not even the least thing in the actual substance or judgments has been changed. Let all these things, however, be submitted to the judgments of all truly learned and orthodox theologians, and especially to that of a free, holy, and lawful synod, if the Lord ever grants us one.

[Part I | PDF page 8 | logical source scan 7 | printed page 3]

## Theodore Beza’s Preface to His Response to the Acts of the Montbéliard Colloquy Published at Tübingen

The acts of that conference of mine with Jacob Andreae have indeed been published by the authority of the most illustrious prince and lord, Lord Frederick, Count of Württemberg and Montbéliard, but at the counsel, urging, and persuasion of Dr. Jacob Andreae, since the things which he wished inserted in them originated from no one else. My business, therefore, is with him, not with the most illustrious prince, whose singular humanity and earnest zeal in seeking the peace of the churches my own experience does not permit me to doubt. As for the reasons which Andreae had for persuading the most illustrious prince to authorize this publication, let him see to them. But neither truth nor my reputation permits me to conceal the reasons which I believe I have for making the most just complaint about this same matter.

First of all, as regards the occasions of this colloquy, Dr. Andreae should know that Lord Baron de Clervant, the King of Navarre’s envoy to the German princes, testifies that what Andreae reports about him—as though he had promoted this colloquy before the king—is utterly empty. He had no mandate concerning it, nor had he learned from anyone that anything had already begun to be arranged at Montbéliard. Indeed, when conversation at Tübingen happened to turn to the establishment of peace among the churches, something so greatly desired by all good people, and Andreae said something about this particular colloquy, the same Lord Baron de Clervant testifies that he urged something far different: namely, that a synod of several learned and godly men chosen by each side should instead be convened by the authority of the princes. “For,” he said, “this conference between two private per-

[Part I | PDF page 9 | logical source scan 8 | printed page 4]

-sons will have either no weight or certainly very little for settling controversies so great.” Andreae, joking in his accustomed fashion, replied in these very words, using Lord de Clervant’s own brother as interpreter between them: “I am called the pope of Germany, and Beza the pope of Gaul. If these two popes agree, the rest of the bishops will easily assent.” Thus he joked in his accustomed manner about the papacy which he fastens upon me, but spoke all too truthfully about his own. For what has this man not dared against the churches belonging to others throughout all Germany?

The whole matter stands as follows. More than four years have passed since discussion of this matter began. Dr. Andreae was the instigator, as most honorable men at Montbéliard wrote to me at the time. For my part, I certainly did not shrink from the pursuit of peace, which I would willingly purchase even with my own life; but I already foresaw then the very thing which has now happened. Then, last winter, when I was sick in body and thinking of nothing less in mind, word was brought to me by nobles then at Montbéliard that I would gratify both the most illustrious prince and the exiles themselves if I could consent to this colloquy. I refused, adding reasons to which most of them readily assented. Soon, however, the same request was repeated. Lest I should perhaps be doing wrong here, I consulted the neighboring churches most closely united with this one, Zurich and Bern, and asked counsel from my fellow ministers. They all replied that, as far as Dr. Andreae was concerned, no good seemed to be expected from this colloquy; but since the most illustrious prince was said to desire it so greatly, his will should be obeyed, because perhaps this undertaking would open a way to obtaining some moderation of these quarrels.

Thus, with the consent of our most distinguished magistrates of Bern and Geneva—to whom, as to me, the most illustrious prince had already written about this matter—Dr. Abraham Musculus of Bern and I reached Montbéliard, with companions assigned to us both from each republic and from our college, in the most bitter weather on March 20. I for my part came most unwillingly, even for the

[Part I | PDF page 10 | logical source scan 9 | printed page 5]

reason that at almost the same time Dr. Andreae, by publishing a most poisonous and plainly bloodthirsty writing, had sufficiently disclosed his disposition toward all our churches. It could therefore rightly appear that he sought nothing else from this colloquy than, by renewing the conflict on this occasion, to deter the German princes from furnishing aid to the godly in France who were suffering the worst extremities—people whom these men have been accustomed to call the most loathsome heretics from their pulpits.

Nevertheless, after being received with the greatest kindness by the most illustrious prince, we began to arrange the procedure of the colloquy. I do not know who composed the account of this matter as it is reported on pages 10 and 13 of these Acts. No notary was chosen or employed by both sides. But this I know: it never slipped from me that this conference had been instituted at the request of the French exiles. I had believed that the whole undertaking originated from no source other than the prince’s own holy desire; and those who transfer so Christian an action to others seem greatly to detract from his reputation. I knew well enough that my Frenchmen had no need at all of conferences of this kind. They pressed me to go there for no other reason than partly to gratify the most illustrious prince and partly lest I appear to evade the disputation because I distrusted the goodness of our cause.

What is omitted from that account, however, is of greater importance. At the outset we requested that everything on both sides be conducted in writing and signed in the disputants’ own hands. When this displeased Dr. Andreae, who preferred that the business be conducted orally, I requested that statements on each side be taken down by approved notaries selected by the consent of both parties, so that each party might afterward review them and confirm them by adding its signature. This was certainly a just and honest procedure for conducting a conference and would prevent falsification of the kind which I had learned about from the twofold publication of the protocol of the Maulbronn disputation. But Andreae would not admit even this and expressly testified that he would not depart so much as a hair’s breadth from the doctrine which he had professed up to that point. I then turned to Dr.

[Part I | PDF page 11 | logical source scan 10 | printed page 6]

Musculus and my other colleagues and said, “Why, then, have we come here?” We already had the most just cause to refuse every conference. Yet, fearing once again that we might seem to have seized opportunities to depart and to have trifled with the most illustrious prince, we accepted the condition contained in these Acts. So much for these matters.

I come now to the reasons for this publication contained in the preface of the most illustrious prince—whether dictated by Dr. Andreae or by someone else. There are exactly two: the benefit of the students of the school at Tübingen and the necessity of refuting calumnies. Both would be entirely just if they were as true as the most illustrious prince was persuaded they were. I most humbly beg his Highness to hear my response with his gracious leave.

As regards the first reason, it is more than sufficiently known to all the churches what Dr. Andreae’s position is in these controversies, and what the position is not merely of me alone but of all the churches of the Helvetic Confession. Moreover, nothing at all is contained in this disputation which has not already been stated a thousand times by each side, most fully, in so many other published writings. What, then, could publication of these Acts accomplish except what we see has happened, namely, to provide ambitious and contentious scribblers with a new occasion for renewing that conflict?

But come: let it have been permissible to publish the theses presented by both sides and circulating in people’s hands, in order to guard against falsehood. Yet by what right could Andreae interpret them according to his own mind by adding glosses in the margin? And if in the disputation itself he sufficiently refuted Beza, who—as he says—defended his ungodly dogmas by nothing but obstinacy and mere glosses, why did he add those further marginal “refutations,” as he calls them, except to lay upon me a necessity of replying so that the conflict might grow raw again? Since I rightly persuade myself that this plan was utterly foreign to the mind of the most illustrious prince, far be it from me to suppose that it proceeded from him. But since I could not obtain a proceeding conducted with trustworthy notaries chosen by

[Part I | PDF page 12 | logical source scan 11 | printed page 7]

both sides, the most illustrious prince will, I hope, permit me to ask those who suggested this publication to him by what right, authority, and conscience—while I was alive and unconsulted—they were permitted to publish as authentic and trustworthy things which were hastily taken down by I know not whom, as the matter itself shows. People are not accustomed to proceed in this fashion even against defendants convicted of a capital crime. Yet they wished the most illustrious prince publicly to testify that nothing at all had been inserted into or removed from this collection of the Acts on which the hinge of the controversies turned.

In saying this I do not prick the conscience of any one of those copyists. I say only that I have all too just reasons for trusting neither Osiander’s hand nor that of the superintendent of Montbéliard, although I judge that of all those present they were best able to understand and record what was being said. Finally, everyone knows how much can often depend upon the omission or alteration of even a single little word. How, then, was Andreae permitted to examine, weigh, and polish his own words—as the matter itself proves he did—while publishing mine into people’s hands mutilated, interpolated, and diminished at his pleasure? Even if we grant that this was lawful, why was it permissible not even then to leave my words untouched, but to add observations and refutations to them?

Indeed, since we expressly agreed among ourselves—as appears on page 564 of this very edition to which I now reply—that those excerpts should possess no force or authentic authority as a protocol, I could with the best right deem them all unworthy of any answer. But I see Andreae and his followers interpreting the request we made at our departure as though, distrusting our defense, we had feared his publication. How false and absurd this is can be established from the very fact that we requested, but could not obtain, that everything be taken down by notaries and afterward confirmed by the disputants’ signatures. Come, then: although drawn into it most unwillingly, I will not shrink from even this labor, at least to some degree.

[Part I | PDF page 13 | logical source scan 12 | printed page 8]

I now turn to the calumnies which are said to have imposed upon the most illustrious prince the necessity of this publication. Some have been directed against the Highness of the prince himself and others against the cause itself. As to the former, if any of them proceeded in any way from me, or from anyone else with my knowledge, I do not plead against being held a slanderer and paying the penalty for that crime.

As for that letter, I call God, the Best and Greatest, to witness that it was neither written nor published here or anywhere else, directly or indirectly, with my knowledge. I did not see it until a certain bookseller of ours brought back two copies of it, and no more, from the Frankfurt fair. I likewise testify that, as was fitting and as I had promised when departing, I everywhere proclaimed, both orally and in letters, the most illustrious prince’s singular humanity in receiving and treating us in his castle and his tireless attentiveness in hearing both sides, joined with the greatest fairness. That letter, therefore, has absolutely nothing to do with me.

The preface of the most illustrious prince adds that I ought to have prevented the letter from being circulated publicly among our people, as though by conniving at it I had betrayed my agreement with its author, whoever he is—for even now I do not know for certain who he is. Whoever suggested this to the most illustrious prince sinned through great thoughtlessness both against the prince himself and against me. For how could I, stationed at Geneva, have prevented things being done in the interior of Germany while I knew absolutely nothing of them and was thinking of nothing less? How could I have wrested from anyone’s hands a letter already dispersed everywhere at that most celebrated fair? Whether those three little pages were offered for sale here, as God loves me, I know absolutely nothing; nor have I ever seen them in anyone’s hands. If someone thinks that I ought at least to have opposed some written piece by which to kill that letter, let him know that I do not have so much leisure from my own affairs as to spend any time reading such scribblings, much less refuting them. And even if

[Part I | PDF page 14 | logical source scan 13 | printed page 9]

it were otherwise, who am I to have imagined that the Highness of the most illustrious prince needed any defense of mine before anyone, when his own dignity and greatness protect him sufficiently? Finally, as for the things said to have been spread by our side in various rumors and writings, to the injury of the other side and indeed of truth itself, I have not the least fear that I can be found guilty of this accusation.

Whether anyone imagined it possible to prevent different people from writing different accounts of this publicly held Colloquy according as they were inclined toward this side or that, I do not know. It has certainly never seemed possible to me to accomplish what no emperors or kings have ever been able to obtain by edicts, however severe. Among the reasons why I long refused this conference, this was not the least: I foresaw that these things would necessarily follow immediately from it. Those inconveniences could have been met by the one suitable procedure we proposed at the outset: everything should be taken down by selected notaries and afterward, if necessity demanded, published in good faith. Since we could not obtain this, we thought that sufficient precaution had been taken against the evil if both parties agreed—as indeed they did agree and each side promised—that whatever private persons had excerpted should possess none of the force and authority of a protocol.

Yet since this hope and safeguard has failed me through no fault of mine, and since Andreae has seen fit by this publication to kindle writings of this sort as though by pouring oil upon them, what was I to do? When many such reports were conveyed to me from various places, I first asked my friends to allow them all to vanish of themselves with the passage of time. I gave a restrained account of how we had parted, testifying that I could not believe certain things so absurd and slanderous were being spread by Andreae’s own will. What more? When I had received from afar certain more definite and more unjust writings about these events, I thought it necessary to admonish Andreae himself by letter to restrain his Tübingen men by his authority. Finally, since I could rightly have no doubt that he had received the letters which I had sent him about this matter,

[Part I | PDF page 15 | logical source scan 14 | printed page 10]

and since he made no reply to them, I did what necessity itself compelled. That is, in letters written to friends I denied certain most manifest lies and endeavored to refute intolerable calumnies. As God loves me, I would not have done even that if I had received any suitable reply from Andreae. I am not ashamed of those letters, and I am prepared to publish them too if there is need. And lest I appear to be inventing anything here, behold a letter written on this subject on May 24 by a certain student at Tübingen. Let everyone understand whether I had more just causes for offense than anyone else—causes which I nevertheless set entirely behind the peace of the churches.

“A disputation and colloquy was instituted concerning five disputed articles: concerning idols; predestination; the person of Christ; infant baptism; and the sacrament. Agreement was easily reached on the first. On the second Beza spoke for an hour and a half and put forward those opinions of his concerning God’s counsel in adjudging some men to eternal life and some to eternal death without regard either to faith or unbelief. These were heard patiently and refuted at length by Dr. Jacob. Little was done concerning the other matters, but Beza endeavored to confirm what had been written in the books of the antagonists. I have no more. I add this: Beza departed from the colloquy in tears. The result was that the Prince of Montbéliard, who had previously inclined toward the Calvinist party, having perceived and heard Beza’s blasphemy, began to shudder at, detest, and execrate that dogma.

“Six French pastors attended the Colloquy. When they heard Dr. Jacob produce certain difficult sayings which Daneau had written during these past few years and which Beza confirmed, they said those things were unknown to them and carefully dissembled. From this it is sufficiently clear that our antagonists profess dogmas which cannot bear the light of truth, of which they themselves are ashamed, and which they offer to foreigners like deadly poisons; unless men abstain from contact with them, they take poor care of themselves. But where am I wandering? And so forth. I write only this: our doctors commonly proclaim that Luther of holy memory said that the Devil took his beginning from the Sac-

[Part I | PDF page 16 | logical source scan 15 | printed page 11]

-rament of the Supper as his material, starting from there, so that through this flank he might pierce the remaining heads of doctrine in which the greatest weight lies. But these things are being conducted in the theological court, in the sight of the whole Church. I do not know how those who speak so impiously about the blessed flesh of Christ, the instrument of our salvation, and about the sacred mysteries do not fear its judgment, and so forth.”

So writes this man, whoever he is. From his words everyone may understand with what empty rumors certain people filled the ears of the curious and malicious. One may also see that the occasion seized by the person who published that other letter at the Frankfurt fair was neither false nor fabricated.

“Agreement was easily reached concerning idols,” he says. Indeed! Our answer on that matter has been published by these very men, and I appeal to it. “Beza put forward those opinions of his concerning God’s counsel in adjudging some men to eternal life and some to eternal death without regard either to faith or piety.” Foul mouth! Who ever heard either that statement or anything like it from me?

What I maintained—on the basis not of one passage but of almost innumerable passages of Scripture, and especially Augustine’s very well-known disputations against the Pelagians—was that the foreseeing of faith does not in the order of causes precede God’s eternal decree concerning the election and preservation of his own, but is subordinate to it. That is, no one is destined for eternal life because God foresaw that he would believe. On the contrary, all who are elected and believe are elected and believe precisely because God, from his eternal and gratuitous purpose, grants this to them when he calls them at his appointed time.

On the opposite side I said that unbelief and its fruits do not indeed arise from God’s purpose—for salvation is from God, but the destruction of the reprobate is from the wicked themselves. Yet unbelief does not precede but is subordinate to God’s eternal decree concerning leaving the reprobate in their native curse, because God condemns no one in whom he does not find the cause for which he is condemned. Whoever does not confess the latter is manifestly a Pelagian and is refuted by all Scripture and the orthodox Church. Once it is confessed,

[Part I | PDF page 17 | logical source scan 16 | printed page 12]

the former also follows necessarily from the doctrine of opposites. Is this, then, to teach either that those destined from eternity for salvation in Christ are saved without any regard for the faith to be granted them in its proper time, or, conversely, that those outside Christ who were appointed by him from eternity to eternal death—he who would wrong no one even if he destroyed every single person—are condemned without any regard for original sin and its fruits in adults?

But what can one do with men who not only do not know, but do not even wish to know, either the sequence and connection of the opposed causes of salvation and condemnation or the distinction between God’s decree, which in order precedes all secondary causes, and the execution of that decree? In this controversy concerning the Lord’s Supper they regard Luther as the supreme and irrefragable theologian; yet in the doctrine of predestination—which is assuredly the foundation of the doctrine of justification by faith, and therefore of all Christianity—they do not perceive that through our side Luther is openly pierced by them as impious and blasphemous.

“Beza departed from the Colloquy in tears.” A great proof that Beza was vanquished! I certainly departed in tears more than once, moved by pity for so many wretched peoples and grieving from my heart for the lot of the most illustrious prince himself and of all too many others. As for this man’s statement that the most illustrious prince, for whom I pray with all my heart for every prosperous and happy thing, had previously inclined “toward the Calvinist party,” as this factious man speaks, I do not know whether it will please his Highness.

But from this I infer that what was openly reported at Tübingen before this meeting was not wholly empty: Andreae had determined that this journey and conference must certainly be procured and hastened in order to turn away from Calvinism the prince who lived among the French exiles. What the same writer relates about the six French pastors—that they pretended certain difficult sayings of Daneau were unknown to them—was devised in the same workshop of lying. For we all acknowledged Daneau, who has deserved so well of God’s Church, as a truly learned and godly brother and colleague. In the matters which Andreae condemned as blasphemous in

[Part I | PDF page 18 | logical source scan 17 | printed page 13]

him, I dare say that Daneau, having been treated altogether unjustly, needed my defense no more than truth itself did. Finally, would that those men did not prove Luther’s prophecy all too true whom we see to have slipped from the dogma of consubstantiation into a ubiquity of Christ’s flesh more monstrous than any Eutychianism, and to cling so obstinately to that infernal mire that they will not allow even the learned and sounder defenders of that same consubstantiation to draw them out from it, whether by arguments or rebukes.

Here is also another letter written from Strasbourg on May 15, full of the most shameless lies:

“Dr. Theodore Schnepf and Dr. Johann Brenz came to us on a friendly visit. They related to us what took place at Montbéliard between Dr. Jacob Andreae and Beza, in almost precisely the same words in which the superintendent of Montbéliard had previously related that Colloquy to Dr. Pappus. Namely, the Count of Württemberg, at whose expense that Colloquy was instituted and whom the Calvinists had previously tried to turn from the way of truth, was now more strongly confirmed in the true conviction of sound doctrine. The Bernese envoy also publicly affirmed that he had never believed their theologians defended so empty a doctrine, one resting merely upon glosses on sayings drawn from the fathers, while ours stand upon the foundation of Scripture itself.

“When Beza interceded with the Count of Württemberg for the Calvinists, so that the exercise of their religion might be permitted to them at Montbéliard, the count altogether refused and replied that it would not be lawful for him to allow teachings of a different religion to be spread under his protection. Finally, this also occurred: on the feast of Easter more than forty men who had previously adhered to the Zwinglians celebrated the holy Supper together with the other defenders of the pure religion. It is reported that this disputation or Colloquy is now being printed at Basel through the work of Dr. Johann Jakob Grynaeus. If, after their custom, they report anything with less than perfect accuracy, our men will doubtless be stirred to publish a true account of it as well. Given at Strasbourg, [12] May 1586.”

What, however, is this liber-

[Part I | PDF page 19 | logical source scan 18 | printed page 14]

-ty of inventing anything whatever? Let his Highness himself testify whether the French exiles tried to turn the most illustrious prince from the way of truth. That what these men report escaped Mr. Samuel Meyer, senator of Bern and a most distinguished man, is no more true than that those who dared invent it are good men. But the calumny which follows is all the more intolerable than this fabrication because it touches more people: namely, that about forty Frenchmen who had previously adhered to those whom they call Zwinglians—that is, who adhered to the French Confession—celebrated the holy Supper with the other defenders of pure religion after that Colloquy was concluded, supposedly recalled to the way of truth by Andreae’s arguments.

Hear, on the contrary, what I answer you both truly and deservedly. You, Schnepf, and you, Brenz, have gravely wronged men most noble both by birth and by piety. All of them, together with the most honorable matrons—indeed, the entire city of Montbéliard—will easily be able to convict you of a falsehood most manifest and especially unworthy of theologians. And the man who is the more highly praised among all the learned theologians of this age in proportion as you are more insulting toward him, namely Dr. Johann Jakob Grynaeus, will without difficulty convict you of the utterly empty fabrication which you here fasten upon him. What he never even thought of doing, your own doctors have in fact dared to perpetrate: hiding beneath the name of the most illustrious prince, they published as though authentic, and contrary to their own signatures, their excerpts of the acts of this conference which they had refused to allow notaries to record. Therefore they can allege no just cause why the false accusation against Grynaeus should not deservedly convict both you and them.

To those two letters I add a third, written by Osiander all the way to Wittenberg in just as many words—and I had almost said, lies:

“To the Reverend Dr. Polycarp Leyser, pastor of the Church of Wittenberg.

“Dr. Jacob Andreae and I were recently summoned to Montbéliard. There Dr. Jacob and Beza appeared and, in the presence of the most illustrious Prince, Count Frederick, and about thirty French exiles,

[Part I | PDF page 20 | logical source scan 19 | printed page 15]

a disputation was held for several days, but no peace was made. For Beza persisted in these errors: that the body of Christ before the last day is only in heaven and nowhere on earth in any manner whatsoever; and therefore that the body of Christ cannot be received by the mouth, either by the worthy or by the unworthy, and that he allows no manner of eating except either a spiritual or a Capernaïtic one. In the person of Christ he allows no communication of idioms except a verbal one, and he understands everything said in Holy Scripture concerning the communication of the properties of the divine nature only of created benefits.

“He contends that Paul improperly calls baptism the washing of regeneration and did not withdraw from that error. For he asserted that baptism is only the sealing of regeneration and that innumerable children are not regenerated in baptism. Among other things Beza said that even when the elect commit crimes against conscience, they lose neither the Holy Spirit nor faith. Although Dr. Jacob brought forward David’s fall—David having lived in the greatest security before the Prophet Nathan’s rebuke—Beza nevertheless did not hesitate to assert that in that fall David lost neither the Holy Spirit nor faith. When the distinction between mortal and venial sins was brought before him, he received it with a Sardonic laugh.

“In the disputation about predestination Beza spoke for more than an hour and a half and mentioned only a single testimony of Scripture, and that merely incidentally. He said that in his eternal counsel before the creation of the world God had found no other means of declaring his mercy—which presupposed misery—and displaying his justice unless there were someone capable of falling, and that in this way he would make known his mercy toward the elect and justice toward the reprobate. Nor did he retract or attempt to explain those propositions of his which we presented to him in writing, by which he asserted that the reprobate were destined, created, and ordained for eternal damnation. Nor did he yield to the testimonies of Holy Scripture which were opposed to him, but persisted in his ungodly opinion.

“With him were Abraham Musculus, pastor of the Church of Bern; a certain doctor, a physician from Lausanne; and another man, a professor of Greek. These men listened, for the speaking parts were entrusted to Dr. Jacob and Beza. Thus the conference ended without peace being made. Although they sought our brotherhood in writing and orally, we neither would nor could assent unless they cast away their errors.”

[Part I | PDF page 21 | logical source scan 20 | printed page 16]

“And although the most illustrious Prince, Count Frederick, has always abhorred the dogmas of the Calvinists, he nevertheless expressly confessed before his own people that he had never believed such great impiety lay hidden under the Calvinist dogma, and that throughout his whole life he would diligently take care not to fall into these labyrinths.

“We also hope that some fruit of this colloquy will flow to the French hearers. For they listened most attentively and doubtless noticed Beza’s evasion. After the Lord’s Supper and the person of Christ had been disputed and the topic of predestination was proposed to him, he presented a written petition, together with his companions, asking permission to return home. He alleged the Easter holidays, which at other times they observe with little concern. I wished to relate these things briefly to Your Reverence, and I hope this recollection will not be unwelcome to your reverend lordship. Fare well and happily.”

Here, Osiander, I am compelled to remonstrate with you and to show my readers how much cause I have to doubt your faithfulness in writing down my words, since you, among the others, and the German superintendent of Montbéliard nearest you took down my speeches most attentively. For this letter of yours is like an epitome of all the material collected among you, which you afterward transferred partly into these Acts and partly into your observations written in the margin.

It is indeed true that Beza said, and says, that Christ’s flesh is now really nowhere else than above the heavens, into which he ascended and from which it will return only on the last day. Whoever denies this denies Christ, either directly or by necessary consequence. But when you add that Beza said it “is not now on earth in any manner whatsoever,” you write slanderously. You cannot be ignorant that our people are accustomed to add that, although Christ’s flesh is not present on earth in its essence—a qualification you craftily omitted—it is nevertheless most present to believers by the power of his Spirit.

Although it remains in heaven while we are upon earth, separated from the very substance of Christ’s flesh—which even now is truly finite, circumscribed, and organic—by as great a distance as the earth is removed from the abodes of the blessed situated above all the heavens, nevertheless by that secret

[Part I | PDF page 22 | logical source scan 21 | printed page 17]

and ineffable power of the Holy Spirit Christ himself truly becomes ours, through the true intervention of that flesh of his which suffered for us; and we in turn are joined to him spiritually through the instrument of faith, so that he lives the spiritual life in us and we in him. For by that spiritual union we adhere to him in an altogether different way—that is, without any conjunction of parts—than these visible members, fitted together among themselves, are attached to the head of each human body, from which they receive natural sensation and movement. Yet we are no less truly, and even more effectually, joined to Christ by a spiritual bond, so that we now draw from him every spiritual sensation and movement and at last, on the final day, receive blessed immortality in our very bodies.

This, I say, you ought to have expressed, in order to forestall the calumny of you and your party, who declaim most falsely that we empty Christ’s testament, leave behind bare sacred signs, are worse than the Turks themselves, and deny God’s omnipotence. As though the mystery of this spiritual—not imaginary, but most true and effectual—union with Christ, which Paul therefore calls great in Ephesians 5, were not far more wonderful and more fitting to God’s omnipotence than that thing of yours: finite and infinite at once, visible and invisible, ubiquitous and local, finally a body without the definition of a body, possessing every majesty. By it both the hypostatic union and the truth of Christ’s body are abolished; his ascension above the heavens is changed into a disappearance, or an outward spectacle only as far as the clouds—as Brenz dared to write in those very words on page 167 of his admirable *Recognition*; and his return from heaven is erased, since to return is not to become visible from invisible, but to become present from truly absent. By it, finally, heaven is mixed with earth and the unworthy are imagined, though to no benefit, nevertheless truly and properly to partake of Christ. All these conclusions follow from your dogma as necessarily as daylight follows day.

Next, what sort of calumny is this? “Beza,” you say, “allows no communication of idioms in the person of Christ except a

[Part I | PDF page 23 | logical source scan 22 | printed page 18]

verbal one.” When you wrote this, either you did not know what you were writing or you employed remarkable sophistry. If by “communication of idioms” you mean that mode of predication by which, in concrete terms, a property of one nature is attributed to Christ when he is named from the other nature—as when it is said that the God of glory was crucified, and that the Son of Man, while speaking with Nicodemus on earth, was in heaven—then it is utterly false for you to say that I treated this predication in the person as merely verbal. That would have been sheer Nestorianism.

But if the predicate in concrete locutions of this kind is taken in its proper signification—for “God of glory” properly signifies nothing other than God and “Son of Man” nothing other than man—then I said, and say, that this phrase or mode of predication is verbal, not real. That is, what the words properly sound forth is not to be transferred to the thing itself, because sheer Eutychianism would follow from it. Yet this predication is not false, because, on account of the unity of the person, in locutions of this kind—which the ancients called by various names—the whole person is denominated concretely from only one of the natures.

But predications in which the idioms of one nature are attributed abstractly to the other—as when you say that the Godhead suffered or that Christ’s flesh is everywhere, or omnipresent—I affirmed, and affirm, to be neither verbal nor real but false, ungodly, and blasphemous; yet you are not ashamed to assert them, to the great disgrace of the Christian name. Then with what shame do you again ascribe to me what follows? “He understands everything said in Holy Scripture about the communication of the properties of the divine nature only of created benefits.” What riddle, I ask you, is this? Nevertheless I will interpret it as far as I can.

If by the term “communication” you understand the hypostatic union itself, as the ancients often speak, this Nestorian impiety must be attributed not to me but to Dr. Brenz and Jacob Andreae. For Brenz writes in these very words on page 40 of his *Recognition*: “The difference between Christ and Peter must be placed in the fact

[Part I | PDF page 24 | logical source scan 23 | printed page 19]

that the Son of God, although in his essence he fills Peter just as he does the man Christ, nevertheless communicates to Peter not all his properties but only some.” And on page 131: “The difference between Christ and other men does not properly consist in the indwelling of the Son of God in the Son of Man”—and behold, from Nestorius you again have two Sons—“but in the communication of properties by which the Son of Man is adorned by the indwelling Son of God.”

And Andreae writes in his *Apology against the Theses of Ingolstadt*, page 24: “All things are sustained subsistentially by God.” According to Andreae, then, let all beasts—and indeed, horrible to say, the demons themselves—be subsistentially God. And on page 25: “The man Christ is called God, not because God is in him—for he is in all creatures, even according to their subsistences—but because in the assumption of the flesh he gave this man all power in heaven and on earth.” And again: “The divinity of the man Christ is not that substantial and eternal essence common with the Father and the Holy Spirit, but one communicated by the second hypostasis.”

And lest anyone take these statements as if in an orthodox sense he meant that the hypostasis neither of the Father nor of the Holy Spirit, but of the Word alone, was incarnate—for one hypostasis differs from another in reality, whereas in the divine being a hypostasis differs from the essence in concept, not reality—he immediately adds: “To state it in one word, when we say that the man is God, we understand nothing other than that the man sits at God’s right hand. For to assume the flesh into God is nothing other than that he communicated all gifts to it.” German princes, cities, and churches, do you hear these monstrosities and allow yourselves to be bewitched any longer?

But if, Osiander, by “communication of the properties of the divine nature” you understand not the hypostatic union of the natures itself but the gifts poured into the very flesh of Christ, which was personally or subsistentially assumed as their subject—the gifts which John understands by the Spirit without measure in John 3:34—then you assuredly speak the truth. For all those gifts are merely created qualities. And when you fasten this upon me as an error, you strike yourself, as you have just understood from Andreae, who assigns to Christ an indwelling divinity—that is, a deity—no different from that assigned to any man. What of the fact that the same man writes even more openly on page 18 that “the Apostle

[Part I | PDF page 25 | logical source scan 24 | printed page 20]

understood by ‘the form of God,’ according to which Christ is said to be equal to God, gifts really poured into the assumed flesh.” These impieties are Nestorian in their separation of the natures and Eutychian in their confusion of the properties, and you now strive in vain to paint them over with the color of Chemnitz—that is, with Monothelite pigment.

Let us proceed. “Beza contended that Paul improperly calls baptism the washing of regeneration and did not withdraw from that error; and he would have baptism be only the sealing of regeneration.” Again you slander me, omitting the distinction between external baptism, which consists of water, and internal baptism, which consists of Christ giving the Holy Spirit. We are not the authors of this distinction; Christ himself is, and Peter followed Christ in it.

I therefore rightly and orthodoxly denied that external baptism, considered by itself and separately, is properly called the washing through which we are regenerated. You cannot affirm this without transferring, at least in part, to the element of water or to the minister who baptizes what belongs incommunicably to the divine power alone and is proper to it in the fourth mode, as they speak in the schools. Finally, you cannot think otherwise without agreeing with the idolatrous papists in the doctrine of the work worked. But if you join both baptisms together, then assuredly let anyone be accursed who denies that baptism, because of and through God its author, is properly and effectually the washing of regeneration, since in that action the external washing is the most certain pledge of the internal.

But of course anyone who does not agree with you is in error. Of the same kind is what you say is erroneous: “to think that innumerable children are not regenerated in baptism.” For tell me, I beg you: if the assertion of the statement which I defend is true, and all who are baptized are also regenerated, why do you not seize and carry off to baptism, as far as you can, all the children of Turks, Jews, papists, and heretics of every kind, so that they may be saved? Why in the entire purer ancient Church did catechesis always precede the baptism of adults? Why, though in great error, did many for this reason not present even children for baptism until they reached a fuller age? Because your doctrine was not then heard

[Part I | PDF page 26 | logical source scan 25 | printed page 21]

in the Christian churches. And why do you acknowledge that in the Lord’s Supper the unworthy receive the signs, and indeed the Lord’s body, not only without benefit but even to their own destruction, while you will have everyone who approaches baptism obtain the fruit of internal baptism, namely regeneration? What distinction, then, will there be between the ignorant or hypocrites and those who truly confess Christ and ask to be baptized into Christ?

And since you require faith in every baptized person to such an extent that you will even have infants endowed with faith in their mother’s very womb, either you plainly contradict yourselves or you must attribute faith even to hypocrites and heretics, so that it may be true without exception that all obtain regeneration in baptism. But come: perhaps you will except that this doctrine of yours is to be understood only of the children of believers. Let us examine this too more diligently.

Come forward, then, you who refer regeneration to the act of baptism, indeed as to its efficient cause and inseparable companion; tell me, I ask, what you call regeneration. For my part, I place the chief part of regeneration in the illumination of the mind, through which by the power of the Holy Spirit we become knowledgeable from ignorant, and believers from unbelievers or even obstinate unbelievers. If this is true, as all who are not Pelagians confess it most truly to be, and if what you teach is likewise true—most manifestly misusing the particular example of John the Baptist through ignorance of the refutation—namely, that infants are already actually endowed with faith when they are brought to baptism, it will follow that all Christians baptized in infancy were already regenerated in their principal part even before they were baptized. Baptism, therefore, will be for them—not an efficient cause or instrument, but merely the seal of infant regeneration, the very thing you ascribe to me as an error. To such a degree do your positions collide with one another.

Furthermore, if we again grant your assertion that all the infants of believing parents are actually given faith in their mothers’ womb, their condition will assuredly be much happier than that

[Part I | PDF page 27 | logical source scan 26 | printed page 22]

of adults, among whom there are very few out of the many whom you can truly judge to be knowledgeable and believing. But if you say that these adults have fallen from the faith which they possessed, where is that tranquility of conscience which rests firmly against every temptation in the immovable steadfastness of faith as a gift proper to the elect, and therefore irrevocable—a steadfastness resting upon God’s immutable eternal purpose disclosed to us in his gospel word?

Next, give me, I ask, some baptized child who has grown to an age capable of understanding and reason but has heard nothing of the true doctrine of the gospel. There are innumerable such people today. Question him about the mysteries of that Christian faith with which you say he was already endowed when he was baptized. What, finally, will you hear from him that he ever believed? What sort of faith, I ask, was that? When did he fall from that infant faith? When did he become an unbelieving adult from a believing child—an adult whom not even you would admit to the Lord’s table?

Furthermore, if the apostolic saying “faith comes by hearing”—specifically, “from the mouth of the preacher”—is true, where does this infant faith of yours come from? Must the preached word be heard so that faith may then first arise in us, or rather only so that a faith already begun in us from the womb may finally grow with age? Again, if the faith which you say is universal in every child of believers arises from a secret inspiration entirely without teaching, then that secret inspiration will today be the ordinary and universal cause of faith in the Church, while the preaching of the word will be merely its appendage. You therefore see how many false, absurd, and foolish things follow from this dogma of yours.

Accordingly, I never said simply that baptism is the sealing of regeneration in children, but the sealing of adoption, according to the covenant formula, “I will be God to you and to your offspring.” Nor did I say that either all or some children are actually regenerated at that moment of baptism. I said that the benefit of regeneration, at the time finally ordained by God, follows that act of baptism in infants through hearing the word,

[Part I | PDF page 28 | logical source scan 27 | printed page 23]

just as, conversely, in adults faith and the effects of faith precede their baptism. Thus in Abraham circumcision did not precede but sealed the righteousness of faith, and so with baptism in the eunuch and in Cornelius and his household. This remains perpetually true in the Church wherever baptism is duly administered: no one is rightly admitted to it unless he has professed the Christian faith.

But when, you will say, does that regeneration follow the baptism of children? Assuredly at the time which has seemed good to God, who calls some only at the eleventh hour and only then blesses the baptism which they received long before. But does this happen in everyone baptized? By no means. For only to the elect, and at whatever time seems good to him, does the Lord grant the gift of faith and the things which necessarily follow it. Christian charity, however, commands us to judge with probability that any person, whether child or adult, is among the elect and to commend him to God.

If even this does not satisfy you, come now: give me even one adult who professes Christ with his mouth and, if you please, with honorable conduct, whom you would dare guarantee to God or men, without exception, to believe sincerely and to be destined for salvation, without incurring a great risk of having given a false promise. This does not introduce papist doubt and abolish the true assurance of faith. For it is one thing to profess to know the hidden places of other people’s hearts, and another to rest constantly in the testimony of the Holy Spirit, whom each believer hears crying within himself, “Abba, Father,” and in whom he possesses the mind of Christ. Nor does this detract from the nature of the sacraments or the truth of God any more than one should think something is taken away from the gospel when, through the fault of unbelievers, it is useless or even a savor of death unto death.

What more? “Beza received the distinction between venial and mortal sins with a Sardonic laugh.” Indeed, good man, if you looked at yourself in a mirror even while not laughing, you would have a naturally expressed image of that Sardonic laugh which you undeservedly reproach me with. I confess that I smiled at the statement, but in such a way that I certainly wished to give no one grounds to suspect a mind sinning against Christian charity.

[Part I | PDF page 29 | logical source scan 28 | printed page 24]

Why should I not, if I did not become indignant at these filthy dregs from the scholastic sewer as ungodly, at least testify by a restrained laugh that I cannot easily bear their being retained in certain ecclesiastical schools under the pretext of some moderation or other in which there is no measure? It is beyond controversy among the orthodox that sins are not equal and that their punishments must therefore be unequal. Matthew 10:15. Yet the sentence of divine majesty itself teaches that sins are universally capital, and the Apostle writes it in express words in Romans 6:23. Paul himself certainly thought no differently when he testified that, although he had sinned through ignorance, he was nevertheless a blasphemer and therefore the first—that is, among the chief—of sinners. 1 Timothy 1:15–16.

If we consider the very nature of sins, then, from where shall we suppose that distinction between venial and mortal sins arose except the brain of men flattering themselves? But if we consider the gratuitous promise in Christ of the remission of sins, all sins are assuredly venial to the repentant—repentance itself being God’s gift, not from us—with the single exception of the one from which no one has ever repented. In this respect also, then, that distinction drawn from the pools of the schoolmen, or rather sophists, is useless and indeed very dangerous, and it should deservedly be abolished, since it can be accommodated to judging the inequality of sins only very improperly. Refute this if you can. If not, cease your slandering.

But since you chose here to rail at my smile, I ask you and the other takers-down of my words why you omitted two other occasions, certainly not of mockery but nevertheless of slightly fuller laughter, when they have no small bearing upon the matter. I asked, insisted, and urged Andreae not to declaim but to proceed with me syllogistically. Yet he said in precisely these words, “Upon my life, I have never seen men dispute in this way in our schools.” I confess that I then smiled, but I could obtain a syllogistic dis-

[Part I | PDF page 30 | logical source scan 29 | printed page 25]

-putation neither by a winning smile nor by entreaties. I bring all the hearers forward as witnesses to you whether what I say is false.

When I opposed Andreae’s intolerable corruption of baptism by midwives with that true decision of Bernard—that a person becomes liable to damnation through contempt of baptism, not deprivation of it, and that one who could not obtain baptism lawfully and by the sacred ministry through which the Lord instituted it cannot be regarded as having despised baptism—he did not rest satisfied with that answer. Andreae then presented the example of some dying man formerly an unbeliever who, touched by God’s Spirit, urgently sought baptism but could not obtain it from any pastor.

In turn I asked whether, if some most wretched and almost despairing sinner most urgently demanded the Lord’s Supper while no pastor, or even any man, was present but only some little woman, he judged that the Supper should be administered to him by the woman. Andreae alleged—as he does on page 501 of these Acts—that there was a great distinction between the cases because in the case of necessity the infant had never before been baptized, whereas the adult Christian had often previously celebrated the Lord’s Supper in the Church’s assembly.

I replied, “No: suppose this man had suddenly been freed from papism, or had recently become a Christian from a Jew or Turk; he had never celebrated the Lord’s Supper and was otherwise nearly despairing in his agony. In this necessity would you have some private person, or even a little woman, provide him the Lord’s Supper?”

“By no means,” Andreae said—a statement omitted from the publication of these Acts, as were very many others—“but I would exhort him to take courage even if he could not partake of the Lord’s Supper.”

“But what,” I said, “if he were not satisfied?”

Then Andreae replied in precisely these words: “I would say to him, ‘Go, then, and die with all the devils.’”

I confess that I smiled and answered, “You would assuredly be, my lord doctor, a miserable comforter of that wretched man.” I would not recount matters at once so serious and so ridiculous if these men had not commended their excerpts so highly

[Part I | PDF page 31 | logical source scan 30 | printed page 26]

both in the very title of the published book and in the preface dictated to the most illustrious prince, as though they had been recorded sincerely and in good faith.

What more? “Beza discoursed on predestination for nearly an hour and a half.” I assuredly spoke at length, partly so that against innumerable calumnies I might explain both fully and clearly this topic which is obscure in itself, and partly so that, as far as possible, I might meet in advance the stumbling blocks with which the following disputation would be filled—a thing not difficult for me to perceive. But what if I had said in a single word what is the fact, namely, that in this matter we agree plainly and in everything with Dr. Luther on the basis of God’s Word? Would I thus have stopped your mouths? I do not know; you must see to that. At least let me at last have been permitted in some way by this one speech to compensate for so many exceedingly wordy declamations of Andreae, compared with which my replies, as you published them, would not equal even the twentieth part of his statements.

But to the point. After first declaring that God is the author of no evil, I said what the matter itself demonstrates, what Paul expounds most manifestly in that express explanation of this whole topic in Romans 9, and what among the ancients Augustine so often presses: God would have had neither anyone to judge nor anyone upon whom to have mercy unless sin had entered the world. Augustine says that sin assuredly would not have existed unless it were good, insofar as God is concerned, for even evils to exist. He does not permit them unwillingly but willingly, so that even what occurs against his will does not occur without his will.

“But,” you say, “our men immediately refuted Beza, and he mentioned the testimony of only one passage of Scripture.” Reader, be an impartial judge of this matter from our theses and replies, and see whether both are not almost sewn together out of the very words of Scripture. “But the result is this,” you say: “the most illustrious prince expressly confessed before everyone that he had never thought such great impiety lay hidden beneath the Calvinist dogma.” Indeed, Andreae himself, in a letter given at Tübingen on May 6, dared to write that the most illustrious prince “caused Beza and his colleagues to be told through his secretary, in the presence of very many of the French disputants, that

[Part I | PDF page 32 | logical source scan 31 | printed page 27]

his Highness had learned so much from this Colloquy that, even if he had been a Zwinglian or Calvinist throughout his whole life, nevertheless at that very moment by God’s grace he would have abandoned that dogma forever.”

For my part, I do not know what the most illustrious prince thought or judged among others concerning our conference. I readily persuade myself that, being not especially practiced in this argument of predestination, which exceeds the grasp of human reason, and having been raised at Tübingen as though in the bosom of these theologians, he easily adhered to his own men. But this I know: I never heard anything, either from his own mouth or through his secretary, from which it was evident to me that he inclined toward the one side rather than the other. Instead, he always presided impartially over both parties in the public assembly.

But let us grant that it was so. All of you are greatly mistaken if you think that, although I show all the powers of this age the reverence which is fitting and which God’s Word prescribes, I can nevertheless be frightened in the slightest by the authority or power of anyone—whether prince or even king—when the defense of truth is in question.

As for your repeatedly saying that I wished to break off this Colloquy as an evasive man, alleging the Easter holidays, “which,” you say, “at other times they observe with little concern,” I will deem it worthy of laughter rather than refutation. The true reasons for that petition of ours are extant, and I am glad its written text was published by you yourselves. As for your trifling in passing about the Easter holidays being observed by us with little concern, you, being of course much more religious, will explain the matter to us more fully whenever you wish.

Since all these things stand thus, let not only the most illustrious Prince of Montbéliard himself but all the most illustrious princes of Germany and of every people judge from them which party wronged the other, which is moved by the Spirit of peace and concord, and which party repeatedly accused the most illustrious prince in written letters of having previously inclined toward those whom they call Calvinists. How long those who seek a name and

[Part I | PDF page 33 | logical source scan 32 | printed page 28]

make their fortune from these controversies will abuse the princes’ readiness, the Lord knows; and in his own time he will hear the prayers and groans of the churches.

But come now: let us approach the matter itself. We will follow this order. First we shall present Andreae’s theses, then our antitheses; and finally we shall append our replies to the observations which he opposed to our antitheses in this Tübingen edition.

[Part I | PDF page 34 | logical source scan 33 | printed page 29]

THE THESES OF THE WÜRTTEMBERG THEOLOGIANS CONCERNING THE LORD’S SUPPER

I.

It is beyond controversy that all believers spiritually eat the flesh of the Son of Man and drink his blood, according to that saying of Christ: “Unless you eat the flesh of the Son of Man and drink his blood, you will not have life in you.” John 6. This spiritual eating takes place through faith, even outside the use of the Lord’s Supper, and is always saving. For Christ says, “He who eats my flesh and drinks my blood has eternal life.” John 6. But eating in the Lord’s Supper is sometimes destructive and results in judgment. It is therefore evident that the latter differs from the former, although spiritual eating is necessary for a saving reception of the sacrament of the Lord’s Supper.

RESPONSE TO THE THESES OF THE WÜRTTEMBERG THEOLOGIANS

Antithesis I of Theodore Beza

We hold that there is [1] no reception of Christ himself, whether in the simple word, as in John 6, or in the sacraments, as in the institution of the Lord’s Supper, except a [2] spiritual reception—that is, one accomplished by the power of the Holy Spirit and the instrument of faith. We nevertheless acknowledge that the reception belonging to the simple word differs from the sacramental reception in its outward form, [3] but not in the thing itself which is signified and offered, nor in its reception. For in the simple word God deals with us through the ears alone, whereas in the sacraments he also employs other things which strike the remaining senses. Hence, unless the unworthiness of those approaching obstructs the efficacy of the sacraments, sacramental communication is more forceful and expressive than the merely and nakedly spiritual communication through the simple word.

Andreae’s Observations

1. This openly conflicts with the words of Christ’s testament, for he speaks of reception by the mouth when he says, “Take, EAT; this is my body.” Furthermore, the eating of Christ’s body which takes place by the mouth was instituted to confirm the eating accomplished through faith.

2. It is called spiritual because its manner is spiritual. This can occur even without faith unto judgment when those who eat unworthily do not discern the Lord’s body. 1 Corinthians 11.

3. It differs not only in outward form but also in the thing itself, because that manner of eating in the sacrament is singular.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

Andreae says nothing by way of preface about the nature and definition of the sacraments or

[Part I | PDF page 35 | logical source scan 34 | printed page 30]

about the sacramental conjunction of the signs—that is, bread and wine—with the thing—that is, Christ himself. He begins his disputation from the distinction between the reception by faith of Christ’s flesh in the simple word and its reception in the Lord’s holy Supper. He says they differ in that the former is spiritual and always saving, even outside the use of the Supper, whereas the latter is sometimes destructive. Reader, to say nothing of the inverted method, you see that this thesis of Andreae is incomplete. If I can infer his mind accurately enough, I supply it thus: the former is spiritual, a reception through faith only of Christ’s efficacy or benefits; the latter is substantial, a reception of the very flesh and blood of Christ effected by the instrument of the mouth. The former is always saving, the latter sometimes destructive.

We, however, acknowledge no oral reception of Christ’s flesh. We establish only a spiritual reception, one effected by the sole instrument of faith both in the word and in the sacraments. Nor do we posit any spiritual reception which excludes Christ’s flesh itself; that is, we say that no one becomes a partaker of Christ’s benefits who does not also possess Christ himself, from whom they flow, just as no one is nourished by natural food unless he takes the food itself.

Third, we affirm that this spiritual reception, whether in the simple word or in the sacraments, is always saving and destructive to no one. Destruction does not flow from Christ’s flesh itself unworthily received, but from its being unworthily rejected through unbelief. Accordingly, the reception of Christ which occurs in the simple word differs from sacramental reception neither in the thing—which in each case is Christ himself with his benefits—nor in the reception itself, which in each case is spiritual, nor in the instrument of reception, which in each case is faith, but only in outward form, since in the sacraments bodily signs are added to the word of promise. Andreae assails this most certain truth with the following arguments in his marginal refutations.

First, because Christ, speaking of his body and its reception by the mouth, said, “Eat; this is my body.” I reply that in John 6 Christ likewise used the words flesh, blood, eating, and drinking. Come, then: why does Andreae contend that these words must be interpreted metaphorically in that passage but in their natural meaning in the Lord’s Supper? Is it not, as Augustine rightly teaches, because Christ would otherwise appear to command an outrage and horrendous crime, namely the real and oral eating of human flesh and drinking of human blood? This is assuredly so. This knot cannot be untied by any appeal to the transcendence of God’s omnipotence, by the distinction between visibility and invisibility, or by the fiction of an incomprehensible mode. In whatever manner you actually drink or eat with your mouth human flesh itself and human blood itself, the supper is Capernaïtic.

But perhaps Andreae will offer another reason, namely that in John 6 there is no

[Part I | PDF page 36 | logical source scan 35 | printed page 31]

mention of some bodily thing being joined to the words, and therefore the words “eating” and “drinking” must be taken metaphorically. The miracle of the five loaves and the objection his opponents had raised about manna also gave Christ occasion to employ that metaphor. Let it be so. Because of the reason just cited from Augustine, I grant and maintain that the words “eating” and “drinking,” when said of Christ’s flesh and blood, are never taken otherwise than metaphorically.

But who has conceded to Andreae that the terms “Christ’s flesh” and “Christ’s blood” are taken figuratively rather than properly there, and that they signify only the effects or benefits flowing from Christ’s death without the apprehension of Christ himself? “The bread,” Christ says, “which I will give is my flesh, which I will give for the life of the world.” Was some metaphorical flesh, I ask, given for the life of the world? Was metaphorical blood poured out for our sins?

It remains, therefore, that in John 6 the words “flesh” and “blood” are taken no differently than in the institution of the Lord’s Supper, namely in their proper and native signification. Likewise in both places the verbs “eating” and “drinking” are transferred figuratively to the spiritual taking and reception by the soul and by faith, in a phrase very commonly employed concerning all the body’s senses.

But there is this difference between the purely spiritual eating of Christ’s flesh and blood in the bare word without bread and wine, which is mentioned in John 6, and that likewise spiritual eating which takes place with bread and wine employed in the institution of the Lord’s Supper: the latter is sacramental—that is, it is accomplished with external signs applied to the word. Accordingly, just as two things are to be received in the Lord’s Supper—bread and wine are offered to the mouth, while Christ’s flesh and blood are offered to our spirit and faith—so there is a twofold eating and drinking. The one, that of the signs, is bodily; the other, that of the things, is spiritual, an act “of the mind, not the tooth,” as Augustine himself aptly speaks when discussing the Supper.

And so that Andreae may understand even more clearly that the ancient fathers established no other mode of eating Christ’s flesh in the Supper than that very spiritual mode mentioned in John 6, let him hear Cyril and Chrysostom discuss this very matter. They ask why, in the institution of the Supper, the disciples were not offended by the commanded eating of their master’s flesh and commanded drinking of his blood. “Therefore,” Cyril says, “the apostles neither stood amazed nor murmured at so great a wonder, because they had previously seen those corrected who were astonished at his promise, and remembered that what was then offered had earlier been confirmed to them.” And Chrysostom says, “How were the apostles not troubled when they heard these things? Because he had already discoursed many and great things about this”—namely in John 6, where he had shown that the mode of this eating and drinking is spiritual.

But perhaps Andreae will say: Christ’s flesh and blood are also bodily things no less

[Part I | PDF page 37 | logical source scan 36 | printed page 32]

than bread and wine. Why, then, should their eating not also be oral? I answer that, eaten in this way, they would be of no benefit for eternal life. They would feed the belly, not the mind, because, as I just said, this supper would be Capernaïtic rather than Christian. While the law was still in force Christ would never have set his own or another person’s blood before his apostles to drink with the mouth, nor would the apostles have drunk it; for many years later they commanded the Gentiles, out of consideration for the Jews, to abstain from blood. Furthermore, the truth of Christ’s body, which is truly organic and one and now resides in heaven rather than on earth, can in no way bear this, whatever distinction you employ.

Finally, this reception of Christ’s flesh and blood, both in the word and in the sacraments, is referred to the mystical union of believers with Christ. This union consists in no real contact of our bodies either with one another or with Christ’s flesh. It is purely spiritual, depending upon the ineffable power of the Holy Spirit, who most truly and effectually connects things separated by even the longest intervals of place; and, as the form of the promise states, it is bound by the instrument of faith as by a bond. For this reason Irenaeus also calls Christ’s body and blood heavenly things, certainly not in respect of their substance but of their sacramental use; while bread and wine are earthly things, although distinguished in use from ordinary things.

Second, in attacking our position Andreae objects that the eating of Christ’s body which takes place by the mouth was instituted to confirm the eating accomplished by faith. In this answer there is the childish paralogism which in the schools is called begging the question. He presupposes precisely what is in question, namely that Christ’s body is received by the mouth—a claim which we proved false just above. It is therefore not the oral eating of Christ’s flesh and blood, which does not exist, but the bodily and oral eating and drinking of bread and wine that is the seal, pledge, and token of that great mystery of which the Apostle speaks: our spiritual and ineffable association by faith with Christ our head.

Andreae says, “The eating and drinking of Christ’s flesh and blood is called spiritual because its manner is spiritual, and it can occur even without faith unto judgment, namely in those who do not discern Christ’s body.” But if it is spiritual, it is not oral. For when something actually present and delivered into the hands is eaten and drunk by the body’s mouth, this is accomplished by a natural mode and faculty. Nor will the body’s fictitious invisibility obstruct this reply. Even if I granted it, it would concern the mode of presence, not the mode of eating. If the eating is oral and common to bread and wine on the one hand and Christ’s flesh and blood on the other, it is assuredly not spiritual but purely bodily and natural. But if Andreae calls this mode spiritual because it depends upon the power of the Holy Spirit, how will it be severed

[Part I | PDF page 38 | logical source scan 37 | printed page 33]

from faith? For we are not dealing here with the gift of prophecy or miracles and other things of that kind which the Holy Spirit grants even to some who lack the true faith of the elect. We are dealing with a person’s association with Christ through participation in his very flesh, and this must therefore be proper to believers alone, since light has no fellowship with darkness.

Nor does the Apostle say that Christ’s body and blood are eaten and drunk by those who approach unworthily and without faith, but that this bread and this wine are eaten and drunk, indeed unto destruction, because they do not discern the Lord’s body. That is, they take no account of that precious treasure which Christ offers in that feast to be used and enjoyed unto eternal life, employing the twofold pledge of that bread and that wine, and which they shamelessly reject by their unbelief.

Andreae says, “That sacramental mode of eating is singular; therefore it differs from the purely spiritual mode which occurs by faith not only in outward form but also in the thing itself.” I deny the antecedent of this enthymeme in the sense in which Andreae speaks. For if it is singular, it is proper. But how is it proper if it is oral, when he himself would have this eating common both to bread and wine and to Christ’s flesh and blood, and consequently natural and common to everything eaten and drunk by the mouth? I further deny the manifestly false inference. Do we teach that one thing is received only spiritually and by faith, while Andreae would have another received orally? Certainly not: it is one and the same thing, namely Christ’s flesh and blood, which are that one and not multiple human nature of Christ assumed for us. There is therefore no difference here in the thing itself, but in the mode and instrument of reception.

Thesis II of the Württemberg Theologians

We have never agreed, and never will agree, with the Capernaïtes, who supposed that Christ’s flesh must be torn by the teeth and eaten in the way beef or other meats are eaten. Therefore there is no need to dispute about Capernaïtic butchery or eating.

Antithesis II of Theodore Beza

Although the eating of Christ’s substantial body and the drinking of his blood are held [1] to differ from [2] the common mode of eating and drinking in that the former occurs invisibly and the latter visibly, we nevertheless [3] do not see how the mode of eating is not one and the same as regards the thing itself, and not as regards that circumstance of visibility or invisibility.

Andreae’s Observations

1. We too say that it is an oral eating.

2. We speak not of sight, but of the Lord’s body having an entirely spiritual character in this mystery; this conflicts with the gross errors of the Capernaïtes.

3. The question is not what you can or cannot see, but whether, if the very substance of the Lord’s body and blood is held

[Part I | PDF page 39 | logical source scan 38 | printed page 34]

to be eaten and drunk by the body’s mouth, this conflicts with Christ’s word and will.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

In this second thesis Andreae testifies that he has always condemned and always will condemn Capernaïtic eating, which we too detest. I replied, and reply, that he condemns it in word but approves it in reality, since eating the very substance of the flesh and blood cannot be oral without being Capernaïtic. It cannot be defined in their words as “tearing by the teeth”; that is an utterly empty evasion. For suppose it is said that the very substance of Christ’s flesh and his substantial blood—which was still enclosed within Christ’s veins when he instituted the Supper—are eaten and drunk by the mouth. Whether the solid body is taken whole and at once or is bitten by the teeth and taken in little pieces, and whether visibly or invisibly, no one but the most obstinate can deny that this is the very Capernaïtic eating and drinking by which any food is eaten and any other drink is drunk. Even if that fictitious distinction by which Christ’s true flesh is falsely said to be present in its first actuality otherwise than its own truth allows were granted, nevertheless the eating of it would remain oral and therefore natural.

But these things are horrendous. I confess it. Therefore let those premises from which they are necessarily established be detested with equal horror.

Against this Andreae says, “The eating is sacramental; therefore it is not Capernaïtic.” I reply that the conclusion would be true if he defined sacramental eating as spiritual and as ordained to be accomplished by the sole instrument of faith while employing the natural and oral eating of the sacred signs. But to deny that an eating which is oral is Capernaïtic, whether or not the sacred signs are employed, is to deny an effect whose necessary cause is established and to deny the thing defined after positing the definition.

Again, he says, “The Lord’s body has an entirely spiritual character in this mystery. Therefore it conflicts with the gross errors of the Capernaïtes.” I reply: if the Lord’s body has a spiritual character in this mystery, then the body is not considered as tangible, since in this respect body differs from spirit. If it is not considered as tangible and extended, it is not present in its essence, for if that essence is not tangible it is not corporeal—that is, it is at once body and not body.

Unless Andreae concedes this, he flees to his refuge, that utterly empty fiction of divine omnipotence and an ineffable mode. I ask whether a true body can be essentially present in the mode of spirits—that is, not circumscriptively, tangibly, or locally—or whether circumscription and a material mass occupying a place are not an inseparable and altogether essential property of a material and organic body.

[Part I | PDF page 40 | logical source scan 39 | printed page 35]

Otherwise, how was Christ not a sophist when he proved the truth and presence of his raised body from these evidences: “Touch and see, for a spirit does not have flesh and bones”? For this negative assertion, “This is not tangible; therefore it is not a body,” and this affirmative assertion, “This is tangible; therefore it is a body,” necessarily correspond with one another and rest upon the same foundation.

I also ask what this spiritual character is. It is certainly either definitive, without circumscription, or repletive, without any limitation. The former is proper to the angelic nature and the human soul. Therefore those who dream that Christ’s body is present in the Supper according to the character and mode of a spirit must change it into an angelic nature in the Lord’s Supper. But is a spiritual thing also eaten and drunk by the mouth?

But what if not even this presence aids them? Even those blessed spirits cannot be definitively present at once in several places, much less innumerable ones. As against the Macedonians, the orthodox Church long ago defined the deity of the Holy Spirit from God’s Word and also proved his distinction from created spirits from the fact that the Holy Spirit exists essentially and acts everywhere.

This was the reason why Luther, carried beyond every limit of true theology by the heat of controversy and not content with that definitive presence of Christ’s body, dared also to invent that ubiquitous repletive presence. Yet he thereby pierced his own dogma twice.
