For in this way he both abolishes the hypostatic definition upon which he falsely relies—introducing Eutychianism, that is, a transfusion of properties—and removes the very thing which elsewhere he rightly wishes to be proper to the Lord’s Supper.

For if by reason of the hypostatic union Christ’s humanity too is everywhere repletively, Christ’s body will not be in, under, or with that sacred bread, nor his blood in, under, or with that sacred wine, because the Lord instituted his holy Supper in that manner. Rather, it will be because from the very moment of the hypostatic union it was, is, and will be present not only with that bread and wine but with all things, for as long as those things endure.

Why, then, was that hypostatic union needed for the repletive presence of the assumed body, if what Andreae writes in his theses—departing altogether from the truth—is true: “All things are subsistentially sustained by God,” page 19 of the *Apology against the Theses of Ingolstadt*; and on page 38, “The man Christ has this in common with all saints: that God was personally united with him”? Behold the fruit of that substantial presence and oral eating by which, of course, the testament of the Son of God is confirmed!

And lest anyone object here that what I have said about definitive and repletive presence is a human gloss, let him know that these are Luther’s offspring, from which he afterward deservedly tried to draw back his foot—but too late, Brenz and his men having subsequently advanced so far upon that same road that he even dared in express words to change Christ’s ascen-

[Part I | PDF page 41 | logical source scan 40 | printed page 36]

-sion into an outward spectacle only as far as the clouds, and to establish ubiquitarian heavens and hells. Germany, will you hear these monstrosities any longer?

And this is what we both sought and found: God, who is truthful and immutable, has testified that he wills the truth—and therefore the locality and circumscription—of the humanity personally assumed by Christ to be perpetual. He thus also plainly declares that he does not will it to be anywhere except circumscriptively and locally, because he neither can nor wills to will contradictory things, that is, to lie.

Thesis III of the Württemberg Theologians

Nor do we receive the pontifical transubstantiation, by which they imagine that the bread in the Lord’s holy Supper is converted into the substance of Christ’s body and the wine transmuted into Christ’s blood. We confess instead that in the holy Supper we eat true bread and drink true wine, with no transubstantiation intervening.

Antithesis III of Theodore Beza

We agree.

For transubstantiation too is purely Capernaïtic, and it is destroyed by the same foundations as consubstantiation. Yet although it is no less false, transubstantiation departs less far from the words of the institution of the Lord’s Supper when understood according to their letter. For the transubstantiators say that by those efficacious divine words what was previously bread, its substance having been changed, suddenly becomes Christ’s very body, so that the proposition “This is my body,” and so forth, is true in that way.

But the consubstantiators’ explanation—“This is my body,” that is, “My body is substantially in, with, or under this bread”—does not declare what the bread has become or what the body is, but only where it is. Furthermore, by transposing the terms, the predicate thus becomes the subject and, conversely, the subject the predicate. In respect of the phrase these fictions are even more absurd than transubstantiation itself and, though by another road, introduce precisely the same Capernaïtic error.

Thesis IV of the Württemberg Theologians

Nor do we establish a physical and local presence or enclosure of Christ’s body and blood in the holy Supper.

Antithesis IV of Theodore Beza

We do not see how the very substance of Christ’s body and blood, however glorified, can actually be established as present with the bread and wine

Andreae’s Observation

1. The question is not what you can or cannot see, but what Christ says and commands: “Take, eat; this is my body.”

[Part I | PDF page 42 | logical source scan 41 | printed page 37]

otherwise than by physical and local circumscription. For glorification, as Augustine rightly says by the perpetual consensus of the whole Church, gave Christ’s body immortality; it did not remove its nature.

Andreae’s Observation

2. Nothing here, therefore, conflicts with our doctrine, since we by no means assert that Christ’s body is present in the Lord’s Supper by physical location.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

Andreae offers nothing here which deserves refutation. I said that I cannot see what he teaches. Mocking this modesty of mine, he recalls me to his “Take,” as though it had not been answered a thousand times. But I recall him to this: how does a person not abolish truly bodily substance when he establishes that it can be present somewhere without circumscription and not as something tangible and extended? I confess that I marvel at being reproached here by him for blindness which on page 176 he afterward acknowledges—and deservedly so—in himself and his people in this very mystery of his. For who could ever see with eyes or understanding a body present without location, when such a body never existed, does not exist, and never will exist?

Thesis V of the Württemberg Theologians

The point in controversy, however, is whether in the holy Supper the true body and true blood of our Lord Jesus Christ are truly and substantially present, distributed with the bread and wine, and received by mouth by all who use the sacrament—whether worthy or unworthy, good or evil, believers or unbelievers—yet in such a way that believers receive consolation and life in the Lord’s Supper, while unbelievers receive it unto judgment. In this question we embrace and defend the affirmative position.

Antithesis V of Theodore Beza

We, however, defend the negative. Yet we do not deny that in a lawful celebration of the Supper that true body and true blood of our Lord Jesus Christ are truly offered to everyone who approaches, but they are to be received by the mind, not the body, and by faith, not the mouth. Hence those who approach unworthily and lack the instrument of faith—although the complete sacrament is offered to them too—nevertheless receive only the signs. They thereby become guilty of the Lord’s body and blood, not because they received them, but because they despised them.

Andreae’s Observations

1. This is verbal trickery and equivocation in the word “offered,” which properly presupposes in explicit words the presence of the thing.

2. This is against Christ, who made no mention of faith but said, “Take, eat; this is my body.”

3. The complete sacrament is not offered to one who receives only a part of it—and that the least part, namely only the outward signs.

4. They receive judgment by eating, not by neglecting or despising. 1 Corinthians 11.

[Part I | PDF page 43 | logical source scan 42 | printed page 38]

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

There is no equivocation in the word “offered,” since we expressly say that this offering is made not to the mouth but to the mind. For an offering is called sacramental when one thing is seen, namely the sign, while another is understood and therefore offered to and received by the mind, namely the thing signified. But what is offered to the mind to be apprehended by it by no means always has to be locally present where the body of the person to whose mind it is offered is situated. Indeed, the truth and efficacy of the reception sometimes do not even require the thing to exist yet in the nature of things. For what is said of Abraham was neither phantastic nor imaginary: he saw the Lord’s day, certainly not with the body’s eyes but with those of the mind and faith, and therefore rejoiced. And Paul expressly testifies that the fathers ate the same food and drank the same drink as we do, namely Christ, because to the eyes of faith even the things hoped for subsist from God’s Word and therefore things not yet actually seen. Hebrews 11:1.

Second, Andreae attacks my statement that true flesh and true blood are offered not to the body but to the mind, not to the mouth but to faith, because Christ said, “Take, eat,” making no mention of faith. Assuredly this is a new theology, unheard before! Who does not know that the gospel is one and the same whether it is preached by the simple word or proclaimed with sacred signs and rites joined to it, and that the object of Christian faith is not different merely because the word “Believe” is not always expressly added?

Finally, if a man has discharged his office and duly celebrated the Supper when he did what Christ commanded to be done, and if in the institution of the Supper Christ did not require faith from those approaching, by what refutation shall we distinguish worthy communicants from unworthy ones? God commanded the foreskin of his people to be circumcised but did not expressly mention the promise of circumcision of the heart. Did the knife therefore circumcise the heart as well as the foreskin without faith? John the Baptist baptized for the remission of sins and did not always add “Believe.” Did the water of baptism therefore wash away sins as well as wash the body without faith? How much more rightly Augustine says, “Believe, and you have eaten,” when discussing this very Lord’s Supper; and elsewhere, “Send faith to heaven, and you have touched.” Thus whoever does not believe neither eats the body nor drinks the blood, but only their signs, and indeed unto judgment, because he does not discern the Lord’s body.

I said that the complete sacrament is offered to everyone who approaches. Andreae replies, “The complete sacrament is not offered to one who receives only a part of it—and that the least part, namely the outward signs alone.” Indeed, what is more ridiculous than to measure the completeness of either the word or the sacraments not from their being offered, but from their reception?

[Part I | PDF page 44 | logical source scan 43 | printed page 39]

For what prevents a person to whom something whole is handed to be taken even by the instrument of one and the same hand from accepting only part and rejecting the rest? This occurs much more readily when two instruments are needed to receive two different things and the person to whom those two things were offered lacks one of them.

Thus in the preaching of the gospel, the complete gospel—the outward word together with Christ, who is the substance of that word—is proclaimed to every hearer. Some reject the whole; others hear and understand the voice but through unbelief reject Christ, the principal part of the gospel. Therefore that word of life is to them a savor of death unto death, not because it was received, but because through unbelief it was either immediately and altogether repudiated or, after some slight reception and tasting, rejected as though by vomiting.

The fourth marginal refutation is therefore false and altogether absurd, unless it refers to the sacramental metonymy by which among the fathers, who followed Christ’s footsteps, the name “body,” meaning the thing signified, is attributed to the bread, meaning the sign. In this sense Augustine aptly distinguished the Lord’s body from the body as Lord.

Thesis VI of the Württemberg Theologians

By the terms “in,” “with,” and “under the bread,” we understand nothing other than this: those who in the Lord’s Supper eat that bread and drink the wine also at the same time actually receive Christ’s body and blood. Likewise, for us the terms “substantially,” “bodily,” “really,” “essentially,” and “orally” signify nothing other than the true presence and eating of Christ’s body and blood in the holy Supper.

Antithesis VI of Theodore Beza

We know that the ancients also used those formulas, but not to establish that the substance of the things signified is in, under, or with the signs themselves in the same place. They meant instead that the Lord’s body and blood are offered in reality to the faith of those approaching, just as truly as the words of institution promise and the outward signs testify to their senses. Yet because we see these formulas now drawn elsewhere, namely to that real consubstantiation, we prefer to avoid them as dangerous and less suitable. As for the terms “subsistentially,”

Andreae’s Observations

1. Our opponents therefore object these expressions to us unjustly, as we said above. Let them cite their evidence.

2. Two things can be present in the same place, one without location and the other locally, just as God himself and the saints are present in the same place: he without location, they locally. But for you, “to be present in the same place” and “to be present locally” are equivalent, which is false.

3. We draw them to nothing other than the true and real presence which they handed down under these particles.

4. It is not consubstantiation but a sacramental and supernatural presence.

[Part I | PDF page 45 | logical source scan 44 | printed page 40]

“bodily,” and “essentially,” [5] we acknowledge that they can be used in a right sense if they are referred to the things themselves which are offered, but not if they are accommodated to the sacramental conjunction or to the mode of presence. We [6] confess that “really” may rightly be said in the sense of “truly and not fictitiously”; [7] nevertheless we judge that those words must not be employed rashly. But we utterly reject [8] “orally” as both false in sense and barbarous in form, just as we reject the papist term [9] “sensibly.”

Andreae’s Observations

5. We refer them not to the mode of presence but to the things themselves, affirming that they are truly present.

6. We assert the same.

7. Why, unless because it conflicts with your error?

8. “Orally” is barbarous in your opinion, yet in sense it is true, pious, and consonant with Christ’s word “Eat,” which he commanded to be done by the mouth.

9. However gross the papists may be, they did not assert that term “sensibly.”

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

Indeed, no one makes you the authors of these phrases. We say that you twist them elsewhere. The ancient fathers did not explain the very words of institution by these phrases, but used them to show that the force of the sacramental promises is by no means empty, very rarely even adding the verb “is.”

What could be said less suitably than this objection? The question is not whether any two or more things can touch one another in the same place. Who is so insane as to deny it? The question is whether the true and organic body which Christ’s body always was, is, and will be can be essentially present anywhere—whether alone or with the bread, or, as they speak in the schools, in first actuality—without being local or circumscriptive, or otherwise than its very essential being necessarily and without any exception requires.

Since we are dealing here with Christ’s true body, therefore, “to be essentially present in the same place” and “to be present locally” are for us assuredly equivalent. Whenever Andreae calls this false, he affirms a falsehood. And what is the purpose of that example of the saints and God coexisting, except to make it utterly manifest that by this dogma Christ’s humanity is transformed into deity?

Indeed, Andreae draws these phrases somewhere altogether different: to establish the utterly false and foolish fiction of a presence at once essential and without location and of oral eating. The fathers employed them only to show the truth of sacramental conjunction and spiritual reception in the sacraments.

Indeed, with what appearance of reason can it be denied that those things are consubstantiated which are said not only actually to be joined at once in their essences but even to be united almost hypostatically? This goes so far that, when their other hiding places fail, they finally number even the sacramental propositions “This is my body” and “This is my blood” among unusual hypostatic propositions.

You will

[Part I | PDF page 46 | logical source scan 45 | printed page 41]

have it, then, that substances are present, but not in the mode of substance: true, I say, corporeal and located things are present not bodily and locally, yet in their substance, which is truly corporeal in itself, here upon earth—and indeed in as many places and with as many loaves as the Lord’s Supper is celebrated. Give us, then, from either the laws of nature or God’s Word, a true substance which is not substance and a true body which is not body.

Indeed, we do not agree even about the adverb “really.” Andreae refers that real—that is, true—presence of Christ’s flesh to the earth, to the bread, and to the mouths of men celebrating the Supper. As regards the thing itself, we say that it is now situated in heaven and nowhere else. As regards us, we profess from the divine Scriptures that it is most truly and effectually present to the mind and faith, not only in the sacraments but also in the simple word.

If Andreae restricted “orally,” as he ought, to the eating of the signs, so that their oral eating was distinguished from the spiritual reception of the things signified, we would give no thought to its barbarous form.

Indeed, these are the words of Pope Nicholas, chapter *Ego Berengarius*, and so forth, *De consecratione*, distinction 2: “I consent to the Roman Church and the Apostolic See, and I profess with mouth and heart that after consecration the bread and wine placed upon the altar are not only a sacrament but also the true body and blood of our Lord Jesus Christ; and that SENSIBLY, not only sacramentally but in truth, they are handled and touched by the priests’ hands,” and so forth.

Luther so expressly approved this horrendous and ungodly confession in his *Greater Confession*—behold what the spirit of contention does!—that he severely rebuked the canonists and gloss no less than those whom he calls fanatics, because they reproached Pope Nicholas; and he wished that all popes had acted as Christianly as Nicholas acted with Berengar in that confession. Andreae must therefore dissent from Luther or openly cross over into the papists’ camp, for consubstantiation is separated from transubstantiation by the thinnest thread and strikes precisely the same rocks of falsehood.

Thesis VII of the Württemberg Theologians

These are the foundations of our faith. The first is the words of Christ, God and man, who in the institution of the Lord’s holy Supper, extending bread to his disciples, said, “Eat; this is my body, which is given for you.” And presenting the cup of wine he said, “This is my blood, which is poured out for you.” Paul, speaking of this sacrament, says, “The cup of blessing which we bless, is it not the communion of Christ’s blood? And the bread which we break, is it not participation in Christ’s body?” In this first foundation of the words of Christ’s testament, what follows is also contained.

[Part I | PDF page 47 | logical source scan 46 | printed page 42]

Antithesis VII of Theodore Beza

Far be it from us not to rest satisfied with the words of the institution of the Lord’s Supper. We only assert that, like every other passage of Scripture without exception, they must necessarily be tested by the analogy and rule of the whole Christian faith faithfully comprehended in the Apostles’ Creed. If this is done, we judge that the consubstantiation of the signs with Christ’s body and blood, as distinct from the relative conjunction which we have established, is not only unproved but manifestly and necessarily overthrown. The cup of blessing certainly cannot be called the communion of Christ’s blood, nor the bread which we break the communion of Christ’s body, without a trope.

Andreae’s Observations

1. Since you do not receive Christ’s words without a gloss, it is manifest that you do not rest satisfied with them.

2. We said above that we establish no consubstantiation. We reject a relative conjunction as effecting nothing.

3. We openly condemn a trope in Paul’s words as an emptying of the sacrament.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

That connection is ridiculous unless Andreae first shows either that there is no figure, that there is no trope in Holy Scripture requiring interpretation, that the words of institution admit no trope, or that the interpretation we offer is false.

Indeed, how can consubstantiation be defined otherwise in this argument than as the real conjunction upon earth of two essences, namely the body in, under, or with the bread and the blood in, with, or under the wine, however much the presence of the latter is established as neither physical nor local? Furthermore, even if I grant Andreae that real presence, will the bread and wine not nevertheless be signs of the present body and blood, namely of the things signified? And what analogy can exist between sign and thing signified unless it is relational and relative?

You do condemn it—but for what reason? Is every tropic expression false and to be condemned?

Thesis VIII of the Württemberg Theologians

The second foundation is that Christ, who is God, cannot lie, Titus 1, and that no deceit was found in his mouth, 1 Peter 2.

Antithesis VIII of Theodore Beza

We believe and proclaim that what you say here is true, but we affirm that it is badly applied to the matter in question.

Andreae’s Observation

1. It is not surprising that you think this way when you speak here; but the pastors of Zurich refuse to dispute with us about the Lord’s Supper as long as we place omnipotence, together with the words of the Lord’s Supper, as the foundation of our faith in this mystery.

[Part I | PDF page 48 | logical source scan 47 | printed page 43]

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observation

This is a sheer and utterly rotten calumny. For which of our dearest brothers at Zurich, with whom we share the Helvetic Confession, ever conceived the thought of denying omnipotence—that is, the deity consubstantial with the Word—whether in the simple word, in the sacraments, or in any work of Christ, as is said here?

Indeed, this is Andreae’s very own doctrine; let him see how he can vindicate it from the impiety and blasphemy of Paul of Samosata and Arius. He wrote in precisely these words: “The divinity of the man is not that substantial and eternal essence common with the Father and the Holy Spirit, but one communicated by the second hypostasis.” Lest anyone interpret this as having been said against the Sabellians, he expressly calls this communicated divinity “gifts poured forth, measured in the saints but immeasurable in Christ.” We will discuss this matter shortly. We, however, denied and deny that God’s omnipotence is rightly employed to establish that real and substantial presence of Christ’s humanity upon earth and its oral eating—that is, to establish a falsehood.

Thesis IX of the Württemberg Theologians

The third foundation is that Christ is God and man in one inseparable person. Therefore nothing is impossible for him. “For what is impossible with men is possible with God,” says Christ in Luke 18. And the angel Gabriel says, “No word will be impossible with God.” Luke 1. And concerning the man Christ John says, “Jesus knew that the Father had given all things into his hands.” John 13. Scripture also testifies that Christ, the Son of Man, has been placed at God’s right hand, which is God’s infinite power, and that all power in heaven and on earth has been given to him. Matthew 28. Therefore Christ can perform everything he promised. Since in the words of his testament he promised that he would give us his body to eat and his blood to drink, and since he can perform everything he promised, it is most certain that in the holy Supper we eat Christ’s body with the bread and drink his blood with the wine.

Antithesis IX of Theodore Beza

We too profess that nothing is impossible for Christ, God and man, but with this qualification: the interpretation of this statement and the inference drawn from it must be tested by the rule of faith. We therefore affirm, first, that this omnipotence is one, not twofold, as though one omnipotence were uncreated, proper to the deity, and not transferable to any created thing, while the other were created, a quality infused into Christ’s humanity. It is no less false and

Andreae’s Observations

1. They do not concede that God is absolutely and simply omnipotent.

2. Not one word can be shown in our writings in which they ever taught a created omnipotence. For there is one omnipotence, eternal and infinite, but in Christ it has a twofold character.

[Part I | PDF page 49 | logical source scan 48 | printed page 44]

absurd, we say, to establish more omnipotences in number than there are omnipotent beings in number, that is, gods. It is therefore not Christ’s humanity in itself which is omnipotent, but the man Christ is omnipotent in the Word, just as that humanity is not deity, but that man is God the Word.

Next, as regards power considered in itself, we say that God himself is omnipotent in such a way that he nevertheless cannot actually do the things which he has not decreed to do or which are contrary to what he has decreed. The reason is not that he is not omnipotent, but that being able to change his will and therefore to change himself would belong not to power but to weakness and to one capable of lying. Among these things we number the immutable circumscription and locality of Christ’s body, because God determined never to abolish what he once assumed. It would be abolished if, either before or after glorification, the substance of Christ’s body could be in several places at once, much less in all places.

Third, we deny that because someone has received dominion over all created things, it follows that his essence is everywhere. It is one thing to explain what someone can do and another what he is in himself, since among creatures without any exception whatever “to be able” is different from “to be.”

With these things established, we nevertheless affirm that in the duly administered Lord’s Supper that true body—which is now in heaven and nowhere else—is most truly and effectually offered to us, who are now upon earth and nowhere else, to be received by faith through that ineffable power of the Holy Spirit for the salvation of soul and body. In this way we judge that we establish the inexplicable power of Christ, God and man, far more expressly than those who think we cannot be associated with Christ himself unless he is also essentially present upon earth, adhering inseparably to the bread and wine.

Andreae’s Observations

3. Christ’s humanity is not omnipotent IN ITSELF—that is, of itself by the property of its own nature—but by personal participation in the divine nature.

4. “The man is omnipotent in the Word” is an ambiguous expression, about which more in its proper place.

5. It is denied that God is absolutely omnipotent.

6. They deny that while preserving the property of Christ’s body God can by his omnipotence cause it to be at the same moment in several places. This is openly to deny God’s omnipotence.

7. This is a human fantasy about Christ’s dominion over all heavenly and earthly creatures without his true and real presence.

8. In God, to be able to be and to be are the same. Christ’s flesh has been made a participant in this power.

9. Since this power is infinite, they deny that his human nature was made a participant in it. They therefore attribute to the Holy Spirit alone what Christ attributes to his flesh.

10. They altogether strip Christ’s flesh of participation in this power. How, then, is this force attributed to it?

11. Beza asserts this concerning a physical and local presence which no one teaches. He therefore attacks his own fiction.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

When at last will there be an end to slandering us? Have these matters not truly been answered a hundred thousand times? If Andreae will not have God’s omnipotence tested by the rule of faith,

[Part I | PDF page 50 | logical source scan 49 | printed page 45]

let him therefore show that God can cease to be, be deceived, be changed, lie, and indeed die. But if these things are false and ungodly, with what shame can it be denied that some rule must be delivered from God’s Word by which these falsehoods and blasphemies may be distinguished from the truth?

At least let Augustine answer for us in *The City of God*, book 5, chapter 10: “God cannot do certain things precisely because he is omnipotent.” And Tertullian, against the Sabellian Praxeas, who inferred from the fact that nothing is difficult for God—not even causing the barren or a virgin to give birth—that it was not difficult for him to make himself both himself and his Son, says: “Plainly nothing is difficult for God. But if we employ this statement so abruptly in our presumptions, we will be able to invent anything whatever about God, as though he did it because he was able to do it. Because he can do all things, however, one must not therefore believe that he did what he did not do; rather, it must be asked whether he did it. On this reasoning something will be difficult even for God, namely anything he did not do—not because he was unable, but because he was unwilling. For God’s ability is his will, and his inability his unwillingness. But what he willed, he both could do and demonstrated.”

And lest anyone here return to “Take, eat,” interpreted of a body actually and essentially present upon earth, though not according to the mode of a body, let us hear Cyril on this subject. “When Christ sent the Spirit to the apostles,” he says, “he could not converse with them in the flesh, since he had ascended to the Father.” Andreae will say that “in the flesh” means by an essential and real presence upon earth, yet not in a physical or local mode. But who could bear that gloss? For, especially according to Brenz’s position, the ascension into heaven would not refute that absence of location as false, but rather confirm it.

And Vigilius, slaying Eutyches in these very words, says, “Because Christ’s flesh is in heaven, it is certainly not upon earth.” Every time Andreae dared in his lectures to call this most learned writer and martyr “Dormilius,” he declared that he himself had been lulled into the deepest sleep.

I do not remember reading in the writings of Andreae and his followers that the omnipotence which they attribute to the humanity is created. But they act just as though they denied that they called something a man while nevertheless confessing it to be a rational animal. For behold these very words of Brenz on page 46 of his *Recognition*: “The distinction between Christ and Peter cannot be established in this way, that we say that the man Christ is God without saying that Peter is God.” And is this not something even worse than Arianism? For Arius acknowledged that Christ was called God homonymously, but not Peter; and what else would Nestorius vomit if he emerged from hell?

And immediately afterward: “From that gospel saying, ‘The Word became flesh,’ which is said of Christ, not Peter, the distinction between Christ and Peter must be established in this: the Son of God fills the man Peter with his essence just as he fills the man Christ, yet he does not communicate all his properties to Peter, but only some.” Again: “If in Christ the di-

[Part I | PDF page 51 | logical source scan 50 | printed page 46]

-vinity dwells wholly according to essence, power, and presence, it dwells wholly in Peter.” Andreae himself likewise says in his *Apology against the Theses of Ingolstadt*: “There is ONE distinction between God’s indwelling in the saints and in Christ: he distributes his gifts to the saints in measure, but to Christ without measure.”

Come, then: these properties common to Christ and the saints are either God’s very essence or created qualities. If they are God’s essence, it follows that Christ is God altogether but the saints gods to some degree. If they are created qualities, Christ’s power will be an omnipotence at once created and immeasurable. Andreae here falsely denied that he and his party say this. The saints’ power, however, will likewise be created, but measured and imperfect, for all and immeasurable properties must differ from some and measured properties not in their whole kind but according to more and less.

The same must be established concerning indwelling. Since it is active in the saints and therefore the efficient cause of created gifts, the same will be established in Christ. But Paul speaks very differently. Speaking of Christ’s person, he employed not the word “activity” but “deity,” and added “bodily,” that is, essentially and according to the hypostasis. But what further can one do with Andreae, who dared to affirm not only that the man Christ has this in common with all the saints—that God was personally united with him—but also that “all things are subsistentially sustained by God,” a phrase more horrendous than any the Manichees ever vomited?

These are mere verbal tricks. “In itself” can signify nothing other than “subjectively,” as they speak in the schools, that is, in such a way that this omnipotence does not inhere in the humanity as its subject. Yet just above we proved that Brenz and Andreae necessarily establish it as a created quality. If it does not inhere in the humanity, therefore, it inheres in the deity, that is, in the Word himself—which is the most absurd thing that can be said—or it does not inhere in Christ at all.

But if it is essential and uncreated, and therefore the divine essence itself, then just as it does not come to the assumed humanity as an accident from the Word but is hypostatically united to it, this man will assuredly be omnipotent for no other reason than insofar as he is God, and therefore not according to himself but according to another. And since in the hypostatic union the properties of each nature remain intact and never leave their own subjects, while abstract terms declare the natures as they are in themselves, according to themselves and according to the property of their own nature, it follows necessarily that the assumed humanity can be called omnipotent neither according to itself nor according to another.

Consequently, this entire personal participation in the divine nature—which the ubiquitarian party previously called a real outpouring—is the most absurd fiction, drawn from the pools of the Monothelites to paint over the mask of ubiquity. Perceiving this absurdity, even the schoolmen rightly and learnedly denied that divine omnipotence can be communicated to any creature, and denied that Christ’s soul was omnipotent with respect either to creatures, its own will, or its own body.

[Part I | PDF page 52 | logical source scan 51 | printed page 47]

They said instead that the humanity was merely the instrument of the Omnipotent, although personally united to him. But the power of one using an instrument and the power of the instrument itself, especially one which also moves itself, are two powers in number, not a single power communicated to the instrument.

Indeed, there is no ambiguity here. For by the same reason that this man is God, he is plainly and manifestly omnipotent, namely through the unity of the person. Just as the humanity did not by this union of itself with the Word become deity, it is no less absurd for it to be called omnipotent in any way than for the body to be said to understand and reason on account of the soul hypostatically united to it.

Andreae plays upon the name “Christ,” which designates the person or the whole Christ, although we are here treating his humanity, considered distinctly though not separately. To imagine either that it was in heaven when it was upon earth, or that now, while it is in heaven, it is present upon earth in that corporeal essence of its own, is sheer Eutychian blasphemy. On this subject Cyril says: “The ability to fill all things, pass through all things, and be in all things must be conceded to no nature other than the divine.”

And Vigilius: “One and the same Christ belongs to both natures, and he is indeed everywhere according to his divine nature but contained in a place according to his human nature. This is the catholic faith and confession which the apostles handed down and the martyrs confirmed.” And Tertullian: “To be invoked and present everywhere belongs not to the nature of man but to God, that he may be present in every place.” Vigilius likewise says: “When the flesh was upon earth, it was not in heaven; and now that it is in heaven, it certainly is not upon earth, since it is awaited to come from heaven.” And Augustine: “Until the age is ended, the Lord will be above, whose body in which he rose must be in one place.” And Fulgentius: “According to his human substance Christ was absent from heaven when he was upon earth, and left the earth when he ascended into heaven.” I pass over other testimonies produced a thousand times and never refuted.

There is no controversy between us concerning the deity. The orthodox have never doubted that what is proper to deity is not said of the humanity, just as conversely what is proper to the humanity is not said of the deity.

Since the Holy Spirit is the essential power of the Father and the Son, what is attributed to the Holy Spirit is not taken away from Christ. The account of his baptism testified visibly that Christ sanctified his humanity by this Spirit of his, and that this humanity was anointed by the Father—insofar as this Spirit proceeds from both persons, for which reason God is also said to have given him his Spirit, and indeed without measure—just as Isaiah had previously predicted in express words. Let Andreae therefore see at this point whether he is insufficiently instructed even in the mystery of the Trinity. He learned nowhere from Holy Scripture to attribute to Christ’s flesh what is proper to the divine nature. He ought instead to have learned that, as the natures must be distinguished without separation against Nestorius, so all their properties and operations must also be distinguished against Eutyches.

[Part I | PDF page 53 | logical source scan 52 | printed page 48]

Indeed, one does not strip the man of God’s power when one never separates him from God the Word. Rather, the person who attributes the proper actions of deity and the Word to the humanity, however united and glorified, confuses the natures with Eutyches.

Let Augustine answer while discussing Christ’s glorified body itself: “Take spatial locations away from bodies and they will be nowhere; and if nowhere, they will not exist.” And elsewhere: “If it is a body, it is in a place.”

Thesis X of the Württemberg Theologians

Holy Scripture does not express the mode by which, in the holy Supper with the bread and wine, the worthy and unworthy receive the Lord’s body and blood. Concerning the mode, therefore, we can say only that it is supernatural and incomprehensible to human reason; consequently it must not be disputed. In accordance with his immeasurable wisdom and omnipotence, God can find more reasons and modes by which he causes Christ’s body and blood to stand somewhere and be offered to those eating than merely the physical and natural mode which human reason alone understands. Therefore in this divine mystery we lead our reason captive and, with sound and tranquil conscience, rest by simple faith upon Christ’s words: “This is my body; this is my blood.”

Antithesis X of Theodore Beza

The very designation of the signs explains the mode by which the thing signified is joined to the signs by divine ordinance and shows it to be relative according to the law of the covenant. This also has a place in human and ordinary contracts themselves.

As for the mode of reception of the thing signified—how that body remaining in heaven can, through the intervention of the instrumental cause, namely faith, be communicated so effectually to us situated upon earth that we draw eternal life from it and already now receive so many spiritual benefits—we profess with the Apostle that it is truly a great mystery. It is therefore to be believed and adored, not searched out.

But if it is true that the very substance of Christ’s flesh and blood is actually present upon earth with the bread and wine, and that it is received by everyone approaching, although invisibly, nevertheless by the same single oral reception, we do not see how that mode can be called inscrutable. It is purely natural and common both to the signs and to the things signified.

Andreae’s Observations

1. The relative mode here is a human fantasy invented to cover an error. There is no relation here, but a true and real offering of spiritual things through bodily things has been instituted.

2. There is an immense distinction between civil contracts and gospel sacraments.

3. They attribute greater power to faith than to Christ: faith can ascend to Christ in heaven, but Christ cannot by his participated divine power be present upon earth.

4. Indeed, it is manifest precisely because it does not occur in a physical and natural mode.

[Part I | PDF page 54 | logical source scan 53 | printed page 49]

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

Indeed, Andreae himself confessed on page 94 of the *Proceedings* which he published that a sacrament consists of two things: an earthly thing as the sign, and a heavenly thing as what is signified. And the same Andreae, on page 28 of those *Proceedings*, contends that the oral eating of Christ’s body was instituted to confirm the spiritual eating by faith. What, in truth, is that but an analogy of relation? For the sacramental rites themselves are signs. Thus Augustine says in Epistle 5: “If sacraments did not bear some likeness to those things of which they are sacraments, they would not be sacraments at all.” Therefore to concede the sign and the thing signified, yet deny the *schesis*, or relation, between sign and thing signified, is as manifest an insanity as to deny what one affirms. It belongs to the same error to oppose the true offering of the thing signified—whether present here upon earth or absent—to the *schesis*, or relation, as though the one were abolished by the other, when on the contrary the one is subordinated to the other.

There is assuredly a very great distinction between civil contracts and gospel sacraments, but it lies in the things themselves, not in the fact that signs and things signified are employed in both. Augustine therefore rightly says in Epistle 5: “When signs pertain to divine things, they are called sacraments.” Thus there is the greatest difference between one who speaks of earthly things and one who speaks of heavenly things, yet neither differs from the other in the action of speaking itself, since each speaks with his tongue and often even employs the same words—the one to signify heavenly things, the other earthly things.

Nor is Andreae ignorant of these golden words of Bernard: “Some things are done merely for their own sake, but others in order to designate other things, and these are both called and are signs. To take an example from ordinary things: a ring is given absolutely for the sake of the ring, and there is no signification; but it is given to invest someone with an inheritance, and then it is a sign, so that the person receiving it may even say, ‘The ring is worth nothing, but it is the inheritance that I was seeking.’ In this manner, therefore, when the Lord was approaching his Passion, he took care in his grace to invest his own, so that invisible grace might be conferred by some sign.” So says Bernard, producing besides these other examples of signs of the same kind, such as a book and a staff. From these things it appears that the distinction between signs instituted by men and by God must be located not in the fact that they are signs, but in the fact that the things signified are altogether different, whether they are established as present or absent.

Indeed, Andreae slanders us here. For we do not suspend the sacramental offering and conjunction upon anyone’s faith, since we confess that complete sacraments are always offered to all who approach, just as the divine ordinance testifies. But we assign to faith alone the eventual reception of the thing signified, spiritually offered to the mind along with those bodily signs, as the sole instrument suitable and effective—according to the rule of faith—for receiving Christ and applying him to ourselves. Moreover, we teach from God’s Word that this efficacy of faith in embracing Christ proceeds not from itself,

[Part I | PDF page 55 | logical source scan 54 | printed page 50]

but from the Spirit and power of Christ, who gives it to us and renders it thus effective, so that all praise and glory for our salvation may be attributed jointly and wholly to God the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit: to the Father, namely, as the one who gave us the Son; to the Son, as the one who is our righteousness and redemption; and to the Holy Spirit, proceeding from them both, as the one who leads us into all truth. Moreover, the person who denies that Christ can perform contradictories simultaneously—that is, can lie—detracts nothing from Christ’s omnipotence as God, nor from his power as man, to whom all things are subjected (excepting him who subjected all things to him).

If it is not by a physical mode, therefore, it is not oral. Otherwise let Andreae teach us that the action of the mouth in eating and drinking is not physical.

THE FOLLOWING DOGMAS ARE JUDGED BY THE WÜRTTEMBERG THEOLOGIANS TO CONFLICT WITH HOLY SCRIPTURE

Dogma I

That the words of the testament of Jesus Christ are not to be understood simply, or *kata to rheton*, as they sound. For they are obscure, and therefore their true sense must be sought from other passages of Scripture.

THE RESPONSE OF DR. BEZA AND HIS COLLEAGUES TO THE DOGMAS WHICH THE GENTLEMEN COLLOQUUTORS JUDGE TO CONFLICT WITH HOLY SCRIPTURE

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Dogma I Condemned by Andreae

We, on the contrary, hold that the interpretation of the Lord’s words of institution—and indeed of every passage of Scripture without any exception—must be recalled to the norm of faith comprehended in the Apostles’ Creed, as was said above, and explained from a comparison of the Scriptures. We hold that the words cannot be taken *kata to rheton*, according to the bare wording, while the analogy of faith remains intact; nor, on the other hand, ought the institution of the Lord’s Supper to be considered obscure merely because a trope is said to be present in it.

Andreae’s Observations

1. Obscure passages need illumination from another source. But the words of the Lord’s Supper are open and clear.

2. This is simply false, as the comparison which follows teaches.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

They are certainly clear enough, if with us you understand the sacramental institution in the form of speech customary in sacraments—that is, if you understand the words according to the subject matter.

[Part I | PDF page 56 | logical source scan 55 | printed page 51]

Otherwise, just as the fathers refuted the Arians, who cited their saying, “The Father is greater than I,” and the Anthropomorphites, who cited, “Your hands made me,” as words perfectly clear in their own favor—and likewise refuted the ungodly interpretations of almost all the other heretics—on the ground that they transgressed the analogy of the articles of the Christian faith, so too do we: however many testimonies prove the perpetual reality of Christ’s body, and consequently its circumscription and locality, and likewise his ascension into heaven and return from heaven, by so many invincible arguments do we convict the literal interpretation of the institution of the Lord’s Supper in the attribution of predicate to subject.

Dogma II

That in the holy Supper only bread and wine are received by the mouth, but Christ’s body is received only spiritually, namely by faith. For one must ascend by faith into heaven so that there we may become participants in Christ’s body and blood, because after Christ’s ascension into heaven his body is nowhere but in heaven before the last day.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Dogma II Condemned by Andreae

We seem sufficiently to have proved what is condemned here.

Andreae’s Observation

1. It has been demonstrated from Christ’s words that absolutely nothing has been proved.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observation

This is childish begging of the question. For the controversy concerns the interpretation of Christ’s words, which we oppose as utterly false from the analogy of faith and a comparison of other passages of Scripture, but which Andreae assumes as though true. When Christ instituted the Supper, did he truly wish to contradict himself, his angels, the apostles Peter and Paul, and finally the entire ancient orthodox church? For in that sacred action he himself commands the remembrance of his death to be celebrated until he comes. Does “to come” truly mean the same thing as to become visible from invisible, or local from non-local? Or is one who is already present going to come?

When the angels saw that the apostles had followed with their eyes the Lord ascending upward, they did not admonish them henceforth to seek that body not in heaven, toward which their eyes remained fixed, but upon earth—present there definitively or repletively, as Luther writes, or in some omni-majestatic mode. Rather, they teach that it truly departed upward and will return from there only at the appointed time, just as it had ascended.

Peter says: “The heavens must receive him until the times of restoration of all things.” How absurdly those men distort this passage with their gloss is evident even from this: in place of “the heavens receive him,” they interpret it as “he receives heaven,” that is, heavenly glory. For if this were so, the restriction which is added—“until the times of restoration of all things”—would by no means fit, since Christ’s heavenly glory will then be most conspicuous. Paul, moreover, says, “While away as pilgrims,

[Part I | PDF page 57 | logical source scan 56 | printed page 52]

we are absent from the Lord,” and, “I desire to depart and to be with Christ.” Augustine likewise says: “See him ascending; believe in him while absent; hope for him as one who is coming; yet by his hidden mercy also perceive him as present”—namely, as he says elsewhere, according to his divinity. The same writer says: “Someone will answer, ‘How will I hold one who is absent? How will I send my hand into heaven so that I may hold him who is sitting there?’ Send your faith, and you have held him. Your forebears held him in the flesh; you, hold him in your heart. Christ is both present and absent, for unless he were present, he could not be held by us. He departed, and he is here: he carried his body to heaven; he did not withdraw his majesty—that is, his divinity—from the world.”

Ignatius says: “Having lived with the apostles for forty days, he was assumed to the seat at the Father’s right hand, where he now remains until his adversaries are subjected beneath his feet.” And Justin: “That God the Father of all was going to lead Christ, raised from the dead, into heaven and detain him there until he has struck the demons, his enemies, hear these words of David in Psalm 110: ‘The Lord said to my Lord.’”

Dogma III

That God cannot, even by the whole of his omnipotence, cause Christ’s body to be present at one and the same time in more places than only one.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Dogma III Condemned by Andreae

We said at Antithesis 9 how God does not cease to be omnipotent even if he cannot cause Christ’s body to be essentially present at one and the same time in several places, or now to be anywhere other than in heaven.

Andreae’s Observation

1. This is an open denial of God’s omnipotence, because with God NO WORD is impossible, that is, no thing (Luke 1).

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observation

Against this slander, as filthy as it is shameless, see the reply to the first, fifth, and sixth refutations of Antithesis 9.

Dogma IV

That Christ’s body is therefore no more present for us upon earth in the sacrament of the Eucharist than it once was for Abraham.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Dogma IV Condemned by Andreae

We confess that Christ, God and man—since otherwise he could not have been Mediator—was not yet actually man before the real incarnation. Nevertheless, we teach that he was present to Abraham’s faith (which is called the *hypostasis* of things which do not exist),

Andreae’s Observations

1. The presence of Christ as man in Abraham’s time is one thing, and his presence in the New Testament after the incarnation has taken place is another.

2. Paul does not say that faith is the *hypostasis* of things which do not exist, in Hebrews 11, but of things hoped for and not seen.

[Part I | PDF page 58 | logical source scan 57 | printed page 53]

not by an illusory opinion, but truly and effectually, just as he was present to the other holy fathers. For Abraham saw that day of the Lord, and—as far as the fathers’ faith is concerned—the Christ who was going to come was not numerically another than the Christ who came for us. Augustine therefore rightly asserted in Tractate 26 on John that the sacraments of the ancients were equal to ours in the thing, though different in their signs.

Andreae’s Observations (continued)

3. The fathers were participants in Christ’s benefits, but they did not have Christ present according to his humanity; they believed that he would come.

4. He was indeed the same Christ, but he was not present to both parties in an equal manner.

5. They were equal with respect to end and efficacy, but not in the presence of Christ according to his humanity.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

Indeed, in the thing itself—that is, in Christ’s humanity itself—the circumstance of time establishes a great distinction, since it did not yet actually exist in the fathers’ times. But insofar as Christ, though still to come, was offered then to the fathers, both in the Word and in the sacraments of the old covenant, to be enjoyed already by faith, and is offered to us now, as one already manifested, in the Word and sacraments of the new covenant, he is the same Christ in both cases. There is the same sacramental conjunction between the signs—though different and unequal—and the thing signified, that is, Christ even as man; the same reception, namely spiritual and by faith; and also the same faith belonging to both parties, the fathers and us. This faith regards the same humanity of Christ, which, although not yet existing in itself in the fathers’ times, was nevertheless most present to their faith as it is to ours. Augustine was therefore entirely right to say that the sacraments of the ancients and ours are different in signs but equal in the thing.

And so that Andreae may know that the distinction he adds is empty—as though the ancients were participants only in Christ’s benefits and power, apart from Christ’s flesh itself, since it did not yet actually exist—I would have him consider that those benefits of Christ, namely the fruit of his death and entire obedience, depend upon those things which he performed for our sake. Is it not equally absurd to make the fathers participants in the benefits and power of Christ’s sacrifice, though it had not yet actually been offered, as he thinks it absurd when I affirm that the fathers were also made participants in Christ’s own humanity, though it did not yet actually exist?

But if the former absurdity carries no weight because, although that sacrifice had not yet been accomplished in itself, God the Father—to whom the future things he has decreed are already present—and the fathers’ faith beheld it as though already present, why shall we not establish the same thing concerning the incarnation of the Son of God himself? For what Vigilius says is altogether true: “No one can possess this treasure without the field, nor this field without the treasure.” Therefore Abraham did not behold

[Part I | PDF page 59 | logical source scan 58 | printed page 54]

the day of the Lord spiritually with the eyes of faith apart from the Lord himself. Nor does Paul say that that same food and that same drink were Christ’s benefits, but Christ himself, and indeed the whole Christ. For the Son of God would not have saved the fathers in any other way than as incarnate. Augustine says in Tractate 26 on John: “For they ate spiritual food, surely the same food; for their bodily food was different, since they ate manna and we eat something else. And all drank the same spiritual drink. They drank one thing, we another, though in a visible form it signified this same thing by spiritual power. How, then, the same drink? ‘They drank,’ he says, ‘from the spiritual Rock that followed them, and the Rock was Christ.’ From there came the bread, from there the drink: the Rock was Christ in the sign; true Christ was in the Word and in the flesh.”

I am not at all surprised that Andreae separates Christ’s benefits from Christ himself in the sacraments of the ancients, as though they could be received without him, since in the simple Word of the gospel he admits the same error. In the spiritual eating of John 6 he takes “flesh” and “blood” figuratively, merely for the benefits provided to us by Christ through his flesh. This is certainly contrary to Christ’s own words. Explaining the bread or food about which he had previously discoursed, and expounding what that flesh was which he had commanded to be eaten spiritually, Christ says, “The bread which I will give is my flesh.” He thereby designates that very same essential flesh which he offered for us upon the cross. Origen, in Homily 16 on Numbers, and Jerome, on Ecclesiastes, affirm this in express words both concerning the simple Word and concerning baptism. Augustine likewise more than once equates the simple Word with the sacraments on this principle.

What more? If Andreae does not believe us, let him believe the Swabian *Syntagma*, that is, Brenz himself before he became a ubiquitarian, whose words are these: “At the time of the Passover lamb Christ was indeed remote from bodily ears and eyes, yet he was most present to faith. Setting its wings in motion, faith flies from this world into the spiritual realm and, in keeping with the nature of the vision of God, saw the things that belong to Christ. For just as God sees all things, even future things, as present to himself, so faith makes a thing situated at the greatest distance according to the world’s apprehension most present to itself, and sees it.”

I am nevertheless aware that the same Brenz later established this distinction between the fathers and us: that to them God gave the Spirit of the Word, who presented Christ as present to the fathers in the promises, whereas to us he additionally gave the letter, by which Christ’s body and blood are presented to us as present. As though the fathers did not also have their own letter! Yet if he refers that distinction to what Augustine says—namely, that our sacraments are clearer and more august—and keeps it within the presence belonging to faith, not even in this does he disagree with us.

The Apostle indeed did not write that faith is the *hypostasis* of things which do not exist, but of things which are hoped for. Yet that things not yet existing, but hoped for by the fathers because believed, are designated in this way is plainly evident both from the antithesis involving *hypostasis*, by which things already subsisting are declared, and from the examples of that faith of the fathers.

[Part I | PDF page 60 | logical source scan 59 | printed page 55]

Dogma V

That in the holy Supper only the power, operation, and merit of Christ’s absent body and blood are dispensed.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Dogma V Condemned by Andreae

So far are we from separating Christ’s power and benefits in the Lord’s Supper from the communication of Christ himself that, on the contrary, we assert that in the simple Word as well, and in baptism, both are inseparably joined and are offered to the mind to be received by faith.

Andreae’s Observation

1. Christ offers and presents his flesh and blood in the sacrament to be received not by the MIND alone, but by the mouth of the body, whether a person truly believes or does not believe.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observation

See the reply to the second observation upon our fourth Antithesis.

Dogma VI

That among those who eat unworthily and take judgment upon themselves by receiving the Lord’s Supper are numbered even those who labor under some weakness of faith or other infirmities, although they have true faith.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Dogma VI Condemned by Andreae

It never entered our mind to establish perfect faith in anyone, since such perfection cannot coexist with the remnants of the flesh. So far are we from failing to distinguish true faith from perfect faith, or from barring from the Supper those who labor under this weakness of faith, that we say the Supper was instead instituted for this very reason: that we might be confirmed and increased in faith.

Andreae’s Observation

1. Why, then, do you make two classes of unworthy persons, and assert that even those who truly believe receive judgment?

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observation

To labor under weakness of faith, from which no mortal is exempt—since we must all pray daily that we not be led into temptation—is one thing; to approach the Lord’s table unworthily is another. And we rightly distinguish more than one degree among those who approach unworthily. Some are altogether ungodly and Epicureans. Others are endowed with merely historical faith, labor under the gravest sins, and have no concern for repentance. It is certain that by profaning the sacraments these call destruction down upon themselves, and their profanation redounds to the most unworthy contempt of Christ himself. Others are not destitute of true faith, but give no thought to preparing their soul and are not reconciled with the brothers whom they have offended;

[Part I | PDF page 61 | logical source scan 60 | printed page 56]

in short, they live more dissolute lives than they ought. We would not say that, like the former persons, they receive nothing but the sacred symbols; nevertheless, they deserve the gravest correction. Such were the Corinthians. In admonishing them, Paul was surely speaking, in all probability, neither to Epicureans, nor to profane persons, nor to those endowed with merely historical faith: “Let each person examine himself,” he says; and, “For this reason many among you sleep.”

Dogma VII

That some affirm—not without horrible blasphemy—that it follows from sacramental eating that Christ’s body is carried into the stomach and there digested or not digested, and consequently is at last either excreted or not excreted, together with any similar things horrible to hear and unworthy of Christian people, whether spoken or written.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Dogma VII Condemned by Andreae

We readily believe that it never occurred to the defenders of either transubstantiation or consubstantiation to affirm such monstrosities. Yet we confess that we cannot see how these things—however absurd, false, and blasphemous—do not necessarily follow that oral eating, unless they prefer to establish that the substance of Christ’s flesh and blood vanishes once it has passed the teeth and palate. We wish this, however, to be said with the goodwill of all.

Andreae’s Observation

1. It is horrible to hear that, if Christ’s body is received by the mouth with the bread, it is also excreted in a bowel movement.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observation

I marvel at the shamelessness with which Andreae writes that I, Beza, infer that these things follow from sacramental eating. Do I deny sacramental eating? I have not said, therefore—and do not say—that those things follow from the true sacramental conjunction and eating, but that they follow just as necessarily from that essential consubstantiation and oral eating as from the papist transubstantiation. This is so unless, just as the transubstantiators take refuge in the disappearance of the Lord’s body and blood when the species cease, these men terminate their nearly hypostatic union of essences and oral eating at the very entrance into the mouth, so that the bread alone is ground by the teeth and carried into the stomach, while the Lord’s body and blood suddenly withdraw themselves from the mouths and teeth of those eating.

The former things are indeed horrible to say, as Andreae rightly judges, while the latter are exceedingly absurd. But what can one do? Let them therefore detest the premises from which these things necessarily follow, or teach us how, when the antecedent is posited, that which necessarily coheres with it is not also posited. Andreae will perhaps take refuge in what he wrote against our

[Part I | PDF page 62 | logical source scan 61 | printed page 57]

second Antithesis in his second refutation: namely, that in this mystery the character of Christ’s body—though actually and essentially present upon earth with the bread and eaten by the mouth—is spiritual, which he opposes to the Capernaite character. But granting this, since the rationale of bodily nourishment does not apply to a body stripped of the character of a body, what will become of the chief sacramental analogy posited between the bodily nourishment of this life and the spiritual nourishment of the other life?

And if, as Andreae previously contended in the first refutation of our first Antithesis, the oral eating of Christ’s body was instituted so that the spiritual eating by faith might be confirmed through it, while there is no fruit of eating apart from the nourishment supplied by the thing eaten, let him see how he is not compelled to establish that just as the eating of Christ’s own body takes place in the mouth, so its digestion and nourishment take place in the stomach, by which the spiritual nourishment that takes place by faith is confirmed. But if this is so, and it is horrible to establish a bodily digestion of Christ’s body and some bodily nourishment arising from it, why do we not all detest these monstrosities together and with one consent, give glory to God, and restore peace to the churches?

A WRITING OF DR. BEZA CONTAINING HIS AND HIS COLLEAGUES’ CONFESSION CONCERNING THE LORD’S SUPPER

SUMMARY OF OUR DOCTRINE CONCERNING THE LORD’S SUPPER

At the outset we testify to the reverend men with whom we must now deal that we who, by the will of the most illustrious Prince Frederick, Count of Württemberg and Montbéliard, and with the consent of the churches of Bern and Geneva, have come to this colloquy, conduct all these proceedings as private persons, altogether without prejudice to any church or any pastor.

The Marginal Refutatory Annotations of Dr. Jakob Andreae

1. Although we too prejudice nothing in our churches, we nevertheless testify that we are by no means uncertain or doubtful about our doctrine, since it rests upon the open and express Word of God.

Thesis I of Theodore Beza

ON SIGNS

When the word “signs,” “symbols,” or “sacraments” is taken in a narrower sense,

Andreae’s Annotations

What Beza’s sacraments signify.

2. Equivocation in the word “SACRAMENT” is the refuge of error, because

[Part I | PDF page 63 | logical source scan 62 | printed page 58]

in our churches it always embraces two things, earthly and heavenly, sacramentally and really joined.

we call things which strike the external senses and which, by the Lord’s institution and command, have been set apart from common and natural use to signify and communicate spiritual and sacred things to us. Such are the bread and wine in the Lord’s Supper, together with the rites prescribed by Christ for their right administration.

Andreae’s Annotations (continued)

We say, moreover, that this sacramental signification is by no means a bare external representation of the things signified, by which the mind is merely admonished to conceive that which is presented to the senses, such as the common use of images and pictures. Rather, so far as God is concerned, the true offering of the things signified, presented to our souls, is always joined with it.

3. Christ ordained external instruments not for the signification but for the offering of spiritual things.

Foundation of This Thesis

We judge that we agree in all respects on this matter, with this one exception: we teach that the things signified are offered to the mind alone, whereas you hold that they are also exhibited to be received by the mouth. This will have to be discussed in the second question, in which we treat the offering of the signs and of the things signified.

4. There is no agreement here, but open disagreement. For the external elements in the sacraments are appointed for the offering, not the signification, of spiritual things.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

I wonder from what Andreae infers that we are not sufficiently certain of the truth of the doctrine we profess merely because, not furnished with any special mandate from the other churches of our confession, we testified that what we were doing was being conducted by us without prejudice to anyone. By God’s grace, we have not the slightest doubt about the goodness of the cause we defend. But it did not belong to our modesty, nor to the private office we hold in the church, to transgress the limits of our calling.

Whether it was lawful for Andreae himself and a few others—either self-appointed, or called outside their own churches without the free and legitimate votes of well-ordered churches—to prescribe by their own judgment formulas of the confession and “Concord,” as they call it, and to assume dictatorial power; and indeed whether they rightly persuaded princes to grant them this authority in the Christian church: let Andreae see to it, and God most good and great will judge at his own time.

How can Andreae attribute the fallacy of homonymy to us, when we began this treatment with that very distinction? But if Andreae denies that the designation “sacrament” is sometimes—indeed, very frequently—

[Part I | PDF page 64 | logical source scan 63 | printed page 59]

referred specifically to the signs, let him abolish this customary definition received without controversy, according to which sacraments are said, on Augustine’s authority, to be visible words; and also this saying of Augustine: “When signs are employed for divine things, they are called sacraments”; and this: “Sacraments receive their name from the things signified”; and this saying of Cyprian: “The effect of eternal life inheres in the visible sacraments”; and also this rule: “Nothing has the character of a sacrament outside its use.”

Things which are subordinated do not conflict. In Andreae’s inference, therefore, there is a fallacy of division. And since he himself testifies on page 172 that no one has ever denied that sacraments are signs—though not merely things which signify—he must undo his own work and delete this inference as slanderous. For in this very passage we teach that sacramental signification always has the offering of the things signified joined with it, although that which is signified by external signs in a bodily and sensible mode is offered only spiritually to the mind.

Thesis II of Theodore Beza

ON THE THINGS SACRAMENTALLY SIGNIFIED

We teach that by that bread is signified that body of Christ delivered for us; by that wine, that blood of his poured out for us; by the breaking of that bread and the pouring of that blood, those dreadful torments which Christ endured for us both in body and soul; by the outward distribution of those symbols, the inward and spiritual offering which Christ makes to our minds; and finally, by the outward reception of those symbols, the spiritual reception by faith of the same Christ—sacramentally, yet truly, as we have said—in accordance with Christ’s own ordinance.

Andreae’s Annotation

1. By divine ordinance the bread and wine are employed for the sake not of signification but of communication.

Foundation of the Second Thesis

Since the Lord not only showed which signs must be used in these mysteries, but also commanded that what he did be done, we teach that not only the bread and wine, but also the rites for administering them, must be diligently observed, because they are not without their own signification, and indeed one which is by no means empty.

Andreae’s Annotation

2. In the sacraments those rites must be observed which Christ commanded to be preserved.

[Part I | PDF page 65 | logical source scan 64 | printed page 60]

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

See the preceding reply to Observations 3 and 4 upon the preceding thesis.

This is nothing against us, but rather against those who have not yet restored the breaking of bread—round individual loaves having been substituted for it—and reception into the hands.

Thesis III of Theodore Beza

ON THE SACRAMENTAL CONJUNCTION OR COPULATION OF THE SIGNS AND THE THINGS SIGNIFIED

Since sacraments in the narrower sense, as has been said, are signs, we place the sacramental conjunction in the mutual relation and disposition between the signs and the things signified. By this relation those things which strike the senses are, by Christ’s ordinance, withdrawn from their common and natural use and employed both to signify sacred things to us and actually to present them by divine action.

Andreae’s Annotation

1. The external elements are not sacraments, but merely a part of the sacraments. Therefore the snares concealed beneath the varying senses of the word “sacrament” must be diligently observed and avoided.

Foundation of This Thesis

The first foundation of this thesis is the truth of Christ’s body as truly circumscribed and local, both before and after glorification. This truth cannot stand if Christ’s body is said to be present with the bread in any way other than by that relational disposition. Therefore, by however many testimonies the truth of Christ’s body is confirmed—and they are almost innumerable—by so many testimonies is real consubstantiation overthrown.

2. The truth of Christ’s circumscribed and local body does not conflict with the real presence of the same body of Christ in the sacrament, since that presence is not physical and local but sacramental.

Second: however many passages confirm Christ’s true and physical departure from earth into heaven, and his return from heaven which will take place only on that last day, by so many passages is this very dogma refuted, since it conflicts diametrically with them.

3. So too Christ’s physical bodily departure from earth does not conflict with his sacramental presence.

Third: Paul expressly says in 2 Corinthians 5 that we are now away from the Lord as pilgrims and desires to be released so that he may be with Christ. And when he commands us to seek the things above, it is not without reason that he adds, “where Christ is, sitting at God’s right hand.” This would be superfluous if Christ were still dwelling with us upon earth.

4. Bodily or earthly pilgrimage away from Christ does not conflict with sacramental presence and conjunction with Christ’s body, which is not earthly but heavenly and supernatural.

[Part I | PDF page 66 | logical source scan 65 | printed page 61]

Likewise—to pass over innumerable other testimonies—Christ expressly testifies that he is going to prepare the place where he will receive his own.

Two things follow from this. First, when he left this world he departed to a definite place. Second, those who are in the world are not with Christ—that is, they are not in the place where Christ’s flesh now is—but when they depart from the world they are received into the place where Christ already is, namely paradise, as he himself explains in addressing the thief in Luke 23.

Andreae’s Annotations

5. Beza and his party think that we cannot be with Christ, nor Christ with us, in any way except locally and by a physical presence. This is the foundation of this whole error concerning the Lord’s Supper.

Fourth: we affirm that the ancient church perpetually agreed upon the true absence from us of the essence of Christ’s flesh after he ascended into heaven, and upon the perpetual organic circumscription and locality of that same essence of Christ’s flesh, which is now situated in heaven, not upon earth. These can cohere with no sacramental conjunction other than one which is relational and relative, any more than contradictories can stand together.

6. It has been demonstrated in the solid refutation of the *Orthodox Consensus* that this was not the consensus of the ancient and purer church.

7. The conjunction of Christ’s body with the signs upon earth is not relational and relative, but real, heavenly, divine, and supernatural.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

There are no snares here on our part, since we state the distinction of homonymy expressly. We showed a little above that the designation “sacrament” is sometimes attributed to the signs alone, and this cannot be denied except with the greatest obstinacy. Indeed, I think Andreae would not dare deny even this: that the sacred actions themselves are sometimes called sacraments. The very names “baptism” and “the Lord’s Supper” indicate it, for they are certainly predicated not in the category of substance but in that of action, though they concern the twofold material—namely the signs and the things signified—as what are called their component parts.

What do I hear? Can the modes of any thing’s essence and of its actual presence be set in contradictory opposition to one another without falsehood? Then let both sides of a contradiction be true! Those who think this violate the truth even more than those of whom the prophet says that they call evil good and good evil, set darkness in place of light and light in place of darkness, and bitter in place of sweet and sweet in place of bitter. Certainly, unless the mode of actual presence and the essence of the thing itself always cohere, Christ had no need to take his argument from the palpability and visibility, and consequently the locality, of his body

[Part I | PDF page 67 | logical source scan 66 | printed page 62]

to establish true faith in the true resurrection of a real body. And everything the fathers disputed against the Marcionites to assert the reality of Christ’s flesh is empty. If, however, Andreae were to define the sacramental presence as truly spiritual with us—namely that presence which is said of Christ’s true, circumscribed, and local body, but which is offered only to the mind contemplating it through the eye of faith, both in the Word and in the sacraments, by imagination indeed, that is, by the apprehension and conception of the mind, yet an altogether true, solid, and effectual apprehension—Andreae could have written nothing more correct or apt in our favor and against himself.
