Just as the union of the humanity truly hypostatically assumed by the Word and dwelling upon earth did not take away its essential properties, that is, those by which it is corporeal—for otherwise it would have been absorbed, not assumed—so neither the ascension nor glory took away from it this corporeality, that is, its circumscription and locality. Therefore, just as that body was truly and physically taken upward, so it truly and physically is, and will remain, absent in its essence until it returns on the last day in the same way that it ascended.

Vigilius therefore rightly and necessarily concluded against Eutyches: “Just as, when it was upon earth, this flesh was not in heaven, so now that it has ascended into heaven and is there, it is not upon earth.” And Augustine: “It is not now upon earth, because it ascended into heaven, and we await it to come from there.”

Whichever way Andreae turns, the absence of our bodies from Christ’s body which Paul establishes is true and real. For that very reason Paul also desires to depart from this body, so that he may be with Christ in reality and in his spirit. Next, there is childish begging of the question in this inference. Andreae assumes as true and as proved by himself what he will never prove, since it is diametrically opposed to the articles of faith: namely, an essential and actual but non-local presence of Christ’s body, whether upon earth or in heaven.

Yet in this same observation he says with perfect truth that the conjunction of Christ’s body with its members is heavenly and supernatural, and consequently does not consist in any real approach of his body toward ours, but is spiritual, that is, a conjunction of mind and faith. Once this is established, that real consubstantiation—whether visible or invisible—and the whole fiction of oral eating are also abolished.

Andreae plays the sophist by employing the name “Christ” in place of Christ’s humanity. For although Christ is true God and man and is never separated from his humanity, what Christian would deny that he nevertheless dwells in his saints not according to the essence of his flesh, but by his divinity—not only essentially, but also by a peculiar activity and grace, of which grace the Lord’s Supper is the token and pledge? And what Christian denies that from heaven Christ rules the church by the dominion and power of that humanity of his, and by the power of his Spirit sustains and wonderfully preserves his members who are still situated upon earth? Who,

[Part I | PDF page 68 | logical source scan 67 | printed page 63]

then, denies it? But Andreae’s statement that Beza thinks Christ can be with us and we with Christ only locally is also a filthy slander. For on the contrary, what is principally disputed in this place between him and me, except that I say, cry out, and shout that while we live here we are truly joined to Christ spiritually—that is, by mind and faith—in such a way that he lives in us and we in him? Andreae, however, on the other side urges, insists, and clamors that in addition an essential real presence in, under, or with the bread, and an oral eating, must be required, yet one which is non-local. I, on the other hand, affirm that this is not supernatural but anti-natural, and accordingly purely false and imaginary. For this is finally what Beza affirms, and will always affirm with the entire orthodox church: wherever Christ’s essential body is actually present, it is necessarily present both in a place and locally.

The person who simply denies something proves nothing.

As though the sacramental relation, insofar as it depends upon divine ordinance and leads us to that great mystery of our spiritual fellowship with Christ by faith, were not truly divine and heavenly! As though, finally, we did not in any way distinguish signs which—because they are transferred to divine things—are called sacraments on Augustine’s authority, from any other signs whatsoever, whether natural signs or those employed in human affairs! Let the person who denies a relational relation also deny that sacraments are signs. Therefore I appeal to Andreae himself on page 172, where he thought and spoke a little more correctly.

Thesis IV of Theodore Beza

ON THE RECEPTION OF THE SIGNS AND THINGS SIGNIFIED IN THE LORD’S SUPPER RIGHTLY ADMINISTERED

Since sacraments—taking this word in its broader denotation—consist of two things, one earthly and the other heavenly, we teach that the earthly thing, namely the bread and wine, is received by earthly instruments, namely the hand and mouth; and likewise that the sacramental rites are performed by bodily instruments. The heavenly thing, however, namely whatever is sacramentally signified through them, is apprehended by the mind alone through faith, just as it is presented to the mind alone.

Andreae’s Annotations

1. Equivocation in the word “sacrament” is a refuge for error. For among the doctors of the purer church it signifies two things, an earthly and a heavenly, just as does the word *eucharistia* in Irenaeus and other ecclesiastical writers.

2. In the words of the testament Christ commanded his body to be eaten by the mouth, with no mention made of mind or faith.

3. Chrysostom testifies that Christ dispenses spiritual things through bodily things when, presenting the bread, he says, “Take, eat; this is

Foundation of This Thesis

Although Christ’s body, which is offered to be received in the Supper, is a true organic body,

[Part I | PDF page 69 | logical source scan 68 | printed page 64]

Andreae’s Annotations (continued)

3. “my body.” Thus he also commanded it to be received through a bodily instrument, that is, by the mouth. The analogy therefore fits perfectly: just as Christ’s body is given WITH the bread, so it is received BY THE MOUTH.

nevertheless analogy requires that the mode by which this nourishment is received correspond to the character and end of the nourishment. But the nourishment and end of the thing sacramentally signified—that is, Christ’s own flesh and blood—are spiritual: they look toward spiritual conjunction with Christ and the drawing of eternal life from him. Therefore the mode of receiving these things must be spiritual and must be accomplished by the soul with its proper instrument, namely faith, both in the simple Word and in the sacraments.

4. That the mode of reception is bodily is demonstrated from Christ’s words, “Take, eat,” which is done with the mouth. He does not say, “Believe.”

Next, the earthly reception of the earthly signs is a pledge of that other, spiritual reception. Consequently, just as “eating” and “drinking” are said properly of the reception of the signs, so by a sacramental metonymy they are said figuratively of the things signified, in which what belongs to the signs is attributed to the thing signified.

5. The eating of Christ’s body in the Lord’s Supper is by no means figurative, but true and real, taking place in the mystery. Metonymy therefore has no place here.

Both receptions, therefore, neither can nor ought to be accomplished by the mouth. For in that case either both things would be a pledge of some third thing different from them in its entire kind, or one of them would be its own sign and pledge—either at once the sign and the thing signified, or the pledge and the very thing of which it was the pledge. Each alternative is utterly absurd. For this reason, in this very argument Irenaeus calls the one thing heavenly and distinguishes it from the earthly.

6. Both receptions can take place by the mouth, although the rationale of each is different. Thus Mary bore God and man in one birth, although she bore God in one respect and man in another. Therefore the absurdities inferred from this by no means follow, since the mystical eating of Christ’s body takes place in the physical eating of the consecrated bread in the sacrament.

Second, if the substance of Christ’s own body were received by the mouth, it too would remain, at least in the godly and faithful. They would therefore become substantial members of Christ, from which another utter absurdity would follow: namely, that the church was not Christ’s mystical body, but a body actually and essentially composed of the substance of Christ’s own body and the bodies of all the godly.

7. If the eating of Christ’s body in the Lord’s Supper were physical, those absurdities would follow. But because it is heavenly and supernatural, and therefore its mode is altogether spiritual, this consequence is null. And just as the church is a mystical body with Christ, so this reception also takes place in the mystery.

Third, since that flesh of Christ is the bond by which all the godly coalesce into one mystical body, it would necessarily follow that the substances or persons of the godly are not separate, but that, just as they cohere with Christ the head, so they cohere with one another in their very

8. With respect to their own substances, Christ’s members remain separate persons in themselves; nevertheless they

[Part I | PDF page 70 | logical source scan 69 | printed page 65]

own essences and persons. But since the connection of the faithful members with one another is spiritual, yet altogether true and most intimate—so much so that they are said to be one heart and one soul—it follows that they are also joined spiritually and by faith to their head. With him they are therefore said to be one body, namely a mystical body, and also one spirit; and Christ dwells in them through the Spirit.

Andreae’s Annotations (continued)

8. are mystically joined with Christ—that is, really, not relationally or relatively, but truly in this mystery by a supernatural mode.

9. That conjunction is not relational or relative, but real; not significative, but exhibitive.

Fourth: that the conjunction of Christ, God and man, with the church his bride is purely spiritual and is accomplished by the instrument of faith is evident from what are, as it were, the offspring of his marriage, which are called the fruits of righteousness. For the condition of both conception, generation, and the very mode of marriage must correspond to the condition of the offspring.

10. Spatial distance does not abolish this conjunction, since before God and with God there is neither place nor time.

11. This spiritual conjunction, however, takes place through bodily and external instruments, by which spiritual things are joined with external things.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

We have already spoken about the emptiness of this refutation, in the reply to the second observation upon our first Thesis and several times elsewhere.

We have also spoken about this in the reply to the second observation upon the fifth Antithesis.

Andreae ought to have identified the passage in Chrysostom so that it might appear whether he cites it aptly or wrongly. Yet it is most true that not only in the sacraments, but also in the simple Word, spiritual things are offered to us through sensible things—that is, through the intervention of sensible things and instruments. But I affirm that it is no less absurd to say that Christ is essentially eaten and drunk by the mouth in the Lord’s Supper than to say that in the simple preaching of the gospel he is received by the ears themselves.

For what the proclaimed Word does here through hearing, the visible symbols do in the sacraments through the eyes: they bear witness to the mind and faith—to the mind, that it may understand the things offered to it; and to faith, that it may apprehend the things understood by the mind. As for those expressions, frequently occurring among the fathers, that the Lord’s body “enters the mouth,” “is eaten,” and “is drunk,” they must be understood partly tropically and figuratively, with the name of the things signified transferred to the signs themselves, as the fathers expressly teach, especially Theodoret and Augustine. In part, however, they must be referred solely to the efficacy of the sacramental offering, not to declaring the conjunction itself of the signs and things signified. This is clear from very many other similar expressions

[Part I | PDF page 71 | logical source scan 70 | printed page 66]

which Andreae would assuredly be ashamed to interpret *kata to rheton*, according to the bare wording. For in the celebration of these mysteries the Lord’s body is said to be created, to fall upon the ground, to be seen, touched, and consumed, and the tongue to be reddened with Christ’s blood. In this same sense Luther rightly and truly wrote that, because of the sacramental conjunction, things which properly belong to the flesh are said of the body. He was nevertheless deceived in referring these things to a real and actual coexistence upon earth of the substances of the Lord’s body and blood and of the bread and wine in the sacramental conjunction, offering, and reception. The reality of Christ’s body in no way admits such a coexistence, and even if it were conceded to be true it would in no way help us toward the spiritual end of these mysteries, namely the constitution and nourishment of the mystical body.

Finally, Andreae adds something about analogy. He imagines that it consists in this and holds beautifully for him: that just as Christ’s body is given with the bread, so it is received by the mouth. Even if that were as true as it is false, however, it would contribute nothing to my Thesis. For there I discuss not an analogy between giving and receiving, but between reception and the end attained through that reception. Since this end is one of purely spiritual, not bodily, nourishment, I rightly inferred that the reception of that food must likewise be spiritual, not bodily, and consequently is accomplished not by the mouth but by mind and faith. Nevertheless, the most fitting pledges of that spiritual reception are employed, namely the oral eating and drinking of the symbols. The distinction between the sacraments and the simple preached Word consists finally in this external form, not in the thing which is offered and received.

See what we said in the reply to the second observation upon Antithesis 5.

As though a person who speaks tropically were lying! As though what is present only to mind and faith were not truly and actually present to them, and what is spiritually received by faith were not truly and actually received! The fact itself proves that the mind is said figuratively to eat and drink, whether the Lord’s body is established as actually and presently there or as absent.

And lest Andreae object that he is treating not mental but oral eating and drinking in the Lord’s Supper, I answer that one cannot rightly deny that the very same rationale on account of which Augustine says the Lord’s command in John 6 concerning eating his flesh and drinking his blood must be understood figuratively also applies in the celebration of the Lord’s Supper. For in both places the subject is one and the same flesh and the same blood, and it is as horrible and wicked to eat essentially present human flesh by the mouth invisibly as visibly, and to drink essentially present blood by the mouth. The latter was expressly forbidden in the law of Moses even concerning any blood whatever.

These are mere tricks. For in their entire kind and in every

[Part I | PDF page 72 | logical source scan 71 | printed page 67]

mode, the hypostatic union differs from sacramental conjunction, and the natures—deity and humanity—are altogether different, from which arises that difference in Christ’s birth. But Christ’s flesh and blood, insofar as they are corporeal and material things, circumscribed and local by their own nature, agree completely with bread and wine in the genus of bodily things. Therefore, when joined, they must be present by one and the same rationale if essential and actual presence is attributed both to the former and the latter. From this it will also follow necessarily that they cannot be offered to the mouth and received by the mouth even in different modes, much less in contradictory modes—the bread and wine in a natural, visible, and sensible mode, but the Lord’s body and blood invisibly, non-locally, and hyperphysically.

The same absurd and manifestly false things recur here. For Christ’s body was physical when he instituted the Supper, not yet glorified and indeed near to its humiliation. So far was it from being present with that sacred bread by some anti-physical rationale, or being capable of reception by the apostles in such a manner, that it did not admit even some hyperphysical essential and actual presence with the bread or reception, but only that spiritual presence which we establish. For this presence, though altogether true and effectual, neither then detracted anything from Christ’s truly physical and circumscribed, and still mortal, body, which was sitting truly and locally at the table before the disciples, nor does it now detract the least thing from that same body in glory, exalted above all created things yet truly physical according to all the essential properties of a true body.

Is the sacramental conjunction or reception now different from what it was when that holy Supper was first instituted by Christ? Furthermore, the rationale of receiving that flesh must necessarily be established as corresponding to the mystical unification of Christ’s members with the head himself through participation in his flesh. Yet that unification is purely spiritual and depends upon apprehension by faith alone, apart from any actual approach or contact of the essence of Christ’s body with our bodies, or of our bodies with his. Who therefore does not see that neither in the Word nor in the sacraments is there any need here for an actual coexistence of Christ’s own flesh with the bread, or for bodily and oral eating or drinking?

Who among us was ever so insane as to make either the offering or the reception of Christ’s body relational or relative? Andreae therefore changes the state of the question either ignorantly or slanderously. What I said about the mutual conjunction of the signs and the things sacramentally signified—which themselves are proposed in the word of promise to be understood and believed not by the mouth but by the mind and faith of those approaching—he transfers to the mode of offering and reception.

I have just answered the first part of this refutation. As for the lat-

[Part I | PDF page 73 | logical source scan 72 | printed page 68]

ter part, what more absurd thing could be said? For what prevents something which consists in a mere relation with another thing from being truly offered, and indeed sometimes even visibly and palpably? Thus when a ring is given, a husband gives himself to his wife; when a key is given, a house is delivered to be possessed. And to come closer to our subject: by the voice of the gospel, with which Christ coheres assuredly by no mode but that of relation, Christ is truly offered to be enjoyed, together with eternal life in him; and so the gospel becomes a sure and effectual instrument of salvation to all believers.

Likewise in baptism there is a mutual and by no means empty sacramental relation between the water as sign and Christ’s blood as the thing signified, and likewise between external washing and the washing away of sins. The things signified are offered just as truly, though spiritually, and received by faith, as the sprinkling of water is truly employed bodily and sensibly with the invocation of the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.

So far are we from denying this observation of Andreae that, on the contrary, we rely upon this very foundation when we say that in the sacraments the conjunction of the signs situated upon earth and the things signified situated in heaven is spiritual, yet most certain and true. We say likewise that these things, though distant from us in their essence by an interval as great as the blessed seats above all the heavens are distant from earth, are apprehended by faith through the Holy Spirit’s power.

By the same reasoning, we affirm that through the same faith, which is the *hypostasis* of things hoped for, and by the power of the same Holy Spirit, the fathers ate that very food and drank that very drink, although they did not yet then actually exist. Employing other signs, we now eat and drink them, although in their essence they are situated at the greatest distance from us; that is, having received them spiritually, we apply them to ourselves most effectually.

For this reason, finally, we assert that they were already then participants in Christ’s sacrifice, although it had not yet been offered, and in our redemption proceeding from it. For in their Word and sacraments they beheld with the eyes of faith as already present the things which were to be accomplished in their own time. In the same way, in the Word and sacraments of the new covenant we behold with the eyes of the same faith that same sacrifice, although now past and completed, and Christ himself, although no longer hanging upon the cross, yet as though his blood were still dripping from the cross; and by the same faith we apprehend him as with a hand. On this matter see the reply to Andreae’s first observation upon our response to the fourth dogma.

Does Andreae number Christ’s essential body itself among the instruments? By no means, I think, since it is the very thing given to us by employing those instruments. If that is so, what purpose is served by the oral eating of Christ’s body itself which he imagines? Indeed, if in the holy Supper of the Lord he numbers no other subsisting instruments than that bread and that wine, why does he not also number only their external

[Part I | PDF page 74 | logical source scan 73 | printed page 69]

offering and reception among the organic sacramental rites? Andreae will say, “Because Christ established yet another organic sacramental rite, namely the external and oral eating of his body itself.” But what is this except begging the question—that is, assuming as proved the very thing under controversy? So far are we from denying what Andreae writes here that, on the contrary, if it is rightly understood, we testify that he could have offered nothing more suitable for refuting his own essential presence and oral eating.

Thesis V of Theodore Beza

ON THE EFFECTS OF THE LORD’S SUPPER

It is evident that the Lord’s Supper was instituted not for the sake of bread and wine but for human salvation. Its proper effect, therefore, is the salvation of those who approach it worthily—that is, with repentance and faith—through the confirmation of their spiritual association with Christ. Through this association, just as in a natural animate body the members which naturally cohere with the head receive sense and motion from it, so those spiritually joined to Christ increasingly obtain all the spiritual gifts proper to the regenerate, and finally eternal life itself, flowing from Christ the head into the members which cohere with him.

For this reason they are also called his members individually and his mystical body collectively, while the gift of love is also increased in them through the Holy Spirit. For members separated from one another can neither make one body nor be the one body of one and the same head.

Andreae’s Annotation

1. Although the Lord’s Supper was not instituted for the sake of bread and wine, nevertheless it pleased Christ sacramentally to join his body and blood with the bread and wine and distribute them to those eating in the Lord’s Supper, just as his words expressly sound: “Take, eat; this is my body,” and so forth.

Its other effect is the condemnation of those who approach unworthily—that is, either in ignorance of this mystery or with mere unbelief and impenitence—flowing not from the Supper itself but gushing forth from its unworthy use.

2. In his Supper Christ works judgment in unworthy guests just as effectually as life and consolation in worthy ones, because it is not only an aroma from life to life but also an aroma from death to death (2 Corinthians 2).

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

This does not refute our position, but once again begs the question, as though Andreae had proved that these words of Christ—“Take,”

[Part I | PDF page 75 | logical source scan 74 | printed page 70]

“Eat,” and “Drink”—words which he does not deny must be understood metaphorically in the preaching of the simple Word—are spoken in the holy Supper of the Lord *according to the literal wording* no differently of the Lord’s flesh and blood than of that bread and that wine. But we have already refuted this invention several times with solid and invincible arguments drawn from the abiding reality of Christ’s physical body, from his true ascension and return, and finally from two most certain axioms. The first is that anti-physical properties—that is, properties which abolish the very existence of a physical body—cannot truly be attributed to a truly physical body such as Christ’s body is, however glorified it may be. The other is that, with absolutely no exception, one of two contradictories must necessarily be false; consequently God cannot will both at once. This is not because he is not omnipotent, but rather because he cannot at the same time will not to do what he wills, or will what he does not will, since he is truly and immutably omnipotent.

Moreover, as for Andreae’s admission here that the Lord’s Supper was instituted not for the sake of bread and wine but for the sake of human beings, let him see how this agrees with that real, essential, and actual—and, if he is to be believed, almost hypostatic—union of the Lord’s very body and blood *in*, *under*, or *with* that bread and wine. For, as regards those who approach worthily, eating Christ’s body with the mouth and drinking his blood, and thus becoming his mystical member—that is, one governed by his power and grace—would be a far lesser condescension than coexisting so closely, intimately, and inseparably with his body and blood. (Unless perhaps Andreae means that believers, just as in his opinion they eat Christ’s body with the mouth, likewise cohere really and actually in their own bodies with his flesh and blood.) Thus, just as by reason of the hypostatic union that assumed man is God, and God the Word is man, and wherever he is, he is there whole—that is, God and man—so also by reason of the sacramental union this bread is the Lord’s body and that wine is the Lord’s blood, evidently by the same kind of predication which they call “unusual”; and wherever one says, “Here is bread,” one should likewise say, “Here is essentially Christ’s body and blood.” As for those who approach unworthily, however, that oral eating would be not merely a lesser condescension but the height of misery.

Andreae distinguishes his spiritual reception of Christ, which takes place in the simple Word apprehended through faith—a matter which we discussed more fully in our first Antithesis—from sacramental reception, not only in outward form and administration but also in the thing itself. It is as though, in the simple Word and likewise in Baptism, only Christ’s benefits are received through faith, while through unbelief the preached Word itself and the outward washing of Baptism are rejected; but in sacramental reception at last the very essence of Christ’s flesh and blood is received with the mouth both by the worthy and by the unworthy—

[Part I | PDF page 76 | logical source scan 75 | printed page 71]

by believers for salvation, but by the unworthy for destruction. He therefore twists Paul’s passage in 2 Corinthians 2:16 improperly if we say that the apostle is speaking only of simple preaching through the Word. But if this apostolic passage is taken generally, both of the simple preaching of the Gospel and of the administration of the sacraments, Andreae is caught in his own net. For the apostle establishes no distinction between those two receptions, either as regards the thing received or as regards that twofold end of life or death, but uses the same words in each case. Who, then, does not see that according to the apostle’s words Christ’s essence is offered no less in the simple preaching of the Word and in Baptism than in the Lord’s Supper? Andreae denies this, separating that essence from the reception of Christ’s benefits both in the simple preaching of the Word and in the sacraments.

Thesis VI of Theodore Beza

ON THE CAUSES OF THE SUPPER’S SALUTARY EFFECTS

We distinguish the causes which concur in this mystery in such a way that we credit the association of us with Christ entirely to the infinite and ineffable power of the Holy Spirit. Paul therefore calls it a great mystery in Ephesians 5. We believe, however, that all those heavenly gifts of which we have spoken, and finally also that eternal life both of body and of soul, flow into us from the one Christ, God and man, crucified for us and at last exalted in that flesh above all things, according to the ordinance of God the almighty Father.

With respect to God himself, we establish as instrumental causes partly the pastor, doing what he does in God’s name and by his command; partly the words of institution; and partly the symbols and sacramental rites. With respect to us, the instrumental cause is faith implanted in us by God’s gift. Yet we teach that God uses these instruments of his in such a way that he infuses into them no intrinsic efficient power; rather, through them he testifies to us that he alone acts inwardly—namely, bringing about that spiritual association of Christ with us and everything we obtain from it.

Andreae’s Annotations

1. Although Christ acts through the Holy Spirit, nevertheless it was not said in the Lord’s Supper: “Take, eat; this is the Holy Spirit.” Christ’s flesh, therefore, must by no means be stripped of this efficacy and the efficacy attributed to the Holy Spirit alone, by participation in whom the flesh gives life.

2. The sacramental reception of Christ’s body and blood occurs not only by faith but also by the mouth of the body, because he says, “Take, eat”; he does not say, “Take and believe.”

3. If this intrinsic sacramental power is removed, the substance of the sacrament is dissolved. For Christ does not merely testify, but acts and exercises that power in the use of this sacrament.

[Part I | PDF page 77 | logical source scan 76 | printed page 72]

We think that these latter two theses require no proof among you.

4. Proof was especially necessary, because they strip the sacraments of their spiritual power.

Montbéliard, 22 March 1586.

Theodore Beza, minister of the Church of Geneva.

Abraham Musculus, minister of the Church of Bern.

Antoine de La Faye, minister of the Church of Geneva.

Peter Hübner, professor of Greek in the Academy of Bern.

Claude Aubery, doctor and professor of philosophy in the Academy of Lausanne.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

To 1

Is this really to dispute seriously, or rather to play games in so serious a matter? “Christ,” Andreae says, “did not say, ‘This is the Holy Spirit.’” For why and to what purpose would he have said this? But if Andreae were to deny that the Holy Spirit—I mean that third person of the Trinity—is the essential and immanent power of the Father and the Son, proceeding from both, whose action and operation, though not separate, is nevertheless distinct; that he regenerates us, enlightens us, leads us into all truth, moves and consoles us, and finally excites every spiritual movement in the saints and distributes those various gifts to them, I would certainly judge him unworthy of any further disputation. Nor is anything taken away from the person either of the Father or of the Son when it is also attributed to the Holy Spirit, since he is said to be sent both by the Son and by the Father, and Christ did not hesitate to say, “He will receive from what is mine and will teach you.” This is not to establish inequality among the persons, but so that, just as the persons are properly distinguished, so also are their operations in the administration of our salvation. This is taught truly and correctly among the Scholastics, however thornily. Now as regards the power and might of Christ’s flesh, far be it from us to take anything away from it. Yet with due care, those things which belong to divine operation must be distinctly attributed to the deity of the Word, and those which are human in turn to his humanity, so that the person is neither divided with Nestorius, nor the natures themselves or their essential properties confused with Eutyches, nor one single will and operation established with the Monothelites. What Andreae is concealing here will become clearer from the theses on Christ’s person.

To 2

This is a begging of the question now repeated for the sixth time, and cabbage reheated a thousand times.

To 3

What is the point of this? Our thesis is dealing with instrumental causes. With respect to God himself we set down four of them: the pastor speaking and outwardly doing what God commanded; the words of institution plainly and intelligibly explained; the sacred symbols of bread and wine; and finally the sacramental rites. With respect to us, however, we set down a single instrumental cause, namely faith. But if any—

[Part I | PDF page 78 | logical source scan 77 | printed page 73]

intrinsic efficient power is attributed by Andreae to any instruments, he fashions just as many idols. For it is the work of God alone and of the Deity to work salvation in the very hearts of human beings. Thus John attributes the baptism of water, that is, the outward baptism, to himself as minister, but the inward baptism to Christ as Lord—not separately, but distinctly—in Matthew 3:11; and Peter does the same in 1 Peter 3:21. Paul makes the same declaration without exception concerning the whole ministry of the Gospel in 1 Corinthians 3:7. To attribute to the words themselves any power other than that of declaring the mind and will of the speaker, or of him in whose name the pastor speaks, belongs to magicians and sorcerers. And to assign to the sacred symbols—namely, the water of Baptism and the bread and wine employed in the holy Supper by God’s ordinance—or to the sacred rites in their administration any intrinsic power which acts within us is likewise sheer idolatrous madness. No such thing should be attributed even to angels themselves, much less to inanimate things or, as the Sophists say, to the *opus operatum*, the work merely performed. Faith remains, certainly a most effective instrument, but effective only in apprehending and receiving what is given by God to the believer and instilled into the believer’s mind by God’s power alone; it is neither fit nor effective for anything else. Andreae’s objection here, therefore, is irrelevant: that in the use of the sacrament Christ not only testifies to his power by those outward instruments, but also exercises it in believers’ minds. Who among us has ever denied this? But if he imagines some other intrinsic and spiritual sacramental power, let him explain himself more clearly, so that we may either agree with him or, as in most other matters, oppose him if truth so requires.

[Part I | PDF page 79 | logical source scan 78 | printed page 74]

DR. JAKOB ANDREAE’S NARRATIVE INSERTED INTO THE ACTS

After the first article, on the Lord’s Supper, had been concluded, the theologians of Württemberg prepared a separate document—as had been agreed among the colloquy’s participants—in which they showed in what points agreement existed and in what points disagreement still remained. They also showed a way by which a godly concord might be established in this article without offense to consciences and with the truth kept safe and unharmed. When this had been presented to the other party, they too comprised their opinion in a separate document. But since the parties could not come to agreement, each side’s written record was returned to it, and nothing further was attempted to establish concord on this article. For most matters were explained, both in the writings communicated on each side before the conference and in the conference itself, in such a way that everyone can easily see where agreement or disagreement existed. And the path by which a godly concord may be established was shown clearly enough.

Theodore Beza’s Answer to the Foregoing Response Prepared by Andreae

I have placed here this final passage, which Andreae wished to append to the acts of that first disputation, because I do not sufficiently know whether, before this response must necessarily be published at the next fair, I will have enough time to understand and refute the whole disputation as he has compiled it. Yet it is of great importance that everyone understand how peace-loving is the disposition of those who foster these dissensions.

In every properly conducted debate one must first ensure that the subject under inquiry—that is, the state of the question—is settled, and then that there is never any departure from the question during the disputation. Neither of these things could ever be obtained from Andreae, as he passed from one question to another, piled up arguments, and concluded absolutely nothing syllogistically. We therefore thought we would do something worthwhile if, after that former debate had ended, we could obtain that, once the discussion of each individual heading was finished, we would comprise in a short document—one submitted on each side—the matters on which we agreed and those which remained in controversy. For we hoped that in this way provision would be made against those who, as though afraid that there are not already enough dissensions in the churches, persuade themselves and others that we disagree on certain matters on which there is nevertheless com-

[Part I | PDF page 80 | logical source scan 79 | printed page 75]

plete agreement; and that we might gain at least this fruit from the colloquy: once it was established that only certain matters were contested between us—matters about which some may remain ignorant without undermining the foundation of the doctrine of the Lord’s Supper—then, as minds were gradually softened, each side might draw closer to the other. But this too was in vain. We diligently gathered the headings of our disagreement both from the written Antitheses and from the disputation that had taken place, and offered them to the others; they, however, both rejected ours and immediately requested back certain documents of their own which they had offered us. I have therefore decided to append them here, so that every reader may understand with what good faith all things were done by us, and which of the two parties brought an upright and sincere disposition to this colloquy.

Between the Reverend Württemberg participants in the colloquy and the participants sent from Bern and Geneva at the request of the Most Illustrious Prince and Lord, Frederick, Count of Württemberg and Montbéliard, etc.—both sides having declared that they acted as private persons and that what they did should prejudice no one—two matters were discussed in the business concerning the holy Supper of the Lord: namely, the sacramental presence of the signs and the things signified, and the reception of those same things.

As Regards the First Part, Both Sides Agreed

1. The sacrament of the Lord’s Supper consists of two things, namely signs and things signified.

2. By the Lord’s institution, the signs are bread and wine; the things signified are that very body delivered for us and that very blood poured out for us.

3. In the legitimate use of this sacred action, Christ’s benefits are not separated from Christ himself, from whom they proceed.

4. These signs and things signified are joined in no other way than by a sacramental union.

5. As regards God, who covenants with us and is always true, the signs are never bare; rather, these two—signs and things signified—so joined sacramentally are always truly offered to all who approach, whether worthy or unworthy.

6. In this sense—that is, with respect to this sacramental union—the ancient Fathers said that the Lord’s body is in, under, and with the bread.

[Part I | PDF page 81 | logical source scan 80 | printed page 76]

But There Was No Agreement on These Points

1. The Reverend Württemberg participants in the colloquy hold that in the sacramental union both the signs and the things signified are joined on earth in their own substances and in actual fact—though not physically or locally, nevertheless truly, simultaneously, and inseparably—and consequently are offered to the mouth of everyone who approaches, whether worthy or unworthy.

The participants of the other party, however, make the sacramental union merely relational. They teach that the things signified—that is, the Lord’s body and blood—now exist and remain nowhere but in heaven, while their signs are on earth; consequently, the former are offered to the mind, but the latter to the mouth, of everyone who approaches.

2. By the phrases “in,” “with,” and “under the bread and wine,” the Reverend Württemberg participants understand their real sacramental union of substances on earth, by which both are present truly and simultaneously on earth: the signs, indeed, physically and locally; but the Lord’s body and blood neither physically nor locally, but in a certain inscrutable manner. Both are offered to the mouth of everyone who approaches.

The participants of the other party teach that those phrases express no union other than the relational one.

On the Second Heading, Concerning the Sacramental Reception of the Signs and Things Signified, There Was Agreement

1. Just as the signs are offered to everyone who approaches, whether worthy or unworthy, so they are received by the mouth of everyone who takes them—by the worthy for life, but by the unworthy for condemnation.

2. Only the spiritual reception through faith, which belongs to those who approach worthily and by which they receive the things signified, is saving.

3. The mode of that reception of the things signified is inscrutable and, as Paul says, truly a great mystery, to be adored rather than searched out.

But There Was No Agreement on This Point

The Reverend Württemberg participants hold that, just as both things together and inseparably are offered, as stated, to the mouth of everyone who approaches,

[Part I | PDF page 82 | logical source scan 81 | printed page 77]

so they are also received by everyone with the same instrument of the mouth—though, as regards the things signified, not in a physical and local but in an inscrutable manner—by the worthy for life and by the unworthy for condemnation.

The participants of the other party teach that, just as the things signified are offered to the mind alone, so they are spiritually received by the faithful alone, since they alone bring that unique instrument of the mind by which Christ together with his benefits is apprehended. Consequently, the unworthy become guilty of the Lord’s body and blood, not because they have taken them unworthily, but because they have rejected them through their unbelief and impenitence.

[Part I | PDF page 83 | logical source scan 82 | printed page 78]

THESES OF THE WÜRTTEMBERG THEOLOGIANS ON THE PERSON OF CHRIST

I.

It is beyond controversy that the eternal Son of God assumed in time a human nature consisting of soul and body, from the substance, flesh, and blood of the Virgin Mary. In this respect he was made like us, his brothers, in all things except sin. He assumed the human nature into the unity of the person, so that in Christ there are not two persons, but one person only and one Christ only; nor can this person be dissolved for all eternity.

II.

Although the two natures in Christ are joined most closely, they have not been confused, as happens when some third thing is made from two whole things—for example, when a honey drink is made from water and honey. Neither has one nature been changed into the other. And just as the divinity was not converted into the humanity, neither was the humanity changed into the divinity, either before or after Christ’s ascension into heaven. For the two natures are required for the complete person of Christ the Mediator.

III.

It is also established that each nature in Christ has and retains its own properties. For example, the properties of the divine nature are to be omnipotent, eternal, infinite, and so forth. The properties of the human nature are to be a bodily creature, finite and circumscribed, capable of suffering and dying, and so forth. Nor for all eternity do the properties of one nature become the properties of the other nature, for from that would follow the confusion and indeed the abolition of the natures.

IIII.

Besides the properties of the natures in Christ, there are also created gifts in Christ the man, by which he surpasses all human beings and angels. But these do not enter into consideration when the properties of the natures in Christ are being discussed.

[Part I | PDF page 84 | logical source scan 83 | printed page 79]

RESPONSE TO THE THESES ON THE PERSON OF CHRIST PRESENTED BY THE LORDS PARTICIPATING IN THE COLLOQUY, 23 MARCH

Antithesis I and II of Theodore Beza

We approve both.

Antithesis III

We agree, and strongly approve the formula which says: “Properties never leave their subjects.”

Andreae’s Annotation

1. We did not use this phrase, although when rightly understood it is not disapproved; but you abuse it for your verbal communication.

Antithesis IIII

We agree, and here approve the distinction between the grace of union, by which that man is God, and the grace which they call habitual, flowing from the former. By this latter grace that humanity has been adorned with so many and such great gifts and qualities that, having received dominion over all created things, it has been inexpressibly exalted even above the angels themselves.

Andreae’s Annotation

2. The dispute concerns what the grace of union is and what it includes.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

To 1

If Andreae is speaking seriously here, let him see how he can defend both those many statements of Brenz and this statement of his own: that there is this ONE difference between God’s indwelling in the saints and in Christ—namely, that he distributes his measured gifts to the saints, but immeasurable gifts to Christ; and that Christ the man has this in common with all the saints, that God was personally united with him. If Andreae has sincerely renounced the manifest Nestorianism in the division of the person and the evident Eutychianism in the confusion of properties found in this statement, we heartily congratulate him and his associates on the deed. Moreover, if he said that we use the phrase “properties never leave their subjects” to assert our verbal communication, he would speak truly; but he says falsely and slanderously that we abuse it. For we call the communication of properties “verbal” in concrete propositions in which the whole person is named from only one of the two natures and a property belonging to the other nature is attributed to the concrete subject. Thus the God of glory is said to have been crucified, and the Son of Man, speaking on earth with Nicodemus, to have been in heaven. We call this communication verbal not because it consists merely in words or is true only as a form of words. Quite the contrary: if the proper sense of the terms is kept throughout, and “God” in reference to Christ

[Part I | PDF page 85 | logical source scan 84 | printed page 80]

is understood only as God, and “Son of Man” only as man, the proposition would be utterly false and impious. Rather, this is a kind of predication or form of speech by which, on account of the inseparable unity of the person, the whole person is understood even under the concrete names “God” or “man” drawn from only one of the two natures. So too, because of a hypostatic union in which two complete natures nevertheless do not concur, “flesh” and “soul” are sometimes understood synecdochically for the whole human being. This trope, which more recent writers call the “communication of properties” and the ancient Greeks called by various names, must be used only with concrete terms. We defend this by that very truest axiom, “Properties do not leave their subjects,” while those concrete terms designate in predications of this kind not the subjects of the natures, but the person himself.

Moreover, we call that manner of speaking verbal in order to distinguish it from the communication by which not the properties of the divinity themselves—since in this mode of communication they are incommunicable—but created qualities emanating from those properties of the Deity were poured into that assumed humanity and truly and actually inhere in it as their subject. We confess that by gifts of this kind Christ’s humanity was exalted above all created things: that is, that it sits at the Father’s right hand and possesses all power in heaven and on earth, though still below him who subjected all things to Christ. In this respect the ancient Fathers also said that Christ was lesser than himself and greater than himself. This, then, is that verbal communication of properties which we assert, although the ignorant assail it.

But as for that communication which the Ubiquitarians have borrowed from Eutyches, by which they contend that on account of the hypostatic union the realities themselves belong actually not merely to the man “according to another respect”—which is true on account of the indissoluble unity of the person—but to the humanity itself: whether, as at first they absurdly wrote, gifts were poured into Christ as into Peter and the saints, though unequally—that is, into the saints in a measured way, but into Christ immeasurably—or, as they now say, having borrowed from Chemnitz that “wise medicine,” though it is applied no less ineptly, that whatever the Word does, he does with the assumed humanity: that utterly false communication, I say, we certainly do not call verbal, much less real, but detest it as false, impious, and blasphemous. And because I see that from this invented omnimajestatic communication the Ubiquitarians derive that twofold and most grievous error of theirs—namely, consubstantiation, however much they now verbally deny it, and oral eating—I wish to alert the Christian reader to the manifest disagreement between the true Ubiquitarians and the others who nevertheless assert Dr. Luther’s opinion as they understand it. Andreae deliberately concealed this throughout the present colloquy, even though it is of the greatest importance—

[Part I | PDF page 86 | logical source scan 85 | printed page 81]

for the latter indeed hold that the real presence of the essence of Christ’s body in, under, or with the bread, and an oral eating common to both worthy and unworthy people, should properly be derived from the words of institution. We nevertheless disagree with them in their interpretation of those words. The Ubiquitarians, however, derive both doctrines from the hypostatic union—which they even define by the sitting at the Father’s right hand, as though by its consequence—and thereby show clearly enough that they establish no real and essential presence of Christ’s body in the bread other than that which they establish in every other thing. Consequently, they attribute nothing to the words of institution except that oral reception. Indeed, Andreae has openly said and written more than once that unless that omnipresence or omnimajesty is conceded to him as an effect of the hypostatic union, he will never exchange even a word about the words of institution with those whom he calls Zwinglians and Calvinists.

Would that both these men and the others might at last perceive, instead of contending any longer with one another and with us, that neither doctrine can stand unless the falsest principles are assumed. By those principles the definition of the hypostatic union, defended so clearly and lucidly against the Nestorians, Eutychians, Monophysites, and Monothelites, is necessarily overturned; and the reality of Christ’s body, its ascension into heaven, its remaining in heaven, and its return from heaven—all of which are most fully attested by the sacred Word of God in the most proper and in no way figurative terms—are manifestly abolished. Grant this in answer to the prayers of all who love goodness and peace, O Son of God, Jesus Christ, our Savior and Redeemer; and in your singular mercy grant to your Church, now being reborn from such dense darkness, that most longed-for concord against our common enemy, the Roman Antichrist, and all other heresies.

If Andreae wrote the preceding theses truly and sincerely, we agree about the union itself and also about its two effects: namely, the subsistence of the humanity in the Word and not in itself, and those innumerable created gifts poured into the glorified flesh. But that omnimajestatic effect—anti-physical rather than super-physical—attributed to the humanity, whether subjectively or non-subjectively, is precisely the point on which Andreae does not agree even with the sounder Lutherans.

Thesis V of the Württemberg Theologians

In the person of Christ there is a certain communication of properties, by which that which is proper to the other nature is attributed to the whole person and is predicated of one nature. From this arose in the Church the doctrine of the communication of properties.

[Part I | PDF page 87 | logical source scan 86 | printed page 82]

Antithesis V of Theodore Beza

The person of Christ is sometimes signified by words which fit both natures, or the whole Christ, as when he is called Christ, Jesus, Mediator, and by other names of this kind. At other times, on account of the unity of the person, he is named from only one of the two natures, and this happens in two ways. Sometimes he has predicates which fit that very nature from which he is named, as when he is called the blessed, eternal, omnipotent God, and so forth, or likewise when you say that this man suffered, died, was crucified for us, and so forth. Propositions of this kind are proper in the highest degree. At other times he has predicates which indeed truly and properly fit the whole Christ, because there is one indissoluble person, but which nevertheless do not belong to the nature from which he is named when that nature is considered in itself, not even when considered in the union. Thus God is said to have redeemed the Church with his own blood; the Lord of glory to have been crucified; and the Son of Man, speaking on earth with Nicodemus, to have been in heaven. The ancients called this manner of speech *antidosis*, and more recent writers call it the communication of properties. This predication cannot be used except with concrete names, because the whole person of Christ is never named in the abstract from either of his two natures. From ignorance of this distinction between concrete and abstract terms arose the heresy both of Nestorius, who denied that God—namely, the Word—was the son of Mary and had been crucified, and of Eutyches, who asserted that Christ’s deity suffered. That universal rule therefore stands: neither can one nature be predicated of another nature, nor can the properties of one nature be attributed to the other.

Andreae’s Annotations

1. You understand and describe this unity as a mere combination, without a real communication of the proper things; this will be demonstrated to be false.

2. He is not merely named from one nature, but is really communicated to the other nature.

3. Those propositions are not proper but unusual, because the reality—that is, the mystery of the union of the natures—is unusual.

4. “IN ITSELF” is equivocal, at one time denoting the property of the nature, at another signifying a real communication which has occurred.

5. This is not merely grammatical naming, but a real communication.

6. The error of our opponents lies concealed in concrete terms, but abstract terms expose it.

7. Nestorius’s heresy by no means arose from ignorance of these terms, for Nestorius confessed one individual person but denied the communication of properties; from this his heresy arose.

8. He was not judged a heretic for that reason, because Cyril said the same thing, but because he asserted only one nature in Christ after the assumption into heaven.

9. The rule that the properties of one nature cannot be attributed to the other is utterly false, for this was the Nestorian heresy.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

To 1

The word “combination” is thoroughly Nestorian, establishing two persons, as is apparent from the eighth anathema of the Council of Ephesus. The re-

[Part I | PDF page 88 | logical source scan 87 | printed page 83]

al communication, however, is purely Eutychian and overturns the definition of the hypostatic union. For whatever actually becomes common to several natures, however one dreams this may occur, ceases to be proper to one nature; but in the hypostatic union the properties remain distinct, yet undivided and unconfused.

To 2

Let anyone who can divine what Andreae means here—that is, what it means for Christ not merely to be named from one nature, but also to be really communicated to the other nature.

To 3

It is as though nothing could be said properly about an unusual thing, that is, explained by words used in their proper meaning. Let Andreae therefore teach us, in the propositions “the Word is man” and “this man is the Word,” whether either the subject, the predicate, or the copula is taken in a meaning other than its native one. Once the individual propriety of the subject, predicate, copula, and attribution itself is established, it follows that the predication and the proposition’s very sense are indeed unusual, yet nevertheless proper. But what does this have to do with the matter? For we were not dealing here with propositions of that kind, but with these: “the Word is eternal, omnipotent,” and so forth; and “that man suffered, was crucified, and died for us.” What, at last, could anyone find in these that is said either figuratively or unusually?

To 4

Indeed, there is no equivocation here. For it is clear that “in itself” can signify nothing here but “with respect to itself,” whose opposite is “according to another respect,” not “according to another person.” Nor can it be taken for “through itself,” a formula which sometimes means the same as “separately” or “distinctly,” and at other times means “from itself”—that is, by its native and intrinsic power and not by a power coming to it from elsewhere. It is not we, but the Ubiquitarians, who very frequently abuse the phrase in this way. Then to what purpose does Andreae answer this point about a nature? For in those propositions made by *antidosis*, neither the word “God” nor the word “man” is said of the nature, but of the person considered as a whole and insofar as it is one. If Andreae is ignorant of this, or pretends not to know it, are we heretics on that account?

To 5

Again, how inapt this is! For the issue here is not the naming of the predicate, but of the subject. Since the subject “supposits for” the whole person, as they say in the schools, far be it from me to say that this naming is anything other than real and most fitting in actual fact, since it arises from the real and most true personal union of the natures.

To 6

Indeed, the whole error of Andreae and his associates flows from their failure to distinguish between abstract and concrete terms—such as “deity” and “God,” “humanity” and “man,” in the one supposit of Christ. They therefore wish whatever is attributed to this man to be attributed also to the humanity, in a thoroughly Eutychian error contrary to the definition of the hypostatic union. It must therefore be understood that the abstract term “humanity” signifies the human nature as it is in itself, distinguished from the other natures by its essential properties

[Part I | PDF page 89 | logical source scan 88 | printed page 84]

and furnished with qualities inhering in it as their subject. An abstract term is an intelligible something abstracted by the mind and thought. Sometimes it is abstracted from all individuals of the same species—which the logicians call a species—without any account of individuation or of any individual, and is therefore one and the same, as humanity considered universally. At other times it is abstracted not collectively from all individuals but from one individual alone, having no account of individuation or subsistence, yet having some reference to the individual in which it actually exists and from which it is abstracted in thought, as the humanity of Christ, the humanity of Peter, or, as they say in the schools, this or that humanity. In this sense there are as many humanities as there are human beings.

By concrete terms, however, there is always signified—or, as the Scholastics say more precisely, connoted—something beyond the nature which possesses that very nature. It may be considered generally, as when we say “man,” that is, an animal endowed with humanity, in which sense “man” is likewise the name of a species and not of an individual. Or it may be viewed as subsisting in some individual, as “this man” or “that man,” “Christ the man,” “Peter the man,” and so forth. These points apply to created things, such as Christ’s humanity—I mean created substantial terms, not denominative ones. For we are not dealing here with accidental terms, such as “white,” or with substantial denominative terms, such as “animate.”

In the divine persons, however, because of supreme being and simplicity, essence and existing—that is, deity and God—are one and the same. Neither of these terms is posited only in the mind, as we said of humanity and man, but truly and properly IS. Yet this does not prevent the two from being distinguished in thought, even when the name “God” is taken essentially: “deity” as that which is possessed, and “God” as that which possesses deity. Although what is possessed and the one who possesses it are actually one and the same, relation alone, without composition, has a certain character of a principle of individuation in the divine persons, as Thomas writes correctly and precisely.

The surest proof of this distinction is that it would be blasphemous to say that deity begot deity, because deity would thereby be multiplied; but all Christians confess God begotten from God, by that generation which so distinguishes the persons that their consubstantial unity is not divided. It must also be noted that “deity” is always taken as one and indeed essentially, against the Tritheists, although through the addition of proper notions it is regarded distinctly in each person, against Sabellius. But the name “God” is taken in three ways: essentially, as when we say that we believe in one God; personally, both for the Father, for the Son, and for the Holy Spirit, examples of which usage occur everywhere; and finally, in a third way, for the person of Christ, God and man,

[Part I | PDF page 90 | logical source scan 89 | printed page 85]

or for the whole Christ, named from only one of the two natures, the divine, through the phrase which we call *antidosis* or the communication of properties, as when the blood by which he redeemed us is attributed to the God of glory. This, then, is the distinction between abstract and concrete terms. Although Andreae seemed to sketch it in the disputation on page 224, over the next three pages he in fact pretends not to know it. For he says that “MAN,” if it is posited without a definite designation—that is, unless it is restricted to a definite individual—is NOT a concrete but an abstract term, like the name “humanity,” because, he says, they belong to the same predicable, namely species. He says that it becomes a concrete name only when we say of someone, “this man.” We will discuss this again more openly below at the ninth thesis.

But if that were true, it would necessarily follow that the designation “this humanity” is just as concrete as “this man.” Let Andreae therefore say that this proposition, “this humanity is deity,” is just as true as this one, “this man is God.” Since the former is utterly false but the latter most true, I implore him in the name of Jesus Christ at last to understand that he is confusing abstract terms with concrete ones and how great an error arises from this, to undo his own work, and restore peace to the churches.

For, to settle this entire controversy in one sentence: Andreae himself admits that the omnipotence, omnipresence, and all the other things which he imagines and wishes actually to be attributed to this humanity depend upon the union of the natures. In the same way that this proposition, “Christ’s humanity became deity through the hypostatic union,” is false, so also every alleged communication of the properties of deity attributed to that assumed humanity is necessarily just as false, impious, and blasphemous. Nor can anything be said less truly or aptly than by that refuge of Chemnitz: namely, that those things are attributed to the humanity non-subjectively. For Chemnitz saw that otherwise he would be compelled to establish two substantial omnipotences, or one substantial and the other accidental, or to say that a substance inheres in a substance. But what does he accomplish by that refuge? Certainly nothing except to erect a false building upon a false foundation.

For from the true definition of abstract terms it is clear that nothing can be said truly of the humanity except either what it is essentially—as when you say that Christ’s humanity is a bodily, animate, rational substance—or what inheres in it accidentally and subjectively. Of this latter kind are both the things which, for our sake, it endured during the time of its infirmity, and those ineffable gifts with which Christ’s humanity is now adorned above all created things, especially after laying aside all that infirmity of the assumed flesh. But the simple reason why this falsehood is not found in concrete terms is not that “this man” is a concrete term while “this humanity” is abstract. For the properties of the divine nature can no more be attributed to this man in himself or subjectively

[Part I | PDF page 91 | logical source scan 90 | printed page 86]

than to this humanity. Rather, the reason is that neither is “this man” in Christ a separate hypostasis, nor does “this humanity” exist by itself—that is, according to itself or with respect to itself—but this man, endowed with this human nature, subsists in the person of the Son of God, with the properties both of the assuming person of the Son and of that assumed humanity preserved. Hence, although this humanity did not become deity, because the properties remain preserved in the union, nevertheless on account of the unity of the person this man is the true Son of God—not insofar as he is man or with respect to his own nature, but because he subsists in the Son of God. Hence also the whole person of Christ is often named in concrete terms both by “God,” namely “of the Word,” and by “that man”; this is the foundation of that *antidosis*. For as the union is as cause, so are the phrases which flow from that union. I wished to explain all these matters more fully here because, once they are rightly understood, the entire resolution of this controversy—that is, the overthrow of all Nestorianism and Eutychianism—depends upon them.

To 7

Thus, if Andreae is to be believed, it will be not he but I who am the Nestorian—an insult from him which I also endured patiently during the disputation. The matter, however, stands as follows. Nestorius, a cunning man endowed also with natural eloquence, neither abolished the subsistence of the Son of God with Paul of Samosata, nor the distinction of the persons with Sabellius and Photinus; nor did he deny the reality of a complete human nature, or indeed even deny that two natures concur in Christ. He even called the union “personal,” though, as Damascene testifies in book 3, chapter 3, it was badly explained. Consequently, both before he was condemned and after his condemnation in the orthodox Council of Ephesus, where he had Cyril of Alexandria as his keenest opponent, he seemed to some to agree with the orthodox faith. But it was discovered partly from his many writings and partly from his own confession—when he denied that Mary was the God-bearer and that God had been crucified by the Jews—that just as he established two natures, so also he established two persons. One of these, namely the Word, was indeed inseparably present to the other, but as one to another, according to dignity or authority and power. Thus he wished some terms which fit the man alone to be attributed to him, and others which fit the Word alone, who is from God the Father, to be attributed to the Word. He taught that the man was worshiped, glorified, and praised together with God the Word, and was even called God, but as one joined with another. That this, I say, was Nestorius’s heresy is clear both from the acts of the Second Council of Ephesus and of Chalcedon; from Cyril; from Socrates’s history; from Theodoret on the divine names; from Vigilius, Nicephorus, Cassian, and Theodore of Rhaithu; and finally from every approved Greek and Latin writer. But what does Andreae say? “Nestorius,” he says, “confessed one individual person—”

[Part I | PDF page 92 | logical source scan 91 | printed page 87]

But to which testimony shall we give credence? The passage he quotes from Nestorius’s second letter to Cyril does indeed show that Nestorius believed in two natures and misused the name of Christ. But he never confessed the hypostatic union, not even verbally, but only a conjunction. Let us demonstrate, without going any farther, from Andreae’s own express words—words in which he contradicts himself—that this error also arose because Nestorius did not distinguish the concrete terms “God the Word” and “man” from the abstract terms “divinity” and “humanity,” something Andreae denies here. On page 239 of this colloquy he says: “Under the term ‘God,’ Nestorius understood the divinity of the Word, which cannot be separated from the person of the Word. Consequently, although he openly and frankly confessed that Jesus Christ is true God, he nevertheless did not wish to say that God suffered or was crucified. For he said that these were properties of the humanity and therefore must be referred to his humanity alone.”

From these words I would first have the Christian reader observe how knowledgeably Andreae speaks about these matters, and whether the account Socrates gives of Nestorius himself and his presbyter Anastasius in book 7, chapter 34 of the *Ecclesiastical History*, repeated by Nicephorus in book 14, chapters 32 and 33, may not truly be applied here: namely, that Nestorius had neglected the writings of the ancients and did not sufficiently understand even what he himself was saying and thinking. For what is Andreae’s statement that the divinity of the Word could not be separated from the person of the Word? Why does he not say instead, “which is the Word himself”? Then why does he not say “from the person of Christ” rather than “from the person of the Word”? Who ever suspected Nestorius of thinking that the person of the Word was something composite? Perhaps not even Arius would have said that, although he taught that the Word was created. Indeed, the question is whether Nestorius thought the Word truly became flesh, or rather established as many persons as there are natures in Christ.

Nor does Andreae’s claim that Nestorius did not separate the Word from Christ’s flesh suffice to excuse him. For this very point deceived some orthodox men until Cyril opened their eyes and showed that Christ’s person is also dissolved and divided by one who confesses that the Word is inseparably conjoined with the assumed flesh, unless instead of “conjoined” he says “united”—and united to constitute one subsistent entity through the flesh’s subsistence in the person of the Word, the second person of the Trinity—so that Christ is no longer a “God-bearer,” as Nestorius said, but truly “God-man,” without division and likewise without confusion. Andreae therefore errs greatly when he argues: “Nestorius did not separate the natures; therefore he acknowledged the unity of the person.” In sum, Andreae’s own words make clear that Nestorius understood “divinity” by the name “God,” and therefore confused the concrete and the abstract by a most serious error. Consequently, he believed that these propositions were equally false and blasphemous: “God—that is, the Word—is that man,” and “that man is God,” namely according to the hypostasis—

[Part I | PDF page 93 | logical source scan 92 | printed page 88]

as these: “the divinity of the Word is that humanity,” and “that humanity is the divinity of the Word,” since, as Andreae himself testifies, Nestorius regarded abstract and concrete terms as the same. Yet the former propositions are true and orthodox on account of the unity of the person, just as the latter are false and blasphemous on account of the confusion of the natures. Thus Nestorius and Eutyches, setting sail as it were from the same port—from the same error, namely ignorance of the distinction between concrete and abstract terms—struck two opposite rocks. Nestorius fell into the error of as many persons as there are natures, though inseparably conjoined; Eutyches, by contrast, into the unity of the natures as well as of the person. And just as Nestorius imagined a conjunction of the natures and of the essential properties of each, so he conceived in his mind a corresponding communication of accidental properties on each side. Thus he considered it just as blasphemous to say that God was two or three months old, was conceived in the Virgin Mary, born, suffered, died, was buried, and was raised from the dead, as if someone attributed these same things to the deity. Nor can there be any doubt that he regarded it as equal and identical blasphemy to teach that this man is eternal, uncreated, omnipotent, and omnipresent, as if these same things were said of the humanity. For he did not distinguish God from divinity or man from humanity. Yet it must be confessed that the former things are said truly of both God and that man—unless we wish to dissolve Christ’s person—whereas it would be horrific, by confusing both the natures and their properties, to apply to the humanity those things proper to deity, that is, to transform the humanity into deity. For the union of the properties is altogether the same as the union of the natures themselves, and on both sides: namely, personal.

Having set these points forth and proved them both from God’s Word, which uses these phrases in no other way, and from the authority of the ancient Fathers, it remains for us to see by what right Andreae reproves us as Nestorians, and by what right we in turn accuse him both as a Nestorian in the dissolution of the person and as a Eutychian in the confusion of the essential properties of the divinity. He says: “Whoever separates the properties of the divine nature from Christ’s humanity also separates the deity from it. But Beza separates those properties when he denies that, by the power of the hypostatic union, Christ’s flesh has been made omnipresent, omnipotent, and omniscient—though not subjectively or in itself, but in the Word, and not from itself, that is, by its own nature or power, but by that of the Word to which it is personally and inseparably united. Therefore Beza is a Nestorian, indeed worse than Nestorius. For Nestorius opposed only the communication of the properties of the humanity; Beza, however, also denies the communication of the properties of the divinity.”

What do I hear? Then Andreae too is a semi-Nestorian, since with Nestorius he denies the communication of the properties of the humanity—indeed, in this respect he is more ignorant than Nestorius. For Nestorius was not igno-

[Part I | PDF page 94 | logical source scan 93 | printed page 89]

rant that, although the deity received nothing from the humanity, and indeed in assuming it in the form of a servant emptied itself in a certain manner, as the apostle says, nevertheless the conjunction of the deity with the humanity and of the humanity with the deity is reciprocal and altogether one and the same. From this follows by necessary and unavoidable conclusion—something I am compelled to repeat a thousand times in these replies—that the conjunction of the essential properties of the natures is likewise one and the same on both sides, although Nestorius was rebuked only concerning the one side on which he had blasphemed. For who does not know that like cases must be judged alike? And who does not see that the conjunctions of deity with humanity and humanity with deity are entirely reciprocal—indeed, that by the same definition each is altogether the one and the same conjunction?

But to the point. I distinguish the proposition in that argument. If by the word “separate” Andreae means dissolution of the union, by which the natures, although said—as Nestorius replied—to be inseparably conjoined, nevertheless each constitute their own subsisting entity, then Beza concedes that proposition to be most true and indeed concedes the entire syllogism; but in this sense he denies the assumption as utterly false. If, however, Andreae takes “separates” to mean “distinguishes,” both in essential properties and also in operations and effects—as the ancients sometimes used the word “division,” though it is a harsh phrase, against Sabellius—then Beza certainly acknowledges the assumption but denies the conclusion, and the syllogism will be composed of four terms. Beza therefore is not a Nestorian but orthodox, confessing one and the same union—namely, the hypostatic union—both of the two natures and of the essential properties by which one nature is distinguished from the other, without any separation or confusion.

But let us consider the simultaneous Nestorianism and Eutychianism of the Brenzians, foremost among whom is Andreae. Thus Brenz writes on page 40 of his *Recognition*: “By these words, ‘the Word became flesh,’ nothing else is signified than that the Son of God so fills the Son of Man”—note, Christian reader, two sons—“that he communicates to him all wisdom, power, and knowledge.” See how he passes over subsistence. And why is the word “filling” restricted to those properties? Likewise, on the same page: “By the Evangelist’s own saying, ‘the Word became flesh’—which is said of Christ, not of Peter—the distinction between Christ and Peter must be established in this: that although the Son of God fills Peter by his essence just as he fills Christ the man”—therefore not by personal union, or according to hypostasis, but only by an operative presence, which is sheer and most manifest Nestorianism—“he nevertheless does not communicate all his properties to Peter, but only some.” Christ, then, will be most divine but not God, while Peter is merely divine to some degree. Indeed, the Word did not pour all his properties even into Christ the man, for Brenz is compelled to except—

[Part I | PDF page 95 | logical source scan 94 | printed page 90]

eternity and self-existence, although these belong most properly to the deity. Andreae, moreover, deprives that flesh even of supreme simplicity, uncircumscription, and every kind of infinity with respect to its presence in heaven.
