In this matter, certainly, he is wiser than his master, who contends that Christ's flesh truly and actually was at one and the same moment in the Virgin's womb, in Bethlehem, in the manger, in the tomb, and in heaven. Indeed, on page 143 of the same book he calls what Theodoret and others say a childish conception of heavenly things—that is, what the orthodox church has defended by perpetual consent: that in the next age Christ's human body possesses the same circumscription which it possessed in this age. It is therefore no longer surprising that Christ's flesh should also be *in*, *under*, and *with* the bread.

Likewise, on page 63—hear, Christian reader, the very words of Nestorius, expressly struck down by Cyril's third anathema—he says: “We equate Christ's humanity with the divinity, not in essence but in authority, not in being but in power, not in nature but in glory, not in substance but in majesty.” Essence alone, therefore, severed from its essential properties, is left as proper to the deity; that man will not truly be Jehovah and that blessed God, and those other properties will be doubled—unless perhaps Brenz imagines equality without multiplication. But if they are doubled, the two natures conjoined together will both be omnipotent, omniscient, and omnipresent: the deity, certainly, essentially—as I suppose Brenz believed. But is Christ's humanity so essentially as well? Then multiple essences will have existed in Christ's humanity, and the Lord did not cease creating new essences—indeed, new gods—on the seventh day. If, however, accidentally, then the divinity of Christ the man will have been merely accidental. And what will not follow?

But let us now hear Andreae himself, dividing the person and confusing the essential properties still more openly, and finally attacking himself in express words. Thus, in the *Disputation on the Majesty of Christ the Man*, thesis 20, he says: “No presence of God in Christ”—an ambiguous and dangerous phrase in the writings of Nestorius—“other than in all the rest of the creatures is to be imagined or devised. Just as this operative power is distinguished in creatures because it acts and works differently in different ones, so it is poured whole into Christ.” Who, then, is so foolish that he does not see that Andreae establishes no other conjunction of the Word with that assumed humanity than that universal operative conjunction, differing only in inequality, and truly Nestorian—the one expressed in that familiar little verse: “Essentially and presently God is, and everywhere powerfully”? To make the matter still clearer, behold the words of Andreae himself in his *Apology to the Ingolstadt Theses*, page 19: “All things are sustained subsistentially by God.” What therefore prevents all things from being just as many gods as that man is God merely because he subsists in the Word and not in himself, as the whole Christian church

[Part I | PDF page 96 | logical source scan 95 | printed page 91]

confesses? And on page 38: “Christ the man has this in common with all the saints, that God was personally united with him.” But what sort of monster is this, simultaneously to build Nestorius up and tear him down? For Nestorius conjoined God the Word with that man in no other fashion—although with unequal efficacy and inseparably—namely by attendance, however eminent, together with authority and power. Yet he never confessed that the Word was personally united even with Christ’s flesh. Why, then, do we vindicate the hypostatic union for Christ alone? And why would Andreae have paraded those unusual predications if Christ has a personal union in common with the saints—indeed, monstrous to say, with all things? For to be united subsistentially and personally is the same thing. And how will that mode of presence and oral eating be ineffable, if it depends upon a union which Christ shares with the saints and God with all things? And what persons, finally, shall we imagine in all things? Likewise, repeating his master’s words in thesis 26, he says: “We equate Christ’s humanity with his divinity, not in essence but in authority, not in being but in power, not in nature but in glory, not in substance but in majesty.” But the orthodox church judges it the height of impiety to equate any created thing with divinity, even across the longest and ineffable interval. Therefore, against Eutyches, it confesses Christ to be less than himself according to the flesh and greater than himself according to the deity of the Word, confusing neither the natures nor their essential properties. Yet against Nestorius, on account of the hypostatic union and the unity of the person, it confesses this man to be the true and only Son of God the Father. What difference, then, is there between Nestorius and Eutyches and the followers of Brenz? Certainly not the slightest. For in express and explicit words the Brenzians equally establish in actual fact as many persons as there are natures, asserting: “Between God’s indwelling in the saints and in Christ”—Andreae repeats Brenz’s words in his *Apology*, page 37—“there is this ONE difference: that he distributes his gifts to the saints in measure, but to Christ without measure.” Yet with Eutyches these same men also confuse the essential properties of divinity—or rather, they forbid properties either to be or to be possessed, since what is communicated ceases to be proper—as Andreae writes in thesis 22 of that disputation of his: “What belongs to the divine nature essentially is communicated to the human nature accidentally.” All these men, however, share this error: they confuse abstract with concrete terms by an intolerable mistake. Nestorius was certainly more prudent in this respect, because he saw that unless the hypostatic union were changed into a *parastatic* conjunction, his blasphemy would be deprived of every foundation; therefore, in order to establish the latter, he denied the former. Eutyches likewise, when he perceived that his confusion could not stand unless the two natures were fused into one or one were absorbed by

[Part I | PDF page 97 | logical source scan 96 | printed page 92]

the other, affirmed both errors. But the Brenzians unite what Nestorius dissolved—although in this colloquy Andreae condemns a bare combination, which he nevertheless posits in those theses of his—and distinguish what Eutyches confused, inventing the petty distinction “non-subjectively.” Thus they seem neither to have understood what those men thought nor themselves to know sufficiently what they say and write. Come, then: let us prove from Andreae’s own words, which wholly contradict one another, that I rightly attribute ignorance of the Nestorian dogma to Andreae and his followers. He says on page 234 of this colloquy: “Nestorius maintained that a mere”—that is, *psilos*, naked—“man was born of Mary, suffered, and was crucified, which is diametrically opposed to the articles of faith. For all the articles of the Apostolic Faith speak expressly about the Son of God in the person of Christ.” Andreae certainly speaks truly here, and I congratulate him on this recantation, if it is sincere, since these words oppose as truth opposes falsehood those statements we cited above from his disputation and *Apology* concerning the one distinction between Peter and Christ and God’s personal union with all the saints. But did he truly attribute these things to Nestorius? Come, let us learn from Andreae himself, who says on page 238: “Nestorius was not so ignorant that he did not understand that God the Word was born of the Virgin not without divinity.” And on page 236: “Nestorius did not deny that Christ, who was born of Mary, suffered, and was crucified, is true God and therefore the only-begotten Son of God.” Is this, indeed, to say that a naked man was born of Mary? The matter stands as follows. Nestorius did not say that Christ was a naked man in the sense in which his ancestor Paul of Samosata did—Socrates also clears him of that slander in his *Ecclesiastical History—nor did he deny that God the Word was conjoined with that flesh. But he always denied that the Word was united with that flesh, that is, that the man subsisted in the person of the Son of God. Therefore by his conjunction he established not simply one thing or another in that one and the same subsisting entity, which is orthodox, but one or another person. Thus, just as he denied the union of the natures themselves, so he denied the union of the properties both in concrete and in abstract terms: the former impiously, but the latter truly.

To 8

We have spoken most fully about the Nestorian and Eutychian error in the preceding refutation. I shall believe that Cyril said Christ’s deity suffered when Andreae produces the passage. For the saying “the Word suffered impassibly,” which he cites on page 238 of this colloquy, is spoken concretely, not abstractly. What he cites on the same page from Vigilius—that “the divinity was fastened with nails”—is harsher. Yet Vigilius adds that “it could not be pierced by the nails,” and soon substitutes “God the Word” in place of that abstract term. He thereby sufficiently shows that nothing was further from his intention than to confuse the terms “God” and “divinity” in this treatment. Still, I do not deny that the ancients, especially before the Nestorian and Eutychian controversies were stirred up, did not always observe this distinction

[Part I | PDF page 98 | logical source scan 97 | printed page 93]

precisely; nevertheless, they carefully showed what they meant.

To 9

Indeed, although the natures are personally united, they nevertheless remain distinct in their properties. Abstract terms, however, do not designate the person, but—as Chemnitz correctly teaches in his earlier writing *On the Two Natures in Christ*, page 185—the natures themselves and their properties, whether considered in themselves, outside the union, or in the union itself. Therefore to posit any communication which is some effect of the union and something other than either the union itself or what they call habitual grace is pure and intolerable Eutychianism.

Thesis VI of the Württemberg Theologians

When it is taught that the Son of God communicates his properties to the assumed human nature—for example, omnipotence and omnipresence—this must not be understood as though the Son of God had poured his properties into the assumed human nature, as something is poured from one vessel into another, so that the human nature, by itself and from itself—even considered in the abstract, or outside the personal union—possessed a peculiar omnipotence which was now not merely proper to it but its own property. For in this way there would be two divinities established in Christ, and consequently in God there would be not a Trinity but a quaternity. It must therefore be understood thus: if the personal union could be dissolved and Christ’s human nature could nevertheless subsist as other instances of human nature do, neither omnipotence nor omnipresence could rightly be attributed to it.

Thesis VII of the Württemberg Theologians

Nor must it be maintained that the human nature in Christ was made an infinite and uncircumscribed essence, or extended into every place in heaven and earth, or that it is everywhere in the same manner as the divinity is everywhere, or that it was equated with the divine nature. For none of these things follows from the true and real communication of properties.

Antitheses VI and VII of Theodore Beza

But as we said in the immediately preceding reply, we teach from the definition of the hypostatic union that neither the deity nor the properties of deity can in any way be predicated truly of the humanity, not even in the union.

Andreae’s Annotation

1. This heresy is far worse than the Nestorian. For Nestorius opposed only the communication of the properties of the humanity, but these men also deny the communication of the properties of the divinity.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Annotation

A twofold dream occurs here. The first is Andreae’s utterly false imagining that Nestorius acknowledged the personal union of the Word

[Part I | PDF page 99 | logical source scan 98 | printed page 94]

and the assumed flesh. If this is true, then Nestorians and the orthodox must certainly have had the highest agreement in confessing the personal union. Let both those most celebrated universal councils, the Second Council of Ephesus and the Council of Chalcedon, therefore be erased—councils approved not only by the perpetual consensus of all the ancient Christian churches but also by the express testimony of the Augsburg Confession. Let so many most learned writings of the Greek and Latin Fathers against the Nestorian heresy be erased. Let controversies condemned by the labors of so many godly men and scarcely at last abolished, which almost brought the entire Christian world to ruin, be summoned back from the dead. No: let the truth rather remain safe, and let the disturbers of the churches come to their senses. They have recalled Nestorius from the dead and placed the distinction between God’s indwelling—and therefore his personal union—in Christ and in Peter and all the saints in nothing but the inequality of the gifts poured into the flesh. They thereby show more than sufficiently by what spirit they are led.

The second dream consists in Andreae’s thinking that Nestorius opposed only the communication of the properties of the humanity, while we are worse than the Nestorians in this matter because we also deny the communication of the properties of divinity. But again, if this is true, how little distance will separate Andreae from Nestorius? First, as regards the union, he openly professes agreement with Nestorius. In denying that the properties of the humanity are communicated to the deity of the Word, however, he differs from Nestorius by scarcely a finger’s breadth, confessing—correctly, indeed—that “no perfection or imperfection can accrue to the divine nature,” on page 239 of this colloquy, and consequently that “the divinity cannot receive sufferings in itself”—and therefore neither conception, birth, death, burial, nor resurrection—“but is said to have suffered with respect to its flesh,” which it permitted to suffer even though it could prevent its sufferings. For Nestorius would never have denied even in this sense that the divinity suffered, not in itself, since he did not unite it with the flesh but joined it merely as an inseparable companion, but that it in some manner suffered together with the flesh, as he was accustomed to misuse that particle “with.” Therefore, although Andreae is not a Nestorian here either, in reality he is certainly not a hundred parasangs distant from him. There remains the communication of the properties of divinity poured into the flesh. If Andreae writes truly that Nestorius did not deny this, not even the slightest distinction between Andreae and Nestorius will be found; indeed, they will also have the highest agreement in this, that each alike abolished the distinction between concrete and abstract. The matter stands as follows. Since, according to Andreae on page 239, the union of the natures in the person of the Son of God is the cause of that communication of properties which he establishes between the natures themselves, while we have proved that Nestorius denied the union, it follows that no less the com-

[Part I | PDF page 100 | logical source scan 99 | printed page 95]

munication of the properties of divinity to humanity than of humanity to divinity was abolished by Nestorius, whatever Andreae may say. For who can doubt that Nestorius would have been no less offended by this proposition, “That man is from eternity, uncreated, infinite, omnipresent, and omnipotent”—for these properties of divinity are inseparable and no one of them can in any way be communicated without another—than by this: “Mary is the God-bearer; God was conceived in the Virgin’s womb, born from her, suffered, and was crucified”? And since, according to Andreae on page 236, Nestorius understood by the name “God” the divinity of the Word, and consequently in turn by the name of that man understood that humanity, and joined divinity to humanity no otherwise than humanity to divinity, he certainly would have recoiled no less from the abstract propositions I just mentioned, formulated with the words “divinity” and “humanity.” But we, whom the most Nestorian Andreae calls Nestorians, abominate with the orthodox church the blasphemy of Nestorius dividing Christ’s person. From the true definition of the personal union we detest the Eutychian communication—that is, confusion—of properties in abstract terms, such as Andreae defends. But against Nestorius we defend the true communication of properties in concrete terms, both of God, that is, the Word, and of the man, from both the hypostatic union itself and the unity of the person. Let the church now judge whether we have anything in common with Nestorius, much less whether we are worse than he.

Thesis VIII of the Württemberg Theologians

When we speak of a real communication of properties, we do not understand a physical communication by which one nature passes into the other. Rather, we oppose the word “real” to a verbal communication, which makes only the names of the properties common to the natures.

Thesis IX of the Württemberg Theologians

The question, therefore, is whether, on account of the personal union between the divine and human nature in Christ, there occurs a real communication—that is, one that is true and actually exists in the person of Christ—so that one nature communicates its properties to the other. It is asked further how far that real communication extends.

Antitheses VIII and IX of Theodore Beza

There is homonymy or equivocation in the word “communication,” which is taken sometimes for the hypostatic union and sometimes for its effects. Therefore we admit a real communication, that is, a union of the natures, in which each nature remains of itself, even in the union, distinct by its own properties, without any other

Andreae’s Annotations

1. There is no equivocation here, but the native signification of the word “communication,” which is attributed, on account of the union, both to the natures and to their properties.

2. To assert a union of the natures without communication is the Samosatenian heresy.

[Part I | PDF page 101 | logical source scan 100 | printed page 96]

3. This assertion—that Christ’s humanity is not omnipotent even verbally—is blasphemous, contrary to Christ’s saying: “All authority has been given to me” (Matthew 28).

4. It does not follow, because the deity is called omnipotent in one way, namely of itself; and the humanity in Christ in another way, namely by participation in omnipotence.

communication. But the communication mentioned here, by which the humanity is said to be everywhere and omnipotent in some fashion, we say is not even verbal; instead, we consider it as false as the claim that through the hypostatic union the humanity became deity. Nevertheless, as we said above, all things proper to God are rightly and truly attributed to the man united with the Word—that is, to the whole person concretely named from the other nature, namely the humanity—or to Christ as a whole, not to him considered in part or distinctly in the natures.

5. Tricks of words by which the eyes of the unlearned are dazzled. For all these things are verbal, not real, and this is to give words in place of reality.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

To 1

It cannot be denied without extreme shamelessness that the word *koinōnia* is applied to every kind of communication and even fellowship, of which there are many very different species. Accordingly, varied and manifold homonymy can occur in this word even if “to communicate” is always taken in its native signification of “to make common.” Andreae therefore should not have denied equivocation in this word, but rather should have shown what distinction exists between the union of the natures itself—which is itself a species of communication, and indeed its closest species—and that communication of properties devised by the Eutychians and refurbished by the Ubiquitarians, for which they have not yet been able to find any special name. But here we repeat what we said in our antithesis: when the name *koinōnia* designates this mystery of the hypostatic union of the natures, it declares nothing other than that very union. Thus Athanasius too in his letter to Epictetus uses “communication of the union of the natures” in precisely the same sense; Hilary uses “communion,” and Augustine “fellowship.” I do not think Andreae will deny this. As for the properties, there is no small distinction between them. With regard to divinity, all the things we call its properties are its very essence, whether it is considered absolutely or in the person of the Son, whose proper identifying property is filiation. In the humanity, however, some properties are essential, though its being and essence differ in reality—that is, properties by which human nature itself is universally distinguished from other things and without which humanity would cease to be. There are also certain accidental properties peculiar to this humanity of Christ, by which it is distinguished from the humanity of other human beings: for example, that it was granted to subsist in the person of the Son of God and to be conceived by the Holy Spirit without sin,

[Part I | PDF page 102 | logical source scan 101 | printed page 97]

and whatever else is of this kind. But leaving aside all these distinctions, I say that there can be no other communication of all those properties, whether of deity or humanity, than that very union by which the natures themselves are united. For they can never actually be separated from their natures, since if you take them away from the natures themselves, the natures must also be taken away—a point that applies especially to the deity, in which being differs in no respect from essence. Indeed, if you remove even those things which we said were accidentally given to that humanity, it will cease to be that humanity. How, then, could we say that the natures themselves are united without their properties likewise being conjoined at the same moment and by altogether one and the same union? It is therefore utterly vain to imagine another union in the properties of the natures besides that in the natures themselves. But if the communication or union of these natures and their properties is hypostatic, as it certainly is, then just as this hypostatic union of two natures does not make one become the other, neither in turn does it make the essential properties of one nature become those of the other, nor allow what belongs to one nature to be truly predicated of the other. For then what is proper would cease to be proper and would become common with the other nature, and the definition of the hypostatic union would be abolished. In that union the two natures are said against Nestorius to constitute one subsisting entity—the human nature having been assumed by the person of the Word according to the hypostasis—yet against Eutyches the natures themselves, though personally united and inseparable, remain distinct by their properties. Therefore either that whole communication of properties which Andreae makes different from the union of the natures themselves, and by which he wishes the omnipresence, omnipotence, and all the other things of the deity to be attributed to Christ’s humanity, must fall; or, God forbid, the definition of the hypostatic union must necessarily be abolished. This falsehood is chiefly and irrefutably exposed in the communication by which the properties of divinity are transferred to humanity, for these are the only properties those men are now accustomed to press. What more? Brenz and Andreae explicitly write that this communication of properties, by which they define the hypostatic union from its effect, is the same as God’s indwelling in all the saints. Yet it is agreed that this indwelling of God in the saints is wholly operative and productive of created qualities subjectively inhering in the saints themselves. Is not the essential union of the natures thereby abolished, and consequently the entire foundation of that imagined transfer of divine properties overthrown by these very men? Does not that Chemnitzian refuge also vanish, according to which the properties of deity are attributed actually but not subjectively to that humanity? Finally, since, as we have proved, the union of the natures and of the properties is one and the same, and since the assuming person is united with the assumed nature in no other way—although with a different effect—than the assumed nature is united with the assuming person,

[Part I | PDF page 103 | logical source scan 102 | printed page 98]

does it not necessarily follow—unless someone wishes through sheer obstinacy to resist the truth—that if the properties of divinity are truly attributed to the humanity, the properties of the humanity must also truly be applied to the divinity; and if one is blasphemous, the other is blasphemous too, as each certainly is? Nestorius therefore correctly perceived that his blasphemy could not stand unless the hypostatic union were abolished; and Eutyches, conversely, that his impiety could not be admitted unless the human nature were absorbed or the natures fused and confused into one. What, then? From one and the same union arises not a unity of the natures, but of the person. Since abstract terms designate only the natures as they are in themselves and as they remain even when united, no place is allowed for abstract terms here. But the person is signified only by concrete terms. Some of these fit this person according to both natures, as when he is called the Son of God: both because he is such according to the deity and because this man also subsists in the person of the Son; and when he is called Christ, because by that name both the anointing deity and the anointed humanity are understood. Other concrete appellations, although in their own most proper signification they are named from only one of the two abstract natures, nevertheless sometimes designate the whole person on account of the unity of the person, as when he is called Christ, Lord of glory, Son of Man, and man. For the name “flesh” is sometimes taken abstractly for humanity, as when it is said that Christ suffered in the flesh; at other times concretely, as when it is said that “the Word became flesh,” that is, became man. In sum, on account of that same union and the unity of the person, whatever is attributed to the Word is likewise and by the same rationale attributed to the man, and conversely, without any exception whatsoever. Thus this proposition, “The Word is man, the son of the Virgin Mary, created, finite, visible, and whatever else man is, except sin”—in which consists that emptying of the Son of God—is just as true as this: “That man is the Son of God, God consubstantial with the Father, eternal, uncreated, infinite, omnipotent, omnipresent, and whatever else the person of the Son of God is,” from which arises that supreme exaltation of the man. Whoever does not acknowledge both dissolves the person. For the natures are not confused in this way, since the former things are said of the person of the Son of God, not according to deity, but because with that man, who subsists in him and not in himself, he is one subsisting entity. The latter things, conversely, are not predicated of that man according to humanity, but insofar as the man has been assumed by the Son into the unity of the person. The same rationale also applies to those propositions in which not the essential properties themselves but certain things connected with them and proper to the individual natures are predicated, as if we should say that the man

[Part I | PDF page 104 | logical source scan 103 | printed page 99]

who was speaking on earth with Nicodemus was then also in heaven, and that he is now present with us and will be present until the consummation of the age—not, namely, according to the humanity, which certainly is not deity, lest the natures be confused, but insofar as he is one subsisting entity with that Son of God, lest the person be dissolved. Conversely, the Lord of glory is truly and orthodoxly said to have been conceived by the Holy Spirit, born of the Virgin Mary, to have hungered, thirsted, slept, suffered, died, been buried, risen again, and so forth—not according to the deity with the natures confused, but because, just as this flesh subsisting in him is his own, so he counts all the sufferings of the flesh as his own. This is that true communication of the essential properties and of those things which follow upon the properties: not in the natures themselves but in the person, since it arises from the hypostatic union and the unity of the person, and neither confuses the natures or their properties nor dissolves the person. This also has a place in operations, which are attributed to the supposit in such a way that they are distinguished without separation: the divine operations being attributed to the deity, and the human to that humanity.

To 2

Paul of Samosata denied the subsistence of the eternal Son of God. In our own memory that blasphemy was renewed by the impure dog Servetus, who at Geneva was deservedly punished not simply as a heretic but on account of horrible blasphemies against the Son of God and the whole holy Triad, from which he could by no means be drawn away; so afterward was the tritheist Gentile at Bern. Did the Swiss churches therefore immediately fall into these heresies? Far be it. Paul of Samosata’s other heresy was to posit nothing in Christ beyond mere humanity. Can those be said to be guilty of this crime who with all their strength—praise and glory be to God—have defended against the Arians and Tritheists the coeternal and coessential subsistence of the Son of God, and who now also defend the true hypostatic union and the true communication of properties against Nestorius and Eutyches reborn? Or ought those rather to be held guilty of this impiety who transform the hypostatic union into an outpouring of gifts common to Christ with Peter and all the saints apart from their measure? Who say Christ is God not because God is in him, but because of all the gifts communicated to him? And this, of course, is what Paul calls “dwelling bodily”! Who teach that God is in all creatures not only by nature but also according to subsistences—that is, hypostases—than which the Manichaeans said nothing more absurd? Who, finally, write that Christ the man “has this in common with all the saints, that God was personally united with him”—but why “was” rather than “is”? Yet Christ would seem to Andreae to be God in word only unless, beyond subsisting in the Son of God and being adorned eternally above even the angels with ineffable supernatural qualities, his humanity were also clothed with the spoils of deity.

[Part I | PDF page 105 | logical source scan 104 | printed page 100]

To 3

Rather, let Brenz and Andreae see whether they themselves are guilty of inexcusable blasphemy. For, as is clear from the preceding refutation, they establish that the authority given to Christ the man and that granted to Peter and the saints differ not in their whole kind but only in measure—unless perhaps they do not number power or authority among the gifts granted in some degree to the saints. Moreover, great ignorance is detected here. *Dynamis* is one thing and *exousia* another; and “all authority in heaven and on earth,” that is, the right and dominion of administering all created things above and below—for so Peter explains that passage of Matthew in Acts 2:36—is not that essential and infinite omnipotence proper to deity. Besides, that final gift conferred upon the humanity is falsely said to begin from the hypostatic union, since it could in no way stand together with the weakness of the flesh, which Christ, having truly become for our sake the most wretched of all men, though without sin, did not lay aside until his ministry was completed. Finally, since Andreae chiefly abuses this omnipotence to establish his omnipresence—although if the latter is posited, the reality of Christ’s flesh must be abolished—he acts just as if he said that Christ was also granted power to will contradictories, that is, to contradict himself on one side or the other—may the blasphemy be far from the expression. Lastly, has Andreae forgotten that an argument from *can* to *is* is invalid? Meanwhile, we by no means deny that this man is omnipotent, for then we would divide the person, although we by no means concede to the Eutychians that the humanity is omnipotent.

To 4

If God’s omnipotence is infinite, as it necessarily is, then it is as impious to establish several omnipotences as to make several infinities and deities—which is also absurd in itself. Therefore in the Athanasian Creed we confess three persons but one omnipotent, neither aggregated from the persons nor divided among them. Omnipotence, then, is one and essential, and is not from elsewhere but from itself; hence there is no participated omnipotence—that is, as Andreae necessarily understands the word here, one actually communicated to another nature, namely, the assumed humanity. Even if “participation” is taken for the hypostatic union, this will not help Andreae either, because the distinction of the natures remained in it, each nature retaining what is proper to itself. Hence the Scholastics rightly conclude that omnipotence cannot be communicated to any creature, so far that they confess Christ neither by his body nor by his soul could or can alter the customary order of nature except insofar as he was and is the instrument of the Word of God personally united to him. If Andreae either does not know or does not acknowledge this, shall we therefore have been heretics?

To 5

We answered this calumny most fully in the first refuta-

[Part I | PDF page 106 | logical source scan 105 | printed page 101]

tion of Antithesis 3. But I would gladly ask Andreae whether the man is giving words in place of reality and using tricks who denies that a body understands and reasons, even when united to its soul, although an understanding and reasoning soul is personally united to it.

Thesis X of the Württemberg Theologians

We believe that from the personal union there follows such a real communication of properties by which the Son of God communicates to the assumed human nature his omnipotence, omniscience, omnipresence, and power or capacity to give life, and so forth. Through this communication the divine nature in Christ is not made weaker, while the human nature is exalted, not abolished. Learned antiquity, in explaining this mystery, used the likeness of soul and body and likewise of glowing iron. For the body truly lives, but lives by the soul’s power; and glowing iron truly burns, but burns by the fire’s power. Yet the body is not changed into the soul or abolished on account of that communication; nor is the iron converted into fire or ceases to be iron.

Antithesis X of Theodore Beza

We have several times answered concerning the omnipotence, omnipresence, and omniscience of the humanity: that these are attributed not to the humanity, even united, but to that man according to another respect; and below we shall discuss the point again in detail. As for those likenesses of glowing iron and of a body joined with a soul, the hypostatic union cannot be defined from them, but only sketched, much less can the communication which you establish arise from them. For many things in these likenesses are dissimilar. They must therefore be drawn no further than the sense in which the orthodox Fathers applied them. As regards the likeness of glowing iron, the Fathers used it against the Eutychians and Monothelites to teach that the properties and operations of the natures, though united with each other, are not communicated and do not leave their subjects; so far is it from favoring your position. The likeness of the union of soul and body to constitute a human being is well known to differ in many ways from the union of the two natures in Christ. Nor was it applied by the orthodox Fathers for any other purpose than to show the unity of the person in two complete natures; it must not be drawn further.

Andreae’s Annotations

1. This is wordplay and a corruption of the distinguishing particle, which not only shows to which nature something is proper but also indicates the cause why what is proper to one nature is attributed to the other: namely, on account of the union with the other nature, by which it is communicated to it personally, that is, truly and really.

2. Through these likenesses the Fathers explained the mystery of the union and that the real communication of proper things can occur without confusion of the natures.

3. Indeed, the contrary is true. For from them the Fathers demonstrated a real communication against the Eutychians in such a way that no confusion of the natures or abolition of either follows from it.

4. No likeness runs on all four feet;

[Part I | PDF page 107 | logical source scan 106 | printed page 102]

but it agrees in the respect for which the Fathers applied it.

This rule therefore stands: in this hypostatic union the natures themselves remain distinct, and each acts distinctly in what is proper to it. Consequently, the Word is distinctly what the Word is and distinctly does what belongs to the Word; so too the flesh remains distinctly what flesh is and distinctly carries out what belongs to the flesh. Thus, to sum everything up briefly, just as there are two numerically distinct essences, though not separated, so there are two wills and operations and two acts, but one result, just as there is one person. Nor must the statement of Athanasius be understood otherwise: that it pleased the Word to exercise his divine power through that flesh, in it, and with it.

5. This rule is false. For actions in the person of Christ are by no means distinct—that is, performed separately or by divided natures—but are attributed to the whole person.

6. The Word does nothing without the flesh, just as the soul does nothing distinctly without the flesh, nor the flesh without the soul. Although the natures are and remain distinct, the whole person Christ acts.

7. The grossest Nestorianism. There are two distinct wills in Christ and two distinct essences and operations, but the actions are common and directed to one end.

8. This opinion of Athanasius does not support the error concerning distinct actions of the natures, but refutes and overturns it.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

To 1

From these insults of Andreae I appeal to the tribunal of God himself, who knows both his conscience and mine—indeed, I appeal to this very observation of his. For when he says that the distinguishing particles show to which nature that which is truly and actually communicated to the other nature is proper, who can think Andreae wrote otherwise than in jest? For when two different natures are posited, such as deity and humanity, that which actually becomes common with the other must necessarily cease to be proper. To say that “proper” and “common” are the same—what else is this than to make sport of people? Further, what he adds is ridiculous: that distinguishing formulas, such as “according to another respect,” show why what is proper to one nature is attributed to the other. For in this way there would be shown not why this occurred, if indeed it occurred, but in what sense it was to be understood: namely, so as to signify that what was attributed to the other possessed this not from itself but from elsewhere. For example, when on account of the unity of the person it is said that God the Word is man and suffered by *antidosis*, and conversely that the man is God and eternal, “according to another respect” is added in both cases, so that the former is understood of God, yet not according to the deity by which he is God, and the latter of the man, yet not according to the humanity. But why these propositions are true must be sought not from this formula, “according to another respect,” or “according to the thing seized,” or “according to the Spirit,” or “according to God,” or “according to that in which he was contemplated,” as Damascene speaks and correctly explains in book 3, chapter 4, but partly from the hypostatic union of the natures and partly from the reciprocal attribution of the properties. Neither of these is signified by those dis-

[Part I | PDF page 108 | logical source scan 107 | printed page 103]

tinguishing formulas, which instead declare the distinction of the natures. Finally, if this is not to mock people, what is: to explain the adverb “personally” as “truly and really”? For if this is the true and proper explanation of that adverb, what will become of those things said about the individual natures naturally and according to themselves, in the most real and true manner, yet not personally—that is, with respect to the person? For example, the Word is said to be God, eternal, and consubstantial with the Father; and that man is the son of Abraham, who hungered, thirsted, died, and is consubstantial with us. Unless, perhaps, Andreae wishes to confess that he confounds the natures and the person according to Eutyches’s doctrine. If not, since whatever is said truly and really of Christ extends much more widely than what is said personally, who does not see that Andreae, who accuses us as wordplayers, might rather appear to have played when he wrote this—just like some sophist in a school who would say that he had correctly defined man by expressly naming “animal,” the genus, while omitting the difference “rational”? I would not pursue these minute matters if, rightly understood, they were not of the greatest importance for settling this controversy. I set them forth in Damascene’s words from book 3, chapter 7 of *The Orthodox Faith*: “THE WHOLE represents the natures, but WHOLE the hypostasis or person, just as WHAT is of the natures but WHO is of the hypostasis.” He proves this by the following example: “The whole Christ is perfect God, but not the whole of Christ is God; and the whole Christ is perfect man, but not the whole of Christ is man. For he is not only God but also man, not only man but also God.” Let us therefore understand that “the whole Christ” signifies the same as Christ considered compositely—that is, as one conjointly, or according to composition, with the distinction of the natures not indeed abolished but not taken into account. In this way many things are said of Christ, and indeed most truly, and not in one manner only, as Damascene explains. If Andreae attempts to refute these points, I have no fear that he will not be disproved both from God’s Word and from the perpetual consensus of the orthodox church. Let him see, then, which of these many and very great errors he intends to remove from himself—would that he would rather correct them. The first is this:

That, following Nestorius in his earlier writings—as I have now often shown him—he wrote: “Christ the man has this in common with all the saints, that God was personally united with him.”

The second is that, attacking himself here, he writes on page 233 that Nestorius never denied the unity of the person, although the contrary is clear from the preceding error.

The third is that earlier, following Brenz his master, he established this one distinction between God’s indwelling in Christ—which certainly is the personal union, unless perhaps he wishes to disagree with Paul—and in Peter: that he distributes measured gifts to the saints but immeasurable ones to Christ; or, in Brenz’s words, that although he fills Peter with his essence just

[Part I | PDF page 109 | logical source scan 108 | printed page 104]

as he does Christ the man, nevertheless he communicates not all his properties to Peter, but only SOME.

The fourth is that, now contradicting himself, on page 310 of this book he says he will never concede that Christ’s gifts and those of holy men differ only according to more and less. This is as true if it is understood of the gift of union as his earlier statement was false. But who of sufficiently sound mind, or who does not treat his hearer as a laughingstock, denies that immeasurable and measured gifts—or, as Brenz speaks more plainly, all properties and some properties—are referred to the same genus? Andreae seems indeed to have recognized this error but to have wished to correct it evasively on page 312, when he adds the specific difference, namely “uncreated” and “created,” which he explains more fully in thesis 4. Let him therefore delete and once for all expunge that blasphemy of his, that the divinity of Christ the man is not that essence, and that one—one, I say—distinction between Peter and Christ so often inscribed on those apples of discord of his.

The fifth error can be excused still less in Andreae. In wishing to correct what we have discussed, by which he had presented Christ to us as most divine in place of God, he wanders so far into the opposite direction and dissents from his fourth thesis that he writes concerning Christ the man—these are his words on page 112—that “all the communicated gifts are uncreated, immeasurable, infinite,” and denies that the knowledge of Christ the man is a created gift. For if that is true, either the whole human nature was not assumed—which was Apollinaris’s error—or, if the Word assumed a mind too, he assumed a mind lacking the quality of reason and natural knowledge: a mind which was no mind.

The sixth error partly exposes and partly increases the preceding ones. Since Andreae could not retract those universal expressions “all properties” and “all gifts, none of which is created,” and was therefore asked whether he thought eternity, infinity, incorporeality, immateriality, and similar things were poured into that assumed nature, he resorted on folio 271 to a distinction among the attributes of the divine essence. “Some of its acts,” he says, “are substantial and operative, such as wisdom, power, justice, mercy, and so forth. Others concern the essence of deity and are not operative, such as to be from eternity, to be infinite in essence, incorporeal, immaterial, and the like. These are said to be communicated to the humanity because deity itself, from which they cannot be separated, is communicated. The humanity, however, is not named from these, because they are not acts perfecting human nature, as those mentioned in the first order perfect it and give it its designation. Therefore, although the human nature has had eternal and infinite essence communicated to it, just as omnipotence has been, it is nevertheless not called infinite and eternal as it is truly called omnipotent.” These are his words. What in them shall I censure first, and what last? First, as regards that distinction of God’s attributes,

[Part I | PDF page 110 | logical source scan 109 | printed page 105]

how much more plainly would he have spoken if he had said that, among those things through which God has declared to us what he is like and which are called attributes because God attributes them to himself for our sake, some are so proper to God that they can have no reference to created things, since by these God is chiefly distinguished from all created things—such as true simplicity, eternity, immensity, and others of that kind. Others, however, bear some relation to us, such as mercy and omnipotence, which has a relation to things made and things which can be made, as do love, hatred, wrath, and the rest of this kind. Now when Andreae wishes both kinds to be communicated, because, he says, “the deity itself is communicated to that human nature,” if he understands this of the union of the natures themselves, he speaks well concerning the latter class of attributes and destroys his communication of properties, which he makes different from the union of the natures themselves. But in the other class of attributes, which he mentioned first, if he again takes communication for the union of those essential attributes also, he passes over what especially needed to be stated explicitly: namely, that these too are said to be hypostatically united in such a way that Christ’s humanity has further received them into itself, not as they are and in the sense in which they are essentially attributed to deity—namely, insofar as they are deity itself and one without multiplication—but insofar as certain qualities produced by those attributes of deity and actually conferred upon that humanity flow afterward from its fullness into all believers through the benefit of regeneration. To this also tends our spiritual union with Christ, whose token and pledge is the holy Supper of the Lord. But, I ask, what kind of thing is this: that eternity and infinity are communicated to that humanity as power is, and yet the humanity is not called infinite and eternal as it is called omnipotent, because, he says, they do not perfect human nature? Why not rather because they would abolish human nature? Since this is so, to what end and for what use shall we say those properties too were communicated, when it should rather have been said that they were not to be communicated lest they abolish it? I ask this also: why did he pass over omnipresence in his enumeration of those attributes, and in which class of attributes does he think it should be placed? If in the latter, as though included by him under the name omnipotence, I ask why, contradicting himself and committing the very thing he elsewhere falsely charged against us, he prescribes fixed bounds to this omnipotence. For otherwise, if it is actually omnipresent because it is actually omnipotent, why does he not also extend this omnipotence so far that infinity too is attributed to that humanity? But if he numbers omnipresence in the former class, we have Andreae himself confessing with his own mouth that this humanity cannot

[Part I | PDF page 111 | logical source scan 110 | printed page 106]

more truly be called omnipresent than eternal or infinite. And the matter certainly stands thus. For although omnipresence has a relation to all created things, it is nevertheless so proper to God’s nature—being utterly and necessarily connected with infinity, which cannot in any way be multiplied—that it cannot be attributed even to an angelic nature so that angels might be definitively present in several places at once. Finally, if human nature ought not to be named from those attributes of God which would not perfect but destroy it, let Andreae teach us how that omnipresent majesty of Christ’s flesh which he dreams of does not abolish the nature of a truly organic, circumscribed, palpable, visible, and therefore finite body—much less how it perfects it. Again, if those attributes which concern God and are not operative—such as eternity, finitude, and others of that kind—are indeed communicated to the humanity but are not said of the humanity because they would not perfect human nature, what shall we say of deity itself? For we can think of nothing about God which concerns him more than deity itself. And certainly we must confess that this is so. Who would say that humanity is deity? Yet if what Andreae writes is just as true—namely, that because eternity and infinity are not attributed to the humanity, that man cannot therefore be called eternal and infinite—it will also follow, on Andreae’s judgment, that this man cannot be called God, since deity can by no means be predicated of humanity. Nor is this obstructed by what he might perhaps object: that deity is not an attribute but signifies God’s very essence or being. For this distinction has no place here, since the matter concerns the union by which God himself, namely the Word, truly united to that man both himself and whatever is attributed to him, so that whatever God is must therefore be said of that man, since by this union he united himself and whatever he is—even if, according to Andreae’s opinion, we distinguished some communication of properties from the union of the natures itself as effect from cause. Let Andreae therefore deny that this man is rightly called God, or else, retracing his steps, confess that in following Nestorius he wrongly confounds abstract with concrete—that is, deity with God and humanity with man. Again, to bind him still more tightly with his own bonds: if, as he writes, certain substantial acts in God, because they are operative and can perfect the assumed human nature, are so communicated to that humanity that it is thereby named omnipotent and omnipresent, how is this whole communication of such acts not merely operative rather than essential, as Andreae himself once wrote in express words? Certainly, unless it were so, and unless those acts subjectively effected something created in that humanity, how would they perfect it? Therefore, on Andreae’s own principles, unless he prefers to establish two omnipotences, omnipresences, and

[Part I | PDF page 112 | logical source scan 111 | printed page 107]

omnimajesties—one substantial and incommunicable in God, and the other created in the assumed humanity and effected by that substantial divine energy—that entire real communication of all God’s substantial properties into the assumed humanity will collapse. The explanation of “personally” as “truly and really” will fall. That entire real and essential presence on earth of Christ’s flesh and blood in, under, or with the bread—considered certainly not supernaturally but contra-naturally—will be abolished. Finally, that oral eating will be utterly destroyed. What more? If, as Andreae writes in the passage cited above, those properties of divinity from which the humanity is not named, such as eternity and infinity, are nevertheless said to be communicated to that humanity because they cannot be separated from deity, let Andreae tell us, I beg, whether those other operative properties, communicated to the humanity in such a way that it is even named from them, can any more be separated from deity. Certainly not in the least, as Andreae himself now acknowledges after being admonished by Chemnitz, and for that reason now abstains from the name “outpouring.” But by what species of communication did the Word become flesh, that is, man? Certainly not by that false Nestorian conjunction according to relation—whether according to attendance, will, equality of honor, conjunction (which Brenz and Andreae call equating), or cohabitation—as the God-hated Nestorius taught, according to Damascene, book 3, chapter 5 of *The Orthodox Faith*; but according to hypostasis. By this the Word, while the natures, their properties, and their actions remained distinct without separation of the person and without any confusion, gave subsistence to the flesh which he assumed. Thus he became God-man and the uncircumscribed subsisting with the circumscribed—not as though properly from component parts, but as two natures coming together in the preexistent and eternal hypostasis of the Son of God himself.

Once this is established, and also this most certain principle which Chemnitz rightly and expressly asserted—namely, that by the abstract names “humanity” and “deity,” even in the union itself, the natures are signified as they are and remain forever in themselves and according to themselves, endowed with their properties—Andreae’s first perpetual and intolerable error appears from it. Namely, by those abstract terms, divinity and humanity, he attributes something other than what they have in themselves: divine properties to the humanity really, though not subjectively, and human properties to the divinity by a certain overflow and a mode which he cannot explain in any way. For this can assuredly never occur in an orthodox manner—not even with the distinguishing particle “according to another respect” added—without violating the definition of the hypostatic union and the very nature of abstract terms which we have described. The only exception is when the abstracts are used improperly in place of concretes, as the Fathers sometimes did, especially before the controversies of Nestorius and Eutyches; such usage must be candidly excused and interpreted.

[Part I | PDF page 113 | logical source scan 112 | printed page 108]

This is also the reason why, conversely, the proper things of each nature are attributed according to that nature to concrete appellations. These express the person of Christ himself, considered not according to the natures from which he is composed according to the hypostasis, but as a whole or insofar as he is one thing—for wholeness, or “totality,” so to speak, is a certain species of unity, as the Philosopher rightly writes in *Metaphysics* 4. Thus Christ is called God and man, infinite and finite, passible and impassible. Yet we would never rightly say, “Christ is the divinity of the Word and the assumed humanity, a finite and infinite nature, or an impassible and passible nature,” because Christ is not a nature but the name of a subsisting entity, while abstract terms signify the natures, not the hypostasis itself. Indeed, because on account of that unity and union of the person, and the one whole, the whole person resulting from the hypostasis is sometimes named by the concrete name of one nature—for example, Word or man—human things are therefore truly attributed to the Word, and divine things to the man. Yet not according to the nature itself, as Eutyches wished when he established one nature just as he did one person; nor according to one person and another, as Nestorius wished, joining the natures by a merely attendant conjunction without union and not uniting them into one subsisting entity; but according to one thing and another, that is, according to the apostle’s interpretation in Romans 1, attributing human things to the Word according to the flesh and divine things to the man according to the Spirit. This occurs by *antidosis*, or reciprocal giving, or the communication of properties—not that fictitious communication which our opponents press, but the one drawn from God’s Word and explained no otherwise by the Fathers in their highest consensus. Therefore, just as the natures are communicated—that is, united—according to hypostasis, so also the properties and operations inseparable from the natures are united. Thus, with concrete terms of the subsisting entity and without any exception—as we have now said several times and are compelled often to repeat—just as truly as Christ is called God and man, so truly are the concrete terms both of properties and actions attributed to him as the person himself. This is done in the subsisting or attendant entity according to another respect, but not in abstract terms. For the Word makes this man and whatever belongs to this man—whether properties or acts—his own and proper, granting him a subsistence common with himself; yet he remained what he was, and in turn the flesh thus assumed retained its properties. Although, as Damascene correctly warns, the authoritative or reciprocal attribution of the natures—by which that man is said personally of God the Word and God the Word of that man, that is, of the whole Christ, though not of the whole of Christ according to itself, just as the properties and actions of the natures themselves are also reciprocally attributed—arises from the Word as giver and not from that man

[Part I | PDF page 114 | logical source scan 113 | printed page 109]

as receiver according to the flesh, nevertheless, since God is united to man by no other kind of union than man is united to God, Andreae absurdly thinks that in the reciprocal attribution divine things are communicated by God to the man in one way, but human things by the man to God in another. He also ignorantly distinguishes between those properties which he calls operative in act and those which concern God himself absolutely. On the contrary, without any distinction these propositions are equally and altogether true: “God sprang from David’s seed, was conceived by the Holy Spirit, suffered, died,” and so forth; and “The Son of Man is eternal, infinite, omnipotent, omnipresent.” For the union of God the Word with this man and all that belongs to the man is one and the same as that man’s union with God the Word and all that belongs to the Word: namely, personal. Conversely, whatever Andreae may advance in defense of this error, he attributes divine things to humanity and human things to divinity not merely absurdly and ignorantly but also impiously. The inequality of this reciprocal attribution must be regarded not in the union itself, nor in that utterly false communication of natural properties between the natures themselves, but in what they call habitual grace: namely, in the fact that the humanity was endowed with ineffable created qualities, while the deity, being immutable, neither acquired nor lost anything. Who, then, would assent to Andreae when he writes on page 139 that “the whole Christ” and “the whole of Christ” are equivalent, and that the natures are considered sometimes in the union and sometimes outside it? “For what,” he says, “is the whole of Christ other than the Son of God and the assumed man; and what is the whole Christ other than the Son of God with the assumed human nature?” I answer that both expressions are defined most badly in this manner. For, as we showed above from Damascene, “the whole,” in the neuter gender, is an abstract term designating the natures, however united and considered in the union itself, yet still distinct—that is, as they are in themselves and never otherwise. And since abstracts are not predicated of concretes—not even Andreae dares say that humanity is God or deity is man—“the whole of Christ” must not be defined by the concrete terms “Son of God” and “assumed man,” but by “the deity of the Word” and “the assumed human nature.” Conversely, since abstracts cannot be predicated of concretes even in the union itself, I would not correctly define “the whole Christ,” considered compositely, as the deity of the Word and the assumed human nature; but I would rightly and orthodoxly say that it is God the Word and that man subsisting in the Word. Therefore, although the Word is said to have become incarnate and that man to have been deified and “Wordified,” as Damascene speaks—that is, so to speak, made divine and made Word—according to hypostasis and without confusion, nevertheless from this arises against Nestorius a God-man and a man-God, but not a Eutychian divine-humanity or human-divinity. Come now, let us also weigh other things arising from the same

[Part I | PDF page 115 | logical source scan 114 | printed page 110]

error and defended as true by Andreae in this disputation. On page 243 he explains the formula “IN ITSELF” in three ways: first, when it is taken absolutely and without respect to another; second, when it is taken for “OF ITSELF,” that is, from the property of its nature—two manifestly different categories which he wrongly confounds; third, insofar as something is found in another, in which it is said to inhere, even if it came from elsewhere. I do not wish to spend time criticizing these statements, confusedly and not altogether truly spoken, lest I seem to seize occasions for rebuke when Andreae should rather think of defending himself. But I say this: when “according to itself” and “according to another respect” are opposed in this argument, “in itself” pertains to signifying the essential properties of the natures. In the deity these are its very essence; in the assumed humanity they inhere formally. Of this sort are all the most pure and holy essential properties of that nature. To these must be joined the peculiar gifts of God by which that human nature assumed in that man was exalted above even the angels and above every created name: first, insofar as it became proper to the Word and one subsisting entity in him according to hypostasis; then insofar as it was adorned above all the angels with ineffable created gifts both in soul and in body, and obtained dominion over all created things. But we acknowledge no other operative communication from God’s Word or the orthodox Fathers. Nor do we ever consider either that human nature outside the hypostasis of the Son of God or that man outside union with the Word, even when we set both the nature and the man before our thought in themselves, that is distinctly but not separately.

What sort of statement, finally, is what Andreae says on page 251? “The union of the natures is one thing and the communication of properties another, just as human nature is one thing and the properties of human nature another.” A sharp argument indeed—as though two or more things differing even in their entire genus and by the greatest distance could not be given by one and the same donation. Further, what will become of the properties of divinity, whose communication to the humanity chiefly—indeed, exclusively—occupies Andreae here, since, as he himself testifies on page 260, deity is nothing other than its own properties and its own essence? But his inference in the same place—that we deny Christ’s divinity if we say nothing except created gifts were communicated to Christ’s humanity—is spoken partly against us and partly against himself. Against himself it is most true, if compared with those things we cited above which he wrote on page 112; against us, however, it is false. For we do indeed say that it was not granted to this humanity that it should be deity, which is Eutychian. But to this man, through the subsistence received in the Word, we say that not only ineffable created gifts were given, but above all this: that the man is true God. Consequently, insofar as they are one subsisting entity, the Word and the man

[Part I | PDF page 116 | logical source scan 115 | printed page 111]

(that man), divine things are also truly attributed to that man as an instrument personally coexisting with the Word and—although the property and operation of each individual nature remain distinct—contributing toward one common result. Conversely, by precisely the same reasoning, the Word is said to do and suffer those things which that man subsisting in him does and suffers. But we shall discuss this matter—that is, orthodox divine-human actions, and the Eutychian or Monothelite actions—in its proper place. Before we proceed, however, we could not pass over even these matters, so that the most unskilled may understand with whom we have to deal. You have heard, Christian reader—and not without the most wearisome repetition, to which Andreae continually compels me by repeatedly returning to the same thing—how carefully we distinguish abstracts from concretes, and into what shameful errors those who do not observe that distinction fall, as Damascene rightly warned. You have also heard how ignorantly Andreae supposes that by a concrete term we properly and always signify the person. Now hear a monstrous calumny. “By MAN,” he says on page 291, “you understand the deity alone, and that by the trope called metonymy.” I ask: how could one who truly said that we understand the person by concrete terms truly say that by the appellation “man” we exclude the humanity and understand only the deity? Unless perhaps one who posits the whole thereby removes one of its parts. But if we removed one part, why would we not take deity rather than humanity—which approaches more nearly to man—away from the man? Indeed, how would we not rather abolish the whole man by taking away that very thing by which every man is man? And what species of trope could possibly be found by which “man” is taken for “deity”? If any trope can occur here, whence could it ever have come into Andreae’s mind to dream that we dreamed of any metonymy here? But whence, finally, come so many mutually conflicting monsters? “You openly say,” he continues, “in your response to the eighth and ninth thesis, ‘Man is omnipotent,’ that is, ‘The deity united to the human nature is omnipotent, without any real communication being made to the humanity.’” I appeal to the context of my Antithesis, reader, where you will not find that interpretation, which we shall discuss at last in our response to the fourth dogma. But you will find these two things: we admit the same real communication—that is, union—of the properties as of the natures themselves, and no other; and to the man thus united with the Word—that is, to the whole person denominated concretely from the other nature, namely humanity, or to Christ as a whole, not according to a part, or considered distinctly in the natures—we rightly and truly attribute whatever is proper to God. I ask: does one remove the man and substitute only the deity in his place who professes that man to be united and, on account of the unity of the person, attributes to him whatever is proper to God, yet without confusion of the natures? And the deity,

[Part I | PDF page 117 | logical source scan 116 | printed page 112]

how does one establish it ALONE who posits it united? But Andreae appears to think that one removes the concrete who distinguishes the abstract from it, and abrogates one of the natures who retains both them and the distinction of their properties and actions. “For,” he says on page 308, “if anyone says, ‘The whole Christ is omnipotent,’ and understands this only of the divine nature with the humanity excluded, it is an evident deception. For the divine nature alone is not the whole Christ.” But who speaks thus: “The whole Christ is omnipotent”? For why would this particle “whole” be added, when one who names Christ names that entire hypostasis? Therefore, when we distinguish “the whole Christ” from “the whole of Christ,” we do not join this particle “whole” to Christ grammatically, as though “whole” were opposed to “half.” Rather, we signify that the appellation of Christ is sometimes considered as a whole—that is, as one whole thing—with no account taken of the distinction of the natures from which that one thing is constituted according to hypostasis; and this for us is “the whole Christ.” At other times it is considered according to the distinction of the natures, according to which it is a composite person, as when Christ is said to be God and man, infinite and finite, and, for example, to have died and been buried. The Fathers were compelled to observe and establish this distinction, first against Nestorius—whatever Andreae, deceived by Nestorius himself, may assert. Nestorius denied that one entire whole, for just as he posited two natures, so he established two sons and consequently two persons—or, as the Fathers speak, one and another: one the Son of God, God; the other the son of Mary, man. Therefore, just as he rightly posited two abstract natures, so he falsely posited two concrete entities and as many persons, joined only by attendance, relation, and sameness of name—which is also Brenz’s and Andreae’s communication and equating of the natures. The Fathers then established the distinction against Eutyches and his offspring, the Monothelites and Monophysites, by whom, in the precisely contrary error, just as one person, so one nature was established. What more? “It is Nestorian,” Andreae says, “to remove suffering altogether from the divinity.” On the contrary, it is merely impious and blasphemous to attribute any undergoing of suffering to the divinity. Yet Nestorius would not have been numbered among the heretics unless, just as he removed suffering from the divinity—which he did not distinguish from God—so he removed it from God the Word. But what does this have to do with us, when Andreae himself, as I said a little before, accuses us of understanding the deity alone by “man”? Yet it is nothing new to hear him dissent from himself. “You interpret,” he says on page 267, “‘God suffered,’ that is, ‘The humanity or flesh united to the divinity suffered.’” We shall discuss this in our response to the fourth dogma. What if Andreae agrees with us in this very matter? For in precisely these words on page 251 he says: “We too say and predicate the proper things of one nature of the other”—therefore, the proper things of humanity of divinity, and those of divinity of humanity—“according to another respect; that is, not absolutely and in the abstract, but in the concrete, that is, in

[Part I | PDF page 118 | logical source scan 117 | printed page 113]

ion, with respect to the other nature.” “According to another respect” is indeed true; but “in the personal union” is thoroughly inept and wholly unnecessary. For the natures signified by abstract terms must never be considered as separated, not even in thought, but always as distinct even in the union itself, since in this union they always remain such as they are in themselves. And immediately afterward: “You say that when it is said, ‘God suffered,’ this must be understood according to another respect, that is, with respect to the humanity. On this point there is agreement between us.” Andreae is therefore guilty of the very crime of which he accuses us. “By no means,” he responds on the same page. “For ‘with respect to the humanity’ is interpreted thus: unless God had become man, this proposition, ‘God suffered,’ would never have been true, because God is impassible of himself”—that is, outside the union—“and by his own divinity.” An excellent gloss, to be sure. For “of himself,” whether taken for “outside the union” or for “from the property of his nature,” is spoken ignorantly, since, as I have just said, the natures must never be considered outside the union, and nothing can accrue to the properties of deity. It remains, therefore, that nothing can be predicated of the deity of the Word except what it has in itself—or rather, what it is in itself. But in itself it is not passible. Therefore, suffering cannot be attributed to it in any way without impiety. “Then,” Andreae says, “do you deny with Nestorius that God suffered?” Far be it from me; for then I would divide the person. But conversely, I refuse to profess with Eutyches—or with Brenz or Andreae—that the deity suffered in any way. For although deity and God do not differ in reality, nevertheless the weakness of the human mind causes us to comprehend deity absolutely, but God as endowed with deity. This is so in its own manner: not as though deity were some intelligible entity subsisting in the hypostases as a species subsists in created individuals; rather, the individual hypostases subsist in the whole and indivisible deity, their relations having in them, as Thomas truly and acutely says, a certain character as though of a principle of their personality. Hence it comes about that, since by this union deity did not become humanity, but God—that is, the Word, or the person of the Son—became man, God is far more properly said than deity or the divine nature to have assumed the human nature rather than a man. That nature, having obtained in the Son of God who assumed it subsistence—that is, actual existence—became man. From these things I think that anyone even moderately instructed in divine matters will easily judge with what sort of theologian this affair is being transacted; that is, which of us is orthodox, retaining the unity of the person and the distinction of the natures, and which both divides the person with Nestorius and confounds the properties of the natures with Eutyches. This will shine forth still more from what follows. For Andreae says on page 252: “To attribute solely to the humanity the suffering by which the human race was redeemed, so that the Son of God in his divinity is wholly excluded from it—or so that the suffering pertains in no way to

[Part I | PDF page 119 | logical source scan 118 | printed page 114]

the deity—is an evident corruption and depraving of the article concerning Christ’s passion.” Do you hear, Christian reader, how far Andreae’s zeal boils over? I fear that it is even against conscience; certainly it is not according to knowledge. For in what school did he learn that the particle “according to another respect”—by which elsewhere he himself said not only the distinction of the natures but also the cause of that very distinction, namely the closest union, was declared—removes either deity from humanity or humanity from deity? But what are you to do with this disputant, who, now having repeatedly asserted that abstracts are not confounded with concretes, now confounds the concrete with the person; now takes IN ITSELF for OF ITSELF, that is, either by the property of its nature or outside the union; now accuses us of excluding humanity as though we took man for deity; now affirms that deity is taken away by us when humanity is posited—who says anything and denies anything? Anathema to him who says that the divinity suffered. Anathema to him who denies that God the Word suffered. For from the former necessarily follows the Nestorian dissolution of the person; from the latter, Eutychian confusion. Therefore, this suffering pertains to God the Word, for this flesh which suffered is his own flesh. If Christ reckons the afflictions of his mystical members—who are nevertheless by no means hypostatically conjoined with him—as his own, crying out to Saul, “Why do you persecute me?” did not those who pierced his own flesh and assaulted his own soul with curses more cruel than the crucifixion of the body itself sin against him all the more? But Andreae is greatly deceived with Eutyches if he dreams that Christ’s deity—united either then in this life or now in that other glorious life to our flesh, to which it granted subsistence—was affected by any undergoing of suffering; or if he now supposes that in that glorious life Christ’s humanity is so injured, whether in itself by his enemies or in his mystical members, that it undergoes even the slightest alteration of that most blessed Majesty. No one will marvel that Andreae, following his master, fell headlong into these inexplicable errors and cannot extricate himself, nor that all their disciples after them did the same—to such a degree that he dared write those inexcusable blasphemies which I have so often adduced from his writings: “The divinity of the man Christ is not that substance”; “All things are sustained subsistentially by God”; “The man Christ has this in common with all the saints, that God is personally united with him.” No one, I say, will marvel who reads these words of his from page 257, which I certainly do not remember hearing from him when he was not disputing with me but declaiming. For although he was heaping up so many things ambiguously that I could scarcely follow each one in my mind, much less respond to them one by one, nevertheless I think it could scarcely have happened that I passed over such absurdities without a response. They are these: “If Christ’s divinity is removed from his suffering, and you admit no other communication than that the divine nature sustained the human nature lest it succumb while suffering, Christ

[Part I | PDF page 120 | logical source scan 119 | printed page 115]

will have this in common with all the saints, since God sustained all the saints in their suffering so that they would not succumb.” Here we hear what Andreae himself thinks is signified by the name “sustentation”—from which the hypostatic union takes its name—while he falsely alleges that we understood it most ignorantly.
