Therefore, if his dream were true, for us the statement that the human nature is sustained by God’s Word would declare nothing other than that it is supported by his power and strength. Andreae, however, with his associates insists not only here but a thousand times that the hypostatic assumption of the flesh must necessarily be defined, in his sixth thesis from the disputation *On the Majesty of the Man Christ*, by the equating of humanity with divinity in substance, power, glory, and majesty. And in the *Apology to the Ingolstadt Theses*, at one point it is denoted by “a real outpouring into the assumed flesh, by which he distributes measured gifts to the saints and immeasurable gifts to Christ”; at another, by “the omnipotence granted to Christ both in heaven and on earth from the very moment of the Word’s incarnation.” But indeed, as regards what he charges against us, what audacity to calumniate is this: that by the name “hypostatic sustentation” we understand nothing beyond the support which sustained Christ’s flesh in the affliction he underwent for us? Or, as he speaks on page 266, that only created gifts are attributed by us to Christ’s humanity? Whence, I ask, could Andreae rightly suspect us of being either so ignorant, so profane, or so like himself? For who would dare deny that Brenz and Andreae himself, at least at the beginning of that ubiquity brought out upon the Church’s stage, held no other view? Besides the things we have just cited, they wrote “that Christ is filled with God’s substance no otherwise than Peter,” and “that Christ is not called God because God is in him—who is in all creatures not only by nature but also according to the subsistences.” And to weigh this dogma of theirs as well: what could be more inept than that definition of the hypostatic union, taken from the effect Andreae imagines, in which the proximate and proper cause of that effect is omitted? For upon what, finally, does that outpouring of all the properties of deity into the humanity—or, as he now speaks a little more prudently, their communication with the humanity—proximately depend? Andreae contends that the hypostatic union must be defined by this communication alone and distinguishes the union from that communication of his as cause from effect. Does it not depend upon the union of the deity itself, from which those properties cannot be separated, as he himself acknowledges on page 271? Yet so far is even the slightest mention of the union of the natures themselves from being made in that definition of the hypostatic union, that, on the contrary, Brenz expressly insists and Andreae repeats that God’s indwelling in Christ is no other than in the saints, except for the inequality between all properties and some; and that the divinity of the man Christ is not that substance, but one communicated with that flesh by the second hypostasis. But if this divinity is not substance, it is certainly either a created quality or the emptiest fiction. Nor do I wish to repeat here what I said before at greater

[Part I | PDF page 121 | logical source scan 120 | printed page 116]

length in refutation concerning certain united divine properties, such as eternity and infinity. Andreae imagines—still more ridiculously than if he denied that the Word can be denominated created and finite—that the man, although hypostatically united, ought not to be denominated from them. But on this matter, come, let us once hear the orthodox faith speaking in these very words through the mouth of Damascene, book 3, chapter 4, and first concerning abstracts: “When we speak of deity, we by no means assign to it those things which are proper to humanity; for we do not call the deity passible or created. Nor, conversely, do we attribute the properties of deity to the flesh or humanity. For we do not call the flesh or humanity uncreated.” Whence, then, except from the bogs of Eutyches, comes that omnipresence, omnipotence, and omnimajesty of Christ’s humanity? For Damascene speaks generally and admits no communication of properties between the natures—not even a verbal one, much less any real one—either in the divinity or in the humanity. Thus it is altogether of the same impiety to call the humanity or that flesh omnipresent, omnipotent, omniscient, infinite, eternal, and uncreated, and to imagine that the deity was born of the Virgin Mary, suffered, died, and was buried. Nor at that time had those Chemnitzian subterfuges ever been heard in abstract terms: “subjectively” or “not subjectively,” “in itself” or “in another,” “of itself”—that is, “outside the union”—or “in the union,” “by its own power” or “by another’s.” Disguised as it were by these cosmetics, Nestorius, indeed, for dissolving the union, but Eutyches for bringing back a real communication of properties between the natures—that is, confusion—were produced by the actors in the tragedy of ubiquity and appeared again in our age. Concerning concretes, however—the terms Christ, God, and man—the same Damascene speaks thus in the same place: “But when we speak of the hypostasis, whether we name it from both parts together or from only one, we impose upon it the properties of both natures. For Christ—which term embraces both—is called both God and man, both created and uncreated, both passible and impassible; and when from one part he is named Son of God and God, he receives the properties of the conjoined nature, that is, of the flesh. For he is both called the passible God and the crucified Lord of glory, not insofar as he is God, but insofar as the same one is also man.” Therefore he is not said to have suffered according to the divinity, nor does one exclude the divinity who interprets “God suffered” according to the united humanity. In the same way, since there is no distinction in the attribution of properties such as the ubiquitarians imagine—although in the interpenetration the Word holds the preeminence—“when he is called man and Son of Man, he receives the properties and ornaments of the divine essence. For the child is called older than every age and the man without beginning”—why, then, does Andreae not allow him to be denominated eternal, infinite, and uncreated?—“not in respect of his being child and man, but in respect of his being God, who, prior to every age, at the last became a child.” And

[Part I | PDF page 122 | logical source scan 121 | printed page 117]

this is the mode of reciprocal attribution, that is, of mutual or reciprocal giving: each nature in turn grants its properties to the other because of the identity of the person—not, therefore, in abstracts, that is, with respect to the natures themselves—and because of their mutual reciprocity with one another—namely, God and man, not deity and humanity. By this rationale we may say of Christ: “This our God was seen upon earth and conversed with men”; and “This man is uncreated and impassible and free of all circumscription.” Therefore this proposition is not Christian but Eutychian: “Wherever the divinity is, there is the humanity.” Neither is this one Christian: “Christ’s flesh and Christ’s blood are ESSENTIALLY and actually at once on earth and in heaven, or everywhere, or IN, UNDER, or WITH this bread and wine.” But this proposition is true and Christian: “The man Christ is everywhere, insofar as he is one and the same God and man.” The true presence and presentation of Christ’s flesh and blood in the Lord’s Supper depends not even upon the hypostatic union, much less upon a fictitious and merely Eutychian omnipresence, but upon the sacramental conjunction arising from the Lord’s covenant. Just as this tends toward obtaining spiritual life of soul and body in Christ as its ultimate end, so it is directed to the mind of those who approach, and indeed with external signs employed, by which a strengthened faith applies Christ and all his benefits to itself more and more. Thus, just as this bodily life is nourished by bodily food, so the inward man, spiritually refreshed by heavenly food, grows up in Christ until in the heavenly kingdom he wholly enjoys him in full. More than enough has been said about these things, unless I were compelled to say something more by the calumny which Andreae directs on page 295 against that most learned man of blessed memory, Peter Martyr. Andreae took the occasion from Martyr’s response to Brenz. Brenz also established the real and essential omnipresence of Christ’s flesh from the fact that it possesses all power in heaven and on earth for administering all created things; Martyr said that the former did not follow from the latter, since that divine strength by which the hypostasis of the Word sustains and supports his humanity is fully sufficient for exercising that dominion in and through this flesh. But when Brenz drags this most true statement to the hypostatic union itself—as though that great man defined the subsistence of the assumed flesh in the eternal hypostasis of the Son of God by that Nestorian support—who does not see how greatly he wronged so eminent a man? Andreae, on the same page 295, defends on this ground his master’s definition of the hypostatic union by the communication of properties as correct, indeed necessary: because the union cannot be defined *a priori* by the application of specific differences. We have long since proved false the communication of properties which he posits; but I demonstrate that this too was written by him most falsely and ineptly,

[Part I | PDF page 123 | logical source scan 122 | printed page 118]

as follows. It is agreed that union is numbered in the category of action, and therefore its nature must be considered and defined from what it effects in another. It is also agreed that union is a species of conjunction. What prevents us from finding its specific difference as we do those of the other species of conjunction? For the fact that this mystery surpasses the comprehension even of the angels certainly does not arise simply from the nature of the hypostatic union itself, since this mode of union is neither the highest nor the simplest; rather, it proceeds from the dignity and incomprehensible majesty of the person of the Son of God, who in some manner emptied himself in this mystery. Come, therefore, since there are only two such hypostatic unions—one in any human being, the other in Christ, God and man—let us seek a definition common to both by which this conjunction is distinguished from all others, and then see what is proper to each of the two. I therefore think that both can suitably and truly be defined thus: it is a conjunction through which two distinct natures, together with the properties by which they are distinguished from other natures and while remaining in themselves, concur in constituting one actually subsisting person. Thus there is no other conjunction from which this hypostatic conjunction is not distinguished, whether we consider the things conjoined, the mode of conjunction, or the terminus and that which results from the conjunction. These matters have long since been individually explained by most learned writers against the Nestorians, Eutychians, Monothelites, Monophysites, and other thoroughly condemned heretics of this kind. The distinction between the hypostatic union in Christ and that in any human being consists in three things: first, in the difference of the natures united; then, in the somewhat peculiar mode of their union; and finally, in the terminus of the union itself. For two diverse natures, distinct with their properties remaining, concur in constituting a human being, but neither is something perfect and actually subsisting before the union. Neither a human soul nor a human body is by itself a man. Then those natures come together by natural composition, both as parts and as form and matter, to constitute a whole. Third, that one and whole is a third thing actually subsisting, then first created by this union, which we call a man. Thus when the constitution of a human being is treated, three natures are rightly enumerated: one spiritual and another corporeal, though neither is a perfect subsisting entity; and finally a third composed from them, subsisting as another thing in a perfect hypostasis. But in Christ two natures are united: the divine nature, determined to the person of the Son and therefore perfect and actually subsisting; and the human nature, wholly consisting of soul and body, yet neither of these subsisting by itself or in itself. As regards the mode in which the two

[Part I | PDF page 124 | logical source scan 123 | printed page 119]

natures are united, they have no character of parts. For the utterly simple divinity cannot enter into composition with another. Rather, that human nature was united with the hypostasis of the Word by the very fact that this hypostasis created that nature in assuming it and assumed it in creating it, communicating its own subsistence to it. This union is therefore called “according to hypostasis.” No example of it can exist among created things, since each created nature actually subsists in its individuals. Therefore, from this union of the person of the Word who assumes and the nature assumed, no new hypostasis arises, as some human being begins to exist from body united as matter and soul as form, and thus from component parts. Rather, without any composition or concretion, while the hypostasis of the Word always remains utterly simple in itself, that Christ becomes the God-man: one subsisting entity, namely that eternal Son of God, yet in a twofold respect, insofar as after that assumption two natures subsist in that one person. Therefore Christ is called a composite person only in this respect—not by reason of parts, but of number—because those natures are not composed into a third thing. Instead, there is an utterly simple union through the subsistence of the inferior nature in the more principal one. Thus these natures can be called parts only by a certain analogy: for just as diverse parts are united in a whole, so these two natures concur in one suppositum, which is the Son of God. Hence it also follows that the divine nature ought not rashly to be said to have assumed the human nature, on account of the manifold ambiguity which would appear to favor the Sabellians, and likewise those who dreamed that human nature was assumed not in an individual but universally, although not even the individual subsists in its own species. Indeed, not even the deity of the Word can without ambiguity be said to have assumed human nature, a phrase Cyril was compelled to explain. For although the deity of the Word and the Word, like deity and God, are actually one and the same, nevertheless they are not always considered by us in one and the same manner. For when we say “the nature of the Word,” we signify not the hypostasis itself and the divine subsistence, but the essence itself considered in the person of the Word. Rather, the Word is more properly said to have assumed human nature, and consequently to have become man, been born, suffered, and died; but the deity is not said to have become man, been incarnated, or been humanized. Nor can the Word himself properly be said to have assumed a man, but human nature, because it was not a man before it was assumed. “Man,” however, properly declares the human nature in a suppositum. In Christ this suppositum of the human nature is not something outside the hypostasis of the Word, since this soul and body are so united with one another in Christ—otherwise it would not be a true humanity—that from this union there was not constituted

[Part I | PDF page 125 | logical source scan 124 | printed page 120]

To 2 and 3.

a new hypostasis or person; rather, what was assumed came to the preexistent hypostasis of the Word, while—as we said in the common definition of hypostatic union—the natures and their properties and operations always remained preserved and distinct. So much for these matters. From them I think anyone can understand that it is false that the hypostatic union cannot be defined *a priori*, and that a definition taken from the confused outpouring—or from any communication—of all the properties in the natures themselves, distinct from the union of the natures itself, is not only false but also ridiculous no less than impious.

Similitudes are employed not to prove things but to illustrate them. Moreover, since nothing similar is identical, it is agreed that in their proper use one must always regard the aim toward which they are directed, and must never turn aside from it or range beyond it. Let Andreae therefore first prove that real communication of properties between the natures which he posits as distinct from the union of the natures themselves—that is, prove that darkness is light. Let him further prove that by this similitude the orthodox Fathers, defenders of the faith against the Nestorians and Eutychians, wished to affirm the very thing they opposed, and he will have won. This similitude, however, is altogether heterogeneous, presenting an example of the conjunction either of two substances through mixture or the diffusion of one substance into the other, or, as others hold, of an accident with a subject—as heat, but not the very substance of fire, enters even into water. Whichever we establish, an immense distinction appears here. But if that conjunction is so different from Christ’s hypostatic union, how will the effects of the one be compared with those of the other? The Fathers nevertheless used that similitude suitably, but only according to a certain analogy. First, they used it against those who did not acknowledge two natures in Christ’s person, some of whom removed the divine nature and others the human. Then they used it against those whose chief was Nestorius. Just as they separated divinity from humanity, so they separated God the Word from the man, and thus by a twofold error wickedly established necessarily one person and another in Christ—that is, as many persons as natures. For although divinity must never be separated from humanity, it must be distinguished from it, while the union of the person always remains; and God must be considered conjointly with that man even when the person is concretely denominated from only one nature. Yet these same men, whether deceiving themselves or trying to conceal their error, confessed one Christ, but, as we have often said and as Damascene learnedly and clearly explains in book 3, chapter 3, not according to hypostasis but according to composition—that is, not by hypostatic union, but according to a composition which they also craftily called personal. By the name “person” they meant not the hypostasis of the Word itself, underlying both natures in Christ, but

[Part I | PDF page 126 | logical source scan 125 | printed page 121]

self, understanding him to be a man by his own nature and person, but God by name—that is, by habitual worth, whether by dignity, the same concord of will, equality of honor, or the approval and good pleasure of God the Father. By the same rationale by which these men disjoined the natures, they also separated their properties and operations. Others, in the contrary error, whose chief was Eutyches, established just as there was one person of Christ, so one nature from the two natures—not according to hypostasis, as the orthodox did, nor according to composition, as the Nestorians did, but according to mixture or according to blending and confusion: that is, constituted by a certain mass and intermingling. From this it followed that Christ was neither God nor man, but a third thing constituted from deity and humanity thus confounded—which cannot even be conceived without signal blasphemy. And as the confusion of the natures was, so necessarily was the confusion of their properties and operations which they introduced. The orthodox, however, always defended against both parties the true hypostatic union, in which there is established one hypostasis or person of the Word, which, so to speak, supplies the suppositum to both natures thus united; and in which the distinction both of the properties and of the operations inseparable from the natures is preserved, just as is the distinction of the natures themselves, without any conversion, confusion, change, division, or separation. These, then, are the ends toward which the similitude of iron or a burning coal, as adapted by the ancients, must be directed. Damascene says in book 3, chapter 15: “In a fiery sword the natures of the fire and iron are preserved; so too are two actions and their effects. For the iron retains”—and therefore does not actually communicate—“the power of cutting, while the fire retains the power of burning. The cutting is the effect of the iron’s action, but the burning that of the fire. Their difference and distinction are preserved both in the fiery cutting of the iron and in the cut burning, although after the union neither burning occurs without cutting nor cutting without combustion. Nor, because the natural action is twofold, do we therefore speak of two glowing swords. Nor again, because there is only one fiery sword, do we on that account confound their essential difference. In the same way, in Christ the action of the divinity itself is divine and omnipotent, but that of the humanity itself is human and similar to ours.” And Cyril says: “Understand Christ thus. God, united to humanity, preserved it in himself as what it was and himself remained what he was.” Where, then, is that real communication of natural properties and actions, whether of divinity into humanity or humanity into divinity? For the reciprocal union of the natures is one and the same, although the person assuming the nature holds the preeminence. “Yet once united, he is regarded as though already one with it, making its things his own and himself also conferring upon it the operation of his nature”—namely, as Basil speaks in explaining this same similitude, not a Eutychian transference or reciprocal transference of the natures, but

[Part I | PDF page 127 | logical source scan 126 | printed page 122]

a mutual reciprocal imparting of both properties and operations and their effects, of the same kind as the union of the natures itself. Who, then, would not marvel that it could happen that, for confirming this very dogma—both the Nestorian separation of the natures and the Eutychian confusion of properties and operations—that very similitude should be brought forward which was both introduced and explained by the Fathers to overthrow that very thing? But what may not be brought into the Church today amid the great audacity of most teachers and pupils alike?

To 4.

The similitude drawn from the hypostatic union of soul and body for constituting a man, although insofar as it is hypostatic it approaches more closely to that union in Christ, since it is of the same genus, must nevertheless be applied no farther than to those matters of which I have just spoken. For, as we said before, these two unions differ far and wide from one another with respect to the very natures united, the peculiar mode of the union itself, the terminus of the union, and very many other circumstances. If anyone wishes to be instructed more fully in this matter, I would have him read what has been written both by the ancients against this very Eutychian dogma and by very many more recent authors—even those devoted to the Roman Pontiff, but orthodox with us in this matter—against the same errors, which have sprouted again in our age with ignorance and shamelessness yet more assured. In particular, Lambert Daneau, that most learned man, has disputed against Chemnitz with both the greatest brevity and the greatest aptness. Certainly those who strive to prove that real communication of natural properties between the natures themselves, and especially that omnipresence for the proof of which both Nestorius and Eutyches have been summoned back from the underworld, must first of all prove this: just as from the union of soul and body there arises a human person who did not exist before, and consequently one of whose parts, being imperfect, necessarily and without exception requires the aid of the other to perfect its operation, so the same must be established concerning the person of the Word, who supplies subsistence to the two natures in Christ—of which one, namely the divine, is utterly self-sufficient, while the other, although it lacks a personality of its own, nevertheless preserves its distinct properties and operations. Next they must show that, just as body and soul are things finite by their own nature, though not in the same manner, so that one does not extend more widely than the other, so too the divinity is enclosed within the assumed humanity. And even if I grant that those utterly false things can be proved, could it therefore be gathered from this similitude that the body either lives or senses by that very force or power by which the soul has life in itself and apprehends and discerns things presented to the senses? Or, because the intellect reasons from images of objects in the imagination, can the body therefore be called intelligent and even a participant in that very essential faculty of understanding which the mind itself

[Part I | PDF page 128 | logical source scan 127 | printed page 123]

of a man is? Since these things are utterly absurd, utterly false, and ridiculous even to children, what, I ask, must be thought of the communication of the essential properties of deity introduced by the ubiquitarians? Speaking of it, Andreae says on page 260: “We do not teach, nor have we ever taught, any created divinity or omnipotence in the human nature which inheres in the human nature habitually or subjectively; the preface to the Book of Concord also bears witness concerning this. THE BOOK OF CONCORD?” Rather, it is truly a deadly apple of discord, about which, alas, one all-too-truthful poet sang in these words:

Either art failed Vulcan, or the unskilled tongs;
he wished to forge Peace, but forged Strife.

“For we acknowledge,” Andreae says in the same place, “in the person of Christ only one divinity and that eternal omnipotence which Christ has essentially in common with the Father and the Holy Spirit, but personally, truly, and really in common with the assumed human nature.” What do I hear? For if these things are true, what shall become of these words written long ago by Andreae and necessarily pressed by me so many times? “The divinity of the man Christ is not that substantial and eternal essence common with the Father and the Spirit, but one communicated”—namely to the assumed humanity—“by the second hypostasis.” If Andreae has seriously recovered from this impiety, I speak truly: there is reason for me to congratulate him. Yet I could wish he had approached the disputation of such great matters better prepared from some source other than his master Brenz. If not, who will rightly wonder that I cannot assent to a man who dissents so greatly from himself? “But,” he says, “this divinity, although one in number, has a diverse rationale when it is considered of itself, in itself, by its own nature, and when it is considered in the personal union; and therefore Augustine calls it participating and participated.” Yet neither among the ancients nor among orthodox theologians of any age have I ever read that Christ’s deity is considered in Christ’s person either outside the union or in the union itself otherwise than as it is regarded in itself, since the definition of hypostatic union does not allow it to be otherwise. But I will readily grant him that deity—which is properly to be called Godhead—is considered sometimes absolutely, insofar as it is common to those three persons, in which respect it is one and the same, and sometimes relatively and, although without multiplication, nevertheless determinately with respect to the individual persons. I also grant that θεότης and θειότης differ in reality. For the former is one and essential, by reason of which this man is called and is true God, since he subsists not in himself but in God as determined to the person of the Son. By the latter, however—that is, by the name “divinity”—are understood gifts subjectively poured forth most abundantly into that supremely blessed flesh as into our head, so that they overflow into his members. But the usage has long prevailed among Latin writers that, when they speak of Christ, by the name “divinity” they understand nothing other than the very deity of the Word. But Andreae

[Part I | PDF page 129 | logical source scan 128 | printed page 124]

I am compelled here to charge, if not a bad conscience, certainly great heedlessness against him, since I plainly see that he has either never read or read very negligently the ancients from whom these controversies must be learned. For he dares cite Augustine as though he made the essential deity now participating and now participated. Anyone who wishes to examine Augustine on Psalm 119, the letter Heth, and Psalms 121 and 146, as Andreae numbers them, will recognize this falsehood—unless perhaps Andreae, following also the delirium of Osiander, a man of unhappy memory, contends that the essential righteousness of Christ is communicated to believers.

To 5.

What are you to do with this man, Christian reader, who makes “distinctly” declare the same thing as “dividedly and separately”? As though one cleaves the unity of the divine essence who really distinguishes the three persons from one another by their own several relations and preserves that perpetual distinction even in the common essential operation of those three persons toward what is external, as they speak in the schools. Or as though, in order to establish the person of any man, the distinction of the two natures from which he is constituted as from parts, and of both their properties and actions, must be removed. Or finally—as we also press the similitude of glowing iron—as though he denies that iron and fire are conjoined in the iron who denies both that the fire is iron and that the iron is fire, and therefore distinctly attributes cutting to the iron and burning to the fire.

To 6.

Who of sufficiently sound mind can deny that, if the natures themselves remain distinct in the hypostatic union, the properties of the natures likewise remain distinct, and so too their operations? Yet since Andreae grants both antecedents, surely this inference of his—that, if this is posited, the whole person does not seem to act, as though the parts themselves concur to constitute the whole but their properties in the same manner and the distinct actions of those same properties do not concur in the common personal result—needs not refutation but hellebore.

To 7.

Again, in Andreae’s judgment one who distinguishes the natures is a Nestorian, although he himself acknowledges no other unity of the person than the Nestorian, seeing that he champions it as orthodox. But when the distinction of the natures is removed, what communication of operations can be posited other than the Eutychian? And yet Andreae himself opens for himself a way to the truth, if only he can bring himself to return to the road from which he has wandered so far. For if he judges the actions of the individual natures common, not because the action is one—since there must be as many operations as there are natures, and they are as distinct even in the union itself as the natures are distinct—but because they tend toward one end, that is, toward one personal result, as though converging from a fork in the road, this whole controversy between us is settled. But what does this have to do with the real and essential omnipresence of Christ’s flesh and its oral eating?

To 8.

Again Andreae misuses the name “distinction” for “separation”

[Part I | PDF page 130 | logical source scan 129 | printed page 125]

and the refutation of this exceedingly gross error need not be sought any farther. For the humanity cannot be called a merely inanimate instrument, since it possesses its own voluntary will, its own operation, and its own effect. Indeed, not even the human body is an instrument of the soul in such a way that it is not also said, in its own manner, to cooperate with the soul. What more? Even inanimate instruments, which act in no way except through a wholly extrinsic motion or application—as in that similitude of glowing iron—have a certain distinct action of their own. How much more, then, when the divinity acts through the humanity, however personally united to itself—for it would be the part of a Nestorian, not of a Christian, to call this an external instrument, though a proximate one—must there be, just as there are two natures in number and two wills, distinct not merely in number but also most diverse in reality, two actions distinct in number and diverse in reality? Yet when they concur in the one person of the Son of God, and hence in the one Christ, distinctly willing and operating—the divine divinely and the human humanly—thence arises a theandric action: not the Eutychian sort, but the orthodox, which Damascene beautifully explains in book 3, chapter 19 of *The Orthodox Faith*. In describing it, the Fathers said against Eutyches that properties and operations belong to the natures, not to the union; yet against Nestorius, that because of the unity of the person neither nature acts without the association of the other—that is, as separated from the other. But I deny that it follows from this that it is universally true to say of the Word: whatever the Word does, he does through the humanity as a cooperating agent, although he is never separated from it. And that I may deal with this matter once for all, I shall briefly repeat the arguments by which these claims were long ago refuted both by other most learned men and by me as well. First, then, if—as Andreae now speaks, having slipped from that Eutychian outpouring to a Monothelitic communication of properties—the hypostatic union is truly defined by that effect which he fabricates, namely by a real communication of all the properties and actions of the divinity with the human nature, that is, by the fact that the divinity performs all things in that humanity, with that humanity, and through that humanity, this utterly absurd and false conclusion will necessarily follow: that the divinity in Christ was the entelechy of the humanity through this hypostatic union, and consequently that the soul in Christ was not the entelechy of that man furnished with an organic body; or, what is even more absurd, that there were two entelechies of one humanity. This is no less absurd than if you said that one and the same man acts by the power of two essential forms—that is, that he is two men. But if, to escape this rock, Andreae wishes those entelechies to be subordinated, then surely the union will be dissolved by establishing two subsisting entities, because the humanity will not be

[Part I | PDF page 131 | logical source scan 130 | printed page 126]

an instrument of the acting divinity in any different way than the angels and holy men, whose ministry the Lord so uses as instruments that they do not on that account cease to be moved and to act separately by the intrinsic power of their own form. Next I ask this as well: through this hypostatic union, did the Son while dwelling on earth—whose will, action, and operation always was, is, and will be one and the same with the Father and the Holy Spirit, just as the essence and nature of these three persons is one—know, effect, and administer each and every thing in this entire universe through this humanity of his? If Andreae should perhaps affirm this, who does not see him introducing a quaternity, or—what would be even more absurd—bringing in a certain borrowed and derivative yet essential deity, that is, a monster more monstrous than every monster? For essential properties of divinity are at issue here. Otherwise, far be it from us to deny that now, since Christ received all authority in heaven and on earth, all things are governed even by the dominion of his humanity, and consequently that his soul is endowed with ineffable power and knowledge. But this is irrelevant. For that power, knowledge, and dominion too are created gifts and qualities conferred and poured by the deity of the Word into his flesh; here, however, the question concerns those properties which are the very essence of God. Furthermore I ask whether, when that man was still on earth and not yet glorified, the deity, present by its essence to all things and in every place, administered all things through that humanity of his. “Certainly,” Andreae will reply, since in this disputation he dared to say that Christ, through his humanity too—and not by revelation, but by his own essential divine power really communicated to the humanity—was actually a knower of hearts, and that, although Nathanael was absent, he nevertheless saw him under the fig tree through his humanity. But was Christ’s humanity then also under the fig tree by its own essence? If these things are true, what was the whole sacred history—which assigns local motion to Christ and, in various places, a true presence and absence according to the flesh—other than mere deception and the foundation of Marcionite delirium? I ask this too: does Andreae wish to extend that real communication of all the properties of divinity to the whole of the humanity, and the divinity to do whatever it does through the whole of the humanity? For if, yielding to the truth, he denies this, and if his argument holds that humanity is separated from deity unless humanity too is established as essentially present wherever deity is, it will follow on his position that some part of the humanity is separated from deity—namely, that part of the humanity which has no share in that communication. Consequently he absurdly defines the hypostatic union of the natures themselves by this real communication of all the properties, because in that case the union of the na-

[Part I | PDF page 132 | logical source scan 131 | printed page 127]

tures, the thing defined, will extend more widely than the communication of properties, the definition. But if, fearing this rock, he dares to affirm the point, what will he gain? He will fall from Scylla into Charybdis, because it will then necessarily follow that Christ’s body too, and not only his soul, participates in the divine essential omnipotence and omniscience, although no one is so foolish as to attribute understanding and reason to it. Indeed, Christ will then be a knower of hearts with his feet and arms too. For the same question will recur concerning the body: if all the properties of divinity are communicated to the whole body too—since otherwise the whole body would not seem to have been assumed or united—must the same be established concerning the whole of the body, that is, each individual part of it, especially since the Ubiquitarians are accustomed to regard the distinction between the whole Christ and the whole of Christ as a childish thought? Moreover, if the divinity does whatever it does through the humanity as through an instrument, all the actions of the divinity will certainly be composite actions arising from the union of the principal mover and its instrument, just as I might say that the mind sees through the eye and hears through the ears. Yet that action cannot be composite except as united. In Christ, however, two natures are united, while the natures themselves remain distinct, as do both their essential properties and their actions. Therefore one composite action cannot be established in Christ. Rather, just as from these two united natures arises one hypostasis providing subsistence to each nature—in which respect Damascene calls Christ a composite person—so from the actions of the natures that remain distinct, two in number and most different both in their manner of acting and in their peculiar effect, there arises one result of the hypostasis, not one action. What more? Although the Word is never separated from the assumed humanity, and in this sense it is at length said truly, though ambiguously, that he never acts without the humanity—for which the Greeks say more suitably and without ambiguity, “through the human nature”—nevertheless this universal statement is false: “Christ’s divinity does all things THROUGH the humanity.” Both the inseparable external actions of the Trinity, of which we spoke a little earlier, and not a few things which he performed after becoming incarnate while discharging the office of Mediator must be excepted, for in those he did not employ the humanity as an acting instrument. The Word did not become flesh through the flesh as an acting instrument, but by the one power of the Most High, which is itself the coessential power of both Father and Son; nor was the Virgin’s substance employed for this mystery as an instrument, but as matter. Did the Word form Christ’s soul by the agency either of that very soul or of the body? Nor, if he was born from the undefiled Virgin in no different manner than other men, did he effect that through the humanity. He did not fast through the humanity for forty days and as many nights, but through the humanity he

[Part I | PDF page 133 | logical source scan 132 | printed page 128]

hungered. Nor did he see Nathanael under the fig tree through the humanity, for nothing is absent from the deity alone and uniquely. Nor through the humanity did he know men’s naked thoughts, that is, thoughts which in no way revealed themselves outwardly—a thing as proper to deity alone as deity itself is, as Scripture testifies in innumerable places. Rather, through his humanity he revealed whatever he pleased, insofar as and when he willed, both to his own humanity and to his hearers by speaking, and to the prophets by visions. Nor through the humanity did he take up again the life he had laid down, but by his divine power he restored to his humanity the life that had been taken away. Nor does he today teach his church the Father’s will through the instrument of his humanity, but through lawfully called men, by the preaching of the Word and administration of the sacraments, having discharged the ministry of his own flesh once for all while he lived on earth. And when I distinguish these things I do not thereby separate the flesh or the actions of the flesh. Rather, against Eutyches I attribute to each what belongs to it, as the nature and definition of the hypostatic union require; and against Nestorius, just as I affirm two natures, so I affirm essential properties, wills, and operations, while attributing one result to the one individual person. For Christ is one in two natures, one endowed with the essential properties of each of his natures, and one who with two wills, diverse in number and indeed most diverse in nature, wills different things differently—the divine things divinely and the human things humanly. He is also one who acts with no less diverse actions and effects no less diverse things; but without separation against Nestorius, and with the distinction always preserved against that Eutychian communication of the Brenzians, he brings all that diversity of natures, essential properties, wills, and actions to one common result, both in the individual works of teaching and miracles and especially in that work of our redemption, so that neither humanity without divinity nor divinity without humanity is the mediatrix of our salvation. Finally, so that I may at last come to the phrase “with the humanity,” how cautiously it too must be used is to be learned from Cyril’s eighth anathema and his defense of it. But what we said concerning entelechy pertains to that similitude of soul and body. It is astonishing that these men can cite it as received from the Fathers in order to establish the fiction of that real communication between the natures themselves, when the Fathers employed it to overthrow that very thing. For they wished thereby to prove two things proper to the hypostatic union. First, that in it two natures come together so that one subsisting entity—that is, one entity subsisting in act—is constituted from them. We have explained above how great a distinction there is in this matter between those two natures united in Christ’s person and any man composed of soul and body as parts. Second, that in this union of the natures one passes into the other neither by its substance—which our adversaries

[Part I | PDF page 134 | logical source scan 133 | printed page 129]

too concede—nor by its essential properties, although they affirm the latter, formerly employing openly the transparent term “outpouring,” but now concealing it under the ambiguous appellation “real communication.” But with what effrontery? For since, as I have already been compelled to impress a thousand times, the essential properties are inseparable from their natures, how could the natures be communicated without them, or otherwise than they are? And since the reciprocal union of one nature with the other—the deity and humanity in Christ, and soul and body in any man—is one and the same, how could the interpenetration of the essential properties not likewise have to be established as one and the same on each side? Likewise, since these essential properties can no more be separated from each other mutually than from their own natures, how could you except any properties, whether of deity or humanity, from this reciprocal communication? Yet our adversaries openly press a real communication of the properties of divinity with the humanity, though not without excepting certain of them. The communication of the properties of humanity with divinity, however, they formerly asserted boldly enough; but now, having become craftier from Chemnitz, they envelop it in ambiguous and uncertain explanations. But why these evasions? What rule is more certain from the foundations just laid down than this: of any thing of which the essence cannot be predicated, neither can an essential property of that essence be predicated? Therefore, since the body cannot be called the soul, nor the soul the body, it follows that neither can the things proper to the soul be truly predicated of the body, nor those proper to the body of the soul. Similarly, because the divine nature cannot be predicated of the human, neither can the divine properties be predicated of the human nature, nor conversely the human properties of the divine. “Does one nature, then,” they say, “confer nothing upon the other in this hypostatic union?” Certainly it confers very many and very great things. Yet at this rock our adversaries again manifestly shipwreck the orthodox faith by confounding essential properties with their effects. For, as we just said, the essential properties of the natures are as incommunicable to one another even in this union as their natures themselves are. This applies especially in the deity, all of whose essential properties are one in it and are its very essence. Both teachings of our adversaries are therefore ridiculous: after defining the hypostatic union by the communication of all the properties of divinity with the assumed humanity, they are nevertheless later compelled to except certain properties—namely eternity, supreme simplicity, infinity, and any others of this kind—by which they acknowledge that the humanity would be not adorned but destroyed. As though anything could more openly destroy the humanity than that omnipresence of the flesh which they establish. Then

[Part I | PDF page 135 | logical source scan 134 | printed page 130]

there is the utterly vain chimera that they wish something to be attributed to the humanity through this real communication which they invent, even though it does not inhere in the humanity itself as in a subject. The matter, however, stands thus. Although the whole soul is essentially united to the body, nevertheless it by no means communicates to the body, however united, either that essential power of its own by which it lives in itself or those things proper to it—namely an incorporeal, invisible, and immortal substance—or the actions proper to the soul, such as understanding, reasoning, and the like, since the body is not capable of those things. Instead, as long as it remains in its body, it imparts to it life, sensation, and motion, which are created effects. And the saying, “Nothing is in the intellect which was not first in the imagination,” must be understood properly, namely of those things which fall under the senses. Does the intellect borrow from the imagination the common notions, whether those that concern contemplation or those that concern practice; the forms which it abstracts; or the very power by which it understands and judges things impressed on the imagination by the external senses and conveyed from the imagination to itself? Even if the intellect received that power from the imagination, nevertheless, since the imagination is not a faculty of the body but of the soul, and since it receives and forms images of things within itself not by any power of a bodily organ—which is passive rather than active—but by its own power, it certainly could not therefore be said simply or immediately that the soul does through the body whatever it does through the imagination. So too, in the hypostatic union of Christ’s two natures, the divinity which rules in that union not only surpasses the humanity by its dignity, just as in the other species of union the soul surpasses the body, but above all grants its own hypostasis to the assumed nature, not to an assumed person. Yet it no more communicates itself or its properties—which are its very essence—to the united humanity than the humanity communicates itself or its properties to the divinity, as we showed above. It did, however, adorn that assumed humanity forever with ineffable gifts surpassing even the comprehension of the angels, especially after the mystery of our redemption had been completed—yet all of them inhering subjectively, created, and finite. Indeed, even in the course of this mortal life, it employed both its soul and body, as most noble instruments personally united to itself and endowed also with their own natural power, in the greatest works as it saw fit. Such works include miracles and also the sufferings of both soul and body by which he made satisfaction for our sins. Finally, even today it effectually calls, justifies, sanctifies, and in due time will glorify us eternally only through the intervention of that flesh communicated to us spiritually by faith, both in the simple Word and in the administration of the sacraments. Since, however, soul and body constitute one determinate composite—which is the second most excellent effect of hypostatic union—therefore the things said of each of those two substances are also

[Part I | PDF page 136 | logical source scan 135 | printed page 131]

said properly of the whole composite as well, that is, of the man considered as a whole: namely, to be incorporeal and corporeal, visible and invisible, simple and composite, immortal and mortal, and other things of this kind. They are not, however, said in the same way, because they would imply a contradiction, but simply of the substances themselves and in a certain respect of the man, because a man is not simply the soul or simply the body, but body and soul. Hence these things are truly predicated of the whole man, but not according to the whole of him. The same rationale must also be maintained in Christ on account of the common definition of hypostatic union, although in him the two natures are united, not as parts properly composing him, but according to hypostasis to the preexistent divine nature, namely the Word. We therefore rightly and properly call the whole Christ God and man, created and creator, infinite and finite, visible and invisible, once both mortal and immortal, and now immortal under a different rationale, omnipresent and circumscribed within a determinate bodily space, and consequently truly and properly present and absent. But we say these things in a twofold manner, since otherwise they would imply a contradiction. They are said of the whole Christ, therefore, that is, of Christ considered as a whole, but not simply or according to the whole of him; of his natures themselves, however, they are said simply. Yet the distinction between the properties of soul and body which we discussed must be preserved, because Christ is neither only God nor only man, but God and man. Furthermore—and this is the third effect of hypostatic union—certain particular things are said of the whole as one supposit which nevertheless do not agree with the whole of it. Thus a man is called intelligent, mortal, and visible, although the first belongs to the soul and the latter two to the body. So too we say that Christ, which is the appellation of the whole person, suffered, died, was buried, dwells in the saints, is with us, and will be until the consummation of the age. All the orthodox agree that the former things must be understood according to the flesh and the latter according to the spirit and deity. Finally—and this has a place only in Christ, in whom two perfect natures are united in one—through reciprocal exchange, the fourth effect of the hypostatic union in Christ, not only is the whole person designated by a concrete appellation of either nature, such as God or man, but predicates of the other nature are also joined to it. This is true without excepting any property at all, contrary to what our adversaries think. Thus, speaking of Christ, we say that God suffered, died, was crucified and buried, rose on the third day, ascended into heaven, and will come visibly from there, and whatever else is of this sort—things which in no way agree with the deity, yet are most true of God in a certain respect, that is, in the flesh, as Peter speaks. By the same rationale, because the union of deity with humanity and of humanity with deity is one and the same, we truly and properly call that man—not the humanity, with Eutyches—

[Part I | PDF page 137 | logical source scan 136 | printed page 132]

God, and therefore eternal, creator, infinite, omnipotent, omnipresent, and, in short, whatever God is, because this man subsists in the Son of God. When Nestorius refused to concede these things, he plainly betrayed two blasphemous errors, although he craftily acknowledged a person-making union. The first was that he did not distinguish abstract terms from concrete and therefore denied both that God suffered and that the divinity suffered. The second, which followed from the first, was that by manifestly positing in Christ not merely one thing and another but one person and another, since he refused to allow the things belonging to that man to be attributed to God, he actually divided Christ’s person.

Thesis XI of the Württemberg Theologians

Therefore, on account of the personal union and communication of properties, we believe that Christ is omnipotent even according to his humanity, or—what is the same thing—that the human nature in Christ is omnipotent. For sacred Scripture attributes all authority, which is nothing other than omnipotence, to Christ even insofar as he is man. “You have subjected all things under his feet,” says the Psalmist in Psalm 8. Paul, explaining this passage of David, proves from it that ALL THINGS are subjected to Christ except him who subjected all things to him (1 Corinthians 15). John the Evangelist says: “Jesus knew that the Father had given ALL THINGS into his hands” (John 13). Christ himself, even before his passion, says: “All things have been delivered to me by my Father” (Matthew 11); and after his resurrection he says: “All authority has been given to me in heaven and on earth” (Matthew 28). Christ’s miracles too must be referred here as undoubted testimonies of his omnipotence: he cleansed lepers by touch, restored sight to the blind, and recalled Lazarus to life by his voice. Christ performed these miracles not by another’s power, as the prophets and apostles did, but by his own authority.

Antithesis XI of Theodore Beza

All this authority, as is evident from all the testimonies you cite here, is altogether different from that which is essential in God and considered absolutely. It is nothing other than the highest authority under God for administering all created things, to which things it is itself directed. It must therefore be numbered among the gifts conferred upon Christ’s flesh, that is, referred to habitual grace, which you nevertheless excluded in Thesis 4 from this communication of yours. As for the miracles performed by Christ, we follow the universal consensus of the ancient orthodox church, drawn from the analogy of faith, by which the Nestorians, Eutychians, and Monothelites were over-

1. These things are said not only without any testimony of sacred Scripture, but against it. They are therefore false.

2. The authority conferred upon Christ the man must never be numbered among created gifts; the consensus of sacred Scripture and the Fathers is clear.

[Part I | PDF page 138 | logical source scan 137 | printed page 133]

thrown. It will suffice to transcribe these things from those Fathers. From Tertullian against Praxeas: “We see a twofold state, not confused but conjoined in one person, God and the man Jesus; and thus the property of each substance is preserved, so that the spirit, that is, the divinity, performed its own things in him—powers, works, and signs—while the flesh underwent its own sufferings, hungering under the devil and thirsting with the Samaritan woman.” From Cyril, book 5 *On the Trinity*: “The things done by Christ in a divine manner must not be attributed to the human nature, but to the ineffable nature which transcends every mind.” From Nyssen against Eunomius: “Neither does the human nature give life to Lazarus, nor does the impassible power weep for him as he lies there; rather, the tear belongs to the man, while the things of life belong to him who is true Life.” From Damascene, book 3, chapter 15 of *The Orthodox Faith*: “Neither did the human nature raise Lazarus from the dead, nor in turn does the divine power weep. For the tears are something proper to the humanity, but the life belongs to him who is life itself subsisting in himself.”

3. By these statements the orthodox Fathers wished to show to which nature the actions or sufferings in Christ properly belong; but they never dreamed that these were performed distinctly and without communication. Tertullian and Cyril themselves, the best interpreters of their own words, teach that their statements must be understood in this way.

4. Nyssen is the interpreter not so much of his own words as of those of Tertullian and Cyril, showing that they speak of the property of the natures, not of communication, with which it does not conflict. Damascene too openly confirms this. Although raising Lazarus is not proper to the human nature, nevertheless the divine power did not raise Lazarus without the human nature. Likewise, it was not a mere man but God who wept, although weeping is not proper to God.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

To 1 and 2

No Christian is ignorant that this man, insofar as he is God by the grace of union, must be held omnipotent as well as eternal, since the deity of the Word in whom he subsists is true. No one denies, moreover, that this omnipotence of the Word lay hidden to some degree beneath the veil of the flesh for a time, and that, so far as manifestation is concerned, it can in a manner seem then to have been restored to the Word himself, even in respect of his deity, when Christ was exalted in this flesh too above every name after the days of his infirmity had been completed. But it is apparent from the meaning of the Greek word *exousia*, from the names “heaven” and “earth,” and from a comparison of other passages by which that text in Matthew must be explained—Psalm 2; Psalm 8; Daniel 7:14; John 5:22 and 24—that the subject here is not that eternal omnipotence of the Son of God, but the dominion over all created things attributed to the victorious and triumphant flesh itself. In John, the term *krisis*, signifying administration or governance, explains this *exousia*. Likewise in 1 Corinthians 15:27 the apostle in explicit words—not that Christ may be divided, but that human things may be distinguished from divine in o-

[Part I | PDF page 139 | logical source scan 138 | printed page 134]

ne and same person of the Mediator—distinguishes this dominion, eternal indeed in the sense that it has no end, yet created and subjectively attributed to the humanity, from the omnipotence of him who subjected all things to Christ insofar as he is the Son of Man and will come as judge to judge the living and the dead in this very flesh. Finally, since Andreae dares to appeal to the consensus of the Fathers too, I bring these very men forward as judges against him.

To 3 and 4

Andreae strives in vain to cast darkness over the clearest light, as I have no doubt all fair readers will judge. But come, let him sit as judge in his own case, provided that he does not dissent from himself, as he often does. For if the essential properties remain proper to the natures, their proper actions also remain, as he quite correctly says. Whence, then, comes that community of those actions with one another? Nothing remains at once proper and common in first act. Therefore the human nature did not raise Lazarus, nor did the divinity weep. All such things are purely Eutychian. Yet he who is one God and man raised Lazarus and wept, but acting divinely insofar as he is God and humanly insofar as he is man.

Thesis XII of the Württemberg Theologians

We believe that Christ, even according to the human nature now in the state of glory and majesty, knows and has known most perfectly all things which were, which are, and which will be. For John the Baptist testifies that the Spirit was given to Christ not by measure, as to other men, but in such a way that we receive from his fullness (John 3). And Christ in the state of emptying saw the THOUGHTS of the Pharisees. Paul, speaking of Christ, says: “In whom ALL THE TREASURES of wisdom and knowledge are hidden” (Colossians 2). Christ must know ALL THINGS, since he will be the judge of the living and the dead (John 5). His ignorance before his passion of the day of judgment must be referred not simply to his humanity, but to the form of a servant which he laid aside.

Antithesis XII of Theodore Beza

Whatever Christ’s knowledge was in the time of his humility, and whatever it now is in glory insofar as he is man, it was and is a created gift, subjectively implanted in Christ’s soul, and therefore has no relation to the communication which you posit. Far be it from us to teach that Christ laid aside or will ever lay aside the form of a servant, that is, the humanity, since he transferred it from humility to that eminent glory above even the angels themselves and placed it above all the heavens.

1. These things are said against the express testimonies of Scripture: “In him are hidden all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge” (Colossians 2).

2. The form of a servant is not the humanity itself, but the servile condition of the man Christ. He is Lord, John 13: “You call me Lord, and you do rightly, for I am.”

[Part I | PDF page 140 | logical source scan 139 | printed page 135]

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

To 1

Certainly all the fullness of deity dwells in Christ, and indeed bodily, that is, essentially, since he is true God; but with respect to his essence, according to which he is consubstantial with the Father and is the very wisdom of the Father, who doubts that every treasure of wisdom and understanding is truly hidden in him? Then, since this wisdom has joined that flesh so closely to itself as to bestow its very own subsistence upon it, that man is certainly omniscient with eternal omniscience insofar as he is God, but not insofar as he is a man endowed with humanity. If Andreae does not acknowledge that Christ’s soul was most abundantly illumined by the created light of all human knowledge and understanding, since on page 312 he explicitly denies that “the knowledge of the man Christ is a created gift,” he will either be a manifest Arian and Apollinarian, thinking that divinity took the place of mind in this flesh; or he will have attributed less to the latter Adam than to the former, who was created in God’s image and whose chief endowment was that true yet created wisdom. Finally, if it is true, as Andreae often impressed in the disputation itself, that by the name of the Spirit with whom Christ is said to have been anointed, no created gift but the very deity of the Word is understood, why is it added in Psalm 45:8, “above your companions”? For he has companions here only in the flesh and in its anointing, not as he is the only-begotten and sole God-man. Therefore this comparison must be understood chiefly of created gifts subjectively infused into Christ’s flesh insofar as he is the firstborn among many brothers, though without excluding the deity, since in the preceding part of the same verse he is explicitly called *Elohim*. Thus in John 3:34 too the appellation “Spirit” is taken for created gifts as well. Their fullness—certainly not gifts of a different species—overflows from the head, who received that Spirit without measure, into the members subjected to him, according to the measure that pleased the Holy Spirit (1 Corinthians 12:11; Ephesians 4:16). But against this position Andreae sets in the disputation what is almost the only syllogism I happened to hear from him in this entire debate: “Whatever lacks measure is immeasurable and infinite. The Spirit which Christ received according to the man was given without measure. Therefore his knowledge is infinite and unmeasured.” Here I appeal to all logicians whether the conclusion answers to the assumption. But I readily pardon this in a man who is no logician at all. Nor do I deny that by the appellation “Spirit” is principally understood the most abundant knowledge of God and divine things, such as not even the angels ever obtained or will obtain. In answer to the major premise, however, I say first that “what is received according to measure” is opposed not only to the infinite but also to that which is imperfect and does not fully attain the complete and finite measure which it possesses; in this latter sense I deny the minor premise. And, to answer without syllogisms, of which

[Part I | PDF page 141 | logical source scan 140 | printed page 136]

Andreae is an enemy, I answer that John does not say a Spirit without measure—that is, the essential infinite God—was given to Christ’s human nature, for this is how Andreae understands it; rather, the Spirit who was given to him was given without measure. Thus the Spirit who gives is that third person of the Trinity, by whose power Christ is also said to have been conceived in the Virgin’s womb. He is the same one who appeared above Christ’s head in the form of a dove when he was baptized by John, namely that coessential and enhypostatic power of both Father and Son. But the Spirit who is given signifies not the very essence of the Holy Spirit, but his operations and gifts. He is said to have been given to Christ as man, or according to the flesh, by the Father and by himself, without measure—that is, so that the gifts which were given and effected by the Holy Spirit in this humanity were conferred upon Christ’s humanity whole, full, and entire: both because there is no kind of gift with which the Holy Spirit adorns the saints of which Christ’s humanity was devoid, and because nothing was lacking in those gifts themselves. Nevertheless, all those graces, whether considered together or individually, were necessarily finite, since they were conferred subjectively upon a finite nature. By contrast, the Holy Spirit grants even to the holiest men only as many gifts, drawn from Christ himself as from the richest treasury, as he pleases and in the measure he pleases, as Christ testifies in John 16:15 and the apostle declares most fully in 1 Corinthians 12:11 and Ephesians 4:11. We need seek no better interpreter of this passage than Isaiah 11:2. Chrysostom too rightly admonishes on this passage that the name “Spirit” must be understood of his operation, not his person. What Cyril writes copiously on that passage against the Arians does nothing against this interpretation. It is directed against those who thought that these gifts were conferred upon Christ from without just as upon the saints. On the contrary, Christ himself, by his deity—whose essential and enhypostatic power, as also that of the Father, is the Holy Spirit himself—poured them into the assumed humanity as and when he pleased. Although John, as Christ himself also usually does, recalls the hearers’ minds to the person of the Father as the very principle and fountain of deity, he does not thereby separate himself from either Father or Holy Spirit, nor detract from himself what he attributes to the Father or Holy Spirit, since he is one and the same God with them. But how do these things pertain to that Eutychian communication of essential properties between the natures themselves? Yet what are you to do with this man, who openly and explicitly on page 299 denies that “the power given to Christ’s human nature is a created gift or a created quality”? So that we may also see how beautifully the authors of these dogmas agree among themselves, let the Christian reader hear what Brenz thought about God’s power operating in Peter and Christ on pages 40 and 41 of his *Recognition*: “Christ performed

[Part I | PDF page 142 | logical source scan 141 | printed page 137]

many miracles, and Peter performed them too. Christ saw future things, and Peter saw them too. Christ died and was buried, rose from the dead, and ascended into heaven; Peter too will rise from the dead and ascend into heaven, and indeed with his body reduced to dust, so that the event in Peter seems almost more marvelous than this one in Christ. You say, ‘Christ rose from the dead by his own power, but Peter by another’s.’ No: just as Christ was raised by divine power, so Peter too will rise by divine power, no less intimately present to him than to Christ, for wherever deity is, it is there whole. Therefore not even by this rationale can it be said that Christ is God but Peter is not.” And at last, drawing his conclusion, he says “the distinction between Christ and Peter must be established in this”—or, as Andreae writes even more expressly, “this is the sole distinction between Christ and Peter in the indwelling of the Son of God: although the Son of God fills Peter by his essence just as he does the man Christ, he nevertheless does not communicate ALL his properties to Peter, but only SOME.” But who can reconcile these claims with what Andreae now writes, namely that no knowledge and no power given to Christ’s human nature can be proved from sacred Scripture to be created qualities or created gifts? Someone will object, however, that Andreae writes the contrary on page 197 of this disputation in these words: “The distinction between the union of divinity with Peter and with the assumed man is perceived from the fact that Christ the man raised the dead and performed miracles by his own power, but Peter by another’s.” Who here would not believe that Andreae, although in deference to his master he tries to persuade the reader that the ancient position of his master and himself was never anything but orthodox, has nevertheless quite openly retracted that plainly impious dogma? And he certainly ought to have done so and professed it publicly. But that saying, unfamiliar to no one who truly seeks God’s glory—“I have sinned”—has long since been expunged from the tablets of all too many men of Reformed theology. What, then, does Andreae mean in sum when he confesses that Christ performed miracles by his own power and Peter by another’s, which Brenz had denied? He certainly does not refer this, as he ought, to the union of the natures itself: namely, that Peter is not one subsisting entity with the Son of God and therefore is a mere man, not God; whereas the man Christ, although he is a true man, is nevertheless not an individual of the human species, because in that case Christ would be divided into two persons, but is supposited in the subsistence of the Son of God—a point which Nestorius either never understood or never wished to understand, blinded by God’s just judgment. Rather, according to Andreae, Christ’s humanity is said to act by its own power because it has this power really communicated to itself, though not subjectively, in the Word; Peter acts by another’s because he must borrow it from Christ by prayers. Hence comes that Nestorian definition of the hypostatic union of the natures by the Eutychian real communication of the natures, and the explanation of this definition through the natures’

[Part I | PDF page 143 | logical source scan 142 | printed page 138]

false and impious equation.

To 2

I do not know whether there is any passage in Paul’s epistles which all the Fathers, both Greek and Latin, pressed more strongly against all the heresies by which Christ’s person was assailed. With the sole exception of Erasmus and those who now follow him in his wandering and twist this passage elsewhere, I think no more recent writer exists who has not embraced the ancient Fathers’ interpretation without controversy. Yet if we believe Brenz and his followers, the passage by no means concerns two forms, that is, two natures—the divine nature in the person of the Son assuming the human nature according to hypostasis—but one humanity which temporarily possessed two forms. One was the ruling form, through which, from the moment of its assumption into God, as they are accustomed to speak, all the essential properties of divinity were actually communicated to it as the effect of that hypostatic union. The other was the servile form, embracing all those infirmities of the flesh down to death on the cross. With respect to this form that humanity will be said to have emptied itself insofar and as long as it did not exercise that omnipotent majesty of its own during the days of its flesh. With all the orthodox interpreters, I indeed acknowledge what the apostle’s own purpose indicates: neither by “form of God” is understood the bare deity of the Son—or rather, his person—nor by “form of man” the bare humanity which he assumed, but the former as adorned with ineffable glory and the latter as joined with the most abject humiliation. From this there is a twofold foundation for Paul’s argument, proposed both to subdue all arrogance and to commend Christian humility to us. These are so connected that the latter cannot stand unless the former is laid beneath it: namely, that Christ, to whose example we must be conformed, endured becoming man when he was God. And what God? True God, equal to his supreme Father. And what man? One so abased that on the cross he even became a curse for us. Supreme exaltation followed this obedience: the glory of the Son’s deity was restored—although he had never actually lost it, it could seem in some way to have been suspended for a time—while the humanity received dominion over all things. Thus to be in the form of God is nothing other than to be such as God is, that is, the very thing which God is. And since the one discussed here is said not to have sought to become equal by robbery, as Satan desires and Antichrist arrogates to himself above God himself, but to have existed equal, and consequently by the right of his own nature, Sabellius is thereby slain, because equality must be established between at least two persons. So too Paul of Samosata is condemned, because one who is God only in thought or designation is not equal to God. Arius is likewise refuted, because one who has a beginning in time and is God equivocally is not equal to God. So too is

[Part I | PDF page 144 | logical source scan 143 | printed page 139]

Nestorius refuted, since this one is said to have assumed not some other person, but another form. And Eutyches is refuted, since he is said neither to have stripped himself of the former form nor to have exchanged it for the other. Finally, Marcion and all the Docetists are thus refuted for abusing the term “form,” since if the equality is real, the things compared with each other are also real and truly subsistent, while an obedience whose subject was merely imaginary would itself be fictitious. This, then, is the true and precious explanation of this passage handed down from the pure Word of God by all the Greek and Latin Fathers with the highest and perpetual consensus. Finally, if Andreae wishes to agree with Luther in explaining this passage, just as in other matters, here are Luther’s own words which I brought forward against that Nicholas Selnecker eleven years ago: “You plainly err,” he says, “often attributing to a nature what belongs to the person, and conversely, as can be useful to someone in that passage of Paul in Philippians 2 where he says, ‘although he was in the form of God,’ and so forth. For what is predicated as having been in the form of God certainly indicates divinity, as does the statement that he emptied himself. Furthermore, what else do the emptying and the form of a servant attest than humanity?” The Brenzians must therefore now depart even from Luther himself. Come, however, let us examine their position. For them, the form of God signifies not the very deity of the Son by whom our flesh was assumed according to hypostasis, but that real communication of all the essential properties of divinity which they imagine. This communication is an effect of the union of the natures, yet by no means inheres in the humanity itself. By this invention, as we have often impressed already, the hypostatic union of the natures, which is necessarily one and the same, is torn into two different things; its definition is overturned; and abstract terms are confounded with concrete. But see, Christian reader, by how many reasons this utterly false interpretation is refuted from Paul’s own context. First, about whom is the passage speaking? Certainly the person of Jesus Christ. The apostle’s entire statement must therefore be referred to this person, not merely to one of his natures—not even the deity, much less the humanity, which certainly is not the whole Christ, but only something, and indeed the least part, of Christ. And lest it perhaps be objected to us here that things which agree with only one of Christ’s natures are sometimes attributed to Christ, I answer that the passage plainly concerns the whole Christ and indeed considers him distinctly. This appears from the fact that the apostle mentions both death on the cross, which belongs to the humanity, and this one’s equality with God, namely the Father, which, as we shall soon say, no one can attribute without blasphemy to Christ’s humanity, however highly it was eventually exalted above all created things. Next, since our adversaries, having at last become more cunning through Chemnitz, refuse to have that form of God inhere in the humanity as in a subject, where will they place it? Certainly either nowhere, in which case this form will vanish into nothing, or in the Son’s very deity, in which nothing truly inheres formally.

[Part I | PDF page 145 | logical source scan 144 | printed page 140]
