They will therefore be compelled to acknowledge with us what they nevertheless deny: that by the form of God the very person of the Word is understood. Since they themselves derive that communication of theirs from the union of this person with our nature, how is it that in explaining this passage they do not rise all the way to that union? And since, I suppose, not even they dare to say that in this union the humanity became deity, either essentially—which would introduce a quaternity—or personally—which would plainly be Eutychian, since nothing more absurd can be said than that a part of a composite became the composite itself—who does not see in what snares they entangle themselves by this interpretation? Furthermore, what will become of the apostle’s verb “to subsist”? It certainly cannot be attributed to the humanity with respect to a form which either does not subsist in act of itself or does not actually inhere in it. But Christ’s humanity does not even subsist of itself, much less can two hypostases be attributed to it, which is in itself exceedingly ridiculous. If this form of God is a quality, however, then it inheres in the humanity; and if that man is established as God on account of this quality, he will be God accidentally. But what is this passion for quarreling, especially among theologians? Who speaks so absurdly as to say that abstract terms, such as “humanity,” subsist in a form, when it is rather the forms themselves, abstracted in the mind’s conception from their individuals, which are signified in this way? But if these men mean that the apostle calls not the humanity but the man endowed with that form of God which they invent, and afterward as having assumed a servile form, then they must define for us that interval of preceding or prior time. But how will they define it, since that servile form began with the very coagulation of the body from the Virgin’s substance and with this man’s most humble entrance into this life, when he was laid in the manger of a most abject stable and even then began to pay for our sins? How, then, did this humanity fall from the form of God to the form of a servant, when it began simultaneously both to exist and to be a servant? And if it was born a servant, how was it equal to God without robbery? Likewise, if this man must be regarded as God on account of that form of God, as he certainly must, although not in the sense these men intend, did he not cease to be God when he assumed the servile form—unless perhaps we judge with Marcion that this form was imaginary and consequently that we were saved by an imaginary death on the cross? For in actual fact he could not possess simultaneously the two forms which these men invent, since, as sense itself testifies and as Damascene rightly says while discussing this very matter in book 3, chapter 3, “no nature receives contrary differences.” Nor will that Chemnitzian refuge, “not subjectively,” help the adversaries here at all. For if this man, or this humanity—since Andreae wishes both expressions to be concrete—existed in the form of God before it assumed the form of a servant, although that form did not inhere in it as a subject, in what manner did it exist? What else could it have been than an unheard-of monster devoid of every

[Part I | PDF page 146 | logical source scan 145 | printed page 141]

form? Yet what need is there of any inquiry so perplexed? The one about whom the passage speaks must have existed equal to God without robbery. But there can be no question of the equality of the man or of the humanity unless the things said to be equal to one another are considered distinctly in themselves, not as one whole. Therefore, if this man can truly—that is, without falsehood—be called equal to God, he must lack nothing pertaining to that equality, nor may anything be present from which inequality would follow. Yet the very nature of the case shows that this can in no way occur except in those three divine persons, since they are one in essence in a far different manner from that in which the individuals of the human species are one in species, and, being free of every composition, they differ only in the mode of relation (*τῆς σχέσεως τρόπῳ*). Grant, then, that the real effusion of the essential properties of deity into the assumed nature, as the Brenzians wrote at first, or their communication with the assumed humanity, as they now prefer to speak in accordance with Chemnitz’s subtlety, is true and is distinct from the union of the natures. Grant further that this communication is rightly defined by one theandric (*θεανδρικῇ*) and Monothelite action, namely, that the Word (*ὁ λόγος*) does nothing except through the assumed humanity. Grant also what Andreae now blushes to acknowledge on page 271: that absolutely no essential properties are excluded from this communication. Will this humanity therefore have been equal to the deity, or this man here to the Word (*τῷ λόγῳ*)? Whence, then, came this statement written by Brenz, pressed by Andreae with intolerable disgrace to the Christian name, and already cited several times by me: “We make Christ’s humanity equal to the divinity, not in essence (*οὐσίᾳ*) but in authority (*ἐξουσίᾳ*); not in essence but in power; not in nature but in glory; not in substance but in majesty”? Whence, then, did those who are accustomed to accuse us of glosses draw this gloss of an unequal equality? Certainly not from the apostle’s words, for in the most explicit words he defines the form of God as equality with God—if not from robbery, certainly from nature. There is no room here for any intermediate grace, because there can be no borrowed deity, since deity would thereby necessarily be multiplied; from this error polytheism (*πολυθεΐα*) arose. And if any such deity could exist, it would nevertheless not be equal to deity by nature. Therefore, Christian reader, whoever you are who read these things, I beseech you: do you not marvel that matters could have fallen so far in so few years that, in those places where it pleased the Lord in our own memory to kindle the light of his truth so that it might shine before the other nations, dogmas so absurd, inept, gross, and ridiculous could have been heard patiently, much less defended so stubbornly, while men vie with one another to seek supports for the single error of consubstantiation? You surely live with the Lord, you live—as I am persuaded, and as is most probable—Doctor Luther, otherwise a valiant and successful restorer of evangelical truth in our times, and altogether a second Gideon. But would that you had been unlike him in this: you left an ephod in Germany, which has been made and

[Part I | PDF page 147 | logical source scan 146 | printed page 142]

still remains a snare to far too many.

Thesis XIII of the Württemberg Theologians

Sacred Scripture clearly testifies that Christ is everywhere present, even according to his humanity, to all creatures, but especially to his church and his elect. “Where,” Christ says, “two or three are gathered in my name, there I am in the midst of them” (Matthew 18). And elsewhere he says: “Behold, I am with you every day, even to the consummation of the age” (Matthew 28). In these sayings there is no need to tear the human nature away from the divine, because not even death itself could divide the person of Christ. Paul writes of the ascension of the man Christ into heaven: “He ascended above all the heavens, THAT HE MIGHT FILL ALL THINGS” (Ephesians 4). And in the same epistle he testifies of Christ the man that he fills all things in all (Ephesians 1). According to the phraseology of Scripture, to fill all things means to be present to all things in a heavenly manner. “I fill heaven and earth,” says the Lord (Jeremiah 23).

XIV

The mode of this presence is not expressed in sacred Scripture. Therefore, we believe THAT Christ is truly present to all creatures—not locally and physically, indeed, but supernaturally. HOW this occurs, however, we gladly acknowledge in true and pious humility that we do not know.

Antithesis XIII and XIV of Theodore Beza

We judge omnipresence differently from the preceding two as well, that is, from power and knowledge, with which we acknowledge that the flesh assumed by the Word (*λόγῳ*) was most abundantly and ineffably endowed and adorned. But this omnipresence, which necessarily presupposes an infinite essence and is altogether proper to deity alone in the fourth mode, as they say, we can in no way acknowledge in Christ’s humanity, however hypostatically united and now glorified, since it nevertheless retains its circumscription. In short, we distinguish completely the supernatural things (*ὑπερφυσικὰ*), which we acknowledge and with which Christ’s humanity is supremely adorned, from things contrary to nature (*ἀντιφυσικοῖς*), by which the humanity is abolished. The testimonies you bring forward here are referred partly to the sole deity of the Word (*τοῦ λόγου*)—though not separated from the humanity, nevertheless distinct from it—and partly concern his power, as head, exerting itself in his members.

1. Absolute omnipresence presupposes an infinite essence. But for participated omnipresence it is enough that the nature which has been made a participant in it is united with the infinite nature, by participation in which it has what the infinite nature is by essence.

2. It is not enough to say that it can in no way occur; it must be proved. For sacred Scripture openly testifies to the contrary (Ephesians 1:4).

3. Omnipotence, omniscience, and omnipresence are not things opposed (*ἀντικείμενα*) to the human nature, but things set above it (*ὑπερκείμενα*), not against it. Therefore they do not overturn it either.

4. Christ’s words are plain: he interprets himself and attributes to the FLESH that power which was communicated to it by the Word (*λόγῳ*) and which is derived from Christ as head.

[Part I | PDF page 148 | logical source scan 147 | printed page 143]

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

To 1, 2, and 4

Andreae has never proved that there are two modes of really possessing the essential properties of deity, or even of any created nature, any more than he has proved that individual natures—not even excepting the divine nature, though we speak of it in this way according to our capacity—are not distinguished by properties peculiar to them, or that a nature to which another’s essential properties are truly and actually attributed is not changed. But if thinking otherwise is to wander altogether from the truth both before God and by human judgment, what must be thought of one who contends that the human nature really becomes a participant in the properties of deity, though it does not possess them in itself, and that this occurs—what is still more monstrous—by the power of that hypostatic union whose definition nevertheless expressly excludes every such communication between the natures? This, therefore, is not to answer the objection but to perform a conjuring trick. For whom will Andreae persuade either that real omnipresence does not presuppose an infinite and uncircumscribed essence, or that a property in the fourth mode can actually be common to two utterly diverse natures?

As for the passages of Scripture cited by Andreae, I certainly confess that Christ is in the midst of the assembly of his own and is and will be present to his church even to the end of the age. But if Andreae thinks that whatever is said of Christ must be attributed to each of his natures, and dreams that Christ’s person is separated unless this is done, then let Christ still be in the tomb; let him not have ascended or gone away to the Father, but instead have deceived the disciples’ eyes. Let him not truly have said, “You will not always have me,” and, “The Bridegroom will be taken away from them.” In short, let him not have traveled throughout all Judea by going and returning, nor be truly about to return from heaven, since he fills the lands too with his body. And let him be not merely in, under, or with the bread, but also among the dead. Finally, what Christian can endure these omn-majestatic monsters any longer? Away, then, with these follies. Rather than serve his readers this cabbage reheated so many times, let him at least consider what was brought forward against him so many years ago from the Fathers themselves concerning these very passages, which he wrongly twisted to the humanity’s presence in first act. Of the two passages Andreae adduces, he ought to have remembered, if he wished to understand from the one in Ephesians 4—where Christ is said to have ascended into heaven that he might fill all things—how ineptly he argues, that it belongs to Sophists, not logicians, to assume more in the conclusion than in the premises. For what, I ask, is this inference? Christ ascended that he might fill all things; therefore he fills all things with his body? Thus the claim does not even need refutation, though these men are accustomed to chant it as an invincible argument. As if what the apostle immediately adds about gifts conferred upon men and the evangelical ministry to be carried out throughout the world did not most plainly indicate to what this filling of all things refers. The oth-

[Part I | PDF page 149 | logical source scan 148 | printed page 144]

er passage, however, comes from chapter 1 of the same epistle, where Christ as head is said to fill all things in all. Since this plainly refers to Christ’s Spirit supplying every spiritual sense and motion to his whole mystical body, namely the church, with what shamelessness, I ask, does Andreae transfer it to a real omnipotence of the flesh? And how does Andreae dare accommodate to that impious monster of his—an omnipresent humanity—what God claims for himself as proper to his one divine essence in Jeremiah 23 when he says, “I fill heaven and earth”?

To 3

We call things contrary to nature (*ἀντιφυσικὰ*) those which are contradictory to natural things (*φυσικοῖς ἀντιφατικά*). Certainly, to be powerful in some degree and to be supremely powerful, and likewise to know in some degree and to be omniscient, are not contradictory things (*ἀντιφατικὰ*) if they are referred to the same kind of knowledge and power, but differ according to more and less. Andreae, however, contends that Christ’s humanity is really omnipotent and omniscient—not by a created capacity for power, and consequently a derivative and finite one; nor by a likewise-created gift of knowledge; nor by knowledge suggested from elsewhere or arising from human reasoning; but by that very essential omnipotence and omniscience which is God himself. I say that these are just as much negative opposites (*ἀποφατικὰ*), and therefore contrary to nature, as being eternal or from oneself conflicts with being created; infinite with finite; most simple with composite; and, finally, being substance with being accident. As for the omnipresence of essence, Andreae will prove it not to be contrary to bodily nature when he has shown that being circumscribed, palpable, visible, and composed of organic parts does not diametrically conflict with an uncircumscribed, impalpable, invisible, and simple nature, of which sort even the nature of angels and human souls is. That is, he will prove it when he has convicted Jesus Christ himself of falsehood, since Christ would not have proved the reality of his body raised from the dead by sufficiently firm arguments if being palpable and visible are not properties of bodily nature in the fourth mode and inseparably. How much less, then, can that which is so proper to the divine nature that it cannot be attributed even to any blessed spirits—namely, that because its nature is most simple and truly and properly infinite in every way, it is therefore essentially everywhere omnipresent—be attributed, under the name of omnipresence, to the human nature, however glorified and exalted above created things, without blasphemy and the destruction of that very nature?

Thesis XV of the Württemberg Theologians

Indeed, with pious antiquity we also believe that Christ’s flesh is truly life-giving; antiquity even confirmed the resurrection of the dead from the life-giving reception of Christ’s flesh. For Christ says: “I am the bread of life. Your fathers ate manna in the wilderness and died. This is the bread which comes down from heaven, so that if anyone eats of it, he may not die. I am the living bread who came down from heaven. If anyone eats of this bread,

[Part I | PDF page 150 | logical source scan 149 | printed page 145]

he will live forever. And the bread which I will give is my flesh for the life of the world,” and so forth.

Antithesis XV of Theodore Beza

As for giving life, we do not deny that Christ’s flesh is life-giving, but not in the sense under discussion here. It is life-giving, yet not by that power which is proper to deity and altogether incommunicable (*ἀκοινώνητος*). First, it is life-giving because in this flesh Christ abolished death for us, having fulfilled all those things required to acquire eternal life for us. Second, it is so because only through the mediation of this flesh, spiritually communicated to us through faith, do we obtain that eternal life from Christ, God and man. The testimonies of Scripture which you adduce must be applied to this.

1. The entire chorus of orthodox Fathers confirms that Christ’s flesh is life-giving BY A POWER PROPER TO DEITY. Therefore, what is asserted in this response is openly false.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

To 1

If anything is proper to deity, it is that by which he makes all things which exist to exist, since he alone is Jehovah. Therefore it also belongs to him alone to restore life to the dead, and much more to bestow eternal life. The communion (*κοινωνία*), that is, union (*ἕνωσις*), is altogether reciprocal (*κατάλληλος*) in the case of the natures, and likewise of the properties, wills, operations (*ἐνεργειῶν*), and results (*ἐνεργημάτων*). Therefore, just as the natures remain distinct in the hypostatic union, so too do all the things we have mentioned. If Andreae or anyone else denies this, there is no reason to dispute any further against them. Thus, just as it is true that the result (*ἀποτέλεσμα*) belongs to the whole supposit, namely Christ himself; and just as it is true that, although both the natures, the wills, and the actions in Christ are distinct and remain so, nevertheless the one does not act without communion with the other (*θατέρου κοινωνία*), that is, separated from the other—both because of the unity of the person considered as a whole (*ὅλως*) and because the human nature served the Word (*τῷ λόγῳ*) in human actions insofar as it pleased him—so it is false and blasphemous to make these attributions either to a Human Deity or to a Divine Humanity, evidently abstract expressions, whether in the natures, the wills, or the operations. And since Andreae was not ashamed here to vaunt the consensus of the Fathers, I am compelled to set before him here those things already brought forward so many times against him. Thus Damascene writes concerning this matter in book 3, chapter 15 of *The Orthodox Faith*: “In Jesus Christ, the power of miracles was the action of his deity; but the employment of the hands in performing miracles, and willing and saying, ‘I will; be cleansed,’ was the action of his humanity. The effect of that action on the part of the human nature was the breaking of the loaves and the leper’s hearing, ‘I will’; on the part of the divine nature, the multiplication of the loaves and the cleansing of the leper.” Likewise: “In Christ, the action of his deity is divine and omnipotent, while that of his humanity is what agrees with our nature.” (Where, then, is Andreae’s

[Part I | PDF page 151 | logical source scan 150 | printed page 146]

one omnipotence and one omnipotent action, really common both to the deity as the principal agent and to the humanity as the instrumental cause?) “The effect belonging to the human nature is that the girl’s hand was grasped and drawn up; the effect belonging to the divine nature is that the girl was recalled to life.” Likewise: “Neither did the human nature raise Lazarus from the dead, nor, conversely, does divine power shed tears, for tears are something proper to humanity, whereas life belongs to him who is life itself subsisting in himself.” And Nyssenus: “Neither does the human nature give life to Lazarus”—let Andreae hear this—“nor does impassible power weep over him as he lies dead; rather, the tear is proper to the man” (that is, to Christ insofar as he is man), “whereas life” (that is, the life of deity) “belongs to the things which are of true life. Human poverty does not feed many thousands of men; omnipotent power does not run to the fig tree.” And Amphilochius of Iconium, a contemporary (*σύγχρονος*) of Basil: “I am God and man, O heretic: God, as the miracles attest; man, as the affections demonstrate.” And Apollinaris: “It belongs to the man” (that is, to Christ insofar as he is man) “to rise from the dead, but to God” (that is, to Christ insofar as he is God) “to raise from the dead.” And Cyril: “The things done by Christ in a divine manner are not to be attributed to the human nature (*φύσει*), but to the ineffable nature which transcends every mind.” Therefore, the one who distinguishes, even within the union itself, the natures and likewise the wills, operations, and the individual results of the operations (*ἐνεργειῶν ἐνεργήματα*) is not a Nestorian. Rather, the one who attributes either divine things to the humanity—whether in itself or “in the Word,” as these men are now accustomed to speak—or human things to the deity is purely Eutychian. Nor does this distinction deny that the actions belong to the supposit itself, since each nature, willing and acting distinctly, subsists in one and the same person. Damascene says: “Although, from that sublime conception onward, not even the smallest part of the one form was separated from the other, and although the actions of each form belonged at all times to one person, nevertheless we in no way confound the things which occurred inseparably; rather, from the very quality of the works we understand what belonged to which form.” Andreae should therefore consider why, in refutation 7 against our tenth Antithesis, he calls it the grossest Nestorianism that we said the Word (*τὸν λόγον*) acts distinctly in what belongs to the Word (*τῷ λόγῳ*); for, he says, there are two distinct wills in Christ and two distinct operations (*ἐνέργειαι*). This is certainly true, and consequently the humanity will no more be omnipotent in the deity than the humanity will have been deity itself. Nor will the humanity operate in the deity; rather, it must be said that the divinity acts in the humanity as the principal agent through an instrument personally united to it. What, then, will this omnipotence be which is really communicated to the humanity itself—though “not subjectively”—and which Andreae wishes to be distinct from the union as the effect of that union as its cause? And what will be the effect of that omnipotence if it lies idle in the humanity itself? Indeed, what if even the deity itself does not act except through the humanity? But now let us hear the supposed Nestorianism. “The actions,” he says, “are common, directed toward one end.” By “actions,” I suppose, he calls the results of the operations (*ἐνεργειῶν ἐνεργήματα*),

[Part I | PDF page 152 | logical source scan 151 | printed page 147]

although shortly before he acknowledged that the operations themselves, like the wills, are distinct. Yet what could be more inept than, while the faculties of acting remain distinct, to make their results (*ἐνεργήματα*) common? For whatever remains distinct in each of the two natures also remains proper to that nature; consequently, it cannot be common even in thought—unless the thought is false—much less in actual fact. To make the results (*ἐνεργήματα*) common because they tend toward one end would be not merely false but ridiculous, no less than if you were to call the sources and channels of two utterly different rivers common because they at length constitute some third river. Therefore, as many as there are distinct operations (*ἐνέργειαι*), so many are there distinct results (*ἐνεργήματα*), and consequently so many distinct proper ends of those results (*ἐνεργημάτων*). Andreae ought to have distinguished from these distinct and diverse ends—just as the natures, wills, and faculties of acting are themselves diverse—the common outcome toward which the results (*ἀποτελέσματα*) concur. Thus Christ taught the will of his Father by the distinct will and action of his body in speaking; by the power and operation of his soul in thinking and moving the tongue as its plectrum; and finally by his deity in most divinely informing his own mind and disposing the souls of his elect hearers to understand and believe—or, as Luke speaks, in opening their heart. All these actions were most distinct, without separation of the person, against the Monophysites and Monothelites, and concur in a common personal and therefore theandric (*θεανδρικὸν*) result—not a Eutychian result, but one agreeing with the hypostatic union—namely, the promulgation of saving doctrine and the salvation of the elect. Likewise, in the miracles performed, the union of the natures, while preserving their distinction, compels us to attribute the divine operations distinctly to the deity and the human operations to the humanity. But the common result (*ἀποτέλεσμα*) of both those divine and human actions is the confirmation of Christ’s own deity and of his heavenly doctrine. Finally, whatever Christ did and said for the sake of fulfilling the mediator’s office must prudently be distinguished according to the property of each nature against Eutyches, and referred against Nestorius to the common result (*ἀποτέλεσμα*) of the one person of Christ, God and man—namely, our redemption. Although I have already set this forth several times above, I have nevertheless resolved to repeat it again here, so that, if it can be brought about, once everyone has understood how great this error is, whence it flowed, and what the orthodox judgment concerning these matters is, all this controversy among us about matters long ago declared so diligently and so clearly against the most thoroughly condemned heresies may be abolished.

Thesis XVI of the Württemberg Theologians

Christ possessed this majesty of omnipotence, omniscience, and omnipresence, and this power of giving life, insofar as he was man—because of the personal

[Part I | PDF page 153 | logical source scan 152 | printed page 148]

union—even when he was in his mother’s womb; but at that time he did not exercise or employ it. Afterward, however, when born into this world, he demonstrated and employed it as often as there was need, in miracles and in the distribution of his body and blood, to the extent required by the nature of his calling and office. For at that time Christ was in the form of a servant and had emptied himself so that he could suffer and die (Philippians 2).

Antithesis XVI of Theodore Beza

The gifts poured subjectively into Christ’s flesh—of the sort which you enumerate here—were not immediately or perfectly bestowed, as they now are, upon Christ’s soul and flesh. This is evident from the fact that he is truly said to have grown, as his body in stature, so his soul in wisdom and grace (Luke 2), and to have been ignorant of that last day (Matthew 24). The apostle also says that during the time of his humiliation he was truly subject to all the infirmities of our flesh except sin. In this sense he is called capable of suffering (*παθητὸς*) in Acts 26, yet not merely “in appearance” (*κατὰ δόκησιν*), as Marcion blasphemed. This flesh could in no way be impassible (*ἀπαθὴς*) and at the same time such as it is held to have been during the time of its true abasement. It follows that through the ascension into heaven and the session at the Father’s right hand, Christ’s flesh obtained not merely the manifestation and exercise of this glory and power, but the consummation of the glory and power themselves.

1. It was demonstrated above that the gifts poured subjectively into Christ’s flesh are not omnipotence, omnipresence, omniscience, and the power of giving life. For these are accidents neither in God nor in the assumed human nature; rather, they are essential in God and personally bestowed upon the assumed man.

2. All these testimonies must be referred to the state of humiliation and emptying.

3. Paul says the contrary, for he says that Christ was in the form of God and at the same time bore the form of a servant (Philippians 2).

4. This consummation was the revelation of the communicated majesty which had previously been concealed by the form of a servant.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

To 1

First, Andreae now rightly denies that the properties of divinity which we are discussing are qualities, whether in God, since they are his very essence, or in his humanity, since they do not inhere in it subjectively. But if they do not inhere subjectively, how do they inhere? “Personally,” he says. Then let the correct explanation of that statement be supplied: namely, insofar as this man subsists not in himself but in the person of the Son of God, so that whatever belongs to God is said of this man insofar as he is God, and whatever belongs to this man is said of the Word (*λόγῳ*) insofar as the Word (*ὁ λόγος*) is man—but by reciprocal predication (*ἀντίδοσιν*) in concrete terms alone, and as said of the whole Christ against Nestorius, not as said of a part of Christ according to Eutyches and his reborn followers. Further, not to press this with the utmost severity: from what writer, pray, was this phrase at last drawn, that things which are essential in God were bestowed “upon the humanity”? (For this is what he understands by “upon the man,” and we have explained, in our reply to his first observation on our tenth Antithesis,

[Part I | PDF page 154 | logical source scan 153 | printed page 149]

how he corrupts the adverb “personally.”) The apostle indeed says that the fullness of deity dwells bodily (*σωματικῶς*) in Christ. This is said both of the very union of the natures and of the gifts and qualities bestowed upon the flesh. But these men will no more prove that this vain and wholly fictitious communication of essential properties to the assumed humanity—a thing intermediate between the grace of union and those created graces—ever entered the apostle’s mind than they will prove that darkness is light. That Brenz and Andreae formerly spoke of that fantasy in such a way as to establish an effusion which inhered subjectively, really, and was merely operative (*ἐνεργητικὴν*) is evident not merely from those statements so often cited by me about God’s common indwelling in Christ and in all the saints, and from these inexcusable, blasphemous words of Andreae, “Christ has this in common with all the saints, that God was personally united with him,” but still more clearly from this twenty-first Thesis of his: “No presence of God in Christ other than in all remaining creatures must be imagined or invented as regards the essence (*οὐσίαν*) of divinity. Just as this presence is distinguished in creatures by operation (*ἐνεργείᾳ*), in that it acts and operates differently in different creatures, so it is poured wholly into Christ. And this is the communication of all the fullness of deity of which the apostle speaks in Colossians 1.” Therefore, let Andreae first agree with himself and only afterward dispute with us.

To 2 and 3

It certainly is as Andreae says here: these things pertain to the state of the Son of God’s emptying. Yet it is also true that he simultaneously bore the form of God, which the form of a servant did not take away, and the form of a servant, which did not diminish the form of God. But what is said of two natures with respect to union (*καθ᾽ ἕνωσιν*), without any change in the united natures themselves, Andreae twists into contradictory states of the one humanity. He really attributes to one and the same nature, in one and the same subject and at one and the same time, contradictory things: supreme glory and deepest abasement, equality with deity and emptying even to death on the cross. And he defends this madness by that most empty invention, “not subjectively.” Who of sufficiently sound mind could endure it? I am certainly not ignorant that a power (*δύναμιν*) does not always exercise its operation, either because it cannot or because it will not. But who has ever said that, on that account, one and the same nature truly and actually possesses contradictory capacities at one and the same time—such as being impassible (*ἀπαθῆ*) and passible (*παθητήν*), immortal and mortal, most powerful and most infirm? We treated most fully a little earlier of these two forms—not of the humanity but of Christ’s person—when we replied to Andreae’s second observation on our twelfth Antithesis. Yet I will add this here. These men call the form of a servant, or the emptying of the form of God, not the hypostatic assumption of the humanity itself,

[Part I | PDF page 155 | logical source scan 154 | printed page 150]

in which they are greatly mistaken, but merely the lowly condition of the assumed flesh in this life. If that were true, the glorification of the flesh would certainly have done away with that form of a servant altogether—which God forbid. For although that humanity, formerly most abased for our sake for a time, has now obtained all power in heaven and on earth over all created things and has been raised up ineffably, it has nevertheless not even now become deity, uncreated, or infinite. In this glory there is always excepted the one who subjected all things to that humanity, namely the Father, and consequently the Son himself too, insofar as he is the one and the same deity, power, and glory of the three persons. Augustine writes concerning this in sermon 58, *On the Words of the Lord*: “Christ himself, the Son of God, equal to the Father in the form of GOD, because he emptied himself—not losing the form of God but receiving the form of a servant—is greater even than himself, because the form of God which was not lost is greater than the form of a servant which was received.” Therefore, although the humanity, having completed the ministry of that abasement for us, now rules even over the angels themselves, this glory did not take its nature away. Consequently, this flesh did not cease to be finite, organic, palpable, and visible; nor did its soul, though resplendent above even the angels themselves with all the most glorious gifts, lay aside that property of human nature. Finally, that man did not cease to be man or to be whatever distinguishes man from all other created things. He always stands below deity—even the deity of the person in whom he subsists—and in this respect he is in the form of a servant. Lest I seem to say this rashly, behold Augustine’s words in tractate 4 on the Epistle of John, where he speaks of Christ’s coming to judgment: “The impious will see him, but the form of a servant; they will not see the form of God.” Why? “Because they are impious. The Lord himself said: ‘Blessed are the pure in heart, for they shall see the Lord.’”

To 4

I by no means deny that, at the very moment when the human nature was assumed into the unity of the person, it also obtained a right to that most glorious dominion above the angels themselves, with which ineffable glory coheres. I also acknowledge that the Word (*τὸν λόγον*) strengthened that humanity of his by bestowing upon it something like a foretaste of that eminence, and took consolation from it, as he judged this necessary for his own sake and ours. But I say that he first had to suffer before he entered into that glory in actual fact. In what, then, does the disagreement consist? Certainly in this most important point: Andreae and his followers, following the erring Brenz, imagine that Christ’s humanity was already in actual fact as glorious as it now is from the very moment of its assumption. For this reason they also confound the article of the Session at the Father’s right hand with that in which he is said to have been conceived by the Holy Spirit. And lest anything be lacking to the utmost madness, although not even now does that heavenly glory permit us to believe that he exists in his flesh anywhere other than in heaven, whence we await his coming—as Christ himself testified after the resurrection, being palpable and visible not by condescension (*συγκατάβασιν*) but according to the law of a true human

[Part I | PDF page 156 | logical source scan 155 | printed page 151]

nature—yet they maintain that this humanity was already then endowed with the essential omniscience, omnipotence, and omnipresence of deity itself. They see that their real consubstantiation would otherwise collapse unless they prop it up with so monstrous an invention. It is astonishing and almost unbelievable that Andreae, who on page 271 judges that those properties which would not perfect but destroy the human nature—such as being infinite in essence from eternity, incorporeal, immaterial, and the like—must be excluded from his real communication of essential properties, is nevertheless unable to recognize that the real omnipresence of essence so thoroughly depends upon the supreme simplicity and infinity of essence itself that, when these are posited, it necessarily follows, and when these are removed, it is removed; and that for this reason it is not attributed even to the angelic nature. In sum, just as the sun’s light is obstructed by the interposition of clouds or even of a thinner vapor, and when these are removed the sun does not acquire light but fully reveals the light which it actually possessed before, so too, they say, when Christ’s flesh laid aside its infirmity, it did not obtain that new glory but fully manifested the glory which it had actually possessed from the moment of its conception in the Virgin’s womb. We detest this dogma as contrary to the whole history of the gospel: first, because on this account those sufferings (*παθήματα*) upon which our salvation rests would have been something alien and would not truly have been endured in that humanity and through that humanity, just as clouds and vapors do not affect the body of the sun itself; and second, because if the humanity is nevertheless said to have been truly and actually subject to all those infirmities except sin, the Christian faith is thus necessarily found false, since it is in no way possible truly and actually to say that one and the same nature was simultaneously endowed both with that supreme glory and with that utterly abject infirmity.

Thesis XVII of the Württemberg Theologians

The state of emptying and the form of a servant, which continued in Christ for a brief time, detract nothing from his majesty, into which he fully entered when he ascended into heaven and sat at the right hand of God the Father Almighty. That right hand of God is the majesty of Christ presently reigning according to both natures in heaven and on earth. Therefore Christ now governs all things in heaven and on earth IN, WITH, and through the assumed human nature. For this is what it means to sit at God’s right hand. Thus, when the apostles were sent throughout the world, Christ worked together with them and confirmed the preaching of the gospel by the signs which followed, which were done by the power and might and in the name of Jesus Christ (Mark 16).

[Part I | PDF page 157 | logical source scan 156 | printed page 152]

Antithesis XVII of Theodore Beza

We assent to this thesis with two exceptions. First, you take the form of a servant and the state of emptying, as you call it, for one and the same thing and suppose that Christ laid both aside. By the form of a servant, however, we understand the humanity itself, according to which he always was, is, and will be inferior to himself as the Word (*λόγῳ*) and consubstantial with us (*ὁμοούσιος*), although, having completed the ministry of our redemption, he laid aside all infirmities. Circumscription must by no means be numbered among those infirmities, since it formally constitutes the humanity itself. Otherwise, when on that last day he comes visible and consequently as a circumscribed bodily substance, he would have to be said to appear not most glorious but lowly, having again assumed this infirmity. Second, we confess that Christ now reigns and possesses all power both in heaven and on earth according to both natures, but not as presently here so far as the flesh is concerned. The apostle says that while we are now on earth we are away from Christ, and he desired to depart from the body so that he might be joined to him. For this reason Christ too is said not already to have come or to be present in his flesh, nor merely to be about to become visible again out of invisibility, but to be coming conspicuously.

1. Let Paul’s words be examined, and the matter will be manifest.

2. You assert it, but do not prove it; that is not sufficient.

3. Although the humanity is by nature always inferior to the Word (*λόγῳ*), it is nevertheless no longer a servant (Acts 2:12).

4. The communication of majesty does not conflict with circumscription, nor does it abolish or overturn it in its nature, but exalts it to the highest degree of glory upon God’s own throne.

5. Most immediately present, because he rules not through deputies but by himself, as was said above.

6. This absence is physical and local, but it does not remove that spiritual presence by which Christ is most immediately present to us.

7. He will come visibly, who is now invisibly present to us here on earth in a heavenly manner.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

To 1, 2, and 3

See observation 2 above on Antithesis 12 and observation 1 on Antithesis 16.

To 4

If this omn-majesty of yours does not remove the circumscription of Christ’s body, how will this statement of Brenz on page 43 of his *Recognition* stand? (He would have acted most rightly if he had recognized the whole.) “What Theodoret and others say, that Christ’s human body has the same circumscription in the other age which it had in this age, is said without any rightly understood passage of Scripture and is a childish thought about heavenly things.” And what of this on page 249? “If Christ’s humanity were circumscribed in a place at the right hand of Majesty, he would not have been made more excellent than the angels, since being circumscribed in a definite place belongs not to supreme Majesty but either to bodily infirmity”—and what else would a reborn Eutyches say? What ignorance it is to number the essential properties of natures, without which the natures cease to be what they are, among the infirmities by which a nature is harmed!—“or to a voluntary dispensation for accommodating the infirmity of others.”

[Part I | PDF page 158 | logical source scan 157 | printed page 153]

And what ignorance it again is to confound dispensation (*οἰκονομίαν*) with condescension (*συγκατάβασιν*)! But how can that statement of Andreae himself on page 56 of his *Apology against the Theses of Ingolstadt* agree with these claims? “By this statement, that it does not follow that what is in God is everywhere in the same way as God, Augustine does not deny that Christ’s humanity is everywhere; rather, he denies that it is everywhere in the mode in which the divinity is.” If this is true, the humanity will therefore not be everywhere through the essential omnipotence and omnipresence of deity. For whether it is everywhere by its own or by another’s power does not change the mode of the presence of deity’s omnipotence and omnipresence. And what is to become of this further passage of the same Andreae on page 63 of the same book? “Since Christ’s glorious body is no longer physical but spiritual, who will say that it is moved from place to place?” A marvelous theology indeed, which deprives Christ of a physical (*φυσικὸν*) body and contends that the nature of a human body is abolished by spiritual glory. If this is true in Christ, it will certainly be true in his members as well. But tell us now, Andreae, if Christ’s body ceased to be natural through glorification, how did it remain circumscribed? Nothing is more proper to a body, insofar as it is both quantified and material, than circumscription, whence it follows that it is also palpable and visible. And since Andreae concedes circumscription, how will he remove local motion? Finally, if Christ’s body is truly circumscribed and organic, how will the divine omnipresence attributed to that very body really, simultaneously, and at the same time—indeed, outside every account of time—not be false?

To 5

A marvelous inference indeed: Christ does not rule the world through deputies, but by himself; therefore he is most immediately present to all things in his flesh. Christ existed before Abraham; therefore did he exist in his flesh? “By no means,” Andreae will say, “because this flesh did not yet exist.” But from the time the Son of God existed as God and man, did this flesh exist everywhere? “Certainly,” Brenz will say. “For he was simultaneously in Bethlehem in the manger and actually in heaven at the same time.” Therefore this body did not go away, did not return, and was not absent from the centurion’s house when the centurion said that it was not necessary for him to enter his house; and it was present, even according to the flesh, with Lazarus as he was dying. “He is not here,” the angels said of the body which had risen. And Christ himself said, “You will not always have me.” “That is,” Andreae will say, “according to the circumscription of the body in itself, but not according to the omn-majesty of the humanity in the deity.” You will immediately hear the rationale for this gloss from Chemnitzian subtlety: “the former subjectively, but the latter not subjectively.” And yet both are said truly and really of the very essence of the flesh. If anyone of us objects that he cannot assent to so false and incoherent (*ἀσυστάτῳ*) a dogma—especially one most shamelessly elicited from that very hypostatic union of the two natures which

[Part I | PDF page 159 | logical source scan 158 | printed page 154]

is nevertheless the surest foundation for overturning these very tricks—he will immediately hear this universal solution to every argument: “Therefore you are a heretic, a most detestable Calvinist; you deny God’s omnipotence, overturn God’s testament, subject God’s Word to your own sense, are a Nestorian, and judge no differently about Christ than Muhammad himself.” But God forbid that we should dread these senseless thunderbolts. Yet you, Germany: when at last will you recognize these tricks?

To 6

I said in the apostle’s own words that we are now bodily away from the Lord, insofar as he is certainly endowed with a body. For who doubts that Christ, both insofar as he is God and through the effects of his Holy Spirit, moves among the saints and in the saints? From this I inferred the necessary absence from us of his body by as great an interval of places as the distance between the earth, from which that body truly departed, and the heavens above which it ascended and toward which we too sigh after him. Andreae replies: “That absence is a physical and local absence which does not remove the spiritual presence.” I acknowledge the former as most true, but the latter only with a distinction. Only that spiritual presence of Christ’s body which we establish from God’s Word does not remove its physical and local presence. By this presence, what is separated by the greatest distances is most truly and efficaciously beheld by the eyes of faith, apprehended, and, once apprehended, spiritually applied to us. With those eyes Abraham long ago beheld the day of the Lord—although the Lord had not yet been actually manifested in the flesh—as though he were already most immediately present in that flesh of his, and therefore rejoiced with the joy of faith. But how can that chimerical and futile omnipresence, which abolishes the reality of both natures under the false pretext of omnipotence, overturns the definition of the hypostatic union, and finally diametrically conflicts with the analogy of faith and the end of the sacraments, stand together with true physical and local presence?

To 7

I argued from the words “going away” and “returning” to establish the true absence of Christ’s body from the earth, since these words necessarily include a change of place when they are said of a true body. God’s Word speaks thus properly and not figuratively everywhere, and the analogy of the articles of Christian faith likewise compels it. Hence Augustine’s statement: “According to the body, he is so far from being here that he is said to be going to come from heaven.” And Athanasius, speaking even of Christ’s glorified body: “It belongs to one who is bounded by the limits of places to go and come, and to leave the place in which he was in order to come to the place where he was not.” What does Andreae say to these things? “He who is now invisibly present will come visibly.” But I ask: by what trope, what idiom, what even faint appearance of reason do “to depart from the earth” and “to ascend into heaven” signify, not changing place, but becoming invisible from visible through the spectacle of the Ascension all the way up to the clouds, as Brenz was not ashamed to write;

[Part I | PDF page 160 | logical source scan 159 | printed page 155]

and how does “to RETURN from heaven to the world to judge it” conversely signify nothing more than becoming visible and conspicuous from invisible? And, to crown this grossest error, they nevertheless teach that this very body actually retains its circumscription. How long at last, Germany, will you endure these tricks?

Thesis XVIII of the Württemberg Theologians

Indeed, because of the indissoluble personal union it is rightly and truly said: “The man Jesus Christ is true God,” and, “The eternal God became man.” For John says nothing else when he says, “THE WORD WAS MADE FLESH” (John 1). That Word, as the same apostle testifies in the same chapter, was GOD, while “flesh,” according to the phraseology of Scripture, signifies MAN. Therefore this proposition, “The Word was made flesh,” signifies nothing other than, “God became man.” For this reason Paul writes thus of Christ: “From whom”—the Jews—“is Christ according to the flesh, WHO is GOD over all, blessed forever” (Romans 9).

XIX

For this reason it is rightly and truly said: “God suffered for us; God died for the sins of the human race,” although we freely confess that the divinity in itself neither suffered nor died, but that Christ suffered in the flesh, as Peter speaks in 1 Peter 4. Nevertheless, although divinity cannot die, the outrages pertained not only to the Son of Man but also to the Son of God, because there are not two persons in Christ, but only one. For unless God’s blood had been poured out for us, we would neither have been redeemed from eternal death nor cleansed from our sins. Therefore Paul, commending the Ephesian church to the ministers of the Word, says: “The Holy Spirit appointed you bishops to govern the church of God, which he acquired with HIS OWN BLOOD” (Acts 20). And John says: “The blood of Jesus Christ, his SON, cleanses us from every sin” (1 John 1).

Antithesis XVIII and XIX of Theodore Beza

We assent, but we further require this of you: just as, because of the hypostatic union and with respect to the whole person, you rightly and piously attribute to the incarnate God through the communication of properties the things proper to the assumed humanity, so you should also attribute to the man the things proper to the deity of the Word (*τοῦ λόγου*) who assumes. And lest we seem to ask something novel of you, we say that we ask what Doctor Luther long ago wrote should be done in these very words, in his work on the last words of David: “Just as two natures are united in one person, so the names

1. The rationale of the communication is diverse because of the diversity of the natures and the immutability of the divine nature.

2. We embrace both Luther’s words and judgment en-

[Part I | PDF page 161 | logical source scan 160 | printed page 156]

tirely. But we embrace them with respect not to a verbal but to the real union of the natures. You understand them only of a verbal union, which is false.

3. This is utterly false, since those two natures are so united that the one gives and the other receives. But here the divine nature can receive neither perfection nor imperfection from the human because of its supreme perfection and immutability. Therefore the rationale of the union is utterly diverse.

4. From the grace of union the human nature possesses the things proper to deity.

of each nature are likewise united in the name of one person; this is called the communication of properties. Thus the man is said to have been, and was, born of the Virgin Mary and crucified by the Jews. The same designation must also be attributed to the Son of God, and it must be said: God was born of the Virgin Mary and crucified by the Jews, because God and man is one person, not two sons, one of God and the other of Mary, but one and the same Son of God and of man. Although the assuming nature excels in every way, and especially in this, that subsisting of itself it supplies subsistence to the human nature, nevertheless the Word is united to the flesh and the flesh to the Word by a union not dissimilar, but one and the same in rationale. And that vast distinction by which the assuming deity receives nothing from the nature which it assumes, whereas the humanity on the contrary is enriched from it, pertains not to the hypostatic union itself but to that effect of it which we have said is called habitual grace.”

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

To 1 and 3

Since the natures and the things said of the natures wholly cohere with one another—which in the humanity has place not only in essential properties but also in accidental ones—we say it follows that, in the same way as these natures become one subsisting entity (*ὑφιστάμενον*), which is God and man, but not one nature which is deity and humanity, Christ is one thing and another thing, but not one person and another person. Similarly, whatever belongs to that man is reciprocally predicated of God, namely the Word (*τῷ λόγῳ*), and conversely whatever belongs to the Word (*τῷ λόγῳ*) is predicated of that man. Damascene calls this *perichoresis* (*περιχώρησιν*). Our adversaries concede both parts of this reciprocal predication in the very designations “God” and “man” in their Thesis 18: that is, they concede that both these propositions about Christ are true, “Man is God,” and, “God is man.” But in the things said of the natures, they accept only the one part, namely that the human things are said of God, as when the God of glory is said to have been born, suffered, and been buried. In this observation, however, they refuse to concede to the man the things proper to the Word (*τῷ λόγῳ*), because, they say, “these natures are diverse, and the divine nature can receive nothing from the human.” But what could have been answered less appositely? First, just as the union of the natures, so too this reciprocal predication of the things said about the natures changes nothing in the natures themselves, which always remain intact and distinct in the hypostatic

[Part I | PDF page 162 | logical source scan 161 | printed page 157]

union. Therefore, just as when I say that the God of glory suffered, died, and was buried, I detract nothing from the deity, since these things are thus said of God not insofar as he is man but insofar as he is God, because of the unity of the person, so too when I say of Christ, “This man is eternal, without beginning, immeasurable, infinite,” and in general attribute to this man whatever is said of God, I arrogate nothing to him which does not belong and remain proper, and therefore incommunicable (*ἀκοινώνητον*), to God. For these things are indeed said of the man, but not insofar as he is man; rather, insofar as he is God, because of the unity of the person, not the nature. Therefore, what these men fear is utterly vain, and the cause of their error is that they confound abstract with concrete terms, as if nothing could be said truly of concrete terms except what agrees with those concrete things themselves. This is utterly false because of the unity of the person. Further, to concede that it is just as true to say of Christ that the man is God as that God is man, and afterward to deny that whatever belongs to God is truly said of the man, while nevertheless acknowledging that whatever belongs to the man is truly said of God—is this not to contradict oneself and, after positing the antecedent, to deny the necessary consequent? True disputants have certainly never been accustomed to do that. Finally, if their rationale were valid—namely, if this personal reciprocation of properties had to be evaluated from the excellence of the divine nature, which can receive nothing from the humanity—who does not see that the opposite of what they do ought to have been maintained? They concede that the things belonging to the man are truly said of God, whereas on the contrary, if their rationale were valid, it ought to have been judged that divine things can indeed be said of the man, but human things by no means of God. Lastly, that diversity of the natures—which God forbid that we should deny—can in no way bring about two unequal unions, or cause God to be united with man by any hypostatic union other than the one by which man is united with God, although the person of the Word (*τοῦ λόγου*), who assumes the human nature, has dominion in that mystery of union. But this inequality varies only the effects and consequences of this union. Among these, we have shown on almost every page of this reply that to reckon a real communication of properties between the natures themselves, distinct from the union itself, is not only false but impious.

To 2

Doctor Luther’s words are plain, although he sets forth only an example in which human things are predicated of God by reciprocal predication (*κατ᾽ ἀντίδοσιν*). I do not know whence or from whom Andreae borrowed a “verbal union,” but I do know that no such thing ever entered my mind. We have already declared most fully several times the reasons for and the sense in which we call—not, certainly, the union (*ἕνωσιν*), which itself is sometimes called communion (*κοινωνία*)—but that phrase and form of speech which recent writers call the communication of properties (*ἰδιωμάτων*) “verbal,” and yet deny that it is merely verbal: verbal, namely, insofar

[Part I | PDF page 163 | logical source scan 162 | printed page 158]

as it is a figurative phrase, but at the same time real and most true when it is said of the whole person insofar as the person is one, against Nestorius. But if the statements are made about the natures, as our adversaries take them, we judge them neither verbal nor real, but blasphemous and detestable, because this is pure Eutychianism.

To 4

Nothing could be said more openly Eutychian than what is said here: “From the grace of union the human nature possesses the things proper to deity.” For this proposition stands—one which it would be extreme folly to deny, and which Cyril and Damascene impress a thousand times—that each nature differs from the others by its properties, and that whatever becomes common with the other nature, whether “subjectively,” as they now speak, or “not subjectively,” ceases to be proper to one nature. Therefore, when Andreae during the disputation demanded that I prove from God’s Word that the humanity cannot receive the essential properties of deity without becoming deity, I saw fit to meet him with no other answer than that he acted just as if he were to order me to prove from God’s Word that we were at Montbéliard and were disputing there. What more fitting answer could I give to so ridiculous a demand? Yet I had not forgotten that Christ everywhere proves his deity from the works proper to deity, and proves the reality of his body raised from the dead from the properties of true humanity. That mode of argument would by no means be necessary and demonstrative (*ἀποδεικτικὴ*), deduced from principles in themselves (*καθ᾽ αὑτὸ*), if in any way whatsoever what is proper to one nature could be common to the other. Nor had it escaped me that Arius was overthrown by the Fathers especially through those testimonies in which the things proper to the one deity are attributed to the Son of God both before and after the incarnation, necessarily proving that this Son is truly and by nature consubstantial (*ὁμοούσιον*) with the Father. But what if Arius had then objected with that distinction of yours: “not subjectively”; “not in essence (*οὐσίᾳ*) but in authority (*ἐξουσίᾳ*)”; “not in essence but in power”; “not by nature but by grace”? Would the Fathers have been struck dumb before these not only utterly empty tricks but truly ill-omened (*δυσφήμους*) blasphemies? Rather, let Andreae hear how Cyril even then answered these things: “If they believe that those dignities have come to Christ from without—namely, that he is creator, omnipotent king, and adorable—and nevertheless think he must be worshiped, then they worship not the Creator but a creature, inventing through ignorance a certain newly arisen God.” For just as only he is truly a man to whom are attributed the properties by which the human nature is distinguished from the rest, so only he is truly God to whom the essential properties of deity belong. Make these properties common between the natures, and they cease to be proper; you will not unite the natures but confound them. Therefore, because Christ is one person, and because of the one hypostasis (*ὑπόστασιν*) in which both the deity of the Word (*τοῦ λόγου*) and the human nature subsist, he is God and man. For this reason both divine and hu-

[Part I | PDF page 164 | logical source scan 163 | printed page 159]

man things are truly, properly, and essentially attributed through reciprocal predication (*ἀντίδοσιν*) to that person, who is expressed not in abstract but in concrete terms, but according as he is man and God. He is not man and God by the substances of the natures being commingled with one another, or by their properties, wills, or operations being so commingled, but by that kind of union which is according to hypostasis (*καθ᾽ ὑπόστασιν*): that is, the natures and the things proper to those natures are united in the one preexisting person of the Son of God, but in such a way that, without separation of the hypostasis, they always remain distinct from each other without any confusion. Therefore, human things are never predicated of the Word (*λόγῳ*) insofar as he is God, nor divine things of this man insofar as he is man, as Damascene so often impresses. Much less can either humanity and the things belonging to humanity ever be predicated of deity, or deity and the things belonging to deity of humanity, in abstract terms without blasphemy. Nor is this blasphemy excused by that most empty Chemnitzian distinction, “not subjectively,” as if the humanity could be deity not in itself but in the deity to which it is united, or as if this man had actually become God through the hypostatic union, not in himself but in the Word (*λόγῳ*) to whom he is hypostatically united. All these things are purely Eutychian and unheard of in the Christian churches. In them it is never said either that the Word (*ὁ λόγος*) became man “in the humanity,” much less that he, in respect of his deity or person, was made man (*ἐνανθρωπισθεὶς*) by some real communication of the properties of humanity non-subjectively conferred upon the deity itself. Nor is that man said actually to have been “deified” (*θεοθεὶς*) or “Word-ified” (*λογοθεὶς*)—as if you were to say “deificated” and “verbificated”—in the deity of the Word (*τοῦ λόγου*), through some essential property of divinity consequently affecting him, though not subjectively; much less is this attributed to the abstract term “humanity.” Rather, the Word (*ὁ λόγος*) is said to have become man by assuming the seed of Abraham, and conversely this man assumed according to hypostasis (*καθ᾽ ὑπόστασιν*) is said to have become God, each nature remaining what it was naturally and essentially. Therefore, let Andreae change what he falsely says in abstract terms—namely, that by the grace of union the human nature received the things proper to deity—and say in concrete terms that by the grace of union this man attained this, that he is one with the subsisting Word (*λόγῳ ὑφισταμένῳ*). Consequently, neither in himself nor non-subjectively in another nature, though united to him—for “one thing and another” (*ἄλλο κἄλλο*) remain distinct even in Christ’s person—is he God; neither subjectively in himself nor non-subjectively in the Word (*λόγῳ*) is he God or really endowed with any divine properties. Nevertheless, Christ is and is called God, although synecdochically denominated (*συνεκδοχικῶς*) from the other nature, just as a man is called intelligent, mortal, and immortal because of the unity of the individual—things which, unless they are understood as said of one subsisting entity (*ὑφισταμένῳ*), no one doubts would be said most falsely.

Thesis XX of the Württemberg Theologians

And this mediator and our redeemer Jesus Christ, whole, must be worshiped with religious wor-

[Part I | PDF page 165 | logical source scan 164 | printed page 160]

ship according to both natures, because we do not have two Christs, one of whom is to be worshiped and the other not. For it is written of the whole Christ: “Let all his angels worship HIM” (Hebrews 1). And those blind men directed worship and invocation not only to the Son of God but also to the Son of David, that is, to the human nature in Christ, saying, “Lord, SON OF DAVID, have mercy on us.” Yet they are not rebuked by Christ for this reason, but instead are healed (Matthew 20; Mark 10; Luke 18). And Stephen the martyr, who certainly possessed a solid knowledge of Christ the mediator, commending his soul to Christ in his agony, said, “JESUS, receive my spirit,” commending his soul to the God and man JESUS Christ.

Antithesis XX of Theodore Beza

In the one worship of our one mediator according to both natures, we by no means divide his person, but distinguish the natures as is fitting. For the Word (*λόγος*) is the true and absolute object of our worship, since worship is due to God alone. Yet we do not so exclude the assumed flesh from that worship that we divide the person with Nestorius. Rather, we worship that flesh together with him—not in itself, but relatively, that is, insofar as it is the flesh of the Son of God and constitutes one person with and in that Word (*λόγῳ*). For “the humanity considered simply in itself and under no other relation,” Cyril says, “numbers itself among those who worship; and Christ, as man, also worships after he became man. But he is always worshiped with the Father and the Spirit, because he is true God according to nature.” And the same author writes in *On the Right Faith to Theodosius*: “Shall we worship Emmanuel as a man”—that is, in himself, absolutely, even when considered in the union? “God forbid. For this would be madness, deception, and error. In this we would differ in no way from those who worship the creature beyond the Maker and Creator.” Finally, to summarize everything briefly, following the eighth anathema of the Council of Chalcedon, “We worship Emmanuel with one worship, but insofar as the Son of God essentially became flesh by hypostatic union.”

1. You do not wish to seem to do it in words, but you do it in actual fact when you take away from Christ according to the humanity the things necessarily required for religious worship, namely the communication of omnipotence.

2. You assert that because worship is due to God alone, it cannot be attributed to the flesh without blasphemy.

3. Here you manifestly exclude the humanity, because by “IN ITSELF” you understand the very substance of the human nature, which is not to be invoked, but the Word (*λόγον*) to whom it is united is.

4. Just like the king’s cap or crown when the king is worshiped, which no one addresses. Book 2 on John, chapter 92, on these words: “We worship what we know.”

5. “In itself.” In this passage and by this phrase, Cyril understands the property of the substance of the humanity if it is considered by itself, and at the same time includes the state of emptying.

6. In words they approve that anathema, but in actual fact they disapprove it.

[Part I | PDF page 166 | logical source scan 165 | printed page 161]

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

To 1

The question of the worship of Christ even as man is decided by the same rationale as the preceding questions. For because of the unity of the person, by which it comes about that the man is God and God is man, but the humanity is not deity nor deity humanity, human things are attributed to God, not to deity, and divine things to this man, not to the humanity—human things indeed to God insofar as he is man, and divine things to the man insofar as he is God. The same judgment must therefore necessarily be made concerning the worship of that man. But what are you to do with Andreae, who thinks that whatever is not attributed even to the united humanity is taken away from the theandric man (*θεανθρώπῳ*)? Just as this man became God by the hypostatic union, did this humanity therefore become God? And yet, if Andreae is to be believed, the person is divided by anyone who does not define the hypostatic union by a real communication of essential properties between the natures themselves—whether a subjective communication, as these men at first thought and spoke, or a non-subjective one which not even Eutyches could have imagined.

To 2

Again, according to the custom of his theology, Andreae confounds abstract with concrete terms and employs a sophistical equivocation (*ὁμωνυμίᾳ*), whether deliberately or unwittingly. For if by “God alone” he means the Word (*τὸν λόγον*) separated in reality or thought from that man with whom he is one by union (*καθ᾽ ἕνωσιν*)—something which I admonished above must never be done—then what he charges against us is utterly false. A person would divide the person with Nestorius if he said that this man is not worshiped when the Word (*ὁ λόγος*) is worshiped, who is truly the God-man (*θεάνθρωπος*) in one subsisting entity (*ὑφισταμένῳ*). Indeed, as is also expressly guarded in the Council of Chalcedon, we deny that God is to be co-worshiped with the man as one person with another. But this does not prevent this man from being necessarily comprehended in that worship—not absolutely (*ἁπλῶς*), as we said before from Damascene, but relatively (*κατὰ τι*). For in the very union of the natures the real distinction both of the natures and of the things inseparably cohering with them is accurately preserved without either separation or confusion; and this man is comprehended in the worship not because he became God in himself or through some non-subjective real communication, but because he subsists with the person of the Son of God, that is, is one actual entity with him.

To 3 and 4

Andreae now strikes the same rock for the third time. Who among us has ever denied that Christ is to be invoked both as man and as God? What Christian denies that we ought even now to look to him from heaven, where he now is according to the flesh and not on earth, and to be ruled, inspired, and guarded by him as the one who possesses all power in heaven and on earth and over all created things, even insofar as he is the Son of Man, and who is especially the head of his church; and finally, that all our prayers ought to be framed in his name alone and under his protection? Yet he certainly could not have performed for us the things which he performed for

[Part I | PDF page 167 | logical source scan 166 | printed page 162]

our salvation unless he had been one and the same God and man; nor could he now hear us everywhere and help us when so far distant unless he were God and man. Therefore, although the invocation of Christ is one, its rationale is nevertheless twofold. We ask both that he intercede for us as our firstborn brother and that he hear us as God; nor do we divide the person on that account. But here it is not without reason asked whether this worship and invocation is directed to the humanity itself as its object. For it is one thing to consider that man, not in his humanity, but insofar as he is one with the subsisting Word (*λόγῳ ὑφισταμένῳ*)—under which rationale we attribute to him, according to the other nature (*κατ᾽ ἄλλο*), whatever belongs to the Word (*τῷ λόγῳ*)—and another to behold him in his own humanity. In this question something different seems to require determination from what we said about the natures themselves and their properties, wills, and actions. For these exist in the natures themselves; indeed, as far as the divinity is concerned, they are its very essence. Worship, however, is something external to the natures themselves, proceeding from those who worship. My answer, therefore, is what reason itself dictates when the theological principles have been posited, and what is confirmed by the authority and testimonies of all the ancient Greek and Latin Fathers, cited hundreds of times by writers on our side and especially by me: namely, that our worship and invocation is directed also to Christ’s flesh itself, that is, his humanity, yet not absolutely but relatively (*κατὰ τι*). It is directed to the flesh not insofar as it is simply a creature in itself—for this would be pure idolatry—but insofar as it is the humanity of the Son of God. In this humanity, just as he willed to exhibit himself to the world and fulfill the sufferings (*παθήματα*) necessary for procuring our salvation, so he now wills to be acknowledged, worshiped, and invoked as our Savior, King, and Governor; and through the intervention of that humanity spiritually apprehended by us through faith, he wills to lead us daily from faith to faith, and at last to that blessed immortality. For how could our worship be directed absolutely to that nature which does not even subsist in itself but in the person of the Son? Yet although it subsists in the Son, it did not therefore become the Son’s deity, but remains the Son’s humanity. This is precisely what the Fathers—including Cyril, whom Andreae here obliquely censures—illustrated by proposing the analogy of royal purple and the other royal ornaments. These too are saluted, not absolutely, but insofar as the king is clad in purple and crowned and adorned with them. The king alone is properly and for his own sake accorded civil worship, although not without the ornaments in which he is clothed. “But,” Andreae says, “no one addresses those ornaments.” Certainly no one does, for they are deaf and mute. Christ the man, however, is neither mute nor deaf in his humanity. Although he is not omnipresent in his essence—because neither is that humanity deity, nor is that man, insofar as he is man, God in that deity—he governs heaven and earth. And by addressing Saul from heaven, at least, he sufficiently demonstrated that he is neither deaf nor mute, still suffering in sympathy with the wretched, as the apostle says, though now in his own manner (*συμπάσχων*).

[Part I | PDF page 168 | logical source scan 167 | printed page 163]

Analogies must not be extended further beyond their purpose; nor should the orthodox Fathers consequently be judged to have supposed with Nestorius that the humanity is joined to the Son of God as nothing more than some external garment.

To 7

I appeal to the very context of the eighth anathema, to the explanation Cyril appended to it, to the other passages of the same writer cited by me hundreds of times long ago, and particularly to Damascene, book 4, chapter 3 of *The Orthodox Faith*. From these I do not refuse to have this whole controversy adjudicated by the application of God’s Word.

Thesis XXI of the Württemberg Theologians

This sound and pious doctrine concerning the inseparable personal union of the two natures in Christ, the real communication of properties, and the exaltation of the human nature to the right hand—that is, to the majesty and power of God—is so far from removing the presence of Christ’s body and blood from the Holy Supper that it instead confirms that presence. For the human nature in Christ, INDISSOLUBLY united with the Son of God, made a participant in the divine properties, and placed at God’s right hand, is always present not only in the Lord’s Supper but also to the whole church. In the Holy Supper, moreover, Christ’s flesh and blood are distributed, and so forth.

Antithesis XXI of Theodore Beza

We too profess the sound and pious doctrine of the inseparable hypostatic union. We acknowledge a real communication of properties with respect to the unity of the person. Against Nestorius, we also acknowledge that this man was raised by that hypostatic union to deity itself, much more to its majesty and power, so that through it he became God the Word (*θεὸς λόγος*); and that he was also adorned above the angels themselves with ineffable gifts. Against Eutyches, however, we deny that the humanity itself became deity by that hypostatic union, or was made a participant in the divine properties by any communication arising from it. We by no means think that placement at God’s right hand must be referred to the incarnation, but that it partly marks the end of the time of the Word’s (*τοῦ λόγου*) emptying and partly declares the communication of glory and of habitual grace to Christ’s flesh. Finally, we refer the presence of Christ’s flesh in the Lord’s Sup-

1. For them the communication of properties is nothing other than the union of natures and properties, although these differ as cause and effect.

2. The man became God the Word (*θεὸς λόγος*), which they have hitherto censured in us; but what they mean will follow.

3. Is this not openly to deny the communication of properties with Nestorius?

4. A perverse interpretation of God’s right hand and the session at God’s right hand, concerning which more will be said in the colloquy.

[Part I | PDF page 169 | logical source scan 168 | printed page 164]

5. There is a manifest contradiction in the words of the Thesis. For they deny that the hypostatic union effects the sacramental presence, but say that he is present to the whole church by his power and because Christ’s person is one.

6. We say that by the property of his nature he is in heaven and not on earth; this detracts nothing from the sacramental presence.

7. Since this imparting does not occur without real presence, why do you not give glory to God and frankly confess that presence with us?

per not to the hypostatic union, but to the words of the sacramental institution. In our preceding writing we said that this presence is spiritual.
