Under the same rationale we say from sacred Scripture that he is now present according to the flesh to the whole church, partly by his power and partly because the person of Jesus Christ is one, and that he will be present even to the consummation of the age. Yet the real substance of his body, just as it was absent from heaven when he was on earth, is now absent from earth when placed in heaven; nevertheless, he most truly and efficaciously imparts his flesh even now to his own unto eternal life, both in the Word and in the sacraments, spiritually through faith, by a marvelous and inscrutable mystery.

Montbéliard, 24 March 1586.

Theodore Beza, minister of the Genevan church.

Abraham Musculus, minister of the Bernese church.

Antoine de La Faye, minister of the Genevan church.

Claude Aubery, doctor and professor of philosophy in the Academy of Lausanne.

Peter Hübner, public professor of Greek in the Bernese academy.

Theodore Beza’s Reply to Andreae’s Marginal Observations

To 1

What I have impressed a hundred times I impress again, relying upon the consensus of all the orthodox Fathers, reason itself, and the testimony of truth itself. Since the natures and their essential properties, wills, and actions cohere inseparably, they can be communicated—that is, united—only by one and the same hypostatic union. Consequently, those who dream that they become common by some effect of this union, so that either the properties of divinity are communicated with the humanity—which these men now timidly suggest rather than explain—or the properties of humanity with the divinity—in which they locate the foundation of their consubstantiation—are just as mistaken as those who would say that the assumed humanity was made deity, though not in itself, nevertheless in the deity by which it was assumed; and conversely that deity was made humanity, if not in itself, at least in the humanity which it assumed. Let all who have not been bewitched by these men judge which side wanders from the truth.

To 2 and 3

But where and when did we deny that the man became God the Word (*θεὸν λόγον*) through the hypostatic union? Yet because Andreae does not define the hypo-

[Part I | PDF page 170 | logical source scan 169 | printed page 165]

static union of the human nature with the Word (*λόγῳ*) by the fact that it subsists not in the human species as an individual of it, but in the person of the Son of God, so that it is a part of that composite according to hypostasis (*καθ᾽ ὑπόστασιν*)—a matter which we treated most fully above, where we showed that a hypostatic definition can and ought to be defined from what precedes—but instead establishes that false and ridiculous real communication of essential properties between the natures themselves, he therefore thinks that whoever denies that the properties of deity were really communicated with the humanity denies that this man is God, that is, is a Nestorian. Yet if Andreae is to be believed, Nestorius did not deny that this man was God, but only denied that human things agree with God or deity. We showed most fully above that Andreae is mistaken in this matter. For Nestorius united the Word (*τὸν λόγον*) with the assumed humanity in words, not according to hypostasis (*καθ᾽ ὑπόστασιν*), but merely according to relation (*κατὰ σχέσιν*), dignity (*κατ᾽ ἀξίαν*), and equality of honor (*ὁμοτιμίαν*)—that is, the recently born Brenzian equation—and by a concurrence of counsel (*συμβουλίαν*), that is, a Chemnitzian or Monothelite theandric operation (*θεανδρικὴν*); but in reality he disjoined them. Therefore he called Mary not God-bearer (*θεοτόκον*) but Christ-bearer (*χριστοτόκον*). But enough, and more than enough, has been said about these matters above.

To 4

By referring the session at the Father’s right hand—which he also most perversely explains through the form of God—to the very moment of the incarnation, Andreae both confounds the articles of the Christian creed and contradicts the manifest Word of God, since Christ first had to suffer and so enter into the Father’s glory; and he lays the surest foundation for Marcion’s ravings, since one and the same nature could not actually be at once most glorious and most infirm. Yet, as though speaking from the tripod, he condemns us as perverse interpreters.

To 5

Andreae thinks that those teach contradictions who say that Christ is present to his church even according to the flesh—that is, even in his flesh—partly by his own power, namely spiritual, and partly because the person of Jesus Christ is one, and yet deny that the hypostatic union effects sacramental presence, since we refer this to the institution of the Lord’s Supper itself. I answer that there is no contradiction here, except insofar as both claims oppose two of his own utterly false dogmas. The first is that he transforms the spiritual presence which we establish into an essentially bodily one disguised under the appearance of some spiritual utopian speculation. The second is that he confounds Christ’s sacramental presence in the Lord’s Supper with that universal presence by which the Son of God, though whole—that is, one and by no means divided—yet not according to the whole of himself, is everywhere present to his church. In this he commits manifold error because of both the peculiar mode and the peculiar end of sacramental presence. For he is indeed spiritually present to that bread, as he is everywhere; but in the Lord’s Supper he must not be beheld by the eyes of faith merely as present in the way he is everywhere, but insofar as, according to the formula of his peculiar covenant, he there offers himself to us as still hang-

[Part I | PDF page 171 | logical source scan 170 | printed page 166]

ing on the cross, his blood still dripping for us from his wounds, to be beheld by the eyes of faith and, as though grasped by the hand of that same faith and introduced as it were into our innermost parts with respect to its efficacy (*ἐνέργειαν*), as nourishment for eternal life. Therefore, in these respects the sacramental presence differs very greatly from that more general presence; and so, with respect to its essential form, even the sounder doctors of the Augsburg Confession rightly defend against Andreae himself and his followers that it has its origin and principle not from the hypostatic union, but from the institution of the Supper itself.

To 6 and 7

We asserted in our Antithesis that the real substance of Christ’s humanity, just as when it was on earth it was absent from heaven, so now, being placed in heaven, is absent from earth. Yet this does not deny his most true and efficacious spiritual presence, both in the administration of the word and of the sacraments, and especially in the Lord’s Supper, by which he offers himself to the faithful mind to be more and more apprehended and enjoyed for the salvation of the whole person. These, moreover, are not our words, but those of the whole of orthodox antiquity. Augustine testifies that *Christ according to the flesh, since he ascended into heaven, is not here; and that he sits there, not here, at the Father’s right hand.* Cyril says that *no one should doubt that Christ is absent in the presence of his flesh, and that, absent in body, he sits and intercedes with the Father; and that when he sent the Spirit to the apostles he could not associate with them in the flesh.* Vigilius says: *When he was on earth, he was not in heaven; and now that he is in heaven, he certainly is not on earth.* Fulgentius says: *Christ as man is local, and according to his human substance was absent from heaven when he was on earth, and left the earth when he ascended into heaven.* Likewise: *Christ, according to the whole man whom he assumed, locally left the earth, ascended into heaven, and sits at God’s right hand.* What does Andreae answer to these things? “By the property of his nature,” he says, “he is in heaven and not on earth, which takes nothing away from the sacramental presence.” Certainly his absence in heaven takes nothing away from the true sacramental presence—that is, the spiritual presence which is set before the faithful mind. But however often Andreae repeats that the essence of one and the same true body can be really present in several places at once, much less in all of them, both the truth of sacred history and all the orthodox writers of every age since Christ’s birth will convict him as the author of a most empty invention. Yet presently, as though singing a triumph, he says: “Since this communication unto eternal life does not occur without real presence, why do you not give glory to God and candidly confess it with us?” As if he had proved, or ever could prove, that this “real presence”—that is, a gross falsehood—is certain truth! Far be it, therefore, that we should differ from these men because we refuse to give glory to God; rather, as Cyril says, it is because *nothing firm and stable, nothing enduring, will be solidly attributed to God if a creature also* (such as Christ’s humanity, although united to the Word and glorious above the angels themselves) *can receive essentially and in its own nature* (whether sub-

[Part I | PDF page 172 | logical source scan 171 | printed page 167]

-jectively or not subjectively makes no difference) *the things which are essentially in God. Where, then, is that real communication of the essential properties of deity with humanity which, as though it were the effect of the hypostatic union, so stubbornly overturns the hypostatic union itself? And who would not shudder on hearing such a confusion of the dogmas of truth? For everything is turned upside down: the nature of deity is brought down to the creature, and in madness the essence of creatures is believed to rise to the station of the supreme nature. Therefore, leaving these waves and this tempest of impiety, let us strive to swim out to the safe harbor of piety.* These are his words. Why should we not obey his sound and upright counsels instead of making shipwreck of the faith with the ubiquitarian party on the rocks of Nestorius and Eutyches?

THE FOLLOWING DOGMAS THE
WÜRTTEMBERG THEOLOGIANS JUDGE
TO CONFLICT WITH HOLY SCRIPTURE

I

*That in the person of Christ there is no other communication of properties with respect to the natures than that which makes names common; on which account the HUMANITY in CHRIST would receive nothing from the deity, nor would the things which befell the humanity pertain in any way to the eternal Son of God. And that when it is affirmed, “God is man, and man is God,” this is only a phrase and a certain manner of speaking; for God has nothing REALLY—that is, truly and in fact—in common with the humanity, nor the humanity with the deity. And that in the proposition “Man is God,” the word “man” does not signify a true man, but the Son of God who sustains the human nature. Consequently, they interpret the proposition “God suffered” in this way: “God”—that is, “the humanity united to the deity”—“suffered”; and “man is omnipotent”—that is, “the deity united to the humanity is omnipotent.” This is the dogma of Nestorius, who said, “Do not boast, O Jew: you crucified not God but man.” For this dogma makes two Christs: one who would possess divine majesty, and another who would have nothing in common with it. The one would have suffered for the human race; but the other would have had absolutely nothing in common with that suffering.*

Theodore Beza’s Antithesis to the first dogma condemned by Andreae

We too condemn those—if there are any such—who think that through the hypostatic union the two natures are united only as to their names, as though through it the Word were not actually man but were only called man, and conversely that this man were not in fact God but were called God in name only. But we deny that through this union properties are communicated to the humanity in any manner other than that in which they are united

1. Although you condemn the words, in substance you assert the same thing, as will become plain shortly.

[Part I | PDF page 173 | logical source scan 172 | printed page 168]

to the Word, as though the humanity were in fact omnipotent and omnipresent in the Word, and so forth. Why? Because this communication and the union are one and the same thing; and, as we answered Theses 18 and 19, by precisely the same reasoning the Word would have to receive something from that humanity. Therefore those things censured here in the first place do not touch us. We do, however, make the communication of idioms real, just as we make the union itself real—not, however, with respect to the natures, but with respect to the unity of the person. And we interpret the statement in which God is said to have suffered in this way: God—that is, the flesh united to the deity—suffered. For we learned this meaning from the apostle Peter, who says that CHRIST—that is, that person—suffered in the flesh, that is, in his humanity, not in his deity. And conversely we interpret: “Man is omnipotent,” that is, “the deity united to the humanity is omnipotent.” Nor do we therefore divide the person with Nestorius, but, as the definition of the hypostatic union requires, we distinguish the natures and their properties against Eutyches. Finally, let Dr. Luther answer you. These are his words in his Christmas sermon on Hebrews 1: *Christ’s humanity, like any other holy and natural man, did not always think, say, will, and perceive all things, as certain people make an omnipotent man out of him, shamelessly confounding the two natures and their operations.*

2. What could be said more openly? The humanity is omnipotent in the Word.

3. Union is asserted without communication.

4. It does not follow, because for that union it is sufficient that the deity give and the humanity receive.

5. There is absolutely no communication if neither nature communicates to the other what is proper to itself. This was the error of Samosata.

6. An open Nestorian denial of the communication of idioms.

7. A corruption of Peter’s words, which overturn the preceding assertion. To say, “Christ suffered in the flesh,” is not different from saying, “Christ’s flesh suffered.”

8. What is attributed here to the man except an empty title? Indeed, the omnipotence attributed to the deity alone is denied even to that man. NESTORIANISM.

9. Nestorius said the same thing, but the fathers of the council, paying no regard to it, condemned him with his heresy.

10. This is not distinguishing, but separating.

11. Luther wrote these things about Christ insofar as he was in the state of emptying and in order to show the distinction of the natures in Christ. He by no means denies, however, that Christ is an omnipotent man in God.

Theodore Beza’s response to all Andreae’s marginal observations on the foregoing Antithesis

I beseech you, Christian reader, whoever you are who is not wholly ignorant of our affairs: have you ever heard of a more atrocious injury done to anyone with more manifest shamelessness than this? Could I—or any one of us—really have thought that the two natures constitute Christ’s person only as to their names, or that the hypostatic union makes names alone common? That Christ’s humanity receives nothing from the deity?

[Part I | PDF page 174 | logical source scan 173 | printed page 169]

That the things which befell the humanity in no way pertain to that eternal Son of God, who even now from heaven cried out to Saul and counted as his own the injuries inflicted upon his elect for his name? What head would not do this for his members? And how much more must those things pertain to him which he endured not in the soul and body of some other person, but in his very own soul and body? Indeed, who is more manifestly guilty of violating both Christ’s doctrine and his person than those who have dared to write in so many most impious words that Christ the man has this in common with all the saints: *that God was personally united with him?* How many blasphemies are contained in these few words! For if Andreae takes “God” essentially, he is openly a Sabellian; but if he takes “personally” with reference to the Word, he becomes worse even than Arius, who, although he denied the consubstantiality, nevertheless did not take away from Christ a certain essential deity of the same name. Indeed, since it necessarily follows either that Christ is God in no other way than holy men are, or that holy men, by one and the same reason—that is, by personal union—are gods just as Christ is God, what else is this than to establish with Paul of Samosata that Christ, however excellent in the highest gifts, is nevertheless a mere man? Moreover, since Andreae would have the hypostatic union defined solely by that “real communication,” which he invents, of the essential properties of deity existing outside the subject of Christ’s humanity, does he not manifestly assert by necessary consequence that Christ the man is God outside himself, and so merely by efficacy, not bodily, in the way God is also said to be both with and in all the saints? But let them themselves see what answer they will make to God and Christ concerning these things. And that they may see it seriously, for God’s glory, their own salvation, and the peace of all the churches, I pray with all my heart; so far am I from wishing to contend with them in curses. I now come to our own position. First I ask: if the things themselves are not made common through the hypostatic union, how can the words be made common? Yet, as our accuser himself testifies, we make the names common; therefore we also make common the things signified by those names, since the one necessarily follows from the other. Thus this calumny refutes itself. But what if our man here does not even understand the subject of which he speaks? For if you ask what he understands by these names, he will be forced either to disagree with himself in his customary fashion or, from what he writes here, to answer that he understands by them the names of the natures—that is, “deity” and “humanity”—as though we were saying that these become common, that is, are said in common of Christ’s person. What could be imagined more unworthy of a man even moderately learned in these matters? Therefore, to return to the subject, I say that by the names which we rightly and orthodoxly say become common through the hypostatic union of the natures, it could have occurred to no one but the most inept of men to understand the names of the natures themselves, namely “humanity”

[Part I | PDF page 175 | logical source scan 174 | printed page 170]

and “deity of the Son.” For neither “humanity” nor “deity,” whether in the union or outside it, can signify anything other than that which is distinguished in itself from other things by its own essential properties; otherwise deity would not be deity, nor humanity humanity. Nor were these natures united hypostatically so that they would cease to be what they were; on the contrary, while they remain wholly the same, there was thus presented to us that God-man, the Lord Jesus, our one Savior, indivisibly, unchangeably, and without confusion. But if their invention were valid, how would we describe Christ from his parts? As though Christ were “the deity, the Word, and that assumed humanity.” How foolish and inept this would be! For the very nature of things does not permit concrete terms to be defined by abstract ones; instead, either the whole abstract must necessarily be described by abstract parts or the whole concrete by concrete parts. Thus in that fashion it would not be “Christ” but, as I may say after Damascene, “Christhood” that should be defined. And you will rightly define not “humanity” but “man” by a human soul and a human body. Let Andreae therefore learn first that by “common names” we mean not Humanity and Deity, but God—namely, the Word—and that man. This distinction is neither ingenious nor subtle, but arises from common human sense. If he neither understands it nor wishes to understand it, what is that to us? Let the filthy be filthy still. Let him also learn this: the statement that *the names of God and man become common* signifies not some empty thing but that which necessarily follows the supposition of the incarnate Son of God, as they speak in the schools, just as the begotten follows from generation. For this human nature, determined to an individual not yet constituted but to be constituted, obtained this: that it should subsist not in its own species but in the person of the Son of God assuming it—that is, that it should exist as an actual man. Therefore that Son of God, not indeed in his divine essence, nor simply and as he is in himself the immutable second person of the Trinity, to whom nothing can be added or from whom anything taken away, but insofar as he humbled himself to this point in order to give it subsistence in himself, is truly said to have become man. And this human nature through this assumption became the man who is God, since it subsists in the person of the Son of God. Is this to understand something imaginary by the name of the Word or of that man? Is this to say that the human nature receives nothing from the person of the Son of God, in whom alone it nevertheless subsists? Is this to say that the statements “God is man” and “man is God” are certain manners of speaking beneath which the thing itself does not subsist? Is this to say that, in the proposition “Man is God,” the word “man” does not signify a true man, but the Son of God who sustains the human nature? In these words I beg all of you, Christian readers, again and again, to weigh what is the gross ignorance in these mysteries of this man who has dared to prescribe a rule of faith to all the German churches,
[Part I | PDF page 176 | logical source scan 175 | printed page 171]

so that at length the whole Tübingen school, its sight restored, may perceive how great and how destructive an error it is fostering—if not maliciously, at least ignorantly. For who does not know that this is the proper essential form of the hypostatic union: that in it the eternal Son of God bestowed upon the human nature assumed by him that singular and ineffable benefit, that although it was taken, as regards the body, from the substance of Adam, Abraham, David, and Mary, it should become an actual man—not in the species of man itself (for so the person would be divided, or would consist of two persons), but should become one subsistent with the Son of God himself? Andreae, however, not understanding this sustaining of the human nature—since elsewhere he wrote that *all things are sustained subsistentially by God,* which, if posited, would necessarily entail that all things are subsistentially gods—therefore concludes that this man is thus abolished and transmuted into the Son of God. Yet, on the contrary, it is for this very reason that by the appellation “Son of God” is understood not only the Word, the ancient God, but also this man, so that now the Son of Man and the Son of God is that one Son of God. Let this suffice concerning the names “God” and “man.” I now come to those names or attributes which are properly predicated of the Word and of that man. Since these too necessarily admit mutual exchange—that is, reciprocal predication—as we have often said already, they too are said to become common in the same way in which the natures themselves are united to one another. For because the one of whom “God the Word” is truly said is also truly called “man,” whatever belongs to man is likewise truly predicated of him, indeed in that respect in which that God is man. And conversely, because “man” is truly said of the Word, “God the Word” is also truly said of him; therefore whatever is predicated of this man is also predicated of God the Word, because that man is God. But our subtle disputant, fearing—as we said elsewhere—to call Christ the man eternal, without beginning, incorporeal, and infinite, while nevertheless calling him omnipotent and omnipresent, denies that we make these names common, since we restrict the statement “God suffered” to the humanity and “man is omnipotent” to the deity. “This,” he says, “is Nestorian, because Nestorius denied that God suffered.” And so that nothing might be lacking to the calumny, he attacks our interpretations as Nestorian: “God suffered,” that is, “the flesh united to the deity—namely, to the Word—suffered”; and “man—namely, that man—is omnipotent,” that is, “the deity united to the humanity is omnipotent.” But how were these interpretations Nestorian? As we have already shown several times, Nestorius—whose dogma Andreae does not understand—acknowledged one Christ and therefore acknowledged Mary as Christ-bearer, under the name of one Christ; yet by this he did not mean, as we do, one subsistent, but two natures joined by mere conjunction, and likewise two persons joined only by agreement of counsel and equality of honor.

[Part I | PDF page 177 | logical source scan 176 | printed page 172]

Thus he denied that Mary was God-bearer, and because in his Christ he established not merely “one thing and another thing,” distinct as we do, but separate things and “one person and another person,” he wished this man to be co-adored with God. But in interpreting those phrases which are expressed by reciprocal exchange, we did not say that naked humanity, or “Christ’s humanity”—as the Nestorians craftily corrupted the expression—suffered, but that the united humanity or flesh suffered, which is opposed to Nestorianism. We attributed passibility to the humanity, but the suffering to this God-man, just as Peter says that Christ suffered in the flesh, and consequently suffered not in the way that he is said to suffer in his saints, but in himself. Likewise, on account of Nestorius’s cunning in explaining the name of Christ, we did not call naked deity, or “Christ’s deity,” omnipotent, but united deity—which Nestorius denied. We assign the quality of omnipotence, so to speak, to the deity, the Word; but we apply that omnipotence to God, that is, the incarnate Word. Therefore we have also so often insisted that in the subjects of these propositions, Christ—although denominated from either nature—is nevertheless to be understood as a whole, that is, insofar as he is one person, but not the whole of Christ; for an orthodox distinction, not a Nestorian separation nor a Eutychian confusion, must always be observed both between the natures themselves and between the essential properties of each nature.

Dogma II condemned by the Württemberg theologians

*That all power in heaven and on earth which was given to Christ in time was given to the person of the Savior according to BOTH natures. And that in the resurrection and ascension into heaven all power in heaven and on earth was again RESTORED to Christ according to his DIVINE nature, as though, while he was in the state of humiliation, he had also laid aside and divested himself of that power according to his deity. This dogma savors of Arianism, because it establishes in Christ a deity which undergoes increase and decrease, whereas nothing can be added to or taken away from true deity.*

Theodore Beza’s Antithesis II to the second dogma condemned by Andreae

We do not know whether anyone has become so mad as to think that something departed from the Word in himself when he assumed flesh and later returned to him when that flesh, having completed the ministry of our redemption, was raised into its own glory above every name. But, following Paul, we do teach that the Word emptied himself in a certain manner—not in himself, but insofar as he endured as though to lie hidden, concealing his glory for a time; just as conversely, when that flesh was fully glorified, he received again in a certain manner—not in himself, but in that flesh—his former glory, which he also requested from the Father in John 17:5.

1. Writings exist by which certain people, moved by them, denied the deity of Christ the man.

[Part I | PDF page 178 | logical source scan 177 | printed page 173]

Theodore Beza’s response to Andreae’s marginal refutation

Andreae will not persuade me that any of our men wrote about the emptying of the Word in such a way that anyone could thereby seize even the slightest occasion for denying Christ’s deity. But if this indeed happened, we are certainly not more cautious than the Wisdom of God himself, from whose words, “The Father is greater than I,” it is well known what tragedies the Arians stirred up. We see today that transubstantiators and consubstantiators are driven by this same spirit of error, confounding everything human and divine whenever the plain and clear words instituting the Supper are recalled to the analogy of faith. Let Andreae consider whether the writings of Brentius and of Andreae himself have not rather given many an occasion to deny Christ’s true deity, since they wrote that this alone distinguishes Christ’s deity from Peter’s: God poured only some properties into Peter, but all of them into Christ; and Christ has this in common with all people, that God personally united himself with him—whatever coloring they now smear upon these blasphemies.

Dogma III condemned by the Württemberg theologians

*That the WHOLE person of Christ, according to EACH nature, with respect to his office, remains UNDER THE DEITY. And this savors of Arianism. For a deity is attributed to Christ which is UNDER THE DEITY, in whatever respect this may be.*

Theodore Beza’s Antithesis III to the dogma condemned by Andreae

We teach that the office of mediator belongs both to the whole Christ and to the whole of Christ, but with his operations distinct just as his natures are. Therefore the humanity has always been below the Word, and remains so even now, however glorified; but the Word—although, insofar as he is redeemer, he is mediator between the church and the Father, purchased us with his own blood, and is the church’s head—is not on that account called “God” merely equivocally, nor is he a person inferior to the Father.

1. By “distinct” they understand operations acting separately, without any communication. This is false. He answers nothing to the thesis.

[Part I | PDF page 179 | logical source scan 178 | printed page 174]

Theodore Beza’s response to Andreae’s marginal observation

Andreae clearly knows our mind better than we ourselves do. Is he perhaps also omniscient and a knower of hearts? Yet he can hardly seem even knowledgeable, since he does not know the difference between distinction and separation. It is as though one dissolved a human being by distinguishing the body and its proper actions from the soul and its proper actions; or cut the one essence of deity into parts by distinguishing the divine persons through their proper notions; or, in the outward operations of the Trinity, as they speak in the schools, which are common, nevertheless attributes to each person what is his own.

Dogma IV condemned by the Württemberg theologians

*That Christ according to his humanity is wholly incapable of omnipotence and the other properties of the divine nature; that he, according to his human nature, has no communication with God’s omnipotence, but possesses a power intermediate between the divine power and that of angels and men.*

Theodore Beza’s Antithesis to the fourth dogma condemned by Andreae

We affirm that Christ’s humanity, as a subject always inferior to deity, cannot without blasphemy be made equal to the Word who assumed it, either in essence or in authority—not an absolute authority, as the matter itself also shows. And Paul expressly declares this in 1 Corinthians 15 when, discussing the power of this kingdom, he says that the one who subjected all things to the Son is excepted. But the Word himself, although distinguished in person from the Father and by office mediator between the Father and the church, is nevertheless one with the Father according to essence and, insofar as he is Son, equal to his Father.

1. “Authority” signifies not God’s absolute power—which is the very essence of deity—but participation in it.

Theodore Beza’s response to Andreae’s marginal observation

If it is participated, then it is a created quality. In this sense the fathers rightly said that Christ was both greater and lesser than himself.

Dogma V condemned by the Württemberg theologians

*That it is asserted that definite limits were set for Christ according to his human spirit—namely, how much he ought to know, and that he does not know more than is fitting for him and necessarily required for carrying out his office as judge. And that Christ does not even today possess a perfect knowledge of God and all his works. And that it is impossible for Christ, in his human spirit, to have known whatever has existed from eternity, whatever is happening everywhere even now, and whatever will come to pass throughout all eternity.*

[Part I | PDF page 180 | logical source scan 179 | printed page 175]

Theodore Beza’s Antithesis to the fifth dogma condemned by Andreae

Luther already answered you in the passage cited above: Christ as man—that is, according to the man—could and knew only as much as the Word willed him to be able and to know. And certainly we must acknowledge that Christ the man knew and knows all things without any mutation according to his deity; but during the time of his humiliation, according to his humanity, he was ignorant of many things for our sake, although in his glory nothing of ours is unknown to him.

1. Luther intended to teach nothing less; therefore let the passage be inspected and carefully examined.

2. Yet they neither wish nor dare to assert openly that he knows all things.

Theodore Beza’s response to Andreae’s marginal observations

Let the teachers of omnipresence dispute as long and as much as they please about the gifts of the divine Spirit, and above all of knowledge and wisdom, poured most abundantly into Christ’s most pure and unspeakably blessed soul, both during the course of this perishable life and in that ineffable glory. Even now we do not believe that the Word’s nature or any essential property is one and the same as the qualities of a man, however glorified, which are their effects; and although these now surpass our comprehension, nevertheless, since they are created and inhere in a finite humanity, they must be finite. Therefore we call blasphemous and intolerable the equalization which Brentius drew from the pits of Eutyches, and which Andreae gulped down and now offers to the churches. Yet who among us would deny that this man is omnipresent, omniscient—in short, whatever God is without exception? Although we profess that he is not this in himself, nor by any real property outside himself, nevertheless we profess that he is truly that God, because by the appellation “man” the whole person is also understood as one subsistent. And Luther certainly agrees openly with us, as anyone who examines the passage I cited from him will acknowledge, censuring the contrary dogma not only as false but also as ridiculous. Yet not even here, considering the present times, did he speak cautiously enough. For it is a very different thing to make Christ’s humanity omnipotent by God’s essential omnipotence,

[Part I | PDF page 181 | logical source scan 180 | printed page 176, ending before the missing printed p. 177 lacuna]

omniscient by omniscience, or omnipresent by omnipresence—which things are plainly to be detested—from calling this man omnipotent, omniscient, or omnipresent, insofar as the whole person is thus signified collectively, but not insofar as the whole of him is considered distinctly. No one can deny this without dissolving the person. In order, however, that Luther’s opinion may be understood, I would have Andreae consider the following words of his also, without prejudice, so that through the same guide by whom he wandered into other byways he might, if possible, be recalled to the way of truth in this argument. “What Luke says, ‘He grew strong in spirit,’” he says, “is not a fiction; rather, the matter came to pass quite simply as the words sound: as much as he increased in age, so much he increased in size; as much as he increased in size, so much he increased in reason; and as he became more advanced in reason, so he became stronger in spirit and fuller of wisdom before God in himself and before men. Nor do these words require another gloss, but this natural understanding of this passage is Christian and free from danger.” And shortly afterward: “Since it is so arranged that all human beings increase in body, reason, spirit, and wisdom, and there is no one who behaves otherwise, Luke wished here to predicate of Christ what Paul wrote in Philippians 2, that Christ emptied himself, and so forth—namely, that Christ conducted himself in the same manner and, with the passage of time, received increases also in other things; but that he received them more excellently than others because he was nobler in natural ability and more richly adorned than others with divine gifts. In this way nothing in Luke’s words will be difficult, provided that the subtle trifles of those arguments are kept away.”

Dogma VI condemned by the Württemberg theologians

*That, because of the property of the human nature, it is impossible for Christ to be present with his body AT THE SAME TIME IN SEVERAL PLACES, much less in all places. And that after Christ has redeemed us by his passion and death, he has no further dealings with us according to his humanity.*

Theodore Beza’s Antithesis to the sixth dogma condemned by Andreae

We affirm the first part of this article; we abominate the latter.

1. They openly reject multipresence and omnipresence.

Theodore Beza’s response to Andreae’s marginal observation

Your essential presence—whether omnipresence or multipresence—of Christ’s body on earth we repudiate in words and heart as a monstrous prodigy by which the foundation of the Christian faith is overturned and Marcion’s specter is established on the throne of God.

[Part I | missing 1588 leaf | printed page 177 supplied from the 1587 first-edition witness]

But the true and most efficacious presence even of Christ’s flesh itself, by which without any change of place he offers himself spiritually and most efficaciously to the souls of believers, spiritually to be beheld, apprehended, and enjoyed through faith, both in the administration of the Word and of the sacraments, and especially of the Lord’s Supper, we not only do not deny, but stoutly defend against all adversaries as the only true presence.

Dogma VII of the Württemberg theologians

*That it is taught that no other power and efficacy of giving life is to be attributed to Christ’s flesh except insofar as Christ redeemed us in the flesh by his passion.*

Theodore Beza’s response VII

Certainly this would have been said falsely against us, as is evident from our response to Thesis 15 above.

1. A concealment of the error.

Response to the marginal refutation

Rather, Andreae thus repeats the stale calumny. For it is added in Thesis 15—something Andreae passes over deceptively—that it is also and is called life-giving because only through the intervention of this flesh, spiritually communicated to us through faith, do we obtain that eternal life from Christ, God and man.

Dogma VIII of the Württemberg theologians

*That he who directs his worship or invocation to Christ’s flesh itself—even as it remains in that union of the person—is accursed from the mouth of God himself, because Christ is our FELLOW SERVANT—as though he were not the Lord of glory—and therefore that he who worships Christ’s flesh, even glorified and remaining in the union of the person, is an idolater and a blasphemer against God, and so forth.*

Jacob Andreae, Doctor.

Lucas Osiander.

Theodore Beza’s response VIII

We also answered that which was undeservedly objected against us—if these things are said against us—at Thesis 20.

1. They are said of Daneau, whose exact words are recited here and frequently repeated elsewhere.

Montbéliard, 24 March 1586.

Theodore Beza, minister of the Genevan church.

Abraham Musculus, minister of the Bernese church.

Antoine de La Faye, minister of the Genevan church.

[Part I | PDF page 182 | logical source scan 181 | printed page 178; return to the 1588 second-edition witness]

Claude Aubery, Doctor, professor of philosophy in the Academy of Lausanne.

Peter Hübner, public professor of the Greek language in the Bernese Academy.

Theodore Beza’s response to Andreae’s marginal observation

I did not wish to produce Daneau—a most learned man and stout defender of the truth against Andreae in this argument also—as a defendant in this disputation, not only because I had no mandate from one who was absent, but also because I knew that in an excellent cause he had no need at all of my advocacy. For we know what he thinks about this matter in this Genevan church, in which for several years he faithfully set forth sound doctrine both by speaking and by writing, until we were compelled to yield him to the school of Leiden in Holland when it summoned him with the most earnest entreaties. Indeed, nothing can be said so well or so fittingly that it cannot be twisted elsewhere; we ourselves experience this daily. Certainly, what is said against him here betrays Andreae’s ignorance as well. For when Christ is called “the Lord of glory” by the apostle, this is said not with respect to his flesh, however glorious, but with respect to his own eternal deity. Nor does anything prevent him who, even insofar as he is man, rules over all created things from nevertheless always remaining in this flesh beneath him who subjected all things to him. In this sense, as we have often said already, he is also called by the Fathers less than himself; and, as Augustine says, glory did not take away his nature. I admit that the name “fellow servant” is harsh. Nevertheless, we showed above from Augustine that the form of a servant is attributed to Christ’s flesh even when he will judge the world in the highest glory. Nor did Daneau ever deny that Christ’s flesh also is to be worshiped, but not simply insofar as it is flesh, for, as Cyril says more than once, this would be pure idolatry; rather, insofar as it is Christ’s own flesh. Since the Fathers teach this most expressly in so many places, the scholastics also vindicate worship for the deity alone—although never separated from the humanity—and Daneau himself answered Andreae most fully, there is no reason for us to repeat what has already been done. It remains, therefore, as regards this passage, for us to append the things which, collected from the entire disputation, were sent to the collocutors so that all might understand on which matters we agreed and likewise on which we did not agree, but which on the following day they judged should be returned to us. Whether they were rightly or wrongly extracted by us from the mutually exchanged theses and antitheses and the matters disputed on both sides, let the judgment rest with the readers.

[Part I | PDF page 183 | logical source scan 182 | printed page 179]

IN THE SECOND DISPUTATION

the same men treated the person of Christ, or the hypostatic union and its effects, concerning which there was agreement on both sides:

I. *That in the hypostatic union two perfect natures—the divine, in the Word (*τῷ λόγῳ*), and the human, created by divine omnipotence from the substance of the Virgin Mary and assumed—remained intact together with all their essential properties, without any mutation, confusion, or commingling.*

II. *That by this union one subsisting entity (*ὑφιστάμενον*), or one person, is constituted. For this human nature was not first created or subsisting by itself, but was created and assumed simultaneously and at the same moment, and received from the assuming Word (*λόγῳ*) the subsistence which is now not twofold.*

III. *That this unity of the person is perpetual and entirely indissoluble for eternity, by which it came about that the Word (*ὁ λόγος*) is forever that true man assumed from the Virgin Mary, and conversely that man assumed from the Virgin Mary is forever that true God, the Word (*ὁ λόγος*).*

IV. *That in this hypostatic union the assuming divine nature far excels the assumed nature, since the latter was created and has subsistence in the Word (*λόγῳ*), while the former is divine, the creator, and supplies subsistence to the assumed nature itself; although by that one union both the Word (*ὁ λόγος*) is united to that man and that man to the Word (*τῷ λόγῳ*).*

V. *That this man, in addition to being God, received through that union gifts infused into him which are ineffable to us and as great as can be divinely implanted in any created thing, so that in this respect also—which is called habitual grace in the schools—he was exalted above even all the blessed angels.*

VI. *That by the communication of properties (*ἰδιωμάτων*), or of essential properties, no transference of those properties is understood, whether from the divine nature into the human or from the human into the divine.*

VII. *That these propositions, in which either human properties are attributed to the Word (*τῷ λόγῳ*)—as when we say that God was crucified—or divine properties are attributed to the man—as when it is said that this man, speaking with Nicodemus on earth, was at the same time in heaven—are not empty and to be understood in name only and without the reality, but are predicated truly and in actual fact of that person of Christ insofar as he is one. Consequently, in these propositions God is understood not simply as God but as God incarnate; and conversely that man is understood not simply as man but as a man received by God (*θεῷ δεκτὰ*), that is, to speak in this way, deified or assumed by the Word into the unity of the person.*

[Part I | PDF page 184 | logical source scan 183 | printed page 180]

BUT THERE WAS NO AGREEMENT ON THIS:

I. *That the Reverend Württemberg collocutors judge that this communication of properties must be established as an effect of the hypostatic union and that it has place both in the abstract term “humanity” and in the concrete appellation “man.” Consequently, they judge that by the hypostatic union that humanity is said truly and really to be omnipresent, omniscient, omnipotent, life-giving, and adorable, just as all those things are said of the assumed man.*

*The collocutors of the other party, however, teach that, just as even in the hypostatic union it is not rightly said that the humanity is deity, although it is truly said that this man is God, so it cannot rightly be said—even in that union—that the humanity is omnipresent, omniscient, omnipotent, life-giving, and adorable, and so forth, although it is truly said that this man, because of the unity of the person, is omnipotent, omniscient, omnipresent, life-giving, and adorable.*

II. *That the Reverend Württemberg collocutors are unwilling that this communication of properties be reciprocal, and therefore assert that it neither can nor ought to be said that the deity suffered, was crucified, and so forth, as it is truly said that the humanity is omnipotent, omniscient, and omnipresent. This is because, they say, in the hypostatic union the deity received nothing from the humanity, while the humanity received from the deity all things communicated to it.*

*The collocutors of the other party, however, assert that, just as in concrete propositions by the hypostatic union it is said as reciprocally that God the Word (*ὁ λόγος*) suffered, and so forth, as that this man is omnipotent, and so forth, so also—because, notwithstanding the preeminence of the assuming deity, there is one and the same and wholly reciprocal union—by one and the same rationale neither of these propositions is true: “the humanity is omnipresent, omnipotent,” and so forth; and “the deity was created, suffered,” and so forth.*

III. *The same is the explanation concerning the power of the flesh to give life and its worship, which the Reverend Württemberg collocutors themselves also attribute to the humanity in the abstract.*

*The collocutors of the other party assign those things only to the man in the concrete. But besides this, they acknowledge that the humanity in the abstract—yet always as united—is deservedly called life-giving for two reasons. First, because in that humanity the Word (*ὁ λόγος*) fulfilled all things that pertained to our reconciliation and through it even now rules and preserves us. Second, because only through the intervention of that humanity apprehended by us through faith does Christ communicate to his members that saving power proper to his deity and, finally, all his benefits. Thus also the collocutors of the other party by no means separate the worship of the humanity from the worship of the deity of the Word (*τοῦ λόγου*), yet they worship the humanity not in itself or considered absolutely, but relatively: that is, because it is united to God the Word (*τῷ λόγῳ*), or because Christ’s deity—which is the proper and absolute object of true worship—is not deity simply, but deity incarnate, and that humanity is not humanity simply, but the humanity of the Word (*τοῦ λόγου*).*

Let what has been said thus far about these matters suffice. But as for the remaining three sub-

[Part I | PDF page 185 | logical source scan 184 | printed page 181]

jects—Baptism, images, and predestination—and the arguments of the colloquy itself held between us, interpolated by him as he himself pleased, I am prevented from pursuing them at present partly by the narrowness of the time remaining before the approaching fair, and partly by better occupations, which I hope will prove more useful to the churches, and which are certainly more suited to my age and condition. I have resolved to spend in these whatever of life the Lord wills should remain to me, as he grants me leisure. So provoke Beza, prick him, stab him, tear him apart, whoever and however many you are and in whatever manner you please, who profit from laying waste churches and schools and seek a name by persecuting those unlike yourselves. He is not so mad as to hope that he can restrain the spirit of error and curb vanity, than which Augustine truly wrote that nothing is more loquacious. Therefore, once he has discharged this final task, he himself will take vengeance on you by silence alone. But that God, the Best and Greatest, will at length heal the wounds of his church, either by suggesting to pious princes and wise governors of commonwealths counsels more suited to restraining ambitious and wanton minds, or in any other manner whatsoever. Meanwhile, as I prepare to complete those things which remain, so that it may be evident to all who are endowed with some judgment and are least contentious how far not we but rather they stray from the truth in the doctrine of predestination—those who today, seizing an argument plausible among the unlearned populace, persecute us with their tribunician clamor and most bitter writings as though we were ill-omened and accursed (*δυσφήμους & καταράτους*), while they themselves plainly overturn the foundation of our full assurance (*πληροφορίᾳ*) and open the way to the most absurd and indeed impious errors—I thought I would do something worthwhile if, as I also did before, I appended to this first part of my response the whole true and orthodox judgment of Doctor Luther on this matter. It was set forth not more ardently than solidly, but in his own writing against Erasmus and all the sophists, explained with the greatest gravity and fullness, confirmed both from God’s Word and by invincible arguments, and indeed highly commended by Doctor Brentius. From reading it, at least better-prepared and fairer readers may approach the hearing of our defense. As for the rest, the Lord is my witness upon my soul: although I am conscious of no dogma of mine that departs from the foundation of the orthodox Christian faith, nevertheless by his grace I have always been, and am, most ready to render an account of all my writings in every lawful ecclesiastical assembly; and if anywhere I am taught from God’s Word that I have erred, not merely not to persevere in the error, but also to give thanks to those who admonished me and to embrace the true truth most eagerly with both hands. But if the adversaries refuse this truly lawful condition of disputation, then, unless I am mistaken, they show more than sufficiently by what spirit they are led.

[Part I | PDF page 186 | logical source scan 185 | printed page 182]

FROM DOCTOR LUTHER’S LITTLE BOOK

ON THE BONDAGE OF THE WILL AGAINST

Erasmus’s *Diatribe*, volume 2,

Wittenberg edition

On the will of God, both hidden and manifest

Why some are touched by the law and others are not, so that the former receive and the latter despise the offered grace, is another question. Nor is that treated in the passage Ezekiel 18, which speaks of God’s mercy preached and offered, not of that hidden and fearful will of God which determines by its own counsel which persons and of what sort it wills to be capable of and participants in the preached and offered mercy. That will is not to be inquired into, but worshiped with reverence as the secret of the supremely venerable divine Majesty, reserved for him alone and forbidden to us, and so forth.

And shortly afterward:

We must discuss God, or the will of God, preached, revealed, offered, and worshiped by us differently from God not preached, not revealed, not offered, and not worshiped. Therefore, insofar as God hides himself and wills to be unknown by us, he is no concern of ours. Here indeed the saying holds: “What is above us is no concern of ours.” God must therefore be left in his majesty and nature. For thus we have nothing else to do with him, nor has he willed us to deal with him thus; but insofar as he is clothed and disclosed in his Word, through which he has offered himself to us, we deal with him, which is his honor and glory, and so forth.

And shortly afterward:

God does many things which he does not show us in his Word; he also wills many things which he does not show in his Word that he wills. Thus in the Word he does not will the death of the sinner, but he does will it by that inscru-

[Part I | PDF page 187 | logical source scan 186 | printed page 183]

table will. For we ought to be directed by the Word, not by that inscrutable will. Indeed, who could direct himself toward a will that is utterly inscrutable and unknowable? It is enough only to know that there is in God a certain inscrutable will. But what, why, and to what extent it wills, one is utterly forbidden to inquire into, desire to know, concern oneself with, or touch, but ought only to fear and worship. It is therefore rightly said: if God does not will the death of the sinner, it must be imputed to our will that we perish. Rightly, I say, if you speak of God preached. For he wills all people to be saved while he comes to all in the Word of salvation, and the fault belongs to the will which does not admit him, as he says in Matthew 23: “How often I willed to gather your chicks, and you were unwilling!” But why that Majesty does not remove or change this fault of our will in all, since it is not in human power, or why he imputes it to a person when that person cannot be free from it, one is forbidden to ask; and even if you ask much, you will nevertheless never discover it, as Paul says in Romans 9: “Who are you to answer back to God?”

And again, on Matthew 23, “How often I willed”:

We say, as we said before, that the secret will of Majesty is not to be disputed, and that human rashness—which in its perpetual perversity always attacks and tests that will while abandoning necessary things—must be called and drawn away, lest it occupy itself in searching those secrets of Majesty which it is impossible to reach, since, as Paul testifies, it dwells in inaccessible light. Rather, let it occupy itself with God incarnate, or with Jesus crucified, in whom are all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge, though hidden; for through him it has in abundance what it ought to know and not know. Therefore, God incarnate speaks here: “I willed, and you were unwilling.” God, I say, incarnate was sent into this world to will, speak, act, suffer, and offer to all all things necessary for salvation, although he offends many who, abandoned or hardened by that secret will of Majesty, do not receive him as he wills, speaks, acts, and offers, as John says: “The light shines in darkness, and the darkness did not comprehend it.” And again: “He came to his own, and his own did not receive him.” It likewise belongs to this incarnate God to weep, lament, and groan over the destruction of the ungodly, even though the will of Majesty deliberately abandons and rejects some so that they perish. Nor must we ask why he does this, but reverence God who can and wills such things.

And a little later:

Here subtle reason will say, “A fine escape has been invented: whenever we are pressed by the force of arguments, we flee back to that dread will and drive the disputant to silence when he becomes troublesome.” We answer that this is not our invention, but a divine precept confirmed by the Scriptures. Thus Paul says in Romans 9: “Why, then, does he find fault? Who resists his will? O man, who are you to contend with God? Does not the potter have power,” and so forth. And Isaiah 58 says: “Day by day they seek me and desire to know my ways, as though a nation that practiced righteousness. They ask me for judgments of justice and desire to draw near to God.”

I think these words sufficiently show that people are forbidden to search the will of Majesty. The reason, moreover, is of such a kind that perverse people especially attack that will which is to be feared. Therefore, this is especially the place to urge them to silence and reverence. We do not act thus in other questions where matters are treated for which a rationale can be given and which we have been commanded to explain. But if anyone persists in searching the rationale of that will and does not yield to our admonition, we allow him to go and contend with God after the manner of the Giants, watching to see what triumphs he will report, certain that he will detract nothing from our cause and contribute nothing to his own.

[Part I | PDF page 188 | logical source scan 187 | printed page 184]

Likewise:

Those conditional expressions—“if you are willing,” “if you wish,” “if anyone wills,” “if you love,” “if you remain,” and so forth—seem to be set down in this way on account of God’s predestination, which is unknown to us, and to enfold it, as if they meant to say: “If you wish, if you are willing”—that is, if you are such a person before God that by this will he deigns to enable you to keep the commandments, you will be saved. By this figure it is given to be understood both that we can do nothing and that if we do anything, God works in us.

Likewise:

What if subtle reason wrinkles its nose here and says: “Why does God will those things—that is, that the faithful be exhorted, comforted, stirred up, and encouraged to persevere by the promise of reward, and that the ungodly be terrified and cast down by threats so that they desist from evils and are not puffed up or grow insolent in sins, and so forth—why, I ask, does God will these things to be done by words, when he effects nothing by such words, the will cannot turn itself in either direction, and everything depends upon the power and work of the Holy Spirit, who could do all these things even without the Word?” We shall say: Thus it pleased God himself to bestow the Spirit not without the Word but through the Word, so that he might have us as his coworkers while outwardly we sound what he alone breathes inwardly wherever he wills, things which he could do without the Word but does not will to do so. Who, then, are we that we should seek the cause of the divine will? It is enough to know that God wills thus, and it is fitting to reverence, love, and worship this will while restraining the rashness of reason. For in Matthew 11 Christ gives no other reason why the gospel is hidden from the wise and revealed to little children than that thus it pleased the Father.

On the saying of Moses, “I will harden Pharaoh’s heart,” Exodus 9

It is not enough for us if you say that a figure of speech can be present here; rather, it is asked whether a figure ought and must be present here. If you do not show that a figure is necessarily present, you have accomplished nothing at all, for the Word of God stands there: “I will harden Pharaoh’s heart.” If you say that it must, or can, be understood thus—“I will permit it to be hardened”—I hear that it can indeed be so understood; I hear that this figure is common in popular speech, as when I say, “I ruined him” because I did not immediately correct him when he erred. But that proof has no place here. It is not asked whether that figure is in use, nor whether someone can employ it in this passage of Paul. Rather, it is asked whether it is safe and certain for it to be employed rightly in this passage, and whether Paul wishes to use it. The question is not about some other reader’s use, but about the use of Paul himself as author. What will you do with a conscience asking thus? “Behold, God the author says, ‘I will harden Pharaoh’s heart,’ and the meaning of the word ‘harden’ is clear and well known; but the human reader tells me that ‘harden’ here means to give an occasion for hardening by not immediately correcting the sinner. By what authority, counsel, or necessity is that natural meaning of the word thus twisted for me? What if the reader and interpreter errs? How is it proved that the word must be tortured in this passage?” It is dangerous, indeed impious, to twist God’s Word without necessity or authority.

[Part I | PDF page 189 | logical source scan 188 | printed page 185]

Will you counsel the struggling little soul thus? “Origen thought in this way, or that way; cease searching such matters, since they are curious.” But it will answer that Moses and Paul, and indeed God himself, ought to have been admonished before they wrote: “Why do they vex us with curious and superfluous writings?”

And shortly afterward:

Although the diligence of all ages agrees with the one who thinks thus, we believe none of it, but proceed and insist that there can be no figure here, and that God’s speech must be taken simply as the words sound. For it is not ours arbitrarily to fashion and refashion God’s words according to our whim, and so forth.

And shortly afterward:

To say it in one word, this license of interpretation comes to this: all things are confounded by a new and unheard-of grammar. Thus when God says, “I will harden Pharaoh’s heart,” by changing the persons you are to understand it in this way: “Pharaoh hardens himself because of my leniency”; “God hardens our heart,” that is, “we ourselves hardened ourselves because God deferred punishments.” Likewise, “You, Lord, made us err,” means, “we made ourselves err because you did not chastise us.” Thus for God to have mercy no longer signifies that he bestows grace, exhibits mercy, remits sin, justifies, or frees from evils; rather, it signifies the opposite: that he inflicts evil and chastises, and so forth.

And shortly afterward:

Therefore the text of Moses proves that the figures are invented and worthless in this passage, and that those words, “I will harden,” signify something far other and greater beyond beneficence, affliction, and chastisement. For what wrath and chastisement is more pressing than when he strikes with so many plagues, so that Pharaoh is not merely once moved by them as though coming to his senses, but is not thoroughly moved and does not persevere? And what leniency is more abundant than when he so readily removes the plagues, so often restores good things, and so often takes away evils? Yet neither does anything, for he still says, “I will harden.” The hardening still stands and must be other than what you dream.

And a little later:

But let us suppose that the figure is valid in this passage, to see how one will escape without being compelled to affirm that all things occur by God’s will alone and by our necessity, and how he will excuse God from being himself the author and cause of our hardening. For if it is true that God is then said to harden when he bears with people through his leniency and does not immediately punish, both things still remain. A person nonetheless serves sin necessarily, and free choice does not become better through the leniency of the forbearing God, but necessarily becomes worse unless the Spirit is added by God’s mercy. God appears just as cruel when he tolerates people through leniency as he is thought to be proclaimed by us when he hardens willingly by that inscrutable will. For through this leniency he will appear most cruel, since he could remedy our evils if he wished and could refrain from tolerating if he wished—

[Part I | PDF page 190 | logical source scan 189 | printed page 186]

indeed, if he did not will it, he could not tolerate. Therefore, since that will without which nothing happens remains, and granting that free choice can will nothing good, whatever is said to excuse God and accuse free choice is said in vain. For free choice always says, “I am unable, and God is unwilling; what am I to do?” and so forth.

On the cause of election and reprobation

Therefore, if free choice has one definition and the same impotence in all people, no reason can be given why one comes to grace and another does not, if nothing is preached except the leniency of the forbearing God and the chastisement of the merciful God. Then God does not choose anyone, nor is any place left for election; rather, the freedom of choice alone accepts or rejects leniency and wrath. But once God is stripped of the power and wisdom to elect, what will he be but an idol of fortune by whose agency all things happen at random? And at last it will come to this, that people are saved and condemned while God is ignorant, since he has not distinguished those to be saved and those to be condemned by a certain election, but, with general leniency that tolerates and hardens and mercy that rebukes and punishes offered to all, has left it to people whether they wish to be saved or condemned. Meanwhile, perhaps he has gone off to the feast of the Ethiopians, as Homer says. Aristotle also paints such a God for us—one who sleeps, to be sure, and permits anyone to use and abuse his goodness and correction. Nor can reason judge otherwise about him: for just as it itself spurns and despises divine things, so it judges of God as if he spurned them and, setting aside the wisdom, will, and presence that elect, distinguish, and inspire, assigned to people this laborious and troublesome task of accepting and rejecting his leniency and wrath. This is where we arrive when we wish to measure and excuse God by human reason, when we do not reverence the secrets of Majesty but penetrate them by searching, so that, crushed by the glory, we vomit forth a thousand blasphemies in exchange for one excuse, and so forth.

And shortly afterward:

Thus it comes about that, while we do not permit to God’s will alone the will and power to harden, have mercy, and do all things, we attribute to free choice the ability to do all things without grace, which nevertheless we denied.

The cause of the false interpretation of these words, “I will harden,” and so forth

Let us examine the reasons for inventing a figure in this passage. “It seems absurd,” says the *Diatribe*, “that God, who is not only just but also good, is said to have hardened a person’s heart in order to display his power through that person’s wickedness.” And Origen admits that the occasion for hardening was given by God, yet casts the blame upon Pharaoh.

The response follows:

Absurdity, therefore, is one of the principal reasons why the words of Mo-

[Part I | PDF page 191 | logical source scan 190 | printed page 187]

ses and Paul are not taken simply. But against which article of faith does that absurdity offend? And shortly afterward: By the same argument you will deny all the articles of faith, because it is most absurd that God is a man, the Virgin’s Son, crucified, sitting at the Father’s right hand. It is absurd to believe such things. Let us therefore invent figures here also with the Arians and Manichaeans, so that Christ is not simply God or true man; thus we shall handle the Scriptures splendidly.

But figures do not help avoid absurdities either. For the absurdity remains in reason’s judgment: that God, though just and good, demands impossibilities from free choice and imputes to it that it cannot will the good; and he acts no more clemently or mildly when he does not bestow the Spirit than if he hardened or permitted hardening. Reason will dictate that this is not the act of a good and just God, because it wants to touch and see how he is good and not cruel. It would indeed comprehend this if it were said of God, “He hardens no one, condemns no one, but has mercy on all and saves all”—so that, with hell destroyed and the fear of death removed, no future punishment would be feared. Therefore it seethes and contends in this way to excuse God and defend him as just and good. But faith and the Spirit judge otherwise: they believe that God is good even if he should destroy all people.

And shortly afterward:

What difference does it make whether free choice is hardened or deserves to be hardened, since hardening necessarily inheres in it as long as that impotence inheres by which it cannot will the good without grace?

And shortly afterward: How God works evils in us

It is asked how God is said to work evils in us, such as hardening, handing over to desires, leading astray, and so forth. We surely ought to have been content with God’s words and simply believed what they say, since God’s works are altogether ineffable. Nevertheless, to humor reason—that is, human folly—let us be foolish, grow foolish, and attempt by babbling to see whether we can somehow move it.

And shortly afterward:

First, reason also concedes that God works all things in all and that without him nothing happens or is effective. For he is omnipotent, and this pertains to his omnipotence, as Paul says to the Ephesians. Now Satan and fallen humanity, abandoned by God, cannot will the good—that is, the things that please God and that God wills—but, turned toward their own desires, they perpetually seek their own things. Their will and nature, thus turned from God, is not nothing; rather, will and nature remain in each. And these are no less subject—as a creature and work of God—to divine omnipotence and action than are the rest of God’s creatures and works. Here you see that when God works in and through evil people, evils indeed are done, yet God does not act evilly, because, being good, he cannot act evilly, although he uses evil instruments which cannot escape the force and

[Part I | PDF page 192 | logical source scan 191 | printed page 188]

motion of his power. The fault, therefore, is in the instruments, which he does not permit to be idle; and evils are done as God moves them, just as if a carpenter cut badly with a jagged and notched axe. For an ungodly person cannot but err and sin while being moved by the impulse of divine power and not permitted to be idle; rather, he wills, desires, and acts in accordance with what he himself is. These things are settled and certain if we believe that God is omnipotent and that an ungodly person is God’s creature who, turned away and left to himself, cannot will or do the good without God’s Spirit. For God’s omnipotence causes the ungodly person to be unable to escape God’s motion and action, but necessarily to obey, subjected to it. His corruption, or turning away from God, causes him to be incapable of being moved or borne rightly. God cannot omit his omnipotence because of the person’s turning away, while the ungodly person cannot change his own turning away. Thus it comes about that he necessarily sins and errs until he is corrected by God’s Spirit, and so forth.

And shortly afterward: Whence hardening arises

The matter of hardening is as follows. The ungodly person, like Satan his prince, is wholly turned toward himself and his own things. He does not seek God or care for the things that are God’s, but seeks altogether his own wealth and glories, his own works, and his own kingdom, and wishes to enjoy them in peace. But if anyone resists him or wishes to diminish any of these things, by the same turning away with which he seeks them he is also moved, becomes indignant, and rages against his adversary. And he is no more able not to rage than not to seek and desire; and he is no more able not to desire than not to be God’s creature, though corrupted. This is that fury of the world against the gospel.

And shortly afterward, on the same page, column b:

This very provocation of the ungodly, when God says and does the contrary to them, is their hardening and aggravation. For they become worse when their turning away is resisted or something is taken from it. Thus when God was about to wrest tyranny from the ungodly Pharaoh, he provoked him, hardened him more, and aggravated his heart when he attacked him through the word of Moses and did not give the Spirit inwardly, but permitted his ungodly corruption, while Satan reigned, to become inflamed, rage, and proceed with security and contempt.

And shortly afterward:

Therefore, let no one think that when God is said to harden or to work evil in us—for hardening is to do evil—this means that he acts as though creating evil afresh in us, and so forth. Rather, let one who wishes somehow to understand these things think that God works evils in us—that is, through us—not by God’s fault but through our defect. Since we are evil by nature and God is good, when he impels us by his action according to the nature of his omnipotence, he can do nothing other than, though himself good, do evil by an evil instrument, even though by his wisdom he uses this evil well for his glory and our salvation. Thus, finding Satan’s evil will, though not creating it—for it became evil when God abandoned him and Satan sinned—

[Part I | PDF page 193 | logical source scan 192 | printed page 189]

he seizes it in working and moves it wherever he wills, although that will does not cease to be evil by this very motion of God. In this way David said in 2 Samuel 16 that the Lord had commanded Shimei to curse him. Yet there was no external command of this sort by which God commanded so venomous and evil a work as cursing. David therefore looks to the fact that the omnipotent God spoke and things were made—that is, that he does all things by the eternal Word.

And shortly afterward:

Thus God hardens Pharaoh when he presents to Pharaoh’s ungodly and evil will a word and work which that will hates because of its inborn fault and natural corruption. And when God does not change it inwardly by his Spirit but goes on presenting and thrusting these things upon it, Pharaoh, considering his strength and power and trusting in them, consequently becomes puffed up and proud, hardens himself, and is provoked more and more the more Moses presses and threatens. His evil will would not of itself be moved or hardened; but when the omnipotent agent drives it by an inevitable motion, like the rest of the creatures, it must will something. And when God at the same time presents externally something that naturally provokes and offends it, it comes about that Pharaoh cannot avoid hardening himself, just as he cannot avoid either the action of divine omnipotence or the turning away—that is, the wickedness—of his own will. In this way Pharaoh’s hardening is accomplished by God, and so forth.

And shortly afterward:

Therefore, casting aside human figures and glosses, we take God’s words simply, lest it be necessary to excuse God or accuse him of unrighteousness. For when he says, “I will harden Pharaoh’s heart,” he speaks simply, as though he said, “I will bring it about that Pharaoh’s heart is hardened, or that it is hardened by my working and doing. Inwardly, by the general motion, I shall move the evil will itself so that it proceeds in its impetus and course of willing; outwardly, I shall present a word and work against which that evil impetus strikes, since it can do nothing except will evilly, while I move the evil itself by the power of omnipotence,” and so forth.

And page 497, column a:

Hence it is that after almost every plague Moses repeats: “And Pharaoh’s heart was hardened, so that he did not let the people go, as the Lord had spoken”—as though to say that the Lord who foretold that he would be hardened might appear truthful. If there were any mutability or freedom in Pharaoh capable of either course, the Lord could not have foretold his hardening so certainly. Now, since the one who can neither be deceived nor lie promises it, it necessarily had to happen that Pharaoh was hardened. This would not happen unless his hardening were utterly beyond human powers and in God’s power alone, in the manner we stated above: namely, God was certain that he would not omit the general operation of omnipotence in Pharaoh, since he could not omit it.

[Part I | PDF page 194 | logical source scan 193 | printed page 190; ending at the last complete sentence]

Why God does not cease from the motion of omnipotence; why he does not change ungodly wills; why he permitted Adam to fall; and why he created us impure

Someone may ask why God does not cease from that very motion of omnipotence by which the will of the ungodly is moved so that it continues to be evil and becomes worse. The answer is: this is to wish that because of the ungodly God would cease to be God, since you wish his power and action to cease—that is, that he cease to be good lest anyone become worse.

But, they will say again, why does he not at the same time change the evil wills which he moves? Answer: This pertains to the secrets of Majesty, where his judgments are incomprehensible; it is not ours to search these things but to worship these mysteries. If offended flesh and blood murmurs here, let it murmur, but it will accomplish nothing, and God will not thereby be changed. Although the scandalized ungodly depart, the elect will nevertheless remain. The same answer will be given to those who ask why he permitted Adam to fall and why he created us all infected with the same sin, when he could have preserved Adam and created us from elsewhere or from seed first purified. The answer is that he is God, for whose will there is no cause or rationale prescribed to it as its measure and rule, since nothing is equal or superior to it; rather, it is itself the rule of all things. For if there were for it some rule, measure, cause, or rationale, it could no longer be God’s will. For what he wills is not right because he ought or had to will thus; on the contrary, what happens ought to be right because he himself wills thus. A cause and rationale are prescribed to a creature’s will, but not to the Creator’s will, unless you set another creator over him.

Another objection and its solution

This plainly offends that common sense, or natural reason: that God by his sheer will abandons, hardens, and condemns people, as if he delighted in the great and eternal sins and torments of the wretched, although he is proclaimed to be of such mercy and goodness, and so forth. To think this of God has appeared unjust, cruel, and intolerable, and so forth.
