But natural reason itself, which is offended by that necessity, is compelled by its own judgment to concede it even when convicted without any Scripture. For all people have this judgment written in their hearts and both acknowledge and approve it, although unwillingly: first, that God is omnipotent, not only in power but also in action—otherwise God would be ridiculous; next, that he knows and foreknows all things and can neither err nor be deceived. Once these things are granted, by an inescapable consequence they are compelled to admit that we are not made by our own will but by necessity; likewise, that we do not do what we please by right of free choice, but according as God foreknew and acts by counsel and by infallible and immutable power, and so forth.

[Part I | PDF page 195 | logical source scan 194 | printed page 191]

On Moses’ saying in Genesis 25, “The elder shall serve the younger”

The *Diatribe* evades this passage by saying that it does not properly pertain to a person’s salvation. For God can will that a person be a servant and poor without that person nevertheless being rejected from eternal salvation.

Answer: Grant, indeed, that this passage has nothing to do with a person’s salvation, but is understood of temporal servitude—which is not true. Nevertheless, it is rightly and effectively adduced by Paul to prove that it was said to Sarah not through the merits of Jacob or Esau, but through the one who calls: “The elder shall serve the younger.” For Paul disputes whether they attained what he says about them by the power or merits of free choice, and proves by the invincible words of Scripture that they did not, but that it was by the grace of the one who calls, since they had not yet been born and had done nothing good or evil, and so forth.

And shortly afterward:

How did free choice help Jacob? How did it harm Esau? For when neither had yet been born or done anything, each was defined by God’s foreknowledge and determination as to what he was to receive—namely, that the former should serve and the latter rule. Rewards are decreed before the workers are born and work.

And shortly afterward:

The question now is not whether that servitude pertains to salvation, but by what merit it is imposed upon one who had not merited it.

Moreover, that Moses does not deal with servitude alone, and that Paul rightly understands this passage of eternal salvation, is proved from the text itself. For the oracle in Moses reads: *“Two peoples shall be divided from your womb, and one people shall prevail over the other, and the elder shall serve the younger.”* Here two peoples are manifestly distinguished. The one, although younger, is received into God’s grace so that it may conquer the elder, not by strength but by God’s favor; otherwise, how could the younger conquer the elder unless God were with it? Since, therefore, the younger is to be God’s people, not only external dominion or servitude is treated there, but all things that pertain to God’s people: blessing, the Word, the Spirit, the promise of Christ, and the eternal kingdom. Scripture afterward confirms this more fully when it records that Jacob was blessed and received the promises and the kingdom. Paul briefly indicates all these things when he says that the elder would serve the younger, sending us back to Moses, who treats these matters more fully.

On Malachi’s passage, “Jacob I loved, but Esau I hated”

The *Diatribe* twists this passage by three devices. The first is: “If you press the letter,” it says, “God does not love as we love, nor does he hate anyone, since affections do not befall God.”

And shortly afterward:

Answer: What do I hear? Is the question how God loves and hates, rather than why he loves and hates? We know perfectly well that God

[Part I | PDF page 196 | logical source scan 195 | printed page 192]

does not love or hate as we do, since we both love and hate changeably; he loves and hates by an eternal and immutable nature. Thus accidents and affections do not befall him. And it is precisely this that forces free choice to be nothing: that love is eternal and immutable, that hatred toward human beings existed eternally before the world was made and not merely before any merit and work of free choice, and that all things occur necessarily in us according as he either loves or does not love from eternity. Thus not only God’s love but also his manner of loving imposes necessity upon us, and so forth.

But grant that God’s loving is an effect of love and his hating an effect of hatred: do those effects occur apart from and beyond God’s will? If they occur only by God’s willing, then what God willed was that he called Jacob before Jacob was born because he loved him, not because Jacob first loved him or moved him by any merit, and so forth.

The second device is that the passage does not appear to speak of the hatred by which we are condemned eternally, but of temporal affliction.

Answer: What has this to do with the matter, since Paul proves that this affliction was inflicted upon Esau without merit and by God’s hatred alone? For what cause did God have to love Jacob and hate Esau when they did not yet exist? Moreover, it is false that Malachi speaks only of temporal affliction or the destruction of Edom. He reproaches the Israelites for their ingratitude: although God loved them, they in turn neither love him as Father nor fear him as Lord. He proves that God loved them both from Scripture and from his work. Although Jacob and Esau were brothers (Gen. 25), God nevertheless loved and chose Jacob before he was born, but hated Esau so as to reduce his region to a wilderness. Likewise he brought Jacob back from captivity and restored him, but did not permit the Edomites to be restored and threatened them with destruction, and so forth.

And shortly afterward:

How can the claim now stand that the Prophet speaks of temporal affliction, when in evident words he testifies that he speaks of two peoples born from two Patriarchs: the former received as a people and preserved, the latter abandoned and finally destroyed? To receive as a people does not pertain only to temporal goods or evils, but to all things. For our God is not only God of temporal things but of all things, and so forth.

The third device is that God is said to hate the not-yet-born because he foreknows that they will do things worthy of hatred; hence the Jews were cut off by the merit of unbelief and the Gentiles grafted in by the merit of faith, and so forth.

Answer: We are not disputing cutting off or grafting in, but whether people can believe or disbelieve by the power of free choice. We are not asking who believe and who do not, nor what follows believers and unbelievers, but by what merit and work they attain the faith by which they are grafted in,

[Part I | PDF page 197 | logical source scan 196 | printed page 193]

or the unbelief by which they are cut off. Here Paul teaches that this comes to pass through no work of ours, but only through God’s love and hatred. Once it has come to pass, he exhorts them to persevere lest they be cut off. But an exhortation proves not what we can do but what we ought to do.

On Paul’s passage in 2 Timothy 2, “In a great house there are gold and earthen vessels,” and so forth

Then follows the similitude: there are different vessels, some for dishonor. This completes the teaching that vessels do not prepare themselves, but the master does. This is also the meaning of Romans 9, that the potter has power, and so forth; and the words in 2 Timothy 2:20, *“If anyone cleanses himself from these,”* are understood not of filth but of vessels of dishonor, so that the meaning is: if anyone remains separate and does not mix with wicked teachers, he will be a vessel of honor, and so forth.

And shortly afterward:

For it does not follow that anyone can therefore cleanse himself by the power of free choice. In sum, if this evasion is valid, Paul’s entire disputation has no force. He would introduce in vain those who murmur against God the potter if the fault appeared to belong to the vessel and not the potter. For who would murmur if he heard that someone worthy of condemnation was condemned? But this is the source of human murmurings: namely, if a vessel which has earned nothing except that it does not belong to its own right is cast into eternal fire, the blame must be imputed not to the vessel but to the potter who creates and tempers the clay. This is what Paul makes the wicked say: “Why does he still find fault? Who resists his will?” This is what human reason can neither comprehend nor bear. It wants God to act by human law, to do what seems right to it, or to render an account of why he is God. God must thus be reduced to order and laws prescribed to him, so that he may condemn no one except the person who, in our judgment, has deserved it, and so that the potter may make a vessel for dishonor from preceding merits, and so forth.

But if God works in this way, looking to merits, why do they murmur and complain? Why does Paul need to restrain them? Furthermore, where does the potter’s power to do what he wills remain if, subject to merits and laws, he is not permitted to do what he wills but is required to do what he ought? For regard for merits conflicts with the power and freedom to do what one wills, as that master of a household proves when he opposed the freedom of his will over his own goods to the workers who murmured and demanded a right (Matt. 20).

But let us suppose that God must be such as to regard merits in condemning. Shall we not likewise contend and concede that he should regard merits in saving too? If we wish to follow reason, it is equally unjust to crown the unworthy and to punish the unworthy. We shall therefore declare God unjust, as one who delights in the evil and wicked and invites and crowns their wickedness with rewards. But woe to us, wretched then, before such a God. For who will be saved?

See the wickedness of the human heart. When God saves the unworthy without mer-

[Part I | PDF page 198 | logical source scan 197 | printed page 194]

its—indeed, when he saves the wicked despite many demerits—it does not accuse him of injustice or complain why he wills this, although even by its own judgment it is most unjust. Rather, because it is advantageous and pleasing to it, it judges it equitable and good. But when he condemns the undeserving, because this is disadvantageous to it, it declares it unjust and intolerable; here it complains, murmurs, and blasphemes.

Reason therefore judges this matter not according to equity but according to affection. For if it regarded equity, it would complain against God equally when he crowns the unworthy and when he condemns the undeserving. It would equally praise and proclaim God when he condemns the undeserving as it does when he saves the unworthy. For in either case there is equal injustice if you look to our perception—unless it were not equally unjust to praise Cain for murder and make him king as it would be to cast innocent Abel into prison and kill him.

Since reason praises God for saving the unworthy but accuses him for condemning the undeserving, it is proved that it does not praise God as God, but as one who serves its own advantage. That is, it seeks and praises itself and its own things in God, not God and the things of God. But if God pleases when he crowns the unworthy, he should not displease when he condemns the undeserving. If he is just there, why will he not be just here? There he pours grace and mercy on the unworthy; here he pours wrath and severity on the undeserving. In both cases he is excessive and unjust before human beings, but just and true before himself. For how it is just for him to crown the unworthy is incomprehensible now; but we shall see when we arrive there, where it will no longer be believed but seen with unveiled face. Likewise, how it is just for him to condemn the undeserving is incomprehensible now, yet it is believed until the Son of Man is revealed.

And shortly afterward:

Conclusion of the whole disputation concerning the condemnation of the wicked and God’s justice

But if what troubles you is the difficulty of defending God’s clemency and equity when he condemns the undeserving—that is, wicked persons of this kind, who, born in wickedness, can by no means provide for themselves so as not to be, remain, and be condemned as wicked, and who are forced by natural necessity to sin and perish, as Paul says, “We were all children of wrath, like the rest,” since they are created such by God himself from seed corrupted through the sin of the one Adam—then the answer is this. Here the most merciful God must be honored and revered in those most unworthy persons whom he justifies and saves, and something must be granted to his divine wisdom, so that he is believed to be just where he seems unjust to us. For if his Justice were of such a kind that it could be judged just by human comprehension, it plainly would not be divine and would differ in no way from human justice. But since he is the true and one God, and moreover wholly incomprehensible and

[Part I | PDF page 199 | logical source scan 198 | printed page 195]

inaccessible to human reason, it is fitting—indeed, necessary—that his justice also be incomprehensible, as Paul too exclaims: “O depth of the riches of the wisdom and knowledge of God! How incomprehensible are his judgments, and unsearchable his ways?” (Rom. 11). They would not truly be incomprehensible if we could fully grasp why they are just. What is a human being compared with God? And so forth.

And shortly afterward:

If, with nature herself as teacher, we confess that human power, wisdom, knowledge, and all our things are nothing when compared with divinity, what is our perversity, that we vex only God’s justice and judgment? Why do we arrogate so much to our judgment that we wish to comprehend, judge, and evaluate the divine judgment? Why do we not likewise say here, “Our judgment is nothing when compared with the divine judgment”? Consult reason itself: once convicted, will it not be compelled to confess itself foolish and rash because it does not permit God’s Judgment to be incomprehensible, although it admits that every other divine thing is incomprehensible? Evidently, in all other things we grant God divine majesty; only in judgment are we prepared to deny it. We cannot for even a little while believe him just, although he has promised that, when he reveals his glory, we shall all then see and palpably perceive that he was and is just.

I shall give an example to confirm this faith and console that evil eye which suspects God of injustice. See how God governs this corporeal world in external affairs: if you regard and follow the judgment of human reason, you are forced to say either that there is no God or that he is unjust—“I am often troubled,” as the poet says, “to think there are no gods”—because the wicked fare most prosperously and the good, by contrast, most unhappily, as proverbs and experience testify (Job 19; Ps. 73). Is it not most unjust in everyone’s judgment that the wicked prosper and the good are afflicted? Yet so runs the course of the world. Indeed, not only have the greatest intellects fallen so far here as to deny that God exists and to imagine that fortune turns all things at random, but even the Prophets Jeremiah, Job, David, and other godly people who believed God existed were still more tempted concerning God’s injustice. Yet this temptation and apparent injustice of God is most easily removed by the light of the Gospel and the knowledge of grace, through which we are taught that the wicked indeed flourish bodily but perish in soul, and that there is a life after this life in which whatever is not punished and rewarded here will be punished and rewarded there, and so forth.

If, therefore, the light of the Gospel, which operates through the Word and faith alone, accomplishes so much that this question, treated in every age and never solved, is so easily resolved, what do you think will happen when, the light of the Word and faith having ceased, the divine Majesty is revealed through himself? Do you not think that the light of glory will then very easily solve this question, which is solvable in the light of the Word or grace, since the light of grace so

[Part I | PDF page 200 | logical source scan 199 | printed page 196; terminal FINIS and errata]

easily solved the question insoluble in the light of nature? Set before me three lights: the light of nature, the light of grace, and the light of glory, and so forth.

And shortly afterward:

In the light of nature it is insoluble how it is just that the good person is afflicted and the wicked fares well. But the light of grace dissolves this. In the light of grace it is insoluble how God condemns one who by all his own powers can do nothing but sin and be guilty. Here both the light of nature and the light of grace dictate that the blame belongs not to the wretched human being but to the unjust God, who gratuitously and without merits crowns one wicked person but does not crown another, perhaps less wicked or at least no more wicked, but condemns him. Yet the light of glory dictates otherwise and then shows that God, whose judgment is now of incomprehensible justice, possesses only the most just and most manifest justice. Meanwhile, let us believe, warned and confirmed by the example of the light of grace, which accomplishes a similar miracle in the light of nature.

And shortly afterward:

Here I think more than enough has been done for the godly person who will believe the truth without obstinacy. If we believe it true that God foreknows and preordains all things, then his foreknowledge and predestination can neither be deceived nor hindered. Further, if nothing happens unless he wills it—which reason itself is compelled to concede—then, with reason itself as witness, there can be no free choice in a human being, an angel, or any creature.

Thus far are excerpts from Dr. Luther’s booklet *On the Bondage of the Will against Erasmus*.

FINIS.
